Constructing a Korean Natural Semantic Metalanguage
Kytlng-Joo Yoon A thesis submitted for the degree of Doctor of Philosophy
The Australian National University January 2003

Abstract
This thesis aims to test the validity of the NSM hypotheses with reference to Korean, and to explore the possibility of utilising the NSM framework as a tool for semantic analysis of Korean concepts. The current inventory of the NSM primitives consists of around 60 lexical items and there are numerous hypothesised combinatorial patterns of these primitives within the theory. On the basis of testing, a Korean-based metalanguage is constructed here to serve in the analysing of the meaning of various aspects of Korean. This research can contribute to the expansion of the boundaries of the theory, to the ·description of universal grammar in detail, and to understanding of the semantic structure of the Korean language. Moreover it can promote cross-cultural communication via semantic analysis of Korean concepts in a metalanguage that is independent from language or culture specificity.
The results of the tests generally support the NSM hypotheses. All the proposed semantic primitives are lexicalised in Korean, and their combinatorics are along the same lines as the proposed universal grammar. However, no generic term for the quantifier SOME has been found (two exponents are proposed in order to capture the primitive meaning of SOME in all contexts). In terms of syntax, the quasi-substantive use of i/THIS and the predicate use of coh-!GOOD with personal substantives are not supported: i/THIS is adnominal and has to be used with an overt noun, and_-coh-/GOOD has to be used attributively with a modified noun in order to avoid ambiguity of meaning. Various allolexes and semantic portmanteaus .are identified on the basis of testing their meanings in the given contexts of canonical sentences. The polysemous quality of the exponent kath- (THE SAME and LIKE) is noted and the syntactic cue for keeping two meanings distinct is discussed. The inter-translatability of NSM texts at the textual level with reference to Korean is also supported. Two different versions of NSM texts (English and Korean) are supplied, demonstrating that the expressive power of the two languages is not different.
In an exploration of the practicality of the NSM method in explicating Korean cultural concepts, five selected words which embody important cultural concepts (maum, kasum, hwa, wulhwa, and ppichita) are decomposed and paraphrased. Their meanings are represented in both Korean and English based metalanguage. The similarities and qualitative differences between them are illustrated and discussed. Thus the first attempt at this kind of semantic analysis in the context of Korean is found to be viable.
111

One major concern is raised in relation to the issue of trading between naturalness and authenticity of metalanguage. The naturalness of the Korean metalanguage involves a few issues mainly resulting from pragmatic markedness: e.g. accommodating honorific alternatives instead of semantically unmarked forms, replacing unnatural expressions strictly transposed from the English-based metalanguage (particularly in relation to FEEL) with conventionalised Korean expressions. Although the metalanguage is not a natural language carrying all the aspects of natural language, the idiomatic way of expressing meaning would certainly enhance the comprehension of lay .people. At the same time the unnaturalness of the NSM text should not be problematic in its semantic clarity.
This thesis opens up several prospects for further research: (i) developing and refining the proposed Korean metalanguage, (ii) testing the innovations of NSM syntax in Korean, (iii) applying the metalanguage to different fields, such as teaching Korean as a second language, (iv) attempting a comprehensive semantic analysis of Korean indigenous concepts, and (iv) experimenting with the utility of the proposed metalanguage in various domains, including non-verbal communication, to promote intercultural communication.

Typographical conventions
• Conventions for Translation
The translations between Korean and English are represented either by K-> E or E-> K to indicate that the text is translated from Korean to English and vice versa, respectively. When translations of original texts are available, they are indicated with year and page number right after this (e.g. K-> E, Shin, 1999: 29).
The square brackets (i.e. [ ]) that are used in translated texts from Korean to English indicate the original Korean word or expression. Except where translators are indicated, all translations are mine. This is indicated by MT: K-> E (My translation from Korean to English)
• Other Conventions
Italics are used for languages other than English (for words and sentences), and
for book titles. They also appear in some quotations as they are originally italic in the quoted reference.
Underline used in text indicates that the sentences or phrases are combinations of semantic primes.
'Single inverted commas' are used for drawing attention to a term, or a concept, either because 'it is a theory specific term or used for a specific purpose.
"Double inverted commas" are used for quotation or citation. SMALL CAPS in interlinear glosses are used for the grammatical function of morphemes. There are also some instances where the original work marked by this convention is copied. CAPITALISED FORMS are_used to indicate the proposed semantic primitive in English, although they are not capitalised in canonical sentences. The slash(/) used between the Korean exponent and the English prime indicates that the two expressions are counterparts.
Bold used in examples indicates that the specific expression is under discussion.
This is also used for the purpose of emphasis in texts. The asterisk marker (*) is used to indicate unacceptability in the language under
discussion. The question mark(?) is used to indicate unusualness of use in the language under
discussion. The double question mark (??) is used to indicate unlikeliness of appearance in the
language under discussion.

C 1apter 1
Cha ter 1 Introduction
1.1 Opening remarks
Language is often defined as a device for pairing meaning and sound sequences, and described as an instrument for conveying meaning. Reference to meaning cannot, therefore, be avoided in any field of language study. However, despite the fact that meaning is at the very centre of the linguistic quest to understand the nature of language, the importance of the study of meaning has been underestimated under the strong influence of behaviourism and the predominant trend of formalism. In orthodox theory (the generative tradition in particular) meaning is treated as relatively inaccessible to scientific investigation. The meaning of linguistic .expressions is often assumed to be describable only in terms of truth conditions, or some kind of formal logic. However, the past approach to semantics has been overtaken by more flexible views in recent years. Among others, Langacker (1988: 4-5) insightfully claims that:
Language evokes other cognitive systems and must be described as an integral facet of overall psychological organization... Meaning is equated with conceptualization, to be explicated in terms of cognitive processing. Formal logic is held to be inadequate for the description of semantic structure, which is subjective in nature.
Therefore, unlike the traditional view of semantics, contemporary semantics takes the view that meaning reduces to conceptualisation. Consequently the task of semantics seems to be in discovering how to represent cognitive structure in the form of explicit formula. In other words, the modelling of meaning has been recognised as one of the most important objectives of semantics. However, there has been less agreement on an approach to a metalanguage for the purpose of representing meaning. Among different contemporary approaches, the Natural Semantic Metalanguage theory (hence NSM) . stands out, as it attempts to make representations of meaning simultaneous with their explanation. This theory advocates that the semantic metalanguage be self-explanatory to make the complex and obscure simple, clear, and intelligible (cf. Wierzbicka, 1972,
1

Chapter 1
1980, 1996a; Goddard, 1998a). This theory has been developed into one of the most useful semantic analysis tools, with strong empirical findings, in the field of semantics. According to Harkins (1995: 17):
The NSM approach has already proved its usefulness in detailed analysis of the meanings of lexical items, grammatical constructions, illocutionary acts, conversational routines, and other aspects of language used in a wide range of structurally and genetically diverse languages. It has also demonstrated effectiveness in describing and explaining cross-linguistic differences and similarities in areas as diverse as the semantics of emotion vocabularies, moral and ethical concepts, the pragmatics of interjections and conversational responses, and the relationship of language use to cultural values.
The NSM approach has become a respected research tool, as well as a theoretical construct in linguistics, through continuing efforts in cross-linguistic investigation for more than three decades. The theory assumes that there are universal semantic primitives, which are not defined further, and that their combinatorial characteristics are inherently universal (cf. Goddard and Wierzbicka (eds.), 1994, 2002; Wierzbicka, 1996a). The lexicon of the NSM theory consists of around 60 lexical items and there are tentative universal combinatorics proposed within the theory. The results of empirical investigations in a number of different languages, strongly support the hypotheses of the NSMtheory.
This approach, however, still seeks to test its hypotheses against a wicfer range of languages across the world in order to complete the lexicon of universal human concepts that are empirically established, and to refine the proposed universal core of grammar based on meaning. According to Goddard and Wierzbicka (2002a: 3), the cross-linguistic investigation may 'lay the foundations for an integrated, semanticallybased approach to universal grammar and linguistic typology'. Therefore, any empirical testing is valuable in discovering the boundaries of the proposed universal human concepts and their combinatorial characteristics.
1.2 The purpose of the thesis
This dissertation has two main goals. First, it seeks to test the hypotheses of the NSM theory against Korean in terms of both lexicalisation and syntax. It also tests the hypothesis of isomorphism in the NSM theory. The semantic metalanguage is claimed
2

Chapter 1
to exist in any natural language as a subset of that language with the same expressive power as the semantic metalanguage in other natural languages. Therefore, this research examines whether the equivalent concepts of the tentative set of primitives are lexicalised in Korean, and if so, to what extent the syntactic properties of the identified Korean exponents are consistent with the proposed universal grammar of the NSM theory. On the basis of findings from testing lexicalisation and syntax, a Korean metalanguage will be constructed and tested in terms of the expressive power at the textual level.
Second, the possibility of using the NSM method as a tool of semantic analysis for Korean concepts will be explored. The meanings of the culturally specific concepts are often difficult to understand especially for cultural 'outsiders'. However, the NSM method has been practiced in explicating the meanings of various culture specific expressions in different languages and found to be a useful tool for it is independent from language and cultural specificity. The NSM framework has shown that cultural peculiarities can be articulated precisely and coherently in a non-speculative way. Therefore, it is worth exploring the effectiveness of the NSM method in the semantic analysis of Korean concepts.
These two aims are separate but complementary in the sense that the hypotheses of the NSM theory are tested (lexicalisation, syntax, and cross-linguistic translatability at the textual level) with reference to Korean first in order to serve as a justifiable semantic tool. The results of the test can, therefore, be solid ground for the"-application of the NSM framework in the semantic analysis of culturally salient concepts. Therefore, the first part of the thesis details the testing of the hypotheses of the NSM theory and the procedure of constructing a Korean-based NSM. The second part, on the other hand, practices the utility of the NSM framework in semantic analysis of Korean concepts on the basis of the findings of the empirical testing in the first part. Working on these two research objectives, I aim ultimately to establish a Korean based-NSM that can function as a versatile semantic tool in various areas of the Korean language.
1.3 Significance of the thesis
The NSM theory has developed its theoretical issues and methodological principles over the last three decades by empirical cross-linguistic investigations as well as by attempting semantic analysis in diverse semantic domains. However, there has been no
3

Chapter 1
attempt to apply this theory in the context of the Korean language. This research, therefore, fills the gap by attempting an exhaustive investigatio:o. of testing the NSM hypotheses with reference to Korean. The results of this research should be valuable as a reference point for further study. Also this research will contribute to the development of the NSM theory by demonstrating both universal and language specific aspects of Korean through the process of testing the full set of hypotheses of the NSM theory. 1
Another significant aspect of the research seems to be in opening a new measure of cross-cultural communication by attempting the systematic semantic analysis of culture specific Korean concepts that do not have equivalent concepts in other languages. Understanding culturally different ways of thinking and feeling is vital for communication with people from different cultural and language backgrounds, particularly in this globalising world. Despite the importance of defining the meaning of critical Korean concepts, such as maum (roughly, heart or mind), hwa (roughly anger), and wulhwa (roughly, suppressed anger or frustration), adequate analytical study has not been much observed. Accordingly the meaning of these terms remains opaque and unclear to non-Koreans. The main reason for the failure to explain the meaning of these concepts seems to be related to the lack of an appropriate tool. A semantic analysis tool for representing the meaning of language specific concepts must be language or culture independent, so that the representation can reveal the conceptual contents of the defined word clearly to anyone of different cultures and languages. The first attempt at a semantic analysis of Korean concepts achieved within the NSM framework (represented both in Korean and in English) is expected to be understood by both insiders and outsiders.2 Consequently, the mutual understanding between Koreans and non-Koreans can be based on a non-ethnocentric illustration of the Korean way of perceiving and conceptualising.
In addition to the potential for promoting intercultural communication, this attempt at constructing the metalanguage for semantic analysis may also be inspirational for the field of language teaching and learning of Korean as a foreign language. This is because the metalanguage can be regarded as a mini-language that has the equivalent expressive power as the natural language itself. Theoretically any complicated concept and word must be able to be reduced into these 'simple words'. Acquiring the lexicon and syntax of the metalanguage is relatively easy compared to acquiring the target language as a whole. Accordingly the possibility of utilising the mini-language in
1 The recent work ofNSM researchers has involves not only identifying universal semantic primitives but also describing as fully as possible the grammar of the universal semantic primitives. 2 In theory explications achieved by the NSM method can be translated into any natural language.
4

Chapter 1
natural conversation at any level of the learning process will also be relatively high. Moreover, because the NSM method is used not only for linguistic data but also for non-verbal communication, the meanings of communicative body or facial expressions can be explained in a systematic way so that language learners of Korean can take advantage of acquiring this aspect of the communication. Although acquiring linguistic competence, as well as socio-cultural norms of a target language, requires considerable time and effort, the NSM method can be a useful aid to achieving these goals with less trial and error.
This research, therefore, is expected to contribute to the field of semantics by providing further support for the expansion of the NSM theory, and to the area of intercultural communication in laying out a foundation of mutual understanding between Koreans and non-Koreans. In addition it may incite further research, including in the area of teaching and learning a second language.
1.4 Research limitations
This research tests the NSM hypothesis in terms of lexicon and syntax in order to construct a Korean metalanguage. The two areas that this research does not deal with are: (i) The justification of each element of the NSM lexicon as a semantic primitive, and (ii) The comprehensive and exhaustive examination of the identified Korean exponents and their allolexes.
The current inventory of NSM primitives includes nearly 60 lexical items and there are numerous possible combinations of these primitives that are claimed to be universal. It is beyond the scope of this research to discuss and justify the members of the posited lexicon of the NSM theory and their possible combinatorial characteristics (see Wierzbicka, 1972, 1980, 1996a for detailed discussion on this matter).
As a result of testing against canonical sentences Korean counterparts are proposed. When there is more than one candidate the possibilities are tested to determine whether there is a difference in meaning. However, when I claim that there is no semantic difference between different variants, it means that they are semantic equivalents only in the given canonical sentences. The proposed Korean exponents and allolexes may have a wider range of meaning outside of the canonical contexts. For instance, the exponent sayngkakha- is identified as the conceptual equivalent of the semantic prime THINK. However, this is only within the given canonical contexts and
5

Chapter 1
not any other contexts. When sayngkakha- takes a direct complement, it can mean something close to 'longing for' or 'taking care of. Therefore in the Korean syntactic frame 'someone thinks something or someone' the meaning of sayngkakaha- is not the same as the proposed primitive meaning, for example na-nun hwankyeng-ul sayngkakha-n-ta (lit: I think the environment, which means roughly 'I care about the environment' and ne-lul sayngkakha-ki-ttayymwun-ey... (lit: because I think you, which means roughly 'because I am longing for you' or 'because I care about you... '). Whereas it expresses the equivalent of the proposed primitive meaning of THINK within one of the canonical valency options: 'someone thinks about something (or someone)'.
Also the relationship between the proposed exponent and allolexes should not be generalised out of the given canonical contexts. Moreover, the syntactic behaviour of each Korean exponent is not pursued beyond what is necessary for the purpose of the present research. This means that discussions on each exponent in relation to both lexicalisation and syntax are limited only to what is relevant for constructing a Korean metalanguage.
1.5 Overview of the Korean language
Describing a language is not a small task, especially when all its specific features need to be summarised. However, in this section, composed of five sub-sections, only those aspects relevant to the scope of the thesis will be outlined. Among the numerous linguistic studies of Korean, especially in the area of grammar, this study primarily follows the approach of Sohn (1994) and Lee (1989) and their views and assumptions.
Korean is spoken by around "65 million people" and "5 million Korean residents overseas" (Chang, 1996: 1). As for the genetic relationships of the Korean language, the Altaic hypothesis is the most persuasive so far (Sohn, 1994: l). This hypothesis proposes that Korean belongs to the Altaic language family, which is composed of Turkic, Mongolian, and Manchu-Tungus groups (cf. Ramstedt 1949; Sohn J, 1973). The present research is carried out based on the contemporary Korean of the Republic of Korea, which has been developed and institutionalised through language planning over the last few decades (cf. Kim, 1978; Hoe, 1994). Although dialects are spoken in different provinces on the Korean peninsula, Korean used in this research is phyocwunmal (standard Korean).
6

Chapter 1
1.5.1 Morpho-syntactic and morpho-phonological characteristics
Korean can be characterised as an agglutinative language due to its morphological productivity and to the abundance of postpositional particles and verbal suffixes that are semantically distinct and formally constant (Sohn, 1994:229). Words are formed by combining a root and one or more affixes. For verbals, affixes agglutinate to a stem, one after another in a fixed order (Chang, 1996). For nominals, various particles agglutinate depending on their syntactic roles. The agglutinative nature of Korean is most distinctly reflected in the morphological structure of verbals (verbs and adjectives), in their inflectional behaviour. There are a number of inflectional slots following a verbal root, which are filled obligatorily or optionally by suffixes that represent various categories, such as voice, subject honorific, tense and aspect, modal, addressee honorific, mood, and clause type.
It is possible, therefore, to have variants of one counterpart for some primes resulting mainly from morpho-syntactic characteristics of Korean. For instance, the proposed Korean exponent po- (counterpart of SEE) has an inflectional variant poasswhere a past event is indicated. The past and future tense is indicated by the phrase before (now) and after (now) 3 in NSM, and the grammatical requirements for this category varies depending on languages. 4 The word poass- in Korean can also be marked in two orthographically different ways: -2_9)- and ~- Both words, of which the former is disyllabic and the latter is monosyllabic, are semantically identical. It is predictable that a number of Korean exponents, particularly predicates, will have these kinds of variants.
Another possible variant ~ategory is a result of morpho-phonological factors. Korean exponents, especially nominals, _may take different postpositional particles (especially case particles). For instance, na and ne (see 3.2.1.1 for detail) are proposed as equivalent concepts of the primes I and YOU, respectively. However, these words are neutral in the sense that they do not have any grammatical function in a sentence. When these words are used either as nominatives or accusatives, the appropriate case particles have to be postposed. For instance there are two different nominative particles, -ka and -i. The choice of particle depends on whether the last segment of the preceding nominal is a vowel or consonant. When the Korean exponents na and ne have to be combined
3 This combination is now revised according to the counterexamples from different languages. However, BEFORE and AFTER are found to serve for marking the notion of past and future tense by being used adverbially. 4 The Korean case ofusingpoass- to mark past tense is parallel to the English case (did for do).
7

Chapter 1
with one of the two nominative particles, -ka has to be chosen because both exponents end with a vowel. The attachment of the particle then affects the original forms of the exponents na and ne changing them into nay and ney, respectively. This kind of variation can be expected in different circumstances.
In Korean, adjectives share many morphological and syntactic properties with verbs, especially in the way that both classes function as predicates of all kinds of sentences. The function of adjectives in a sentence, whether they are used predicatively or attributively, is determined by suffixing. It is predictable, therefore, that several Korean exponents may serve either as an attributive or a predicate according to their syntactic roles in a sentence. This is marked by appropriate suffixes. For instance, the English exponent of the prime BIG can be used either as an attributive or a predicate in English canonical sentences without a formal change. However, the Korean counterpart khu- has to take different suffixes depending on whether it is used attributively or predicatively: khun or khuta, respectively. In order to take this feature into account, the stems will be proposed as Korean exponents with an open suffix slot.
Apart from the reasons mentioned above, if no difference in the meaning between variants of one exponent is found,_they will be rega.rded as allolexes, and explanation for each instance will be given when necessary. Although in descriptions of Korean grammar, a term such as ·allomorphs (phonologically or grammatically conditioned) is generally used, in this thesis I use the notion allolexy to indicate that the proposed lexical variants do not differ in meaning, but differ only in form according to the grammatical requirements of the Korean language.
1.5.2 Honorification
In Korean there are two inflectional slots filled by subject and addressee honorific suffixes. Although there are also other politeness phenomena such as indirect speech acts that occur outside inflectional categories (Sohn, 1994: 359), only honorification of the inflectional category is relevant to the present research. Honorific suffixes occur when there is a pragmatic need, for example the addressee or reference is superior, or distant to the speaker.
The Korean exponents of predicates can be used either as they are (i.e. plain forms) or with honorific suffixes. Since the metalanguage is generally used as a means of semantic analysis, it does not necessarily have to reflect all the aspects of Korean to sound natural. However, the lack of honorification in some cases may cause
8

Chapter 1
awkwardness. This is because in everyday contexts the appropriate use of honorific suffixes and honorific words is so natural for native speakers of Korean that without them it appears that the speaker has bad feeling towards the referent or addressee, an arrogant attitude, lack of education or is a foreigner who does not know how to speak the language properly. In other words, choosing a semantically unmarked form can bring about pragmatic markedness.
For instance, for the equivalent of God says these words to you in Korean, one can have two different choices for both primes say and word. Firstly, the Korean exponent
malha- (say) ·can occur with or without the honorific suffix -si-. Secondly, one can choose between plain and honorific forms of WORD, ma! and malssum, respectively.
The former is semantically unmarked, which is not necessarily rude, while the latter is used when there is a pragmatic need. Since in the given sentence the agent is God, the
pragmatically preferred choice will be malssum hasi- (say words), which is the
combination of the honorific form and honorific suffix. The present study deals with the semantic level. Taking the plain form does not
affect the comprehension of semantic analysis which is mostly impersonal. However, when the semantically unmarked form brings about awkwardness and interferes with the readability of the metalanguage text, honorific forms should be considered as alternatives with an adjunctive explanation.
1.5.3 Word order
Although the word order of Korean is SOV (Subject-Object-Verb), Korean is also known as a 'scrambling' langu~ge due to the fact that constituent order before the predicate is relatively free. This flexibility possibly results from the use of rich case marking devices. Rather than word order, case particles determine grammatical relationships among constituents. In other words, if case particles are omitted, the word order is assumed to be SOV in order to show grammatical relationships. Therefore, any order among the major constituents is regarded as grammatical, as long as case particles indicate the syntactic roles of the constituents. Despite the flexibility in terms of word order, the present research follows the basic rule (SOV), which is known as neutral and unmarked (Lee et al, 1997: 205; Kim H, 2000), since shifting constituent order may imply a different connotation or discourse presupposition for the purpose of focus.
9

1.5.4 Omission of subject

Chapter 1

In Korean sentences, the subject can be omitted unless it has to be clarified, for example for first and second person pronouns. In other words, the subject is not obligatory in the sentence structure. Accordingly the omission of the subject in daily conversation in Korean is natural and is freely accepted as long as the context allows no risk of misinterpretation. On the contrary, overtly repeating the subject when it can be clearly understood may sound awkward and marked. This feature of Korean may be reflected in the Korean-based metalanguage.

1.5.5 Analysed data

In the first part of the thesis, the Korean examples are mostly transposed canonical sentences provided by NSM scholars (cf. Goddard and Wierzbicka (eds.), 1994, 2002).5 Canonical sentences are given for the purpose of identifying primitive meanings and their possible combinations in target languages. The entire set of canonical sentences is attached as an appendix. The sentences have been used for the same purpose in such languages as Ewe, Mangap-Mbula, Acehnese, Kayardild, Yankunytjatjara, Misumalpan, Arrernte, Longgu, Samoan, Kalam, French, Japanese, Chinese, Thai, Lao, Chinese, Malay, Italian and Russian. Examples other than canonical sentences are taken from other sources including Korean-English and English-Korean bilingual dictionaries. Apart from academic materials there are other sources from which ordinary natural linguistic expressions are borrowed including Korean corpus, novels, drama scenarios, essays, popular songs, lyrics, and newspaper articles. All materials used in this thesis are originally written in Korean, and the source is indicated in the Index of the used sources (web directories are indicated for online examples).

1.6 Organisation of the thesis
The two central questions of this thesis are (i) to what extent the NSM hypotheses are plausible with reference to Korean, and (ii) whether the NSM framework is practical for the _semantic analysis of Korean concepts. This thesis comprises six chapters organised as follows:

Cha ter 2 The Research Approach
2.1 Introduction
This chapter is devoted to the presentation of the research approach adopted in the thesis. First, three different semantic approaches in modern linguistics are reviewed and compared to the NSM approach for the purpose of observing differences and similarities between them. Second, the NSM approach is introduced in detail with its main principles and assumptions so that the issues relevant to this research can be clearly comprehended. Moreover, practical issues including methodology and techniques developed within the theory are explained since they are highly relevant to the whole process of testing the hypotheses of the NSM theory. Finally, applications of the NSM theory in different semantic fields, including the field of emotion and cultural concepts are introduced ~ith various examples in order to show the practicality of the theory.
2.2 Other semantic approaches
Among the various approaches to the study of meaning6, some that are particularly relevant to modern semantics incl~de componential analysis, generative semantics, and cognitive semantics. A review of these approaches provides a better understanding of the NSM approach by allowing comparison with their crucial notions and assumptions. However, the other approaches will not be introduced in great detail, and the discussion is only generally concerned with the theoretical and methodological questions relevant to the present research.

6 The concept of meaning in this thesis is used to refer to paraphrasable meaning as opposed to nonparaphrasable 'resonance' effects and other kinds of iconic-indexical effects.

13

Chapter 2
2.2.1 Componential analysis7
Structuralism powerfully influenced linguistics in the 20th century including the study of meaning. From the structuralist point of view, the meaning of a word can be analysed in relation to other words. The general assumption of this approach to the description of the meaning of words and phrases lies in the claim that the sense of every lexeme can be analysed in terms of a set of semantic features (Lyons, 1977: 317). To quote Goddard about the structualist understanding of meaning, "(... ) one can know the meaning only by how it functions together with and in contrast to other, related words" (1998a: 9). The main theoretical assumption of the approach, which was strongly influenced by Saussure's doctrine is that linguistic units are points in a system and they do not have prior and independent existence. The central thesis of structuralism is, therefore, that language itself is a unique relational structure in which there are only differences arising out of that system (cf. Lyons, 1977; Harris, 1983).
Consequently structuralists believe that the meaning of any word is constituted by the totality of relationships with the other words in the language. Componential analysis adopts this view and is often compared with distinctive feature analysis in phonology, which seeks out the smallest number of dimensions of contrast working in the system. For instance, the lexical unit 'boy' and 'man' are analysed as HUMAN, MALE, not ADULT, and as HUMAN, MALE, ADULT, respectively (Bierwisch, 1970: 169). The notions of MALE, ADULT, and HUMAN are, in principle, the 'atomic concepts', or sense-components into which meanings can be factorised. According to componential analysts, there has to be a sharp distinction between the meanings of lexemes and 'atomic concepts'. These concepts are believed to be universal and may or may not be lexicalised in particular languages. In the componential approach, . the semantic features of a category are regarded as necessary and sufficient conditions so that every member of the category must have every semantic feature in order to be a member of the category.
The similarity of this approach ·and the NSM approach seems to be that both acknowledge the existence of universal and basic concepts: semantic primitives vs. atomic concepts. However, the fundamental difference between the two approaches seems to be that unlike the componential approach, the NSM approach assumes that the meaning of a word can be analysed independently of other related words. Consequently one does not have to be forced to choose between binary features, which seem in
7 Although there are several versions of componential analysis, the differences and similarities among them are not discussed here
14

Chapter 2
practice to be problematic. In the real world there are things which do not have clear-cut boundaries. For instance, Labov (1973) found in his experiment on categorising vases, cups and mugs that the same object can be differently classified and categorised, depending on how people use or interact with that object. Secondly, the concept of 'atomic concepts' or 'semantic features' in the componential approach seems to be arbitrary while the 'semantic primitives' in the NSM theory have been established through rigorous theoretical justification on an extensive empirical basis.
Lyons (1977) points out that the semantic features that are alleged to be atomic concepts such as MALE, HUMAN, or ADlJLT cannot be regarded as atomic since they are arbitrary and have never been postulated systematically on the basis of the empiricist
tradition. In the same respect, Goddard (1998a: 50) stresses that:
(... ) it [componential approach] makes no attempt to standardise the inventory of semantic features or to constrain its size, and that it seems highly unlikely that a strict componential technique could ever achieve comprehensive analysis of the entire vocabulary, because it is doubtful that all lexical meanings could be assigned to clearly delimited semantic fields. Classical componential analysis has also come under attack for the assumption that it is possible to reduce meaning to a set of 'necessary and sufficient conditions'.
Moreover, the use of features like +MALE PARENT for 'father' and -MALE PARENT for 'mother' (Bierwisch, 1970: 172-173) do not help in defining the meaning of these words in practice, since the words MALE or PARENT are not simpler or easier than the defined words 'mother' and 'father'. One who does not know the meaning of the word 'father' or 'mother' should not be presumed to know the meaning of words such as 'male' or 'parent', which are even ·more abstract. Therefore the analysis of these terms is still not clear and remains obscure. In fact, it does not define anything about the supposedly defined word. The failure in defining simple words such as 'father' and 'mother' seems to be caused by using more technical or abstract terms for semantic features. The NSM approach, on the contrary, seeks to define meaning using only simple words that are intuitively intelligible.
Therefore, although the componential analysis approach is "widely recognised in certain areas, such as the field of kinship vocabulary" (cf. Romney & D'Andrade, 1964. quoted in Lyons, 1977: 333), where this approach has been practiced, it does not explain or describe the meaning of words in other cases hut postulates the relation between lexical items that are selected for a certain purpose. This approach is critically reviewed
15

Chapter 2
by Lyons who claims that theoretical entities, such as HUMAN, or MALE have to be defined themselves other than in terms of 'human', or 'male'. According to him,
(...) componential analysis is reduced, not only in practice, but also in principle, to the highly questionable procedure of treating as basic sensecomponents in the analysis of any language that the linguist is describing the meanings of certain lexemes, like 'human', 'adult' or 'female' from his own native language or from some other language that is commonly employed as a metalanguage in theoretical and descriptive linguistics. (Lyons, 1977: 335)
2.2.2 Generative semantics
The influence of the 'Chomskyan revolution' on linguistics in the 1970s brought about a semantic approach which was concerned mainly to discover aspects of word-meaning relevant to grammatical properties and processes, and to capture thern in a framework that dovetailed with formal models of syntax (Goddard, 1998a: 51). Although Chomsky's view on the place of meaning in grammar has undergone several changes (cf. e.g., Chomsky, 1957, 1976, 1981), his main concern seems always to have been to investigate the native speaker's ability to distinguish a grammatical sentence from a non-grammatical one. Chomsky's explication of meaning was restricted to finding out "the way in which the syntactic structure is put to use in actual functioning of language" (Chomsky, 1957:102). Therefore, the status of meaning was outside of linguistics proper and secondary to the description of syntax. Harris (1993: 232) states that "Chomsky no longer concerns himself very directly with semantic argumentation. In fact, he never did. Semantics was a carrot, attaching a good deal of attention to his model in the late fifties and late sixties". Harris (1993: 241) comments on the reasons for the failure of generative semantics, "Why did generative semantics die? (...) It made promises it couldn't keep. (... )". He continues by quoting Brame:
Generative semantics is not sufficiently explicit. ... Generative semantics is a breeding ground for syntactic irregularity..... Generative semantics is prescientific .... Generative semantics is antiabstractionist... . Generative semantics gives up the quest for universals ... .
The fundamental difference between the Chomskyan view and the NSM approach seems to be that the former treats meaning as something that can be discovered and understood from the formal structure, while the latter states that sentence structure can
16

Chapter 2
be formed by the nature of the meaning of each word. Accordingly, the NSM theory has been seeking to establish a universal syntax based on meaning. Furthermore the NSM theory suggests that semantic analysis has to be able to reflect the human mind. By contrast, the different versions of meaning analysis inspired by the Chomskyan view are often criticised for its inability to elucidate conceptual structures of the human mind. Harris (1993: 249) states:

Bloomfield banished the mind, making mentalism an umbrella term for aspects of language that couldn't be approached scientifically. Chomsky broke the taboo about discussing the mind, but he has a very similar sort of chastity belt for keeping his grammar pure, performance. Just as Bloomfield's mentalism was one way of keeping meaning away from form, by consigning it to psychology and sociology, so Chomsky's performance is a way to keep meaning and other contaminants away from form (... ).

Norietheless, the Chomskyan approach has been developed and modified in a number of diverse trends by linguists such as Katz (1972), Lakoff (1970, 1972), Mccawley (1970, 1972), and Jakendoff (1972). First of all, Katz introduced lexical decomposition into generative linguistics (cf. Katz and Fodor 1963; Katz 1972). Katz and Fodor (1963) made the first explicit proposals for the integration of syntax and semantics within the Chomskyan framework. They assumed the existence of a selfcontained syntactic component of a grammar, specifying the syntactic structure and lexical content of each sentence of the language (Goddard, 1998a: 50). For -them, the meanings of both lexical items and larger constituents are regarded as concepts, analysable into simpler atomic concepts which are represented by 'semantic markers' and 'distinguishers' (Fodor, 1977: 65), which are very similar to what componential analysts call 'atomic concepts'. Tree diagrams, similar to those used in generative grammar to display phrase structure, are often used in order to illustrate the analysis of meaning. Katz (1987: 186) calls this a 'representation of the conceptual structure' (see Katz, 1972 for detail). 8 According to him, the meaning of the word 'chase' is '(an activity of) fast (physical) movement (in the direction) towards Y with the purpose of catching Y' .
Although the acknowledgement of simpler concepts in this view is similar to the assumption of the NSM theory, a meaning analysis produced by this approach is as complex as the defined word. In other words, the complexity of the word 'chase' and that of the other words used in the definition, such as 'physical', 'purpose', or 'catching'

8 He shows the meaning analysis of the word 'chase' by such a diagram.

17

Chapter 2
is similar. The important issue may · be whether the definition is clear enough to understand and whether the definition can be justified by the use of the word 'chase' in practice. The answer to the first is that the definition does not seem to be easier or simpler than the defined word itself. One who does not know the meaning of 'chase' cannot be assumed to know the meanings of 'physical', 'purpose', or 'catching', which seem to be equally complex. Defining a complex term using even more complex or abstract terms seems to be the most common error found in many approaches such as this one. The answer to the second point will be that the definition is problematic for it is not uncommon to 'chase something without the purpose of catching it' . (Goddard, 1998a: 63). This view of semantics as purely residual has had the effect that the focus of "research has been biased heavily in favour of syntactic solutions to problems", not on the meaning itself (Jackendoff, 1972: 2).
Jackendoff was the major theorist associated with the dominant school of generative grammar led by Chomsky. He believed that word meanings should be thought of as being composed of what "ordinary language calls concepts, thoughts, or ideas" (Goddard, 1998a: 64). He (1983) proposed that among the innate human concepts is a set of conceptual categories that are 'semantic parts of speech', such as Thing, Event, State, Action, Place, Property, and Amount. According to him, each of these can be elaborated into a function-argument organisation, by means of 'conceptual functions' such as GO, STAY, TO, FROM, VIA, and CAUSE. Recently he moved in the direction of a more abstract, feature-based metalanguage. He believes -"~ that the conceptual functions stated above can be decomposed by further employing supercategories such as Material, Entity, and binary features ±b (bounded) and ±i (internal structure).
There is a similarity between this view and the NSM approach in that both assume the possibility of decomposing meanings by using a set of innate conceptual categories. Apart from the issues related to the validity of the features, however, the two views differ fundamentally in the idea of metalanguage. Jakendoff uses an abstract metalanguage, while the NSM approach believes that the metalanguage has to be derived from a natural language so that anyone who knows the language can understand the definition. This is the natural metalanguage principle of the NSM theory, which seems to be opposed to Jackendoff s abstract metalanguage approach and other approaches that adopt symbols and diagrams. Jakendoff s metalanguage is obviously not suitable to explain the meaning of a word or an expression to ordinary people since the concepts used in his approach are highly technical and accordingly obscure. To put
18

Chapter 2
it differently, an analysis by this approach does not make much sense to people who do not have linguistic knowledge. 9 The abstract terms and concepts, therefore, must be translated into ordinary language in order to be understood by the speakers· of the language. In this respect, Goddard claims that "a semantic analysis can only be tested and improved on the assumption that it bears a straightforward relationship with natural language" (Goddard, 1998a: 68). Lyons (1977) also critically reviews this aspect of the abstract metalanguage mentioning that a highly technical metalanguage cannot even be formulated except within the framework of a particular formalisation.
2.2.3 Cognitive semantics
There has been an increasing interest in cognitive linguistics in recent years (cf. Talmy, 2000). Cognitive linguistics aims at an adequate account of the relationship between language and cognition involving human psychology, interpersonal relations and culture. Unlike a formal objective view, cognitive linguists have a great interest in the issue of categorisation related to metaphor and metonymy, which are believed to be ubiquitous in everyday language. These are believed to hold a key for understanding the relationship between language and cognition.
From the perspective of cognitive grammar, meaning is equated with conceptualisation (or 'mental experience') and explained in terms of cognitive processing. It is assumed that language evokes other cognitive systems and must be described as an integral facet of overall psychological organisation. According to this view, there is no discrete separation between language and cognition. Regarding categorising entities, concepts, stat~s, and processes via various types of metaphor (e.g. structural, conceptual, orientational, and ontological metaphors), Lakoff (1987) claims that metaphor and metonymy are not reduced to language per se (i.e. rhetoric), but are embedded in our daily experience, especially in human bodily experience. Therefore, one of the central theses of cognitive grammar seems to be the dependence of linguistic structure on conceptualisation as well as on the perspective of the conceptualiser.
Cognitive linguistics overall seeks to ascertain a global integrated system of conceptual structuring in language. This perspective on the relationship between
9 An example of his analysis is as follows (Jackendoff, 1997: 6) a. [RT (ACT (a))= 'the right to do Action' b. CAUSE (Y, [INCH HAVE (Xa, (RT (ACT (a)))])= 'Y' gives X the right to do Action' c. CAUSE (Y, [INCH NOT HAVE (Xa, (RT (ACT (a)))])= 'Y' takes away the right to do Act from X'
According to Jackendoff, INCH is inchoative, or 'coming to pass'.
19

Chapter 2
language and human cognition is the same as that of the NSM approach which also seeks to study the general patterns of human conceptualisation reflected in language. Therefore, the two views have the same sense of meaning in common. However, the frameworks used to explain meaning are different. For instance, Langacker (cf. 1987, 1988, 1991) uses various image models, and describes a lexical item with the specific usage of the lexical item, schema and prototype, and their interrelationships by virtue of various categorising relations. According to the view of cognitive grammar, semantic structures (predications) are characterised in relation to cognitive structures called 'domains'. Some domains that are more elementary than others and cannot be further reduced, are called 'basic domains'.
Although the 'basic domains' seem to be similar to what the NSM theory calls 'semantic primes', they differ in that 'basic domains' are more abstract concepts and have not been tested cross-linguistically while semantic primes are simple and basic concepts lexicalised in all languages and used with natural language grammar. The former are such concepts as 'space', 'colour', 'pitch', 'taste', 'temperature', 'experience of time', and 'certain emotions', while the latter are words such as I, YOU, SOMETHNG, HAPPEN, SAY, and DO. Another difference between the two approaches is the use of image schemas. The NSM theory avoids using diagrams or schemas that are not intuitively understandable, and which have to be translated into natural language, while cognitive grammar explains cognitive patterns using diagrams that are artificial.
More recently, Talmy proposes that humans have a cognitive system- that has evolved to acquire, manifest, and transmit cultural structure in a way comparable to that of the presumed cognitive system for language. His ultimate aim seems to be "to understand the general character of conceptual structure in human cognition" (Talmy, 2000:17). According to him, one can:
(...) isolate elements separately within the domain of meaning and within the domain of surface expression. These are 'semantic elements' such as 'Motion', 'Path', 'Figure', 'Ground', 'Manner', and 'Cause', and 'surface elements' like verb, adposition, subordinate clause, and what we will characterize as satellite. (Talmy, 2000: 21)
He aims to find a range of universal principles and typological patterns of semantic-to-surface association. In this regard, the NSM theory has been searching for the commonly shared concepts among different languages as well as the patterns of their combination for many years, so that a linguistic typology based on meaning can be established.
20

Chapter 2
Although Talmy' s concept of elements is similar to what the NSM theory calls 'semantic primes' (or semantic primitives), in the sense that the meanings can be analysed by these elements, they are different from the semantic primes in that the former are abstract and seem to be decomposable, while the latter are basic and are claimed to be indefinable words. The most problematic aspect of using Talmy' s elements for meaning analysis seems to be that there is no evidence of the universality of the existence of 'semantic elements'. Consequently, Talmy's elements cannot be used as a cross-linguistic semantic tool, unless they are found to exist in all languages. On the other hand, the set of semantic primes of the NSM theory has been established on the basis of extensive empirical cross-linguistic investigations.
Thus, although the cognitive semantic approach appears to share common ground with the NSM approach in that the study of meaning aims to find out the conceptual content, and its organisation in a language, in various respects it differs in its framework for explaining meaning from that of the NSM theory. Analyses in the cognitive semantic approach are highly technical, and accordingly hard to understand, especially for ordinary speakers of the language in question. Furthermore, it is not clear, as Goddard (1998a: 81) says "(... ) how the various different representational devices are supposed to interact with one another" and "(...) how diagrams and kinaesthetic image schemas can be made to interface with the propositional aspects of meaning".
2.3 The Natural Semantic Metalanguage theory
This section introduces the Natural Semantic Metalanguage (NSM) theory with its theoretical assumptions, principles, methodological issues, and empirical findings, as well as its historical background. This theory has been ·proposed and developed by Wierzbicka and colleagues for more than three decades, and is still under development. Over this period, the theory has been developed and modified through numerous studies. The substantial body of research based in the NSM approach cannot possibly be surveyed here (cf. e.g., Wierzbicka, 1972, 1980, 1986, 1988, 1992a, 1992b, 1992c, 1992d, 1994a, 1994b, 1996a, 1997a, 1999; Goddard, 1990, 1991 a, 1991 b, 1992, 1994a, 1994b, 1995, 1996a, 1996b, 1997b, 1998a, Goddard and Wierzbicka (eds.), 1994, 2002; Ameka, 1990, 1994; Harkins, 1995, 1996, 2001; Harkins and Wierzbicka (eds.) 2001 etc). However, the history of its development of the NSM approach will be introduced briefly, to indicate major milestones for this thesis.
21

Chapter 2
NSM theory is recognised as a significant contemporary contribution to the field of semantics. Semantics has been a marginal aspect of the study of language as for a long time it was overshadowed by the behaviourism of Bloomfield and the strong influence of Chomsky's "syntactic fundamentalism" (Wierzbicka, 1996a: 8). Goddard and Wierzbicka (2002a: 1) strongly claim that:
[NSM theory] provides a comprehensive system equally applicable to lexical, grammatical, and illocutionary meaning. It provides a clear and practical methodology for semantic analysis which has proved itself in literally hundreds of studies in descriptive semantics.
The NSM approach has been built, on the one hand, on a theoretical foundation derived from the long quest for semantic primitives, and on the other hand, on broad cross-linguistic investigations. Both aspects will be discussed in the following section.
2.3.1 Principles of the NSM theory
The NSM theory is built on the assumption that words have meanings that can be articulated. Although meanings are not fixed and are interpreted differently according to context, there are always ·some shared features, and they can be captured if the methodology is appropriate, otherwise humans could not possibly communicate with
each other. Therefore, the NSM approach aims to state the semantic invariant of a word
or an expression in a precise way, striving for clarity and simplicity in defining the meanmg.
In order to avoid obscurity and circularity, this approach assumes a few principles. First of all, the definition of a word or an expression must be represented by the natural language of the defined words or expressions. This is because any arbitrary artificial language, such as the diagrams or formulae used in other approaches, eventually has to be translated into natural language in order to be understood. That is why these symbols, which had no meaning at all before the meanings assigned to them, used in certain semantic analysis systems are not used in this theory. The insightful comment made by Lyons (1977: 12) on the use of non natural language is that " It is probably far more widely accepted than is was at one time that any formalization is parasitic upon the ordinary everyday use of language". Any artificial metalanguage works only in collaboration with ordinary language. Due to this drawback of artificial language, the
22

Chapter 2
NSM theory advocates the thesis that the metalanguage for meaning analysis has to be based on natural language.
Secondly, the NSM approach assumes that human concepts are hierarchical. This means that there are more complicated and sophisticated concepts, as well as relatively simple and intuitively intelligible concepts. Among the latter there are indefinable concepts that are called semantic primitives or primes in the NSM theory. Consequently, a more complicated concept must be able to be reduced into these intuitively understandable concepts so that the definition (often called an explication in the theory) can be clearly understood. This is the reductive paraphrase principle. According to Goddard (2002a: 5), "the reductive paraphrase method prevents us from getting tangled up in circularity and terminological obscurity".
Thirdly, a definition using the NSM approach is claimed to have substitutability. This means that a good explication can be verified by ordinary native speakers of that language as to, whether the explication and the original expression mean exactly the same thing, and therefore, whether the explication can be substituted for the expression.
Another crucial theoretical principle of the NSM theory is that semantic primitives used for metalanguage are universal, which means that these concepts must be lexicalised in any natural language. The concept of lexicalisation in the theory is used in the broad sense that primitive concepts are encoded not only in words or morphemes but also in bound morphemes and fixed phrases (Goddard, 1998a: 59). The assumption of the universality of the proposed semantic primitives is based on the idea that the "alphabet of human thoughts" (Liebniz, quoted in Wierzbicka, 1996a: 13) is innate. They are inherently clear, indefinable, and independent from any specific human language. Wierzbicka (1980: 4) states the Cartesian view on this point: "Clear notions are the same for all human beings. Their clarity and universality are explained by their innate character (emphasis added)". She claims, supporting a long philosophical tradition, that "(... ) human beings have a mental language (lingua mentalis) over and above their particular oral languages (lingua vocalis, lingua gentium)" (Wierzbicka, 1980: I). Therefore the relationship between semantic primitives and universality is like two sides of one coin. In this respect she takes the perspective of "extreme universalism" that is described by Lyons (1977: 331). According to his categorisation of the three distinguishable sub-theses for the most extreme thesis of universalism, the NSM theory subscribes to the sub-thesis that there is a set of semantic components, which are universal in that they are lexicalised in all languages.
23

Chapter 2
At the same time, however, Wierzbicka (1996a: 15) accepts the Humboldtian view that "despite the presence of universals, on the whole the semantic systems embodied in different languages are unique and culture-specific; and that the presence of 'embodied' (that is lexicalised) universals does not mean perfect equivalence in language use". Therefore, language-specific features and variations in the use of exponents of the proposed semantic primitives in different languages are foreseen and taken care of within the theory by various methodological grounds without affecting the principle of universality. 10
Finally, the NSM theory advocates the principle of 'isomorphism' of the semantic primes in terms of both lexicon and syntax: it hypothesises that in every natural human language, there must be counterparts of the proposed semantic primitives with the same ·expressive power, existing as a subset of the language. This can be seen as a mini-language and is theoretically capable of being transposed into any other language-based NSMs without carrying difference in meaning. This principle assumes that despite the differences in 'resonance' between two different exponents of the same prime from two different languages, the two exponents will match semantically (see Wierzbicka, 1996a for detailed discussion). Therefore, the explication achieved by using the NSM method in a certain language, let's say in English, can be translated into a Lao-based or French-based (or any language-based) NSM. Without the isomorphic principle, cross-linguistic comparison between the different semantic systems will not be possible, since "any comparison requires a common measure" (Wierzbicka, 1996a: 16). The principle of isomorphism of the NSM theory has been supported and validated by cross-linguistic investigation.
To sum up the theoretical assumptions of NSM theory, the meanings of words or expressions have to be defined .and explicated by a metalanguage derived from the natural language. The metalanguage consists of a set of 'semantic primes' that are indefinable and self-explanatory. The ultimate definition can be reached by 'reductive paraphrase', and be verified by 'substitutability' which can be tested by ordinary speakers of the language in question. The set of semantic primitives must exist in all languages in isomorphic pattern, and accordingly they can be cross-linguistically translatable.

10 This will be dealt with in detail in 2.4.

24

Chapter 2
2.3.2 History of the search for the semantic primitives

The search for semantic primitives can be traced back to Aristotle. He believed that there were inherently more intelligible and semantically more basic concepts, and an absolute order of understanding. In the following passage, Aristotle comments on whether a person has defined a word's essence or not. He affirms that the definition:

(...) should be examined as follows: First of all, see if he has failed to make the definition through terms that are prior and more intelligible ... Clearly, then, any one who has not defined a thing through terms that are prior and more intelligible has not defined it at all ... Thus absolutely the prior is more intelligible than the posterior...Absolutely, then, it is better to try to make what is posterior known through what is prior, in as much as such a way of procedure is more scientific. (Aristotle, 1937: 141 a, emphasis added)

What Aristotle means by 'terms that are prior and more intelligible' is semantically more basic and absolutely more intelligible to anyone. The belief in the existence of universal and non-arbitrary human concepts was advocated and advanced by great thinkers of the seventeenth century: Pascal, Descartes, Arnauld, Locke and Leibniz. Descartes held that simple concepts are so clear that they cannot be understood any better than by themselves. He claims that:

Further I declare that there are certain things which we render mo~"~ obscure by trying to define them, because, since they are very simple and clear, we cannot know and perceive them better than by themselves. Nay, we must place in the number of those chief errors that can be committed in the sciences, the mistakes committed by those who would try to define what ought only to be conce~ved, and who cannot distinguish the clear from the obscure, nor discriminate between what, in order to be known, requires and deserves to be defined, from what can be best known by itself. (1701/1931 :324, emphasis added)

Arnauld also strongly believed that there are words that cannot be defined further. He strongly claims that:

Further more, I say it would be impossible to define every word. For in

order to define a word it is necessary to use other words designating the idea

we want to connect to the word being defined. And if we again wished to

define the words used to explain that word, we would need still others, and

so on to infinity. Consequently, we necessarily have to stop at primitive

terms which are undefined. (Arnauld and Nicole 1662/1964: 64, emphasis

added)

.

25

Chapter 2
Another philosopher Pascal (1623-1662) makes claims (quoted in Wierzbicka 1996a: 12) along the same lines as the others mentioned above. He states that "It is clear that there are words which cannot be defined; and if nature hadn't provided for this by giving all people the same idea all our expressions would be obscure". Similarly Locke (1612-1704) noted that there are elementary semantic words that cannot be decomposed further. He suggests that:
The names of simple ideas are not capable of any definition; the names of all complex ideas are. It has not, that I know, been yet observed by anybody, what words are, and what are not, capable of being defined; the want whereof is (as I am apt to think) not seldom the occasion of great wrangling and obscurity in men's discourses, whilst some demand definitions of terms that cannot be defined; and others think they ought not to rest satisfied in an explication made by a more general word, and its restriction... This at least I think, that the showing what words are and what are not capable of definitions, and wherein consists a good definition, is not wholly besides our present purpose; and perhaps will afford so much light to the nature of these signs, and our ideas, as to deserve a more particular consideration... This being premised, I say, that the names of simple ideas, and those only are incapable of being defined. (Locke, 1824: 454-455, emphasis added)
Therefore, the existence of indefinables was greatly emphasised by seventeenth century rationalists. They were very much convinced that there was a common basis of human cognition. They _believed that there has to be a stopping point where concepts are understood intuitively, so that one can attempt to define the meaning without circularity. Among others, Leibniz made a step forward by investigating the primitive notions and rules of composition from which all complex notions were composed. He believed in the existence of an 'alphabet of human thoughts' on which more complex concepts are established. He wrote (quoted in Wirezbicka 1996a: 11 ):
If nothing could be comprehended in itself nothing at all could ever be comprehended. Because what can only be comprehended via something else can be comprehended only to the extent which that other thing can be comprehended, and so on; accordingly, we can say that we have understood something only when we have broken it down into parts which can be understood in themselves. (Leibniz 1903: 430, emphasis added)
This belief was shared with several linguists in modem linguistics. Apart from Sapir (18 84-193 9) who tried to search for ' elementary semantic units ', there are others such as, Hjelmslev (1899-1965) in Denmark, and his student S0rensen (1958) who deepened theoretical aspects of the search for the semantic primitives. In the I 960s,
26

Chapter 2
Bierwisch produced the first concrete semantic analyses of the universal and nonarbitrary character of semantic primitives. He (1967: 3) stated; "a semantic analysis of a lexical item is finished only if it leads to a combination of basic elements that are true candidates for the universal set of markers".
In the 1970s, Apresjan believed that semantic analysis was a kind of translation from natural into 'semantic' language, the 'words' of which are what he calls 'elementary meanings' (quoted in Wierzbicka, 1972: 10). Also, the joint work of Zolkovskij and Melcuk on 'elementary particles of meaning' stands out as a critical step in the development of the tradition affirming the existence of basic semantic units. Greimas (1966) also supported the existence of semantic primitives although using such concepts as 'atomic concepts', or 'elementary meaning components' (Goddard, 1998a: 57). Among others, Polish linguist Boguslawski (1965, 1970) brought Leibniz's idea of an "alphabet of human thought' back to the field of semantics and developed a concrete program for the search for universal semantic elements.
Of all the modem proponents of semantic primitives such as Bendix (1966, 1971 ), Katz and Postal (1964), Fillmore (1971), McCawley (1968, 1970), Lakoff (1970, 1972), and Jackendoff (1983, 1990), Wierzbicka has been the most persistent in seeking for the finite set of semantic primitives and attempting to work with a small inventory of hypothesised semantic primitives (Goddard, 1998a: 57-58). Wierzbicka (1996a: 13) recalls:
My own interest in the pursuit of non-arbitrary semantic primitives was triggered by a lecture on this subject given at Warsaw University by the Polish linguist Andrzej Boguslawski in 1965 ... The "golden dream" of the seventeenth-century thinkers, which couldn't be realized within the framework of philosophy and which was therefore generally abandoned as a utopia, could be realized, Boguslawski maintained, if it was approached from a linguistic rather than a purely philosophical point of view. The experience and achievements of modem linguistics (both empirical and theoretical) made it possible to approach the problem of conceptual primitives in a novel way: and to put it on the agenda of an empirical science (emphasis added).
She (1972) formed her original hypothesis about a set of semantic primitives: I, YOU,
SOMEONE, SOJvfETHING, WORLD, THIS, WANT, DON'T WANT, FEEL, THINK OF,
IMAGINE, SAY, BECOJvfE, BE A PART OF. This list was modified in 1980 by adding more tentative primes such as, KNOW and BE IN A PLACE, and by discarding FEEL. Since 1972, when her first book on semantic primes Semantic Primitives was published, the hypothesised inventory of the semantic primitives has been continually expanded
27

Chapter 2
and revised through cross-linguistic trials by numerous linguists. Wierzbicka has tested "the completeness and expressive power of the proposed inventory through studies of diverse areas of the lexicon, including speech-act verbs, emotion terms, simple and complex artefacts, mass nouns, natural kinds, superordinate functional categories, ideological and value terms" (Goddard, 1998a: 325).
The strength of the NSM theory comes from these empirical tests of the proposed set of semantic primitives. From a pursuit of more than three decades, the NSM theory has established a revised lexicon inventory that contains around 60 lexical items. The elements of the current inventory of the theory are presented in Table 1 below. As shown in the table, each lexical item is capitalised to indicate that it does not have to correspond in all respects to a word of the natural language. Even though they are carved out of natural language, the proposed meanings of semantic primitives can only be identified within the given context of the provided canonical sentences. 11

2.3.3 Empirical findings and the current status of the NSM theory
Empirical findings from ·various cross-linguistic investigations and the current interests of the NSM theory are summarised here with a brief discussion of the theory's impact on the field of semantics.
From the beginning, the NSM approach has aimed at establishing a finite set of universal semantic primitives that are encoded in all natural lang;uages. Wierzbicka (1996a: 34) has encouraged cross-linguistic investigations to test the tentative set of primitives. Since the natural metalanguage is based on natural language as the selfexplanatory system for the representation of meaning, it can theoretically be tested against any natural language in the world.
The first large-scale project for cross-linguistic testing of the hypothetical conceptual primitives was carried out in 1994 when the Semantic and Lexical Universals, which contains a series of studies on identifying the proposed semantic primitives was published. This book is acknowledged as one of the remarkable attempts in the field of semantics. The project included different languages typologically unrelated to each other, such as Ewe, Mangap-Mbula 12 , Acehnese, Kayardild, Yankunytj atj ara, Misumalpan, Arremte, Longgu, Samoan, Kalam, French, Japanese, Chinese, and Thai (Goddard and Wierzbicka (eds.), 1994) 13 . In fact, around twenty authors contributed to the project. Cattelain states that:
This cross-linguistic survey therefore differs fundamentally from previous investigations of universal semantics, which were mostly based upon comparative data gathered only from bilingual dictionaries. We want first to acknowledge Anna Wierzbicka' s impressive work in promoting for the last three decades the hypoth~sis of a complete set of indefinable universal semantic elements, represented through words or morphemes of 'ordinary language' (...). (Cattelain, 1995: 159)
12 Bugenhagen uses a different name from this in his second study. The updated name used in 2002 is Mangaaba-Mbula. 13 A set of proposed semantic primitives (around thirty) and their combinability have been tested in divergent languages from different language families as mentioned previously: Ewe (Kwa branch of the Neger-Congo family); Mangap-Mbula (Austronesian language-Papua New Guinea); Acehnese (Austronesian language-Indonesia); Kayardild (Australian language-Gulf of Carpentaria); Yankunytjatjara (Australian language-South Australia); Misumalpan language (Miskitu, Panamahka, and Ulwa-Nicaragua and Honduras); Mpamtwe Arremte (Arandic subfamily of Pama-Nyungan Australian languages-Central Australia); Longgu (Austronesian language- Guadalcanal, Solomon Islands); Samoan (Polynesian group of Austronesian languages); Kalam (Papuan language-Papua New Guinea).
29

Chapter 2
In the first attempt ever in semantics, this empirical investigation in an extensive range of diverse languages brought about a number of significant findings, which have, on the one hand, been a great success in identifying the proposed primitives and, on the other hand, brought attention to certain methodological issues such as allolexy and polysemy. As Wierzbicka (1996a: 14) points out, although not every question is answered by the attempt, the studies included in the volume strongly support the hypothesised set of primitives. The findings can be summarised as follows:
(1) Nearly all the semantic primitives are identified in sixteen languages. Authors of the volume generally state that most of them are straightforwardly identified and there are few problematic primitives that do not seem to have equivalent concepts (HAVING PART14 is found to be the most problematic in some languages).
(2) The need for methodology to deal with polysemy and allolexy is raised for a number of languages in which the same word can express more than one primitive concept (e.g. SAY and WANT in Mangap-Mbula; see Bugenhagen, in 1994 for detail), or in which one primitive concept can have more than one exponent (e.g. GOOD, I and YOU, in Ewe; see Ameka in 1994 for detail).
(3) Doubt has been raised by Hale (1994: 282) about 'isomorphism' although he still believes in the existence of the semantic primitives. This is because he could not find a pure term f~)f a certain prime in some languages.
(4) The difficulty of handling the socio-linguistic or pragmatic aspects of the NSM theory was mentioned, for instance, by Diller and Onishi in relation to--~the Thai and Japanese languages. This problem is closely related to the readability of NSM explications for ordinary speakers of those languages.
(5) Differences in the range of use of the exponents of proposed primes across the languages are reported. Ameka (1994), however, notes that these difficulties could be overcome by identifying language-specific combinatorial properties for the primitives.
(6) The utility of canonical sentences against which different languages are tested in order to identify equivalent concepts in those languages, are found to be much more fruitful than expected. Consequently, the method of using canonical sentences is strongly proposed as a primary tool in the construction and validation of the NSM lexicon and syntax.
These are the main findings from the project of 1994. Wierzbicka (1994a: 445) reviews some problematic situations and questions raised by different authors who worked on the project, commenting as follows:

14 In the latest lexicon, PART (OF) is the proposed prime.

30

Chapter 2
Although the overwhelming . conclusion emerging form this survey of languages is that there is indeed a universal 'alphabet of human thought', this by no means implies that no problems have arisen in testing our hypothetical set of conceptual and lexical universals.
With reference to the findings mentioned, Wierzbicka suggests the following: Firstly, one needs to test whether, when an exponent expresses a different meaning, there is variation in syntactic construction. Polysemy15 has been identified as a major phenomenon in many languages and if the two meanings of an exponent are distinguished by grammatical construction, the exponent's polysemous quality can be justified.
Secondly, one needs to bear in mind that natural languages do not necessarily have one to one relationship between a meaning and its label, as in Apresjan's idea of
metalanguage. In relation to this problem of allolexy, Wierzbicka (1994a: 446) states:
One can of course sympathise with this view, but if our semantic metalanguage is to be carved out of natural language, neither of these two postulates (no polysemy, no synonymy/allolexy) can be met in a straightforward manner: natural language simply does not work that way.
Wierzbicka calls for a . close look at each case stressing that though this may be demanding, it is not impossible to find a "rewarding" solution.
As Wierzbicka suggested different forms of one primitive concept are, in fact, found to be in complementary distribution in many cases (e.g. I and ME in English). Goddard and Wierzbicka (1994: 34) comment on this point that:
The concept of allolexy holds the key to an apparent difficulty for NSM semantics which has puzzled some observers, namely, how to reconcile the existence of language-specific morphosyntactic categories with the NSM claim that the semantic metalanguage is isomorphic across languages
The two phenomena, polysemy and allolexy, appear to have been the main concern for most authors in testing the hypotheses of the NSM theory in diverse languages. However, the study of these phenomena has inspired research on methodological grounds to deal with these issues.
15 Discussion related to the matter of allolexy and polysemy will be presented in detail in the next section: 2.4.2. and 2.4.3.
