Chapter 2
Another fundamental question concerning the NSM' s principle of lexicalisation was raised by Hale (1994). He experienced difficulty finding an exact translation in the language he worked on: Misumalpan- a language of Nicaragua. He wrote:
I doubt that all languages have words for the conceptual primitives. This in no way challenges the idea of conceptual primitives, since concepts do not hav.e to have names to be real. The reality of the concepts can be determined in other ways. And I do not deny that 'shared words' exist, of course, nor do I deny the importance of having a semantic metalanguage based on
universal semantic primitives...In summary, I think that a criterion of
terminological isomorphy for universal concepts is too strong, While the proposed universality of fundamental concepts might be contradicted by empirical data at some points, it is not contradicted by the well-known fact that it is sometimes difficult or impossible to 'find a word for' some universal concept in a given language. (Hale, 1994: 282)
On the basis of the language he studied Hale asserts that while the equivalent concept of the proposed prime exists, there is no pure term by which to call it. The question may be whether it is possible to know the existence of a concept when it does not have a name.
If the principle of isomorphism is relaxed to accept Hale's claim regarding the difficulty
of finding a pure term for a primitive concept, another even more difficult situation arises: that a certain thought expressed by NSM sentences, mostly very basic and found in everyday life, cannot be expressed in some languages at all, while it can easily be expressed in many others.
One more issue related to the pragmatic aspect of language has been raised. Diller (1994) and Onishi (1994) have reported difficulty in dealing with different speech levels for Thai and Japanese. Wierzbicka proposes that the natural semantic metalanguage does not have to have the same readability as the natural language from which it is derived. At the same time she suggests using the pragmatically unmarked form in order to improve readability for ordinary native speakers. Wierzbicka (1994a: 448) comments as follows:
Perhaps the most interesting problem arising in connection with the two primitives under discussion concerns the question of the pragmatic markedness of their exponents in a number of languages (e.g. in Thai, Japanese, and Acehnese) ... At the same time, it must be recognised that the use of unadorned 'basic level' words for YOU and I in the Thai or Japanese version of the Natural Semantic Metalanguage may lead to explications which.would strike native speakers as inappropriate and even shock1ng, and it may thus restrict the practical usefulness of such explications. Difficulties of this kind, however, can be solved by adopting different levels of
32

Chapter 2
explications for different purposes, and by using the art of compromise whenever it may be desirable.
Taking all the issues into account, Wierzbicka (1994a: 498) states: "the aim of tentatively identifying a set of universal semantic primitives and of validating them ·through cross-linguistic correspondences has, in our estimation, been achieved". Therefore, the project in 1994 was fruitful in that it raised a number of practical, theoretical, and methodological issues, and gave general support for the hypotheses of the NSM theory.
Although this comprehensive cross-linguistic research is recognised as ·outstanding and one of the best attempts to support the idea of an 'alphabet of human thoughts' (Cattelain, 1995: 171) in the field of semantics, questions and criticisms are also raised. According to Cattelain:
(... )primitives chosen are hardly 'intuitively clear' per se. Not only do they vary across languages, they are also context dependent in any single language (including English). Furthermore, they seem to be divisible into simpler components, a fact that challenges the principle of undecomposability of the NSM primitives. (1995: 168)
While he claims that the primitives can be divisible into simpler components, Cattelain' s proposals do not seem to be sufficiently convincing, since he does not provide any decomposition of any prime in support of his proposition.
He also argues about the ambiguity of meaning of THE SAME for it can mean either 'identical' or 'similar'. He claims that certain languages do not make this distinction, (he presents the exarp.ple of Longgu where liva' a-na means 'the same' but also 'like this' and where Hill (1994: 319) suggests the canonical sentence I want to do the same is ambiguous). A sentence like I want to do the same can, in fact, be interpreted as I want to do the same thing or I want to do that in the same way. However, this is not a question of impossibility of expressing the equivalent meaning of I want to do the same thing, but of how to distinguish the two possible interpretations
caused by polysemy. In fact, in Lonngu either I want to do the same thing or I want to
do like that can be expressed distinctively, since the language has both exponents. Therefore, this is a practical or methodological problem that can be solved by investigating closely the context where the particular sentence is used. This problem does not seem to be related to the question of the ambiguous meaning of the prime THE
33

Chapter 2
SAME. Instead there is a need of better canonical sentences to represent the proposed primitive meaning more clearly.
Secondly, Cattelain raised questions regarding whether the proposed combinations are translatable. To quote (1995: 171 ):
No doubt the present canonical set has proved to be of great relevance since it has supplied frames for presumed comparable answers. But are we sure that these frames are "semantically primitive syntactic constructions"? Are we sure that the items involved in these frames are adequately translatable?
This is exactly why cross-linguistic empirical investigations are required and continue still in a wide range of the world's languages. The questions he asks seem to be exactly the questions that the NSM theory seeks to answer through empirical research. However, the importance of setting clear ground-rules for the NSM theory should not be treated lightly, since languages differ widely in their morphosyntacic manifestations that one's understanding can easily be interfered with their specificity. In order to avoid any mistake in testing the hypotheses of the NSM theory, one should concentrate on the expressibility of the canonical sentences in the target languages.
A further exploration by NSM researchers involving various languages was reported in Studies in the Syntax of Universal Semantic Primitives (Goddard (ed.), 1997). This research involved various languages, such as Hawaii Creole English, Japanese, Longgu, French, and Cantonese. At that time, the focus was on the combinatorial properties of semantic primes. Goddard recalls (2002a: 10):
Topics included the grammar of mental predicates in Hawaii Creole English (Ryo Stanwood), and ·Japanese (Masayuki Onishi), the grammar of spatial relations in Longgu (Deborah Hill and Cliff Goddard), the grammar of time and space in French (Bert Peeters) and Cantonese (Malindy Tong et al.), and the status of conditionals and counterfactuals in Japanese (Rie Hasada).
This was another significant step for the theory in the development of its description of the syntax of the semantic primitives. While seeking a set of semantic primitives, and testing their universality, the importance of NSM syntactic patterns and the need for a full description of NSM grammar became an important issue as the number of attempts increased. The emphasis of the NSM theory on establishing its lexicon at the first stage of its development had often been the target of criticism. For instance, McCawley (1983) saw the weakness of the NSM theory in its overlooking the importance of the syntax of the semantic primitives. However, concerning the project in 1997, Goddard
34

Chapter 2
was able to say, with satisfaction, that "overall the results were, once again, extremely encouraging" (Goddard, 2002a: 10).
Recently a comprehensive study of the full set of the NSM hypotheses in both lexicon and syntax has been published by different scholars working on divergent languages (Goddard and Wierzbicka (eds.), 2002). The sample of six languages includes Lao, Mandarin Chinese, Malay, Mangaaba-Mbula-, Spanish and Polish, which are diverse in terms of language family (Tai, Sinitic, Austronesian, Indo-European) as well as language type (isolating, agglutinative, inflectional are all represented). The research is concerned mainly with the universal grammar of the metalanguage which is investigated thoroughly. The authors have undertaken to locate the best candidates for each primitive of the current inventory, and to report on the hypotheses about their combinatorial properties (Goddard, 2002a: 11). They also take up the challenge of transposing two different NSM texts in the language they are working on, so that the textual structure of the explications can be demonstrated. In this collection of empirical studies, all the authors follow the same guidelines to test the hypotheses, dealing in detail with practical, theoretical, and methodological issues.
This project brought about remarkable progress through various suggestions of the contributors. Revisions and amendments have been made in relation to several primitives based on this cross-linguistic testing. Innovations in the inventory of primitives and new syntactic properties of some of them have been discussed by Goddard (2002c) and proposed as topics for further cross-linguistic investigation. As he (2002c: 301) claims "the NSM model of universal grammar is still evolving" by the continuing challenge of testing it against a wider range of the world's languages. The recent efforts of the NSM theory seem to be focused on a full description of the universal grammar based on meaning so that the metalanguage can be more refined and coherent. The current interest of the theory seems to be reflected in the following:
(... ) establishing empirically that there is a universal core of grammar which is based on -indeed, inseparable from- meaning, and in this way to lay the foundations for an integrated, semantically-based approach to universal grammar and linguistic typology. (Goddard and Wierzbicka, 2002a: 3)
Apart from the major studies mentioned, there has been a substantial amount of research carried out to test the lexicalisation and syntax and the utility of the metalanguage.16
16 E.g. Maher (2000) carried out an exhaustive study testing the NSM hypotheses in the context of Italian. Stanwood (1999) attempted a text-based study of semantic primes and their grammar in Hawaii Creole English. (See Goddard, 2002a, 2002c for more references).
35

Chapter 2
2.4 Methodological issues in the NSM theory
The NSM theory has built up its unique principles and methodology through constant research over a number of years. Identifying the exponents of the semantic primitives in a particular language is found to involve various issues. Without a firm systematic and analytic dimension the NSM hypotheses cannot be cross-linguistically tested. Therefore guidelines have been developed and provided for those who attempt to test the NSM theory in a particular language. In this section, four different techniques are summarised in order to demonstrate the methodological grounds of the NSM theory. 17 These techniques are crucial in dealing with various language specific features in testing the NSMtheory.

2.4.1 Canonical sentences

A set of canonical sentences is provided as a guideline for each primitive so that all attempts can be undertaken in a strictly consistent way and be comparable on the same ground. Goddard and Wierzbicka (1994: 52) state about the canonical sentences:

This list gives syntactic (combinatorial) contexts in which the proposed semantically primitive meanings could be expected to be found, in any language. Put another way, the following sentences, or sentence fragments, represent things which we would expect or predict could be ~fl.id in any language.

As far as the .lexicon is concerned, the canonical sentences are helpful to identify the equivalent concepts of the proposed semantic primitives. They are particularly helpful for avoiding ambiguity of meaning that may result from the polysemous quality of some words. For instance, not all the meanings of the words 'move' and 'above' are proposed as semantic primitives. These words are polysemous in natural discourse and the proposed primitive meanings (MOVE and ABOVE) can only be understood in the provided frame (this point will be discussed in more detail in the next section). Therefore, canonical sentences are of vital importance in isolating the proposed primitive meaning from others.
Furthermore, the canonical contexts provide sentence fragments composed of semantic primitive.s, which are hypothesised to be expressible in any language. In other words, they demonstrate possible combinations with other primitives, which are the

17 See Goddard and Wierzbicka (eds.), 1994, 2002; Wierzbicka, 1996a; Goddard, 1998a) for detail.

36

Chapter 2
syntactic characteristics of each primitive. The present research is carried out using tests with canonical sentences in order to be comparable with all other cross-linguistic investigations.

2.4.2 Polysemy

As discussed briefly above, there are languages in which the exponents of some semantic primitives have more than one meaning. The phenomenon of polysemy seems to be ubiquitous, for it has been reported from various languages. When a single form has more than one meaning, the syntactic frame often gives a hint, so that the meaning can be .distinguished in distinct combinatorial contexts, or different grammatical constructions. 18 For instance, in Yankunytjajara one lexical item, kutjupa, expresses both the meaning of OTHER when it is adnominal and of SOMEONE when it is the head of an NP in its own right (Goddard, 1994b: 232). Cross-linguistic investigations have shown that the meanings of semantic primes do not always appear in a unique form in natural languages. Therefore one must be prepared to face unpredictable polysemy in the language under test, and to examine each instance thoroughly on its merits.

2.4.3 Allolexy

The issue of allolexy is equally important since semantic primitives are _sometimes realised in different forms. The concept of allolexy is used by analogy with 'allomorphs' and 'allophones' in other fields of linguistic studies. It has been observed that the language of explications cannot be kept entirely free of contextual variation. In the NSM theory, variants of the same concept (meaning) are called allolexes (Wierzbicka, 1996a: 26). This tenn was "coined to designate situations in which several different word-forms in complementary contexts express a single meaning" (Goddard, 2002a: 20). It has to be stressed, however, that the "expressions which are supposed to be in an allolexical relationship do not differ in meaning in any paraphrasable way" (Goddard, 2002a: 21 ). The allolexy may arise for different linguistic reasons and accordingly can be categorised into a number of groups as follows:
(1) Combinatorial allolexy: This notion refers to the case of a prime being realised differently according to its combination with other primes in a sentence. For instance, when one of the primes, SOMETHING, is combined with other primes such as THIS or

18 cf. Wirezbicka, 1996a: Goddard and Wierzbicka (eds.), 1994, 2002.

37

Chapter 2
ONE to compose THIS SOMETHING or ONE SOMETHING, THING can function as an allolex of SOMETHING in order to adjust to the specific English expression. In this case, between the two different forms, SOMETHING and THING, there is no conceptual difference. One form is chosen simply as a result of the combinatorial characteristics of the English exponents. Phenomena parallel to this case may occur in different combinations of other primitives in other languages. Another example of combinatorial allolexy occurs with case-forms of the arguments of semantic primes in some languages; European languages- nominative case, and Indo-Aryan languages -dative case, and Arremte- ergative case. All the different forms must be regarded as combinatorial variants which are lexically conditioned (cf. Goddard, 2002a: 22).
(2) Positional allolexy: This notion refers to the case where a prime is realised differently according to its position in a sentence. One of the simple examples is I and ME (I for pre-verbal and ME for elsewhere) in the English-based NSM. The concept represented by the two different words is the same, and the form in a sentence is determined by the grammatical category of the English language that obligatorily distinguishes these two forms according to their position in a sentence. The distribution of the alternate forms, therefore, is determined solely by position.
(3) Inflectional allolexy: Different forms of a semantic primitive are often caused by their obligatory inflection. There are languages in which a word is differentiated by number, gender~ or tense. The latter are some of the very widely spread language specific features which do not impact on the semantic level. One of the examples is obligatory tense marking in English. The prime DO realized as did when the time reference is past. The past tense is of course marked by the primitive concepts, before now. Therefore, DO and DID are inflectional allolexes in the sentence I did something at some time before now but not in the sentence I do something, since putting did in the latter creates a meaning difference. Goddard's (2002a: 22) guideline on this point is that "do and did are allolexes in an English-based NSM, because time-reference will always be independently represented in lexical terms, and consequently the distribution of DlD as opposed to DO can be predicted from the surrounding context".
(4) Portmanteau: This refers to a combination of primes. There are cases where more than one prime happens to be embodied in a single expression. One example of this kind can be taken from the Russian expression, tak, which carries the same meaning as the combination of the English primes LIKE and THIS, in a context like I did this like this. Although both concepts LIKE and THIS are lexicalised separately in Russian, the particular combination like this is expressed by means of a single word tak (Wierzbicka,
38

Chapter 2
1996a: 27). Portmanteaus with the prime DO are found to be common in some languages; for example, the Australian language Kayardild has portmanteaus for do this, do well, do badly, and do like someone else (Goddard, 2002a: 24). This phenomenon may appear in other languages for unpredictable combinations of primes. As long as one can identify separate exponents of the proposed primes that are jointly expressed in portmanteau, it does not raise any concern. This phenomenon is anticipated to be obligatory or semi-obligatory depending on the grammatical categories of specific languages (cf. Wierzbicka, 1996a).
The methodological considerations summarised above are crucial in identifying conceptual equivalents of the proposed semantic primitives in different languages. The identified metalanguages derived from different languages can be compared crosslinguistically since the theory takes possible language-specific features into account. It is not surprising to face various kinds of surface differences among languages. Goddard (2002a: 31) states: "it would be completely unrealistic to expect every natural language to harbour an ideal 'one form, one meaning' semantic metalanguage" since "natural languages are not designed for the convenience of linguists".
2.4.4 Valency options
The notion of valency option is another very important theoretical concept in NSM theory. The lexico-syntactic characteristics of the proposed primes are not th~ same and the allowed syntactic slots for each prime are taken into account within the theory. For instance, the prime SAY can occur in the following four different combinations:
(i) I said something. (ii) I said something about something [illocutionary topic] (iii) I said: "___" [direct speech] (iv) I said something to someone [addressee]
(Goddard and Wierzbicka, 2002b: 60)
The reason why these options for one prime are allowed is that sentences (ii), (iii), and (iv) cannot be analysed via sentence (i). The valency options are claimed, therefore, to be other faces of the same prime. In other words, they are all manifestations of a single primitive concept. Of course the possible valency options for different primes vary for they are determined by the inherent meaning of a prime. The possible valency options for each prime will be discussed in detail in each section when necessary.
39

Chapter 2
2.5 Application of NSM framework in various semantic domains

The NSM theory has been applied to different semantic domains by Wierzbicka and others; natural kinds, cultural kinds, speech act verbs, emotion concepts, colour te1ms parables, legal language, non-verbal communicative expressions, metaphors, motion verbs, grammatical categories including pronominal systems, noun and numeral classifiers, locational deixis, evidentials and experiencer constructions, particles, interjections, tautology and non-verbal expressions, including facial expressions (cf. e.g., Wierzbicka, 1985, 1987, 1988, 1991, 1992a, 1995a, 1996a, 1999, 2001b; Harkins and Wierzbicka (eds.), 1997, 2001; Goddard, 1996a, 1996b, 1997a, 1998, 2001a; Ameka 1990, 1994a). Explications achieved by using the NSM method contain different numbers of components depending on the complexity of the defined expression or word. These components, which consist only of the semantic primitives, are similar to what are often called 'scripts' or 'scenarios' .19 The components refer to both inherent features of the referents and external frames; habitat, behaviour or typical interaction with people or in the case of artefacts20 the typical situation of use. Applied in different fields, the NSM method is found to be a versatile and powerful semantic analysis tool that provides a necessarily firm ground for various semantic studies.

2.5.1 Emotion

The study of emotion has been a topic for many researchers in different disciplines such as philosophy, psychology, biology, anthropology, and linguistics. Wierzbicka (1990: 137) acknowledges the importance of the subject:

The "language of emotions" is an integral part of any future "emotionology" and research in this area: is urgently needed. The interplay between the universal and culture-specific aspects of emotions must be seen as an interdisciplinary undertaking, requiring collaboration of psychology, anthropology, and linguistics.
A particular concern in linguistics is to reveal the semantic representation of emotion words through which one can speculate how people conceptualise their

19 See Abelson 1981; Schank and Abelson 1977. quoted in Wierzbicka, 1996a: 171 20 See Fillmore 1975, 1977. quoted in Wierzbicka, 1996a: 171.

40

Chapter 2
feelings. Emotion related words or expressions can be used as evidence of a person' s inner self for language is known to reflect the human mind. Wierzbicka states two reasons for why words matter in the study of emotion.
First, words provide clues to other people' s conceptualizations. Pace Harris, Ekman, Lazarus, Pinker, and many others, it is words, more than anything, which allow us access to the "emotional universe" of people from another culture. Second, it is only by studying words that we can go beyond words. For example, if we are interested in "emotions" and uninterested in words ... , we still have to take enough interest in words to notice that English words such as sadness, enjoyment, or anger are no more than the cultural artefacts of one particular language. (Wierzbicka, 1999: 28-29)
Recognition of the need for studying emotion is growing, but applying an appropriate method for the systematic analysis of emotion words must also be emphasised. This is because, despite recognition of the importance of analysing emotion concepts, people often fail to describe emotion by using a biased measure as a tool. Consequently, the analysis cannot avoid being ethno-centric and language-specific. Many are not aware of using particular words from a particular language (in most cases English) for labelling emotions as if they are neutral or language independent concepts. The methodological problem in this area is recognised and has been criticised (cf. e.g., Wierzbicka, 1996a, 1999; Hasada, 1995, 2000; Ye 2000). However, the NSM approach used in describing and articulating the meanings of emotion words and expressions demonstrates the possibility of analysing what is known to be a very subjective and culture-specific area in a simple and illuminating way. The NSM method enables the semantic analysis to be language independent and culture free.
Wierzbicka has applied the .NSM method in explicating emotion concepts (cf. 1992a, 1999), proposing a certain cognitive structure that is typically associated with the defined concepts. She proposes this structure in order to "define a large and coherent" (1996a: 178-182) semantic field of emotion. She insightfully uses a so called 'prototypical cognitive scenario' in order to illustrate the state of the human mind which is experiencing certain thoughts characteristic of that particular situation (cf. Wierzbicka, 1994c; Harkins and Wierzbicka (eds.) 2001; Goddard, 1998a).
Wierzbicka (1999: 13) suggests that even though there are many ways of describing to other people how one feels, they can be reduced to two basic modes; (i) one can tell other people that one 'feels good/bad' and (ii) one can tell other people that one feels like a person who feels in a certain situation and then identify in one way or another that 'prototypical situation'. The second aspect is reflected as the structured
41

Chapter 2
components in the definition for emotion words or expressions. This is illustrated as follows:

X felt something, because X thought something sometimes a person think something like this:

because of this, this person feels something X feels something like this
(Wierzbicka, 1996a: 182)
The prototypical scenario may vary depending on words or grammatical constructions used in a sentence. This is because some words or grammatical constructions present the experiencer' s emotions based on a particular thought, or chain of thoughts (e.g. grateful, disappointed, _disgusted, angry, surprised), while some others do not necessarily imply any particular thoughts (e.g. sad, depressed, happy, anxious) (see Wierzbicka, 1992a: 177, 1992b: 290). 21 Therefore, two kinds of scenarios are possible one of which contains the prime THINK while another one does not. Wierzbicka (1992b: 291) suggests the following for the two different kinds of emotions:

(1) Someone is sad someone feels something sometimes people think something like this:
this person feels like this
(2) Someone is grateful when this person thinks of Y, this person feels something sometimes people think something like this:

this person thinks something like this because of this, this person feels like this
Other exhaustive investigations of emotion words have been carried out by adopting the same approach in Japanese and Chinese (cf. Hasada, 1995, 2000; Ye, 2000) remark on the usefulness and appropriateness of the NSM _approach as a semantic tool to illustrate the culture-specific 'feeling' from the 'universal feeling' by avoiding using a language specific tool. Ye (2000: 305) wrote:

21 The reason for this can be explained by Wierzbicka's examples as follows: A: [ am sad/depressed/happy/anxious today. -I don't know why. B: * I am disappointed/grateful/disgusted/angry/ surprised. -I don't know why.

42

Chapter 2
Without this conceptual tool, the task of distinguishing, in a precise and verifiable way, both the universality and culture peculiarity of emotions would not have been feasible, for NSM allows researchers to position themselves outside, as well as inside, the system under investigation.
The series of semantic analyses carried out in the NSM method demonstrates that the field of emotion can be tackled efficiently, without being tangled a language specific cage, so that one can compare emotion words or expressions of divergent languages on the same ground. For instance, there are two explications for the English concept
'sadness' and the Russian concept peeal (which is treated as the corresponding word for
'sadness'} These can be compared on the same measure since the two concepts are paraphrased into NSM as follows:

sadness (e.g. X feels sad) X feels something sometimes a person thinks: "I know: something bad happened I don't want things like this to happen I can't think now: I will do something because of this I know that I can't do anything" because of this, this person feels something bad X feels something like this (Wierzbicka, 1999: 39)

pecal

X felt something because X thought something sometimes a person thinks:
"I know something bad happened this is bad I don't want things like this to happen I can't thin!<: now: I will do something because of this I know that I can't do anything" because this person thinks this, this person feels something bad X felt something like this because X thought something like this X thought about if for a long time X felt something because of this for a long time
(Wierzbicka, 1999: 43)

Although English-Russian dictionaries usually offer the Russian word presented above as the equivalent of the English word ' sad' , the difference between the two concepts is clearly pinpointed on the basis of the universal primes as the above explications show. Wierzbicka states (1999: 16):

43

Chapter 2 ;
(... ) these scenarios can be understood by cultural outsiders, and the kind of feeling associated with them can be identified, explained, and compared; and both the similarities and differences between scenarios lexicalised in different languages can be pinpointed.
One can see the precise difference of meaning between the two concepts through the different components involved. Therefore, the NSM method enables the researcher not only to capture the semantic invariants, and break complex concepts into maximally simple ones, but also to compare emotion words cross-linguistically.
2.5.2 Key cultural concepts
The relationship between language and culture has been an issue for many for a long time. Among others, Sapir observed the close link between culture and language. He claims that "vocabulary is a very sensitive index of the culture of a people (Sapir, 1951 : 27) " and "it is the vocabulary of a language the most clearly reflects the physical and social environment of its speakers" (Sapir, 1951: 90). He believed that language is a symbolic guide to culture. Language differences, therefore, reflect the different cultures of the world. Wierzbicka claims (1996a: 237) that "words are a society's most basic cultural artefacts and they .provide the best key to a culture's values and assumptions". While an understanding of the norms and values of other cultures is increasingly important in the modem world, a systematic tool for the meaning analysis of cultural concepts has not been widely available.
The NSM method has been found to be able to explain culture specificity through universal concepts. Accordingly, the meaning of a cultural concept can be comprehended with the same understanding by both insiders and outsiders of a given culture. Many attempts to explain the meaning of cultural concepts have failed to reveal the originality of the concepts to outsiders due to inadequacy of the employed measure. Any cultural concept cannot avoid be_ing tangled in its own language specific meaning unless it is paraphrased into universal concepts. From cross-cultural communication point of view the NSM approach offers a language independent measure since the metalanguage is claimed to be universal on the basis of cross-linguistic investigations.22
There have been various attempts to analyse culture specific concepts in diverse languages. For instance, the Japanese concept amae is known to be absolutely culturespecific and thoroughly untranslatable by outsiders of the Japanese culture. Wierzbicka
22 See Goddard and Wierzbicka (eds.), 1994, 2002 ; Wierzbicka, 1991 , 1996a, 1997a for more detail.
44

Chapter 2
(1996a), however, shows the possibility of decomposing this kind of concept into simpler terms as follows:

Amae
X thinks something like this: when Y thinks about me, Y feels something good Y wants to do good things for me Y can do good things for me when I am near Y nothing bad can happen to me I don't have to do anything because of this I want to be near Y X feels something good because of this

(Wierzbicka, 1996a: 239)

This explication can be translated into any language-based NSM so that the meaning of amae can be understood by anyone in the world,
While explicating culturally salient concepts in an exhaustive way, one can discover a pattern of cultural attitudes in the culture under study. It is commonly said that every society has a shared set of cultural norms, specific needs, and values about how to think, feel, or speak. These cultural norms and attitudes can be stated explicitly by using the NSM method. These cultural scripts articulate the native's tacit knowledge rather than an outsider's .objectivist view. 23 The cultural scripts of different cultures described by the NSM method can be compared with each other on the same ground. For instance, Wierzbicka contrasts (1999) the two different 'cultural scripts'; Polish and Anglo-American as follows:

Anglo-American [people think:] when I say something to other people it is good if these people think that I feel something good

Polish

[people think:] it is bad when a person wants other people to think that this person feels something if this person doesn't feel this

Having been aware of the fact that not all Americans or Poles live by these scripts, Wierzbicka claims (1999: 242) that~

23 See Wierzbicka 1991, 1996a, 1996b.

45

Chapter 2
The component "people think:" opening the scripts reflects the fact even people who personally don't identify with the content of that script are nonetheless familiar with it: they, too, belong to the community which shares familiarity with this script (and with other, related, cultural scripts).
One can see cultural norms via these cultural scripts which are a result of exhaustive semantic analyses of extensive linguistic data of the culture in question. The framework can also "demonstrate that patterns of culture can be studied in a verifiable and nonspeculative way on the basis of linguistic semantics, rooted in empirically established linguistic and conceptual universals" (Wierzbicka, 1999: 241 ).

2.5.3 Other semantic domains
Apart from what has been mentioned above, the NSM method has been applied in various other areas and has made it possible to articulate meanings of highly subjective and specific expressions. Among others the attempts in the field of speech acts and interjections are briefly introduced here.
The imp01iant works of Austin (1962) and Searle (1976, 1983) are well known in the field of speech acts. Searle proposed the conditions of illocutionary force and categorised all speech acts_verbs on the basis of ' constitutive rules ' . This approach, however, does not seem to account for the differences in speech act verbs among languages and cultures. To quote Goddard (1998a: 145) commenting on this approach:
Searle himself is on record as saying that speech acts fall into ' natural conceptual kinds'. A weaker version of this indifference to linguistic and cultural variation acknowledges in principle that the speech acts of other cultures may differ markedly from our own, but proceeds to treat the English speech-act inventory as a model in terms of which everything is to be understood. This happens when, for instance, cross~linguistic studies are conducted into ways of ' complimenting' or ' apologizing' in cultures which do not themselves recognize any such speech act categories.
The NSM framework has served for semantic analysis of speech acts (cf. Wierzbicka, 1987) in a somewhat different way from this approach. The differences and similarities of speech acts from different languages and cultures are taken into account by the explicit universal metalanguage. The following are presented as an example:

X promised Y (to do A) X said to Y: I want you to know I will do A

46

Chapter 2
when X said it, it was as if X was saying at the same time: I know you want me to do this I know you think that maybe I will not do it I don't want you to think this I know if I don't do it after saying this, people will think something bad about me (Goddard, 1998a: 147)
X sumimasen to iimashita (X said sumimasen) X said something like this: I know I did something bad I feel something bad because of this when X said this, it was as if X was saying at the same time I know you had to do something because of me I don't want this have ·to do something good for you because of this I can't do anything now (Goddard, 1998a: 162)
Paraphrasing speech act verbs in any language using the NSM method enables not only
the meaning of the word to be understood without obscurity, but it also makes it
possible to see differences of culture reflected in the speech act verbs.
The NSM method has also been applied to the semantic analysis of particles and
interjections, which are known to be particularly difficult to describe. The previous
analysis of this topic has been relatively poor. It is commonly agreed that acquiring
these aspects of the target language is one of the most difficult tasks for most language
learners. This could be due to the lack of appropriate explanations of their meanings.
The proposed hypothetical explications of the English particle 'well' (the response
marker), the English interjection 'yuk', and the Malay interjection lah are exemplified
below:

Well, Yuk!

someone said something just now (i.e. a short time before now) I have thought about it well I say: I have to say something else You will have to think about this
(Goddard, 1998a: 176)

I think: this is something bad I feel something bad because of this I feel like someone who thinks: I don't want to touch this thing I think other people would feel the same

(Wierzbicka, 1991 : 304) 47

Chapter 2
Lah
I say this now after what (just) happened because I think you might think something else that would not be good
(Goddard, 1998a: 183) Although capturing the semantic invariants of particles and interjections is not as easy as it looks, it can at least be tested and verified by native speakers. The application of the NSM theory in different semantic domains has been introduced briefly here in order to show its practicality as a tool for semantic analysis. This method has been put into practice by various scholars for the challenging task of examining the meaning of words and linguistic expressions as well as non-verbal expressions. From an examination of the literature it appears that to date no single semantic approach has been practiced as widely as the NSM approach.
48

Chapter 3
Chapter 3 Testing the NSM Theory in Korean:
In relation to lexicalisation and syntax
3.1 Introduction
The NSM theory assumes that the proposed semantic primitives exist in any natural language as a subset of that language. The two main hypotheses24 of the NSM theory that will be tested in this chapter with reference to Korean are: (i) The proposed semantic primitives are lexicalised ih all languages (around 60 lexical items), and (ii) The combinatorial characteristics of the semantic primitives are universal.
The first hypothesis concerns the universal existence of the semantic primitives. The semantic primitives, which are claimed to be indefinable, are assumed to be lexicalised in all natural languages for they are innate. To put it differently, fundamental human concepts are "part of the human genetic endowment", and consequently they must be the same for all human groups (Wierzbicka, 1996a: 14). However, their existence in a language can only be attested by the lexicalised conceptual equivalents. While the NSM theory posits the most extreme form of semantic universalism25 the concept of lexicalisation in theory is used in a broad sense in that the proposed concepts may be realised in any form of the language in question, for example as bound morphemes, lexemes, or fixed phrases (i.e. any segmental signs), as mentioned previously. Since natural languages differ enormously in terms of morphosyntactic manifestation, the proposed primitive meanings are expected to be encoded in these various fom1s.
Regarding the second hypothesis, the proposed primitives are ass,umed to have an inherent grammar based on meaning. These concepts can only be meaningful when they are put together to fonn a sentence. For this reason not only the lexicon of semantic primitives but also their combinability is posited as innate and universal. A substantial
24 These main hypotheses of the NSM theory have been tested cross-linguistically over the last three decades in many different languages from diverse language families. 25 See 2.2 for more, and see Wierzbicka, 1996a; Goddard and Wierzbicka (eds.), 1994 for detailed discussion.
49

Chapter 3
set of hypothesised combinations between primitives is proposed within the theory. These are highly concrete, (for example, I want to do this may be expressed in any language), and accordingly cross-linguistically testable. The question of to what extent the proposed universal grammar of primitives is universal has been a critical issue for NSM scholars over recent years. Without approved syntactic principles for the semantic primitives, the metalanguage cannot serve for reductive paraphrase, just as any natural language cannot function with vocabulary alone. Moreover, the underlying rules for combining primitives are expected to be analogous across the languages of the world. Otherwise paraphrases using the NSM method would not be inter-translatable.
The Korean counterparts of the NSM lexicon cannot be identified by a checklist procedure since the meanings of semantic primitives are not identical with those of the natural language from which they are carved out. Various methodological techniques are provided within the NSM theory so that every empirical attempt can be crosslinguistically comparable (see 2.4 for detail). The English-based metalanguage is used here as an example for cross-linguistic investigation since English is one of the most internationally accessible languages.
For the convenience of the users of the metalanguage the proposed semantic primitives are grouped together according to their relatedness with other members of the same group. There are seventeen groups in the NSM lexicon and the number of members in each group differs, as shown in Table 1 in 2.3 .2.
The following strategy for identifying Korean exponents of th~_ proposed primitives is used throughout the whole of this chapter: i.e. (i) Identify the meaning of the proposed prime in the context of the canonical sentences provided by the NSM scholars (Goddard and Wierzbicka (eds.) 1994, 2002), see Appendix (ii) Find out the conceptual equivalent of the prime by testing hypothetical Korean counterparts against the same canonical sentences. (iii) If there is more than one candidate for an exponent, the candidates are tested in more extensive examples in order to examine whether there is a difference in meaning between them. (iv) Propose justified Korean exponents for the Korean-based metalanguage. (v) Test each proposed exponent in terms of combinability following the given guidelines of the NSM theory. Discussion follows when necessary.
Therefore, in order to achieve the goal of this thesis efficiently, the proposed primitives are tested, firstly in terms of lexicalisation, and secondly in terms of their syntactic properties, although the distinction between the two sections are not always clear-cut.
50

Chapter 3
3.2 Substantives: I, YOU, SOMEONE, SOMETHING, PEOPLE, BODY
3.2.1 Lexicon
3.2.1.1 na/1 and ne/YOU
In Korean, first and second pron~mns are often omitted and yet people can understand each other from the context. These pronouns are used only when contexts do not clarify 'doer' and 'undergoer'. Although the zero form is most common for first and second person pronouns in natural discourse, the counterparts of I and YOU can be identified in Korean. There are several forms that may be chosen according to pragmatic factors. Sohn (1994:281) has pointed out that Korean pronouns are "selected depending on the speaker's and/or addressee's sex, age, perceived social status, kin relationships, and emotional coloration". Lee (1989: 52) also states that the counterparts of I and YOU in Korean may be distinguished "in respect of the degree of politeness between the speaker and the addressee, paralleling to a large extent the different speech styles marked by the verbal inflections".
Apart from various choices of strictly personal pronouns, such as ne, na, and tangsin, kinship terms oroccupational roles (teacher, professor, or president) are often used in place of.first and second personal pronouns. A similar situation is often found in other Asian languages, such as Thai (Diller, 1994), Malay (Goddard, 2Q02b) and Japanese (Onishi, 1994). The latter is similar to Korean except that there is gender variation involved, in addition to social hierarchy, formality, and intimacy. The question is whether or not there is a meaning difference between the different forms in Korean. The choice among the different forms depends mainly on pragmatic factors and the social norms of the Korean community. The polite form carries the notion of respect toward the listener and humbling of oneself, thus it is used only when the speaker needs to express this notion. The choice of form may give information on the speaker's attitude toward the addressee, which provides extra information in addition to the proposed primitive meanings ofl and YOU.
Having pointed out this aspect of Korean, I propose na and ne as the semantically pure Korean exponents for I and YOU, respectively. Despite the fact that people use the honorific or zero form widely in everyday conversation, these are the most neutral and general forms and thus are suitable for semantic analysis. These forms are also commonly found in informal contexts involving various interlocutors whose age or
51

Chapter 3
status is regarded as similar, or in soliloquies where a speaker talks to himself/herself silently. In a situation with no given context, such as in canonical sentences, the plain
forms: na and ne carry exactly the same meaning as I and YOU, as seen in the examples
below:

(1) nay-ka ne-eykey i- il-ul

hay-ss-ta.

I did this to you. ·

exponents of I and YOU.

3.2.1.2 nwukwu/SOMEONE/WHO
The primes SOMEONE and SOMETHING are proposed as they provide the most fundamental form of human categorisation of the contents of the world (see Wierzbicka, 1996a: 38-39 for further discussion and illustration). The concept of SOMEONE can be identified by the following canonical sentences:
Someone took it. I don't know who ...

26 The nominative particle is determined, between -i and -ka, by phonological factors. The particle -ka occurs after a known ending in a vowel (Sohn, 1994: 281).
52

I saw someone there. Who did it?

Chapter 3

At first glance there seems to be more than one translational equivalent of SOMEONE in Korean: nwukwu, enu salam, etten salam, etten i. Although the canonical sentences pres~nted above do not tell us that SOMEONE may not necessarily refer only to humans, the concept is proposed to refer to both human beings and non-human beings such as God (Wierzbicka, 1996a: 40). Therefore, among others nwukwu and etten i seem to be compatible with the meaning of SOMEONE in that they do not necessarily refer only to humans, while enu salam and etten salam (a certain person or which person) do refer to humans.
Between the two words nwukwu and etten i, there seems to be no difference in meaning but a pragmatic preference depending on context, and a difference in syntactic association with other words. The equivalent of SOMEONE/WHO is expressed by etten-i and nwukwu in the_words of the song 'Someone's dream', as follows:

etten i-nun kkwum-ul kancikha-ko sal-ko

someone-TC dream-AC keep-and

live-and

etten i-nun kkuwm-ul ilwumyen-se sal-ko
someone-TC dream-AC achieve-and live-and

talu-n i-nun kkwum-ul nanwecwu-mye sal-ci

other one-TC dream-AC share-and

live-SUP

na-nun nwukwu-l-kka?
1-TC who-INT

Someone keeps the dream, someone achieves the dream, and someone shares the dream. Who am I (among them)? (etten iuy kkwum [someone's dream] , Samho, 1994: 159)

The expression etten i is bimorphemic and found mostly in literary expressions. It is not commonly used in contemporary Korean. On the other hand, the word nwukwu is monomorphemic and widely used among people regardless of age, gender, or social status. Therefore, I propose nwukwu as the Korean counterpart of SOMEONE not because of the meaning difference but due to its relatively high frequency of use in everyday discourse (presumably because of its morphological simplicity).
The meaning analysis of nwukwu by Kim (1998) defines it as [+ human], or [enu
(which or some) + salam (person)]. However, this analysis falsely implies that
nwukwu!SOMEONE refers only to 'human beings'. The word nwukwu can refer to

At this stage I am inclined to propose to regard salam as a semantic molecule (SOMEONE, ONE of PEOPLE).

3.2.1.3 mwues/SOMETHING/WHAT
As mentioned in the previous section 3.2.1.2, the distinction between person and nonperson is claimed to be so fundamental that across all languages and cultures these concepts are distinctively divided (Wierzbicka, 1996a: 39). The concept of SOMETHING/THING35 (which is not some+ thing), therefore, is proposed as a semantic primitive that exists in any natural language in the world. This concept is identified in the given canonical sentences as follows:
I see something over there.
I see two things.
There is something inside this person's body. Someone did something good/bad to me.
The translational equivalents of the prime SOMETHING/THING in Korean are mwues and etten .kes. The former is one morpheme that is non-referential, while the latter is a bimorphemic consisting of etten (which or some: unspecified or
undetermined) and kes (a general noun for expressing 'thing' and 'event'). It is
generally accepted that etten functions as an indefinite article, and also Lee S (1995: 310-312) claims that the central meaning of etten is indefiniteness.
The occurrence of two expressions for SOMETHING/THING is parallel to the case of SOMEONE with its two candidates: nwukwu and etten i.36 However, unlike the case of etten i, the expression etten kes is used as widely as mwues in contemporary Korean. There is no semantic difference between mwues and etten kes when they are used in the provided canonical sentences. The non-specific term_mwues37 is used naturally when it is not combined with any modifying word in natural discourse. On the other hand, etten kes is more natural in combination ·with attributives such as evaluators or descriptors but not with specifiers such as determiners or quantifiers.38
There are fixed phrases involving the two categories of something and someone, such as, 'nwuka mwues-ul hay-ss-tim ... ' (no matter who did what... ) 'nwukwu-lul wihay mwues-ul ha-l-ttay... ' (when you do something for someone ... ). Here the two words, nwukwu and mwues are used naturally and symmetrically to refer to 'human' and

The three expressions mwues, mwusun i/39 and etten il in sentences, (29)b-d do not differ semantically. The expression ilenan il (an event that happened or what happened) used

39 In fact mwusun and mwues are known to originate from the same word. According to Sohn (1994: 298), "mwusun is derived from the interrogative pronoun mwues (what) and i-n (attributive suffix)". The two words differ in their syntactic behaviour: mwues is nominal while mwusun is an attributive that
obligatorily requires .either a postposed nominal or another attributive (see 3.2.2 for detailed illustration).

Chapter 3
in (29)b is quoted as mwues in the same sentence, as mwusun-il in (29)c, and etten-il in (29)d. This is the evidence that mwues can be used to refer to an event. On this basis I propose the non-referential exponent mwues/SOMETIIlNG as the primary Korean exponent and other expressions, mwusun il or etten il as its lexical variants. These expressions are chosen in preference to mwues when the concept of SOMETHING is the
complement of DO or HAPPEN.
Apart from these proposed Korean exponents mwues(mwe), mwusun il, and etten il, there are other combinatorial allolexes that occur when this concept is combined with
specifying words such as detenniners or quantifiers. Instead of the proposed non-
specific exponent mwues, the bound morpheme ~kes (thing) is readily used in
combination with specifying words. Consider the examples below:
(30) na-nun mwues-ul kac-ko·iss-ta. i-kes-un coh-un kes i-ta.
I-TC something-AC have-PRG-DC this-thing-TC good-PM thing be-DC
I have something. This thing is good.
(31) ne-nun kath-un kes-ul kac-ko·iss-ta.
you-NM the same-PM thing-AC have-PRO-DC
You have the same thing.
This is parallel to the English case where THING is substituted when with determiners
and quantifiers. Therefore the word -kes should be considered as a combinatorial allolex of mwues/mwe that is used with specifiers.
The n~eaning of -kes has been the subject of many studies since it has different functions according to context. According to Bea (1998: 26), the basic meaning of -kes
involves tangible object exists physically and intangible object exists abstractly. She
concludes that the meaning and function of the word -kes is general reference, which
indicates thing, place, event, situation, and fact. The analysis of Lee P (1995: 160) is
also consistent with this view. Although -kes can sometimes be used to refer to humans,
this use has a different scope as it implies that the referred person is treated as a 'thing' (used on purpose to give that connotation). This indicates that human beings are not
included in the meaning of -kes. The word -kes can be substituted by il (event, action) when it is the complement of
DO and HAPPEN, parallel to the relationship between mwues, and mwusun il or etten il. The substitution is justifiable for the meaning of -kes is known to subsume 'thing' and
'event'. This is illustrated in the examples below:

3.2.1.4 sa/amtu//PEOPLE
The proposed primitive meaning PEOPLE can be identified from the following canonical sentences:
These people are (not) like other people. All people do this. Many people don't know this. People think that snakes are clever (or: some commonly held view).
IfI do this, people will say bad things about me.
I propose salamtul as the Korean exponent for PEOPLE. This word is tested as follows:

(3 7) salamtul-i ne-eytayhay nappu-key mal-ul ha-! kes-i-ta.
.people-NM you-about bad-ADV word-AC do-PRO thing-be-DC
People will say bad things about you.

(3 8) nwukwunka-ka manh-un sa.lamtul-ul wihayse i il-ul hay-ss-ta.

someone-NM many-PM people- AC for

this thing-AC do-PST- DC

Someone did this for many people.

(39) i-kos-ey

iss-nun salamtul-un ne-eytayhay molu-n-ta.

this-place-L0C there is-PM people-TC you-about not know-IN-DC

People in this place do not know about you.

In Korean there is no other word except for salamtul that can be used as the counterpart of PEOPLE in the given canonical contexts. The word salamtul consists of salam (person) and tul (plural marker). One may argue that salamtul cannot be the

63

Chapter 3
exponent of the primitive PEOPLE since this Korean word appears to be decomposable via salam (human being) and plurality (-tu!). Although salam is more basic than salamtul in terms of morphological structure, the definition of salam via salamtul is viable as shown in (40)a-b. The example below shows that the meaning of i salamtul cwung hana (one of these people) and salam is the same:

(40)a i kos-ey manh-un salamtul-i iss-ta.
this place-L0C many-PM people-NM there is-DC
There are many people in this place.
i salamtul cwung hana-ka i-kes-ul ha-l·swu·iss-ta.
this people among one-NM this-thing-AC do-can-DC
One of these people can do this.

(40)b i kos-ey manh-un salamtul-i
this place-L0C many-PM people-NM
There are many people in this place.

iss-ta;
there is-DC

i cwung han salam-i i-kes-ul ha-l·swu·iss-ta.
this among one person-NM this-thing-AC do-can-DC
Among these, a person can do this.

It is not clear whether this suggests that salamtul is more basic conceptually than salam. In previous NSM work, the English word person was considered as an allolex of SOMEONE. This is no longer valid (Wierzbicka, 2002a: 66-74; Goddard, 2002b: 97) due to the implication of 'focusing on individuality' as the contemporary use of the English word 'person' often does. Unlike the English word 'person', the Korean word salam refers only to human without the extra semantic component that is contained in the English word person. The Korean word salamis readily combined with determiners or attributives in such expressions as 'i salam' (this person), 'cohun salam' (good person), 'mikwuk salam' (American person), 'khun salam' (big person).
One more comment with respect to the plural form salamtul is that the plural ·marker -tu! occurs selectively depending on contexts. As I will discuss in more detail in 3.4.2, -tu! must not occur with numerical quantifiers while it is optional with other quantifying words, such as manh-lMANY and motun!ALL. Therefore, *twu salamtul (two people) is not grammatical while both manhun salamtul (many-people) and manhun salam (many-person) are acceptable. Therefore, examples (40)a-b could be rewritten with salafn replacing salamtul with no change of meaning because there is another word, manh-lMANY indicating plurality in the sentence. Thus when plurality is marked already in the sentence, salam can mean the same as salamtul.

3.2.1.5 mom/BODY

Chapter 3

Identifying the counterpart of BODY is relatively straightforward. There are two words for the concept of BODY in Korean: mom is pure Korean, while sinchey is a SinoKorean word: with many other terms there are parallel pairs like this. They seem to mean the same except that sinchey is mostly used in written text, in informal or academic contexts, while mom has wider uses, including every day conversation.

(41) son-un mom (sinehey)-uy ilpwu-ta.

hand-TC body-GEN

a part-DC

Hand is a part of the body.

(42) kenkanghan mom (sinchey)-ey kenkanghan chengsin-i kistu-n-ta.

healthy

body-L0C

healthy

spmt-NM settle-IN-DC

In a healthy body, a healthy spirit can be settled.

mwenka-ka iss-ta.

my body

inside-L0C something-NM exist-DC

There is something inside my body.

(44) namca-uy mom(sinchey)-un yeca-uy mom-kwa kath~ci anh-ta.

man-GEN body-TC

women-GEN body-with like-N0M not-DC

The body of a man is not like the body of a woman.

Although bot~ words can be used in canonical sentences with no meaning difference, I propose the word mom as the Korean exponent for the prime BODY in Korean, for the word sinchey may carry the pragmatic implication of formality or an academic context. In fact, this word is acquired much later than mom in first language acquisition.
There is an expression in Korean 'i mom (lit. this body)' used to refer to 'oneself, which means na/1.

(45) i mom-i

say-lamyen ...

this body-NM bird-if ...

Ifl were a bird...

This expression is idiosyncratic and used mostly poetically (e.g. in songs) rather than as an everyday expression. In most cases, contexts clarify whether 'i mom' is used to refer to either 'I' or to 'this body'. However, in Korean based NSM explications i mom (this body) is restricted in reference to the combination of THIS and BODY to prevent possible confusion. For instance the sentence, 'i mom-an-ey mwuesinka iss-ta' has to be interpreted as something is inside this body, not something is inside me.

All the Korean exponents of substantives, except for mwues/SOMETH1NG, and perhaps mom/BODY, can be subjects for the previously mentioned predicates. The use of nonfirst person substantives with mental predicates such as sayngkaha-lTHINK, wenhalWANT or nukki-lFEEL is awkward unless they are used on assumption of previously given information. Therefore the occurrence of substantive with mental predicate seems to be semantically constrained.
The prime mom/BODY can be a preverbal argument of FEEL or WANT in Korean although the meanings of these predicates in such expressions as 'mom-i nukki-n-ta (the body feels), or 'mom-i wenhan-ta' (the body wants) may not be the same as those in other expressions like 'nanun i kesul nukkinta' (I feel this) or 'nanun i kesul wenhanta' (I want this).

Evaluators are claimed to be used predicatively in combination with substantives, as in this person is good/bad. In order to express the equivalent in Korean, however, a positive evaluator, the prime GOOD, has to be used as a modifying word not as a predicate, since as a predicate it can have more than one reading, as exemplified in (25).

(25)a

i salam-un coh-ta.
this person-TC good- DC
This person is good. Or As to this person, it is Okay (I like this person).

(25)b i salam-un coh-un salam i-ta.
this person-TC good-PM person be-DC
This person is.a good person.

The meanings of example (25~b is clearly equivalent this person is good, while (25)a can be interpreted either a_s 'this person is good', or 'as this person, it is fine' (I like this person - when, for instance, someone is choosing the right person for a job). This seems to be due to the fact that the word coh-!GOOD is polysemous. Its second meaning of psychological attraction can be seen in such sentences as na-nun i salam-i coh-ta (I like this person) (see 3.5 for further discussion). In order to express clearly the equivalent of this person is good, the evaluator coh-lGOOD has to be used as a modifying word with an obligatory postposed nominal. This is also the case in Korean sentences with personal substantives such as nail and na!YOU. Examples (26)a and (26)b are semantically different mainly due to the different syntactic role of the word coh-!GOOD.

(26)a na-nun coh-un salam-i-ta.
I-TC good-PM person-be-DC
I am a good person.
(26)b na-nun coh-ta.
I-TC good-DC
I am fine (or 'I like' depending on the context).
Therefore, in order to express the equivalent of this person is good or I am /You are good in Korean, coh-!GOOD has to be used attributively with a following modified
69

Chapter 3
nominal. By doing this the ambiguity of meaning that may result from the polysemous quality of coh-!GOOD can be avoided.
In NSM texts, there are common expressions such as people think this is good. In Korean this can be expressed either by (27)a or (27)b as shown below and these examples do not raise any problem of ambiguity in meaning.

(27)a salamtul-un i kes-i coh-ta-ko
people-TC this thing-NM good-DC-Q

sayngkakha-n-ta.
think-IN-DC

(27)b salamtul-un i kes-i coh-un kes-i-lako sayngkakha-n-ta.
people- TC this thing-NM good-PM thing~be-Q think-IN-DC

No ambiguity of meaning arises in (27). Ambiguity seems to arise only when the word coh-!GOOD is used predicatively with psychological substantives such as nail, neNOU, and i salam!THIS PERSON. The second meaning of coh- does not interfere with non-
psychological substantives such as nns THING as seen in (27). This seems to be
logical since THING is not capable of having psychological attraction towards anything. However, for the negative evaluator nappu-/BAD, there is no problem of
ambiguity of meaning, since the opposite meaning to coh- as predicate ('like') is not nappu- (bad), but silh- (dislike). Therefore, (28)a is perfectly acceptable with no confusion of meaning.

(28)a i salam-un nappu-ta.
this person-TC bad-DC
This person is bad.
(28)b i salam-un nappu-n salam-i-ta.
this person-TC bad-PM person-be-DC
This person is a bad person.

From the examples above the equivalent of something good/bad can be expressed either by 'etten (mwusun) coh-un/nappu-n il' (some good/bad thing), or 'coh-un/ nappu-n etten (mwusun) il' (good/bad something). Although etten (mwusun) il as one concept is proposed as a combinatorial allolex when SOMETHING is the complement of HAPPEN and DO, it can be split into two parts due to its morphological structure (etten
or mwusun + il). This is parallel to 'etten (mwusun) khu-n/cak-un kes ' (something
big/small), as shown in (44) and (45). Another attributive may occur between the parts (theoretically an unlimited number of attributives can be inserted). This is possible because etten (or mwusun) is treated as an attributive grammatically, and requires a
72

Chapter 3
postposed nominal. The order of attributives in a noun phrase is flexible as long as all attributives are followed by a nominal. Therefore there is more than one option for expressing substantive phrases containing modifying words in the Korean-based NSM.
3.2.3 Summary
In this section, the proposed primes of the substantives are examined to see whether they have equivalent concepts in Korean and whether their combinatorial characteristics are along the same lines as the universal grammar proposed in the NSM theory. There are some findings from testing against canonical sentences in terms of both lexicon and syntax as follows:
Firstly, all the proposed semantic primes are found to have equivalent concepts in Korean with different kinds of allolexes depending on the phonological environment, predicate used or the existence of specifiers. The proposed counterparts of the substantives are nail, ne!YOU, nwukwu!SOMEONE, mwues!SOMETHING, salamtul/PEOPLE, and mom/BODY. Allolexes are proposed on the basis of testing the meaning difference through a number of examples. It has been noted that the concept of SOMETHING tends to be specified in Korean by the nature of the predicates. The occurrence of substantives with mental predicates, such as THINK, FEEL, and WANT, seems to be semantically constrained unless they are used based on the pre-given information.
Secondly, the proposed combination of different substantives and other primes is tested and found to be plausible except for the combination of personal substantives
such as, nail, ne/YOU, and i salam/fHIS PERSON (i salamtul!THESE PEOPLE), and the
predicate use of coh-lGOOD. This-is due to the polysemous quality of coh-lGOOD that may bring about ambiguity of meaning when used predicatively. This problem can be solved by using coh-lgood as a modifying word with a modified noun.
73

Chapter 3
3.3 Determiners: THIS, THE SAME, OTHER
3.3.1 Lexicon
3.3.1.1 i/THIS
The word THIS is proposed as one of the universal semantic primes in Wierzbicka (1996a: 42), as this concept is used to "identify what we are talking about". Some of the canonical sentences prov,ided to identify the primitive meaning of THIS are as follows:
This is a big/small thing (dog, basket, etc.).
I did this (thing).
This (thing) happened to me. This (thing) is like this other thing. I saw this other person; these two people; all these people. He did it (sang, danced, etc.) like this. This is one part. This is another part. (about something with many parts) (They know about it.) This (thing) is good/bad.
The concept THIS is often labelled as 'demonstrative', 'deictic' or 'determiner' linguistically. On the basis of cross-linguistic investigations, Wierzbicka (1996a) claims that there is evidence of the universal existence of this concept in all natural languages. In order to test _the existence of the equivalent concept in Korean, however, three different words, i, ku, and ce, have to be explored. In descriptions of the Korean language these are known as definite demonstratives (Sohn, 1994) or deictic adjectives (Lee, 1989). In terms of formality, in modem Korean all of them are used either as prenominal modifiers or as demonstratives, and they can also take different suffixes to be used adjectively (both as descriptives and attributives) or adverbially (Oh, 1998: 59). However, detail of the formal function of these words will not be discussed here since it is sufficient for the present purpose to focus on the semantic level.
The meanings and functions of these words: i, ku, and ce, are known to be somewhat complicated, and have been the subject of much research (cf. Shin J, 1998; Kim, 1985; Cha et all, 1998; Kim I, 1982). In the literature, it is accepted that these three words, i, ku, and ce, are used according to the speaker's judgement of the physical or psychological distance between the three elements involved in deictic use: reference, speaker, and hearer. Oh (1998) claims that the choice among the three words depends on deictic and anaphoric contexts. According to her, in the deictic context, i has to be used only when something is located close to the speaker, while ku is used when it is
74

Chapter 3
located close to the addressee, and ce is used when it is located close to neither the speaker nor the addressee. In an anaphoric context, ce is not used at all in modem
Korean, and i is used with restriction while ku can be used freely. She concludes,
therefore, that the principles of reference are: i refers to the object with [+proximity], ce
refers to the object with [- proximity], [O sharedness], and ku refers to the object with
[-proximity],[+ sharedness] (1998: 101).
If Oh' s (1998) analysis is valid, no word among the three seems to be equivalent to the proposed semantic prime THIS, because a prime is not definable and does not necessarily indicate spatial distance or 'sharedness'. Having discussed the different uses of these words, the first step in identifying the Korean exponent of the prime THIS will be testing these words against canonical sentences.
(1) i lku/ce kos-ey nwuka iss-ta.
this place-LOC someone there is-DC
There is someone in this place.42 (Speaker is referring to the place depending on the distance from him/her)
The context of (1) is vague. However, if this sentence is used in a deictic context, all three words can be used expressing somewhat different meanings from the prime THIS. They are used differently according to the distance between the speaker and the reference. For instance, i is used when the speaker refers to a place close to him/her or a place wheres/he is, ku is used when a place is close to his/her interlocutor or where the interlocutor is, and ce is used when a place is close to neither of them. This principle is parallel to sentence (2).
(2) ilku Ice salam-eykey mwusun il-i ilena-ss-ta.
this person-DAT something-NM happen-PST-DC
Something happened to this person.
The fact that three words can replac~ the English prime THIS in canonical sentences such as (1) and (2) does not mean that these are allolexes. Each of the words is used differently according to physical distance (it can also be extended to psychological distance as discussed in Oh). For the purpose of distinguishing the meanings of these three forms, more examples in different contexts are needed in addition to the canonical

However, the prime THIS is vague in its meaning without a given context like (1) and does not necessarily imply that the reference is physically close, while the Korean word 'i' does in deictic context.

From the example sentences above, it can be noted that the meaning of the three words can be described using primitive concepts such as NEAR, NOT, I, and YOU which
are all proposed as semantic primitives in the NSM theory. For instance, 'i' can mean something along the same lines as 'near me, not near you' . Similarly, ' ku ', and 'ce' can
be defined as 'not near me, near you', and 'not near me, not near you', respectively. This can be confirmed by example (5) below which shows that unlike English, Korean demonstratives are used according to distance, even when they are used to show contrast.

(5)A nay sinpal patak-ey kwumeng-i na-ss-e.

There is a hole in the bottom of my shoe.

that side be-INT

No. this-side-NM

not and this

side be-INS

No! Not in this one but in this (other) one. (pointing out one by one)

The natural English expression will be 'this' and 'that' to refer to 'one side of the pair' and 'another side' in order to contrast two sides of a pair of shoes. The fact that Korean demonstratives are used only according to distance seems to suggest that the previously mentioned meanings of the three words from example (4) are consistent with those of example (5).
The question then arises whether there is among these three words a concept equivalent to the semantic prime THIS. From the examples above, no word among the three seems to mean exactly the same as the prime THIS (which is claimed to be
indefinable) since the possible candidate i seems to be defined as something like 'near me, not near you'. Notice, however, that there are situations where i does not
necessarily mean something 'close to the speaker, not to the interlocutor', as shown in

77

Chapter 3
the -examples below, where a few people, while they are in a car, are listening to very loud music.
(6)A i nolay tule-po-ass-ni? (*ku, *ce)
this song hear-try-PST-INT
Have you heard this song?
B i (*ku, ??ce) nolay-ka Jonh-i cen-eymalha-n ku (*i, *ce) nolay-ya.
this song-NM John-NM before:-at say-PM that song-INS
This song is the one that John told us about before.
In example (6), the song is not closer to any one of the participants and the distance is not practically measurable when someone hears the song in the air.43 The word 'i' in (6) is used not because the song is closer to the speaker than to his/her interlocutor. This phenomenon is pointed out by Goddard (1997b: 22).
It is often said that THIS is a spatial deictic and that its meaning involves a component like 'close to the speaker' , but this claim is inconsistent with the fact that one can speak of this time, this song, this word, this day, and so on, where spatial location is not relevant. (Nor is there any apparent justification · for claiming that this wide range of use is due to 'metaphor' .)
The examples mentioned by Goddard, in which THIS has nothing to do with spatial distance, are parallel to the Korean examples. For instance, the meaning of i in 'i ttay' (this time), 'i ma!' (this word), and 'i nal' (this day), is surely not 'close to the speaker, not to the interlocutor'. The reason why people strongly believe that the meaning of i is like this seems to be that ku (not near me, near you) and ce (not near me, not near you) contain the spatial components in t}Jeir meanings. Consequently choosing to use i instead of one of these paradigmatic alternates, strongly invites a pragmatic inference that the referent is 'near me, not near you'. However, 'near me, not near you' is incompatible with the meaning of i in example (6), and in other cases, such as 'i nal '
(this day) and 'i ttay' (this time) etc. This seems to suggest that the meaning of i cannot
be decomposed into more simple and clear concepts than itself. On this basis, I propose ·i as the counterpart of the prime THIS in Korean. The impossibility of replacing the
43 The use of' ce' in (6) may be acceptable when it refers to the source of the song which is the car audio. This means that 'ce' in 'cekieyse naom;n nolay ' (the song comes from that place) does not re.fer to the song but to the car audio. This is why this expression may be used (though this is not usual) only by someone sitting in the back seat, since the source is not close to him/her. In fact, one would normally signal the source of the song with body movement when using the word 'ce'.
