primitive meaning of i with ku and ce (without a change in meaning) seems to suggest
that these words are not allolexes of i.
Next the question arises whether these two words, ku and ce, can be defined, since those words which are not semantic primes can theoretically be reduced via primes. The NSM approach seeks a reductive paraphrase of non-primitive concepts using semantic primitives. The possibility has already been noted, and a tentative meaning analysis will now be presented. In order to reach a fully satisfactory definition, two more points should be tested. Firstly, unlike the previous examples, 'ku' in example (7) does not mean 'something close to the interlocutor' but refers to 'something commonly shared with the interlocutor'.

(7) 3 nyen cen ku (*i, *ce)-il sayngki-ko cheum manna-nun-kwun-a.

3 year before that-thing

happen-and first time meet-IND-APP-DC

We met for the first time after it/that happened three years ago.(talking to

somebody whom the speaker met 3 years ago when he had lost his whole

family)

The meaning of ku is analysed by Oh (1998: 101) as [- proximity] [+ sharedness] and between these two features, [+ sharedness] seems to be compatible with the meaning of ku in (7).. The word 'ku' in (7) refers to the knowledge that the two interlocutors share in common. This seems to suggest that 'ku' is polysemous. This is not explicitly explained by previous research including Oh's analysis, where the two features are given without indicating how they can be applied and under what conditions. Apart from the obscurity of the technical terms and the symbols, the analysis may suggest that ku means both features at the same time. I propose, therefore, that the meaning of ku has to be defined separately according to its two different meanings. ku 1 is used roughly for 'something that is closer to the interlocutor' and ku 2 for 'something which is commonly shared with the interlocutor'. These seem to be two totally different meanings. One may wonder why the same thing cannot be proposed for i, positing i I for 'something which is close to the speaker, not the interlocutor' and i 2 for other uses.
The answer will be that the single meaning of i as the counterpart of the proposed
semantic prime THIS is sufficient to account for its whole range of use, accordingly there is no need for positing polysemy for i. More importantly there seems to be no definition for the use of i 2, except that it is simply used for cases where the meaning of i I is not compatible.
Secondly, it is necessary to test whether or not the component HERE is somehow involved in the meaning of ce, in other words, whether ce can be paraphrased with the

79

Chapter 3
meaning component HERE. This is because one can hypothesise that ce may mean something like 'something is not here', since it refers to 'something which is close to neither speaker nor interlocutor'. It is not surprising to see a close semantic link between 'something is far from either the speaker and the interlocutor' and 'something is not here'.

(8)a yeki (i pang-ey) sey kaci kwicwungha-n mwulken-i iss-ta.
here (this room-LOC) three CL precious-PM things-NM exist-DC
There are three precious things here (in this room).

(8)b i kes-kwa, ku kes-kwa, ce

kes i-ta.

this thing- and that thing-and that (over there) thing be-DC

This one, that one, and the other one over there.

Example (8) suggests, however, that this hypothesis should be rejected, in other words, ce does not necessarily mean 'not here'. Since the sentence says that 'Three things are here', and the thing referred to by ce is one of them, therefore, the word ce should not include the component 'not here' and can be defined by 'not near me, not near you'.
On the basis of testing canonical sentences and other examples, I propose the word i as the Korean exponent of THIS in Korean and the meaning analysis of ku and ce as follows:

THIS-------• i
ku 1: THIS- not near me, near you (all)44 ce: TIDS- not near me, not near you (all) ku 2: THIS - I want to say something now about someone/something
I think you know who/what

3.3.1.2 ttokkath-lTHE SAME
The prime THE SAME corresponds to the concept of "a fundamental logical relation of identity' (Wierzbicka, 1996a: 43). The canonical sentences containing this concept are as follows:
She did/said the same (the same thing). She thought/wanted/felt the same.

44 Since the prime YOU in NSM is singular, I put prime ALL to indicate the case of having more than one addressee.
80

Chapter 3
I did the same as you. I saw the same person; the same people.
I propose ttokath-, which from a formal point of view consists of the adverb ttok (really) and kath- (the same or similar) as the counterpart of THE SAME. This word belongs to
the adjective class of which there are two kinds: attributive and descriptive (see Sohn, 1994 for detail). This word can easily be identified by testing against canonical sentences.

(9) na-nun ttokath-un il-ul hay-ss-ta.
I-tc the same-NM thing-AC do-PST-DC
I did the same thing.

Having proposed ttokkath- as the Korean counterpart of the prime THE SAME, an explanation is required in relation to another word kath- , which is known to have two meanings: 'sameness' and 'similarity'. This word is not as straightforward as ttokkath-
. and deserves comment for it is equally widely used to express 'sameness' in natural discourse. The main concern seems to be whether the polysemous meanings of the
single word kath-, sameness and similarity, can be distinguished. If the two meanings of kath- cannot be distinguished, then kath- can be used neither for THE SAME nor for LIKE (the word kath- is proposed as the counterpart of LIKE, see 3.17 for detail). From
the NSM perspective, the two primitive concepts THE SAME and LIKE are distinctive and are expected to be lexicalised separately. The proposed meaning of the -prime THE SAME in NSM theory is only ' sameness' , not ' likeness ', and LIKE is proposed as a separate prime to be used to express similarity. One may argue that there is no such
distinction between 'sameness' and ' likeness ' in the word kath- , which can mean both.
Korean dictionary entries describe the meaning of kath-ta (kath + ta-predicative
suffix) as follows:

(a) (this and that) are not different and are one type. (b) The same thing that is not different from the other. (c) The status of being with no change (through time) . (d) Things that can belong to one kind. (MT: K -> E, Saywulimal Khun Sacen [A new Korean big dictionary] ,
1974)

A bilingual dictionary describes the meaning as follows :

(a) the same; identical (b) equal to; uniform

81

(c) like; alike, as (d) similar (e) a sort of; kind of (f) seem; appear; like (g) if it were; in case (h) like; worth of (i) common (Dong-A, 1996)

Chapter 3

Dictionary entries also suggest that the meaning of the word kath-ta is polysemous and
mainly used to express either the 'same ·thing', 'things of the same kind', or 'similar
things'. When kath- is used adverbially it also can mean 'togetherness' in collaboration with the postpositional particle -hako (with). These different meanings are exemplified
below:

(10) John-un ku-uy apeci kath-i khi-ka

khu.;.ta.

John-TC 3SG-GEN father like-ADV height-NM big-DC

John is tall like his father.

(ku-uy) apeci-wa kath-ta.

John's height is the same as (or similar to) his father's.

John-TC (3SG-GEN) father-with like-ADV that work-AC do-IN-DC
John does that work like his father. Or John does that work with his father.

(13) John-un (ku-uy) apeci-hako kath-i ku il-ul ha-n-ta.
John-TC (3SG-GEN) father-with together that work-AC do-IN-DC
John does that work with his father.

(14) John-un apeci-wa (or hdko) kath-un il-ul ha-n-ta.

John-TC father-with

the same-PM work do-IN-DC

John does the same work as his father.

In (10), the word kath-i is used adverbially to express 'similarity' as shown in the
translation, while it is used predicatively in (11) meaning either 'the same' or 'similar to'. Without any pre-given information, the meaning of (11) may be ambiguous as it can be interpreted in two different ways (though they are very closely related to each other).
When kath-i is used adverbially as in (12), with the case particle -(k)wa, it can mean
either 'similarity' or 'together with' as the case particle has two functions: comitative or
relation .word for comparison (e.g. 'as' in English). When kath~ is used with the

45 Intonational clues contribute to the interpretation of the intended meaning.

82

Chapter 3
comitative particle -hako, as in sentence (13), it means only 'together with' . When the word kath- is used attributively, taking a pre-nominal modifying suffix -un, it means the
equivalent of THE SAME as seen in (14). Therefore, the ambiguity of meaning between
sameness and similarity arises only when kath- is used predicatively. This suggests that
the two meanings can be kept distinct by the difference in syntactic construction. Note the following examples. When there is a given statement that 'He bought a
new sports car yesterday'(ku-nun ecey say sports car-lul sa-ss-ta), the following
sentences can be read as indicated.

(l 5)a

na-to kath-un

kes-ul sa-ko·siph-ta.

I-too the same-PM thing-AC buy-want-DC

I want to have the same thing (as that).(referring to an already uttered thing, a

new sports car)

(15)b na-to ku kes kath-un kes-ul kac-ko·siph-ta.
I-too that thing like-PM thing-AC have-want-DC
I want to have something like this.

Sentence (l 5)a is interpreted unambiguously. The only interpretation is 'I want to have
the same thing' and not 'I want to have something like this'. On the other hand, (15)b is interpreted as 'I want to have something like this', and not 'I want to have the same thing'. This is parallel to the following:

(16)a ku il-un talu-n kos-i

anila kath-un kos-eyse ilena-ss-ta.

that event-TC another-PM place-NM not the same-PM place-LOC happen-PST-DC

It happened at the same place, not at another place.

that event-TC this place like

place-Loe happen-PST-DC

It happened at a place like this (place).

The meaning of 'kath-un kos' in sentence (l 6)a is interpreted as 'the same place', not a 'place like this', while 'i kathun kos' in (16)b is interpreted as a 'place like this'. The
contrasts between (15)a and (15)b and (16)a and (16)b show that Koreans distinguish 'sameness' from 'likeness' by a difference in syntactic cons~ructions despite the fact
that the two concepts are encoded in a single word kath- .
It has been noted from the contrast in syntactic frames shown in examples (l5)
and (16) that kath- can clearly mean the equivalent concept of THE SAME only when it
is used attributively without a relatum. However, there are cases where this feature on its own cannot distinguish sameness from similarity. Compare more examples below:

Although there is a relatum (the reference point- i.e. pan salam: person whom I saw) in (17)a kath- means 'the same' while it means 'similar' in (17)b. Therefore, when there is relatum the existence o{ the particle46 -(k)wa or -hako (roughly with) gives the final clue. When kath- is used with the relatum but without the particle -(k)wa or -hako, it
means 'similarity' and not 'sameness'. In order to express the equivalent meaning of THE SAME, firstly, it has to be used as a modifying word as in (15), (16), (17) and (18), and secondly, it does not require the obligatory relatum, and finally when there is a
relatum, the particle, -(k)wa or -_hako, has to be used directly combined with the used
relatum as in (17)a and (18)a (without this particle the meaning is interpreted as similarity).
Based on the test above, two possibilities of expressing the concept THE SAME in
Korean have been discovered: either use the exponent ·ttokkath-, which can be used either attributively or predicatively, or use kath-, though orily attributively, taking the
postposed modified noun without a relatum47 , in order to avoid a possible meaning ambiguity.
In practice, there are also other pragmatic devices used as an aid to understanding the intended meaning of this word. For instance, people may use other lexical items

84

Chapter 3
such as 'wancenhi' (completely) or 'cengmal' (really), which modifies the word kathun
when they want to express the meaning of 'sameness' in natural discourse. Also people
can express themselves more precisely by using such expressions as 'moyangi (in shape), syakkkali (in colour) or khukika (in size) kath-ta' in order to express 'they are
the same in shape, in colour, or in size'. If the difference between 'sameness' and
'likeness' has to be clearly stated, other words such as, ttok-kath48 (which means exactly the same), or pisusha- (similar), can be replaced since they are not polysemous.
In natural discourse, the expression ttokkath- can also be used as below:
(20) na-nun onul ne-hako ttok-kath-un salam-ul po-ass-ta.
I-TC today you-with the same-PM person-AC see-PST-DC
I saw someone who exactly looked like you.
In the above context, ttokkath- does not mean identical. The sentence means that two
people (you and someone) look very much alike. This word can be used predicatively meaning THE SAME without ambiguity in meaning, as in (21) below:
(21) mwues-i mwues-i ttok-kath-ul-kka?
what-NM what-NM the same-PR0-fNT
What are the things that are the same?
ceskalak twu ccak-i ttok-kath-a-yo.
Chopstick two pairs-NM the same-INF-POL
Each pair of chopsticks is the same. (MT: K-> E, A verse of a children's song)

3.3.1.3 ta/u-/OTHER

The meaning of the prime OTHER can be identified by the following sentences:

(It was John, but) she thought (that) it was someone else. (After that), I saw another thing (something else). I didn't do it here - I did it in ·another place (somewhere else). It happened at the same place/time, not at another place/time. This is not a sparrow (robin, magpie, etc.) It is another kind ofbird.

Identifying the Korean counterpart of OTHER is relatively straightforward. I propose
talu- as the Korean exponent, and this word is not polysemous like kath-. In the dictionary (Say wulimal khun sacen, 1974), the meaning of the word talu- is defined by negation of the word kath-. When talu- is used predicatively and adverbially, there have

3.3.2 Syntax

It is claimed that determiners can be used with a combination of substantives or substantive phrases. This claim is tested and fully supported in Korean, and in fact the
Korean exponent i/THIS (This is parallel to other two forms: ku and ce) is mostly used
as an adnominal ·with an overt noun or noun phrase as exemplified below:

(1) i-salam-un khu-ta.
this-person-TC big-DC
This person is big.

(2) i-kes po-a.
this-thing look-INF
Look at this.

(3) i-il-i

nay-key ilena-ki-lul wenha-n-ta.

this-event-NM I-DAT happen-NOM-AC want-IND-DC

I want this to happen to me.

The combination of determiners and the Korean exponent of SOMETHING: kes, and il
(allolexes of mwues/SOMETHING) can be illustrated as follows:

(4) na-nun i-kes-ul hay-ss-ta.
I-TC this-thing-AC do-PST-DC
I did this (thing).

I know this person (these people).

i-ttay ilena-ss-ta.

something-NM this-time happen-PST-DC

Something happened at this time.

i-kos-eyse ilena-ss-ta.

common expression for this is tongsi(ey), which means 'at the same time'. This is a

Sino-Korean word and there is no semantic difference between this expression and kathun ttay(ey) as shown in (16)a and (16)b.

In many cases the expression tongsi(ey) sounds more natural and conventional than kathun ttay, although, either can be used to express the equivalent of at the same time in explications.
All combinations are found to be possible in Korean, and in fact, all the proposed determiners have to be combined with postposed nominals obligatorily, due to their formal characteristics.
It is claimed (Wierzbicka, 1996a: 126) that the prime THIS has a double class membership universally: it can function as both a determiner and as a substantive (which is also called quasi-substantive). However, this claim is not valid for i/THIS which takes a postposed nominal or noun phrase as mentioned previously.

(17)a i-kes hay-la.
this-thing do-IMP
Do this thing.

(17)b *i hay-la.
this do-IMP
Do this.

(18)a i-il-i

nay-key ilena-ki-lul wenha-n-ta.

this-event-NM I-DAT happen-NOM-AC want-IN-DC

I want this to happen to me.

(18)b *i-i

nay-key ilena-ki-lul wenha-n-ta.

this-NM I-DAT happen-NOM-AC want-IN-DC

I want this to happen to me.

In most cases where the English prime THIS is used as quasi-substantive, as in the above examples, in Korean the abstract noun -kes49 must be overtly realised. In natural discourse, however, instead of -kes, specified referents (e.g. event, person, book etc) are

(26) i-kath-un mwues

It is claimed that the prime THIS can refer to anaphoric reference. This claim is fully supported in Korean. When i/THIS is used as an anaphoric reference to a situation described previously, for example as in (28) below, i/THIS can be used either by itself or combined with -kes or -il (SOMETHING).

(28)a

na-nun ney-ka i-eytayhay malha-n-ci mol-lass-ta.
I-TC you-NM this-about say-PM-NOM not know-PST-DC
I didn't know that you said (anything) about this. (referring to a sentence previously spelt out)

(28)b na-nun ney-ka i-kes-eytayhay malha-n-cwul mol-lass-ta.
I-TC you -NM this thing-about say-PM-NOM not know-PST-DC
I didn't know that you said (anything) about this.

In examples (28)a and (28)b, i/THIS and ikes/THIS THING do not refer to either 'thing' or 'event', but to the whole situation spelt out in the previous clause or sentence. Both are quite common in use.

Chapter 3
Goddard calls (2002c: 305) for cross-linguistic investigation about the relationship between the prime OTHER and 'different'. He raises a_question whether they are allolexes or not. In the Korean language, the two different words are lexicalised in the single word talu-, as illustrated in the examples below:
(31) yeki-ey iss-nun i-kes-un ceki-ey iss-nun ku-kes-kwa talu-ta.
here-LOC exist-PM this-thing-TC there-LOC exist-PM that-thing-with different-DC
This thing here is different from that thing over there.
(32) na-nun ne-wa talu-key hay-ss-ta.
I-TC you-with other-ADV do-PST-DC
I did it differently (from you).
The Korean prime talu- is used predicatively and adverbially in (31) and (32), respectively. In Korean, this is the only possible option to express the equivalent of English 'different'. Thus Korean does not draw a lexical distinction parallel to the English distinction between other and 'different'.
The two_Korean exponents, (ttok)kathunl THE SAME and talu-lOTHER can be followed by any nominals except na/1 and ne!YOU. They can also be combined freely with other attributives like quantifiers, descriptors, and evaluators. When both (ttok)kathun/fHE SAME and talu-lOTHER are used in combination with other modifying words, their positions are flexible since the Korean noun phrase has more than one order. For example, both (33)a and (33)b are equally good sentences. In (33)a after the word ne-wa, a comma has to be used in order to avoid a possible confusion in the interpretation.
(33)a na-nun ne-wa, talu-n twu myeng-uy khu-n salam-ul po-ass-ta.
I-TC you and other-PM two CL-ATR big-PM person-AC see-PST-DC
I saw you and two other big people.
Without the comma, the sentence can mean ' I saw two big people who are different from you' . In verbal conversation, the meaning can be distinguished by an intonationally defined phrase (cf. Yim, 2002).
(33)b na-nun ne-wa twu myeng-uy talu-n khu-n salam-ul po-ass-ta.
I-TC you-and two CL-ATR other-PM big-PM person-AC see-ST-DC
I saw you and two other big people.
Either one of the two (33)a-b can be used for the purpose of the Korean-based NSM, without any concern about causing a difference in meaning.
91

3.2.3 Summary

Chapter 3

The proposed semantic primitives of the determiners, THIS, THE SAME, and OTHER have lexical equivalents in Korean and their combinatorial characteristics are found to be consistent with those proposed for universal grammar except for the use of i/THIS as a quasi-substantive.
The conceptual equivalent of THIS in Korean is found to be lexicalised in the word i, and other demonstratives such as ku, and ce are explicated via the prime i. The use of these words in some contexts on the basis of the adjunct definition can be helpful for a reader's comprehension. Therefore, I propose to use these alternatives, ce and ku, rather than the prime i/THIS when they sound more idiomatic due to contextual requirements of Korean.
From testing in canonical sentences and more examples, I propose that the Korean
exponent for the prime THE SAME is ttokkath- and kath-un (kath + prenominal modifying suffix) used without a relatum (i.e. ~ + kathun). When there is a preceding
relatum (a + kathun) the existence of the linking word '-hako' or '-kwa' can be the cue
for the correct interpretation (a + (k)wa kathun: meaning sameness, and a + kathun:
meaning similarity). While all the proposed combinations of determiners with other primitives are
found to be along the same lines as the hypothesised universal grammar, a few portmanteau forms are identified which are more natural and more wi~ely used expressions: ilehkey or ichelem for the combination of LIKE and THIS for adverbial use (did like this) and ilen for that of adnominal use (something like this). All three Korean exponents require the overt use of a postposed nominal when they are used as determiners.

92

Chapter 3
3.4 Quantifiers: ONE, TWO, SOME, ALL, MANY/MUCH
3.4.1 Lexicon
3.4.1.1 han(a)IONE and twu(l)ITWO
For the exact translation of the primes ONE and TWO, I propose han(a) and twu(l), respectively. The proposed primitive meanings can be grasped in the following canonical sentences:
This is one thing. There are two things. There is one person. This is one part ofa person. There are two kinds ofpeople.
These primes are relatively straightforward to identify, although Korean uses a dual numeral system: (i) Sino-Korean (SK) and (ii) pure Korean (PK). From the provided canonical sentences, it has been found that the PK words, han(a) twu(l), are more
appropriate than the SK words: ii (one) and i (two), since PK numerals are normally
combined with PK words and SK words are combined with SK words although there is not a conceptual difference between the two systems. 51 In NSM explications these words play an important role in describing' the concept of singular and plural (not one), body parts, and the grammatical category of duals (Wierzbicka, 1996a: 44).
The proposed Korean exponents can have different syntactic functions by taking different suffixes. They can be used either predicatively or attributively as exemplified below:

(1) i-kes-un

hana-(i)ta.

this-thing-TC one-(be)DC

This is one thing.

(2) i-kes-un twul-i-ta.
this-thing-TC two-be-DC
These are two things.

51 Pure Korean numbers exist only for the numbers 1-99, and are mainly used for the counting of relatively small numbers of objects or people, such as pieces of fruit or students in a classroom. In a word, they are used for 'finger counting'. By contrast, Sino-Korean numbers are used for all larger numbers, and in general for essentially abstract counting such as mathematics, decimals fractions, distances and money. In terms of telling time, pure Korean numbers are used for hours, and Sino Korean numbers for minutes (cf. Buzo and Shin, 1994).
93

(3) han salam-i iss-ta.
one person-NM there is-DC
There is one person.

Chapter 3

(4) twu salam-i iss-ta.
two person-NM there is-DC
There are two people.

(5) hana (twul)-nun manh-ci anh-ta.

one (two)-TC

many-N0M not-DC

One is not many/much.

The words han/ONE and twu/fWO can also be used as quasi-substantives as seen in (5) above. There is no semantic difference between the two forms: hana and han, and twul and twu, since their form is determined by their syntactic role and combination with
other words in a sentence. These forms, therefore, have to be regarded as the combinatorial allolexes, which are in complementary distribution, and this can be illustrated as follows:

ONE • han/ noun
hana/elsewhere
Two• twu/ noun
twul/elsewhere

3.4.1.2 motwu(motun)I ALL
The meaning of the prime ALL can be identified by the following canonical sentences:
They all died. All his sons/daughters are now married. Because ofall this, I was late (missed the train, etc.) This person knows all about it. (No-one lives forever.) Everybody (all people) dies. Everyone (all people) knows it is bad to do this. The fire burnt everything (all things) (e.g. in the house, on the island) (From the top ofthe mountain) I could see everything (all things)
(below me). God made everything (all things).
There seem to be two candidates for the Korean counterpart of the prime ALL: motwu and ta. Both are used equally widely and can be used interchangeably although they differ in terms of syntactic behaviour. The word motwu can be used as a nominal and adverbially. When it is used attributively its form is changed into motun as shown in

Chapter 3
examples (7) and (8). The two variants are in complementary distribution. The .following examples show that the three different lexical items are not conceptually different:

(6)a kutul-un motwu cwuk-ess-ta.

They have all died.

(6)b kutul-un ta cwuk-ess-ta.
they-TC all die-PST-DC
They have all died.

(7) motun salamtul-i i-kes-ul

al-ko ·iss-ta.

all people-NM this-thing-AC know-PRG-DC

All people know this.

Between the two words, motwu and ta, I propose motwu as the primary Korean exponent of ALL owing to a syntactic constraint on the use of the word ta as it can only be used adverbially. However, ta can be treated as a lexical variant of motwu when the concept of ALL is used adverbially. This is based on the evidence shown in (6)a and (6)b. Sometimes the two words are used together to emphasise the meaning as in (8). The sequence of the two words is, however, always motwu ta and not *ta motwu. In natural discourse they are- often found together, presumably to emphasise the meaning of ALL as in (8)a.

3.4.1.3. manh-/MANY/MUCH

Chapter 3

The meaning of the prime MANY/MUCH can be identified by the canonical sentences below:

How many children does she have? She has many daughters. I didn 't say much. How much water is there (in that well/bowl etc.)? There is much/a lot ofwater there. Not many people live here. There are many places like this.

For the counterpart of MANY/MUCH in Korean, the bilingual dictionaries suggest two candidates: manh- and yele-. Both are the most frequently used translational equivalents of 'many' or 'much'. Two relevant questions will be whether these two Korean words are conceptual equivalents of the proposed prime MANY/MUCH, and whether there is any difference in meaning between the two: manh- and yele-. Sohn (1994: 385) defines the meaning of manh- as "many and much" and yele- as "a large number or many". According to him, the difference between them must be the nature of referents: for instance, manh- can be used for age, water, food, and knowledge, and other tangible objects (both discrete and.indiscrete), while yele- is relatively restricted (used only for discrete referents). The suggested referential difference between the two words can be tested as below:

(9)A wuli sakwa-namwu-ey sakwa-ka yele-kay tal-i-e iss-ta.
1pl apple-tree-LOC apple-NM many-CL hanging-CAU-PRG-DC
There are many apples in our apple tree.
B manh-i iss-ni?
many-ADV there is-INT
Are there many?
A ani. manh-ci anh-a.
No. many-NOM not-INS
No, there are not many.
The conversation in (9) suggests that the difference between the two words is beyond the nature of the referents: manh- seems to mean a bigger number than yele although both are vague. The word manh- is an absolute concept of large number or big quantity the same as the English exponent MANY/MUCH. I propose, therefore, manh- as the Korean exponent for the prime pointing out the lack of formal distinction between
96

Chapter 3
countable and uncountable in Korean grammar. The proposed exponent manh- is used
regardless of the nature of referents, as shown in the following examples:

(10) ku salam-un canye-ka elmana manh-ci?

How many children does he have?

(11) na-nun manh-un mal-ul ha-ci anh-ass-ta.

The word manh- is a versatile stem that can take different suffixes according to its
syntactic role: It can be used attributively by taking a pre-nominal modifying suffix
-u(n), adverbially by taking an adverbial suffix -i, and also predicatively by taking different sentence markers (e.g. -ta, -ni), but it is never used on its own. Since there is no conceptual difference between the different forms (manhi, manhun, manhta) but they
have different syntactic functions, they have to be considered as allolexes, which are in complementary distribution.

3.4.1.4 etten Noun -tu/ (myech)/SOME
The English word 'some' is polysemous. Among the polysemous meanings of this _word, 'indeterminate number' is proposed as the semantic primitive SOME. The prime SOME is a "concept which functions as a quantifier, situated somewhere between ONE

97

Chapter 3
and ALL" (Wierzbicka, 1996a: 74). The following canonical sentences contain this concept:
I know about some kinds ofbirds. Some ofthese people moved to another camp, and some ofthem stayed. Many ofthem went to the east, but some ofthem went to the west. Some people can do this - some people can't. Some ofthese yams (lemons, etc.) are good, but some are rotten. In some places there are .lots offish (but it is not good to fish here).
The two possible candidates suggested.by different bilingual dictionaries are cokum and yakkan. Both of them can be used in a sentence like (17):

(17) wuntongcang-ey salam-tul-i cokumlyakkan iss-ta.

playground-Loe person-PL-AC some

there is-DC

There are some people in the playground.

However, neither of them seems to be equivalent to the primitive meaning of SOME, as
they imply being in the minority. The notion of 'something less' is not a part of the
primitive meaning of SOME. The words, cokum and yakkan, mean literally 'a little, or a few' and can be used as an indeterminate small number expressing contrastive meaning to the word manh- (many or much). Since both words, cokum or yakkan imply 'less' rather than 'pure vagueness' in many contexts these cannot be Korean exponents for the prime SOME.
Instead, the translations of the given canonical sentences suggest two different Korean expressions, which appear to correspond to the primitive meaning of SOME: myech and etten -tu!. The reason I am proposing two different expressions is similar to why Goddard suggests (2002b: 128-129) two different lexical items in Malay, beberapa and ada, to label 'vague number SOME' and 'existential partitive SOME', respectively, although the English-based NSM uses a single form of SOME in all cases.. Goddard proposes that there are two different SOMEs. The former is not contrastive in any sense referring to unspecified number, while the latter is normally contrastive and closely linked with an existential element. The Korean word myech seems to be used to express the former, while etten (-tu[) is close to what he calls an 'existential partitive'. The former is used as an indefinite quantifier combining classifiers, while the latter is the combination of the indefinite article and the plural marker. The canonical sentences are transposed into Korean via either of the two expressions. Neither of them on its own,
however, can express the equivalent meaning of SOME in all cases.

98

Chapter 3
The two expressions will be dealt with in tum. The first word myech is used as
follows:

(18) chayksang-wi-ey chayk-i myech-kwen iss-ta.

desk-above-LOC book-NM some-CL

there is-DC

There are some books on the desk.

(19) myech salam-i i il-ul hay-ss-ta.
some person-NM this work-AC do-PST-DC
Some people did this work.

(20) myech myeng-i i il-ul hay-ss-ni?

some CL-NM

this work-AC do-PST-INT

How many people did this work?

(21) myech salam-i i-il-ul

hay-ss-nun-ci molu-n-ta.

some person-NM this-work-AC do-PST-IN-NOM not know-IN-DC

I don't know how many people did this work.

The word myech is used as an indefinite quantifier in (18) and (19) in that it is not
specific in number or quantity and it refers to more than one without implying majority or minority. The same word is used as a questioning word in (20) and a connection
word that introduces an embedded sentence like (21 ). The word myech 52 is a pre-
nominal indefinite quantifier that is freely combined with all kinds of classifiers as are other numerical quantifiers as shown in (18) and (19). No meaning difference is found
between myech and SOME when they are used in these contexts.
However, in some other canonical sentences, the English exponent SOME is used not only to express purely quantification or plurality but the existence of a category of people or things. In order to express the concept of so called 'existential partitive' in
Korean, etten -tu! has to be used, as shown in the following examples:

(22)a etten salamtul-un i il-ul ha-l·swu·iss-ko

some people-TC do-cannot-DC
Some people can do this and some people cannot.

this people among some people-TC other place-DIR go-and

some thing-PL-TC rotten-PST-DC
Some of these pears are good, but some are rotten.
(25)a i salamtul-cwung etten salamtul-un cwuk-ess-ta.
this people-among some people-TC die-PST-DC. Some of these people died.
The word etten can be used to express the non-specificity of referents in tenns of
'identity' rather than in terms of 'number' in daily conversation. This word is not considered as a quantifier in Korean grammar, and is used either with a singular or plural referent. It is considered as an indefinite article similar to the English article 'a' or 'some' in English sentences like (26) and (26)a.
(26) etten . salam-i cip-ulo tuleka-ss-ta.
a person-NM house-into enter-PST-DC
A person went into that house.
(26)a etten salc;imtul-i cip-ulo tuleka-ss-ta.
some people-NM house-DIR enter-PST-DC
Some people went into that house.
In the above examples, the combination of etten (non-specific pre-nominal modifying word) and -tu! (plural marker) expresses jointly indefinite plurality. This combination as
the representation of one concept ·seems to be somewhat unusual or awkward since it never appears as one expression in descriptions of the Korean language in the literature.
Although people often combine the two expressions etten and -tu! in natural
conversations, they are not generally considered as one concept.
Each of the two expressions, myech and etten -tu! has been tested in two different types of canonical context so far. As a result it has been found that myech is the equivalent concept of 'vague number-some', while etten -tul is the equivalent to
'existential partitive-some'. However, the question is in what context can they mean the
same thing. Compare examples (22)a to (26)a, which contain the expression etten -tul with (22)b to (26)b _which contain the quantifier myech.

Chapter 3

(22)b myech salamtul-un i il-ul

ha-Z.swu· iss-ko

It is shown that both expressions can be used in contexts like (22) to (26) without causing any grammatical concern. While no syntactic restriction is found_ for each expression, a slight semantic difference is noted. For instance, in (22)a, the contrastive
meaning (or existential partitive meaning) is expressed with the expression etten -tu!. The expression myech appears to be inappropriate in this context. In (23) to (25), where
the selective valency option is included, the two expressions seem to mean the same.
This means that myech can be substituted for etten -tu!, and vice-versa without carrying
meaning difference in this context. In (26), however, there seems to be a subtle meaning difference between (26)a and
(26)b: when using myech, the focus seems to be on the vague number of the modified noun, while when using the expression etten -tu!, it seems to stress the uncertainty of the
identity of the modified noun. For instance, the intended meaning of (26)a seems to be 'more than one person, which are vague in terms of identity, went into the house' while (26)b implies that 'more than one person, which are vague in terms of how many, went into the house'. This difference is paraphrased for comparison as follows:

101

Chapter 3
(26)a I want to say that more than one person went into the house
I don't know who these people are
(26)b I want to say that more than one person went into the house
I don't know how many people
To sum up these findings: (i) etten -tu! has to be used to express the contrastive meaning (or existential partitive meaning), (ii) either etten -tu! and myech can be used in
selective valency options (iii) there is a subtle semantic difference between the two expressions when they are used in pure indefinite plurality such as in (26).
Goddard (2002b: 129-130) suggests a close relationship between the partitive-
existential concept of SOME (etten -tu!) and the existential prime THERE IS/ARE.
However, it is not clear how closely linked these two concepts are from the Korean perspective. It is possible that sentence (22)a involving a relative clause can be said
using the word iss-lTHERE IS as in (22)c:

There are people who can do this work and there are those who cannot.

The meanings of the two examples_(22)a and (22)c do not seem to differ. Moreover, the

&

The close semantic link between the expression etten -tu! and -iss in the examples above (there is/are) seems to be consistent with Goddard's claims. He believes that the difference between two different constructions such as (27)a and (27)b is 'about-ness'. To quote him on this point (Goddard, 2002b: 311):

A sentence like there are people who don't like bananas is about what does (or doesn't) exist in the world. It is what has sometimes been called a "thetic" statement, meaning that it expresses recognition of an overall state or situation without being divided into a topic part and a comment part (cf. Sasse 1987). A sentence like some people don't like bananas, on the other hand, is a so-called "categorical" statement, meaning that it divides naturally into topic-comment structure: it is a statement "about' people (or about
some people).

The final comment on the two expressions is that they can be used together in the same sentence in·natural discourse as follows:

(28) kath-un il-i

etten talu-n54 myech salam-tul-ekey-to ilena-ss-ta.

the same-PM event-NM some other-PM some person-PL-DAT-too happen-PST-DC

The same thing happened to some other people.

It is not clear whether the possibility of using both expressions in the same sentence as in (28) suggests that they are different concepts although they are very closely linked to each other, or whether they together express the equivalent concept of SOME, and one of the two can be treated as redundant. The question will be what is the exact relationship between the two expressions: Are they allolexes, distinctive concepts or one
concept together (etten+ myech + -tu!)? The difficulty of identifying the equivalent
meaning of the prime SOME in Korean seems to be due to the lack of a conventionalised expression that can mean both 'vague number-some' and 'existential-paiiitive'. Also it is not clear whether the concept of SOME in canonical sentences expresses

54 The addition of the word talun (other) makes the sentence much more natural both in English and Korean.

103

Chapter 3
indefiniteness only with respect to number or also with respect to the identity of the referents since Korean seems to distinguish the two instances.
Both expressions, etten -tu! or myech, have a wider rnnge of meaning besides the discussed ones. In natural discourse, for instance, the word myech is not conventionally used to refer to large number although it is vague. This may be because of the possibility of expressing myech sip (some tens), myech payk (some hundreds), and myech chen (some thousands) and so forth. Choosing bare myech among alternative choices may give an impression of referring to something small although the word myech does not inherently imply minority. Also in everyday life people tend to describe amount or number as precisely as possible using such expressions as manh-(many), cokum (a few), yakkan (a little bit) rather than purely vague number. The word myech can sometimes be reduplicated as in (29) as a fixed expression.

(29) myech (myech) salamtul-i i-kes-eytayhay a-n-ta.

some

people-NM this-thing-about know-IN-DC

Some people know about this.

The word etten is used widely as an indefinite article in natural discourse as seen _in (30) and (31 ).

(30) etten salam-i o-l·kes·i-ta.
some person-NM come-PRO-be-DC
A person will come (Someone will come).

You don't know who this p~rson is.

Based on the tested examples, I propose to include both expressions, myech and etten -tu!, in the Korean-based NSM lexicon in order to express the equivalent of the proposed prime SOME in all canonical sentences without making a final decision on their status as allolexes or distinctive concepts.

3.4.2 Syntax
There are a few points relevant to Korean quantifiers worth mentioning before testing the proposed combinatorics of Korean exponents against canonical sentences. The first point is that Korean has a well-developed classifier system. Korean seems to be one of
104

Chapter 3
the languages that do not have a grammatical category of definiteness and has a rich classifier system. The types of noun phrase that contain quantifier and classifier can be grouped as follows:
(a) Noun+ Quantifier+ Classifier haksayng twu myeng student two CL two students
(b) Quantifier+ Classifier+ Attributive particle+ Noun twu myeng-uy haksayng two CL-ATR student two students
(c) Noun+ Numeral haksayng twul student two two students
(d) Qunatifier + Noun twu haksayng two student two students
Although all the noun phrase types presented above can be used to express the same meaning of 'two students', Park (1990) believes that (a) and (c) are indefinite while (b) and (d) are definite or specified. In the phrase structure of (c), a numeral is considered as having the same function as 'Quantifiers+ Classifiers'. He con-eludes his analysis on the basic quantifier phrase structure as 'Noun I (haksayng/student)+ Quantifier (twultwo + Noun II myeng/classifier)'. Noun II includes not only nouns but also classifiers. For the purpose of Korean-based NSM, sentences containing numerical quantifiers (ONE and TWO) are translated into Korean with this phrase structure: Noun+ Quantifier+ Classifier, on the basis of Park' s (1990) analysis. There does not seem to be any meaning difference between the two sentences (1 )a and (1)b below:
(1 )a i kos-ey salam twu-myeng-i iss-ta.
this place-Loe person two-CL-NM there is-DC
There are two people in this place.
(1 )b i kos-ey salam twul-i iss-ta.
this place-LOC person two-NM there is-DC
There are two people in this place. ·
105

Chapter 3
Korean quantifiers, in particular numerical quantifiers, readily combine with classifiers. Although the classifier system in Korean is rich in number and specified in its use, the relationship between classifier and noun is not that each noun is assigned to a separate classifier. There are disputes on membership of some words between bound noun and classifier, as well as different views on categorisation of classifiers (cf. Ko C, 1996). One relevant issue for the purpose of this thesis seems to be the fact that there is a syntactic difference between the Korean exponents of quantifiers when they are used in combination with other exponents of primitives. The two numerical quantifiers, han/ONE, twu/TWO, and myech!SOME combine with classifier obligatorily while others, such as manh-lMANY, and motun/ALL have to be combined with nouns, not classifiers. This grammatical requirement appears in all cases where quantifiers combine with different kinds of substantives. However, the replacement of classifier for noun certainly does not affect the meaning.
Another point to draw attention to is plurality marking in Korean. According to Sohn (1994: 268-269),
The plural marker is the nominal inflectional suffix -tu! that appears after a noun designating either an animate or an inanimate object... Without the plural marker -tu!, a common noun is neutral with regard to plurality... Wh~n a common noun is definitised, however, marking of plurality becomes obligatory, because unmarked definite nouns always refer to a ·singular. Thus, ku salam is always the singular 'that person' and ku slam-tu! is always the plural 'those people'.
Plurality is marked by the suffix -tu!, as well as by other quantifiers, such as manh-un, myech, or yele. When these attributive words that express plurality modify a noun, the suffix -tu! is optional, since plurality is already marked by the other quantifiers. However, when a numerical quantifier such as, twu (two), is used, the plural marker -tu! is not marked at all. For the purpose of the Korean-based NSM, the plural suffix -tu! is attached only when it makes a mea~ing difference in cases like those noted in Sohn (1994) above. Therefore, in cases like (2), the plural suffix -tu! will not be explicit while in cases like (3), it will be marked.
(2)a manlt-un kes-ul ha-ko·siph-ta.
many-PM thing-AC do-want-DC
I want to do many things.
106

Chapter 3
(2)b manh-un kes-tul-ul ha-ko·siph-ta.
many-PM thing-PL-AC do-want-DC
I want to do many things.
(3)a ku kes-ul ha-ko·siph-ta.
that thing-AC do-want-DC
I want to do that thing.
(3)b ku kes-tul-ul ha-ko·siph-ta.
that thing-PL-AC do-want-DC
I want to do those things.
Having pointed out the two relevant features of Korean quantifiers, the combinability of the Korean exponents of quantifiers is tested.
First of all, it is claimed that quantifiers can be combined with nwukwu!SOMEONE, mwues!SOMETHING, kos!PLACE, •Conglyu/KIND, pwupwun!PART, and pen/TIME-occurrence. These combinations are tested and presented in different sections where each of the primitives is discussed (See 3.2.2. for Quantifier + SOMEONE and SOMETHING, 3.12.2.1. for Quantifier+ TIME, 3.13.2.1 for Quantifier+ PLACE, 3.16.2 for Quantifier+ KIND and PART). All the combinations are found to be feasible in Korean.
Quantifiers are claimed to participate in a construction of "selective relation" (Goddard, and Wierzbicka, 2002: 48). The selective or comparative substantive phrase such as one/two/many of these people is proposed as universal. This claim is valid in Korean. However, the postposition cwung (lit. among) has to be employed i!_l order to form this construction as shown in the following examples:

The role of an independent 'complement' (without support of 'substantives' or 'quasi-substantives'), in relation to the primes MANY/MUCH and ALL requires testing.

The two ways of expressing the same meaning are shown in comparison bet~een (7)a
and (7)b, and (8)a and (8)b. In (7)a manh- is used adverbially without having a modified noun, while in (7)b it is used as a modifying word. Also in (8)a, motwu is used and it does not require a modified noun, while motun in (8)b is an attributive that obligatorily
requires a modified noun. They ar~ proposed previously as different realisations of one concept and their distribution is found to be complementary. In the Korean language the role of independent complement is supported.
One point has to be explained in relation to the combination of motun/ALL with
other substantives. The question whether 'everything' differs from 'ALL +
SOMETHING' is raised by Wierzbicka (2002a: 86). Note the examples below show that
there is no separate word for 'everything' other than 'motun kes 1 (all things) in Korean.

(9) na-nun i kos-ey iss-nun motun kes-ul kac-ko·siph-ta.

The examples (9) and (l0)a suggest that from the Korean language point of view, the two expressions 'everything' and 'all things' are not lexically distinguished. There is no other expression than motun kes to express either of them. One possible semantic equivalent to 'everything' seems to be the word cenpwu as seen in (1 0)b. This is a SinoKorean word that is often translated as the 'whole thing'.
Wierzbicka suggested that 'everything' can be analysed as 'ALL+ ONE+ THING', which has one more component of 'one'. The analysis of 'everything' as 'ALL+ ONE+ THING' suggests that the Korean equivalent of 'everything' is cenpwu, since this word denotes 'one whole thing'. However, in most cases the two words are interchangeable, for instance, in example (11) one can substitute for the other without changing the meaning. except for the subtle difference of nuance in (12).

(11) na kutay-eykey motwu tuli-li.

all give-will

I will gi~e you (my) all.

The substitution of cenpwu for motwu does not seem to alter meaning, both are used naturally in daily conversation. However, the situation in (12) seems not to be the same,

(12) achim-ey mwuntuk nwu,:z-ul tte-po-ni, nachsel-key

The expression motun kes (all things) means 'every single thing' in that room, and the replacement of cenpwu for motun kes is not natural. One would not say that "the whole thing in the room is unfamiliar" since the contents of a room are not normally referred

Chapter 3
to as a unitary thing. In this respect, cenpwu seems close to 'everything (as one thing)' semantically, while motun kes expresses 'all things'.
3.4.3 Summary
The quantifier primes: ONE, TWO, MANY/MUCH, ALL, SOME have been tested in terms of lexicon and syntax. From testing against canonical sentences, it has been found that all proposed quantifiers have lexical exponents, and that their combinatorial characteristics are along the same lines as the proposed universal grammar. The findings can be summarised as follows:
Firstly, there are combinatorial allolexes in the cases of han!ONE, twu/TWO, manh-lMANY, and motwu(motun)IALL. Allolexes are posited for each exponent since they differ in their syntactic roles. They are in complementary distribution, and consequently no meaning difference is found between these variants in the given canonical sentences.
For the case of the prime SOME, however, there seem to be two different expressions that are not entirely complementary, in the sense that in some canonical sentences both can be used (i.e. in subset valency option), while in other contexts only one is allowed in order to ~xpress the equivalent meaning of SOME. Unlike English no generic term is found in Korean to capture all meanings of SOME expressed in the canonical sentences. It is unclear at this stage whether the two exponents proposed as counterparts are allolexes or distinctive concepts.
While the combinatorial properties of the quantifiers are found to be along the same lines as the proposed universal grammar of the NSM · theory, the obligatory requirement of a classifier when forming a quantifier phrase (quantifier combined with noun phrase) is noted. The requirement of a classifier, which is a Korean language specific feature, however, does not interfere with the proposed meaning of the quantifier phrases. Among several different possible combinations of quantifier, nouns, and classifier, two options are found to be basic and neutral in that they are not syntactically
restricted or semantically marked: (i) Noun + Quantifier + Classifier and (ii) Noun +
numeral.
110

Chapter 3

3.5 Evaluator: GOOD, BAD
3.5.1 Lexicon
Identifying the counterparts of GOOD and BAD is relatively straightforward. The meanings of these primes can be identified by the following canonical sentences:
Good people do not do things like this. This was good/badfor me.
It is good/bad ifsomeone does this. It is not good/bad ifsomeone says this about other people. It is good (not good) ifpeople can do something like this.
If this happens, it will be good/badfor you.
It will be good/badfor you ifyou do this.
I know this person (place) well.
I propose coh- (good), and nappu- (bad) as the Korean exponents. They are stems that can take different suffixes, such as -un, -ul, -key, -ta, -aha-, aci-, aha!-, acil, umyen, according to their syntactic roles. In terms of formal characteristics, coh- (good) and nappu- (bad) belong to the adjective class where two types of adjectives are found: predicative and attributive. .When coh- or nappu- is used attributively, the pre-nominal modifying suffix -(u)n is attached, while different sentence markers, depending on sentence types, can be attached when they are used predicatively (see Sohn, 1994:369370 for detailed illustration). In (1) and (2), coh- and nappu- are used attributively taking the adjectival suffix -(u)n, whereas in (3) and (4) they are used predicatively taking the declarative sentence marker -ta.

(1) i kos-ey coh-/nappu-(u)n salamtul-i iss-ta.

this place-Loe good/bad-PM

people-NM there are-DC

There are good/bad people in this place.

(2) i kos-eyse coh-/nappu-(u)n il-i

ilena-ss-ta.

this place-Loe good/bad-PM

event-NM happen-PST-DC

Something good/bad happened in this place.

(3) ilehkey ha-nun kes-un colt-/nappu-ta.
like this do-PM thing-TC good/bad-DC
Doing like this is good/bad (It is good/bad to do like this).

While there is no concern about the attributive use of the two exponents, one explanation is required in relation to the predicative use of coh- (good). The word coh-

111

Chapter 3
(good) is polysemous, having as a second meaning, 'like' (in the sense of being attracted to someone/something) when it is used predicatively. The bilingual dictionaries 56 suggest that the meaning of coh-ta (good) can be summarised as 'satisfaction' 'state of goodness' and 'appropriateness' as well as 'a favourable feeling towards either someone or something'. Also Lee P. (1999) claims that cohta (good) has a second meaning, which is a 'good feeling or an attraction towards someone or something' apart from the meaning of positive evaluation. He points out that this meaning was developed from the evaluative meaning 'a state of appropriateness or satisfaction' used at the end of the 16th century (quoting Lee H, 1999). Therefore, example sentence (4), for instance, can be interpreted either as this thing is good or 'I like this thing' .

(4) i kes-un colt-ta.
this thing-TC good-DC
This thing is good. Or I like this thing.
Case particles and topic markers sometimes can give a hint for a pragmatically conventionalised interpretation: a sentence with topic marker -(n)un would be interpreted as 'this is good' while the nominative particle -i (or -ka) after i-kes (this) is likely to be interpreted as 'I like this'. However, this convention is highly contextual, and accordingly the interpretation can be subjective depending on a speaker's individual judgement. Thus, meaning ambiguity arises when coh- is used predicatively: between the ' evaluative' meaning and 'psychological attraction', as seen in (4) where two different interpretations are possible.
Since the proposed primitive meaning of GOOD in the NSM inventory is only positive evaluation, it needs to be tested whether the predicate use of coh- can be isolated from its second meaning. When it is used as a verb by taking the verbalisers -aha- as in (5) it means only 'an attraction towards someone/something', and when it is used as an attributive it expresses only 'evaluation of someone or something' as in (6). The ambiguity arises only when the word coh- (good) is used predicatively as in (4).

(5) na-nun i kes-ul

co!t-a-ha-n-ta.

I-NM this thing-AC good-CMP-do-IN-DC

I like this thing.

56 Saywulimal Khun Sacen[The new Korean Big dictionary] (1974), Kwuke Taysacen [The Big Korean dictionary] (1965), and Kumsungphan Kwuke Taysacen (1991)
112

Chapter 3

(6) i kes-un(i)

coh-un kes-i-ta.

this thing-NM(TC) good-PM thing-be-DC

This thing is (a) good (thing).

Therefore, there are possible solutions in avoiding meaning ambiguity: The first
way is to avoid the predicative use of coh- (that is coh-ta) by replacing it with the noun phrase cohun kes (good thing), which is a combination of an attributive use of coh- and a modified noun57, and predicate -ita (be+ declarative sentence marker) as in (6) above.
The second way is to distinguish two possible meanings from the different syntactic frames and the nature of subject. The ambiguity arises between this is good and 'I like this' as presented in (4) above. 58 However, there is no meaning ambiguity in example (7), which is different from (4) in terms of number of arguments.

(7) na-nun i kes-un coh-ta.
I-TC this thing-TC good-DC
I like this thing.
Therefore the number of arguments gives a cue for the correct interpretation. However,
the meaning of coh- in all the sentences with two arguments is not necessarily
psychological attraction'. Note the contrast in the following examples:

(8) i-kes-un

mas-i coh-ta.

this-thing-NM taste-NM good-DC

This tastes good (this has a good taste, not I like this taste).

(9) na-nun i mas-i

coh-ta.

I-NM this taste-NM good-DC

I like this taste.

Therefore, first the number and more importantly, the nature of the first NP of a sentence can be a crucial clue for avoiding any ambiguity of meaning for double subject construction. Consider the two sentences (8) and (9) which have the same number of arguments but differ in the nature of their subjects: personal and non-personal. The
meaning of cohta in (8) cannot be interpreted as ' like' since the subject ' ikes' (this
thing) cannot possibly have a psychological attraction toward something. This seems to
support Chang's (1993) analysis: the different meanings of the word cohta (good) can
be understood by the fact that an evaluative meaning can be expressed in sentences with

57 Mostly -kes is used, but whatever is modified it can be explicitly repeated (e.g. cohun salamigood
person when GOOD is used predicatively in a sentence like this person is good) 58 The prototypical situation where (4) can be interpreted as 'I like this' is when someone wants to
express what he/she likes to choose among a few different things.

113

Chapter 3
one NP, whereas a psychological feeling can be expressed in sentences with two NPs and, most importantly, when there are two NPs, NP 1 must be a psychological substantive, although the quality of NP2 may give a circumstantial suggestion (e.g. One would not interpret na-nun him-i coh-ta (lit. My strength is good, means I am strong) as 'I like the strength')
Therefore, although I suggested that the subject and object should not be overtly used in the Korean-based metalanguage since otherwise pragmatic awkwardness may interfere with the reader's comprehension, with the word cohta, the subject must not be omitted since it may cause meaning ambiguity affecting the number of arguments.
The last clue for the correct interpretation, which is additional and indexical, seems to be in the extra information given by contrast, as shown in the following examples:

(10) i kes-i

coh-ko , i kes-i

nappu-ta.

this thing-NM good-and this thing-NM bad-DC

This is good, and this is bad.

(11) i kes-i

coh-ko, i kes-i silh-ta.

this thing-NM good- and this thing-NM dislike-DC

I like this and I disiike this.

The meaning of sentences (10) and (11) becomes clear through contrast, since the word nappu- (bad) means negative evaluation, whereas the other word silh- (dislike) expresses a 'negative psychological feeling'.
Having clarified ways of distinguishing the two different meanings of the predicative use of coh-, one more point deserves comment in relation to the natural use of this word. In NSM, the primes .GOOD and BAD are commonly used to express the moral judgement of a group of people or community. The goodness and badness in a person or their deeds are highly valued and play an important role in Korean society in maintaining morals and traditions. People and things are divided into these two categories on the basis of community rules. Traditionally, doing 'good things' (i.e. helping others, or sacrificing oneself for other people) is encouraged and emphasised. There is another word for this in Korean chakha- (being good). This word is used to refer to either the act of charity or the altruistic personality. The word chakha- is translated as 'be nice; be good; virtuous; kind' .59 The two different sentences below, however, are translated by the same sentence in English.

59 In Dong-A's Prime Korean-English Dictionary (1996)

114

Chapter 3
(12) i salamtul-un chakha-n salamtul-ita.
this people-TC good- PM people-be-DC
These people are good.
(13) i salamtul-un coh-un salamtul-i-ta.
this people-TC good- PM people-be-DC
These people are good (people).
Even though they are interchangeable in many cases, as in (12) and (13), the range of
use of the two words, coh-un and chakhan- are not the same. The word chakha seem, however, to have more semantic components than the prime coh-!GOOD when it is used
in such context as (12).
X-nun chakha-ta.
X does many good things for other people people think: it is very good

The word coh-/GOOD can be used to refer to a positive moral or ethical judgment
on people in NSM explications and chakha- is the pragmatically preferred word for this usage in Korean. The 'good Samaritan', for example, is translated as 'chakhan Samaria salam' in the Korean Bible. The word chakha- contains the meaning doing something
good for other people. This word is not used when one does good things for oneself, for instance, eating health food, following a personal schedule, or concentrating on work. This word is also very widely used by parents or teachers in guiding and encouraging
small children in everyday life. The syntactic features of chakha- are the same as coh(good) and nappu- (bad). The opposite concept to chakha- is also nappu-/BAD. Thus contrasts such as 'chakhan salamtul (good people)' and 'nappun salamtul (bad people)' are more commonly found than that of cohun salamtul (good people).

It is claimed that the primes GOOD!coh- and BAD/nappu- can combine with SOMEONE,

The combination of cohun and nappun with ma! (word) (good word and bad word) is

The semantic relationship between GOOD and 'well' is explored in (8) and (9).
The Korean counterpaii of 'well' in a context like You did this thing well is cal-. The word cal- can only be used adverbially while coh- can be inflected either as an adjective, an adverb, or an attributive by taking different suffixes. The two words cohand cal- are used as follows:

(8) ne-nun i-il-ul cal hay-ss-ta.
You-TC this-thing well do-PST-DC
You did this thing well.
(9) ne-nun mwenka coh-un il-ul hay-ss-ta.
You-TC good-PM good-PM thing-AC do-PST-DC
You did something good.
The two Korean sentences do not have the same meaning. The former can be said to evaluate 'the good quality of someone's deed' in the context of, for instance, cooking, making a cubby house, or writing a book. Whereas sentence (9) is used to express that someone did 'an action of good will', for instance, fundraising for people_jn need or taking care of the elderly. This does not necessarily include the positive quality of the action. This is because one can do 'good things' (actions of good will) not in a
considerate manner. The two words coh- and cal can be used in the same sentence in
order to express 'one needs to do good things well', as shown in (10).

(10) coh-un il-ul ha-! ttay-nun cal hay-ya ha-n-ta.
good-PM thing-AC do-PRO when-TC well do-should do-IN-DC
When one does a good thing, one has to do it well.
The meaning of (10) in Korean implies that one can do 'good things' either well or not. The typical context in which sentence (10) can be said is when one does an act of charity for someone and offends that person's feeling, perhaps by an improper manner.
However, when coh- is used adverbially, it is semantically very close to cal- as
seen in the context like (11).

117

Chapter 3

(11 )a ku-kun ku-il-ul

cal kkuthnay-ko·siph-ess-ta.

3sg-TC that-thing-AC well finish-want-PST-DC

He wanted to finish that thing well.

(11 )b ku-kun ku-il-ul

coh-key kkuthnay-ko·siph-ess-ta.

3sg-TC that-thing-AC well-ADV finish-want-PST-DC

He wanted to finish that thing well.

Both sentences can be used, for example, when one is dealing with a business contract or an agreement on working hours with one's employees. Even though they can be said in the same situation in order to express the speaker's desire to complete the task 'well', there appears to be a subtle difference in meaning. If the speaker chooses to use (1 l)b instead of (11 )a, it implies that s/he wants to complete the task without having any conflict with anyone, while (11 )a is more likely to express that s/he wants to deal with
the task efficiently for a good result. The meaning of coh-key (good-adverbialiser) appears to involve personal relationships while the word cal (well) does not have this
connotation. Sentence (11 )b would not be used when one prepares an exam or a school
project, while (11 )a can be used in the same situation. Thus the adverbial use of coh/GOOD can be substituted for in place of cal- (well), but the substitution can sometimes
bring a meaning difference.
The word cal- can also refer to frequency as in a context like (12).

(12) ku-nun ku-kos-ey

cal ka-n-ta.

3sg-TC that-place-LOC well go-IN-DC

He often goes to that place.

There are instances in which the meaning of cal- is ambiguous between 'frequency' and
'ability to do something well', as seen in (13). One can interpret sentence (13) as 'he lies well' implying that the person has an ability of convincing others by telling lies, and that 'he lies frequently'. The intonation or context can be a clue to distinguish the two meanings in natural discourse.

(13) ku-nun kecismal-ul

He lies frequently. Or He lies well.

well do-IN-DC

Nonetheless the word cal- (well) can modify other predicates, such as sayngkakha-

The meaning of the combination of cal- and sayngkakha- in (14) is 'considered' (think about something for a long time) rather than 'intelligence', while that in (15) is ambiguous. Sentence (15) can be used either to express the abiljty of thinking efficiently or thoughtfully.
The combination of cal- (well) and al-!K.NOW indicates familiarity, as shown in (16).

(16) na-nun i-salam-ul cal a-n-ta.
I-TC this person-AC well know-IN-DC
I know this person well.
The meaning of sentence (17) can be ambiguous as it can mean 'living righteously or morally' and 'living wealthily'. The cue for the correct interpretation is the difference of prosodic characteristics or/and context. The orthographic difference can be another cue for the written text: cal santa (~ {}tj-, meaning 'living righteously') versus calsanta (~{}tj-, meaning 'living wealthily'). In former case, there is space between the two words, cal (well) and santa (live), while in the latter there is not, which suggests that the two morphemes are treated as one word (Park, 1996).

(17) ku-nun cal sa-n-ta.
3sg-TC well live-IN-DC
He lives well.
The adverbial use of coh-!GOOD cannot be substituted for in place of cal- in contexts like (12) to (17). A close examination is required to determine the exact meaning difference between the two words, cal- (well) and the adverbial use of coh-!GOOD, depending on context.

3.5.3 Summary
As a result of testing, the evaluative primes, GOOD and BAD are found to have equivalent concepts in Korean. The two exponents coh- and nappu- are proposed as the
119

Chapter 3
Korean counterparts, respectively. Their meanings in canonical sentences appear to express the equivalents of the proposed English exponents. Their combinatorial characteristics are also found to be along similar lines to the proposed universal grammar. The only concern arises when coh-!GOOD is used predicatively with a psychological substantive in a Korean sentence since ambiguity of meaning between 'psychological attraction towards someone and something' and 'positive evaluation' may result. This is due to the polysemous quality of the word coh- in Korean. It is not about the impossibility of using coh-!GOOD predicatively but about avoiding meaning ambiguity when the Korean exponent coh-!GOOD is used predicatively with personal substantives. However, when it is used predicatively, there are two cues that can distinguish the primitive meaning from the other: (i) the number of arguments and (ii) the nature of the first subject when there are double subjects in a sentence. In order to avoid any possible ambiguity, the simple and best solution appears to be to use coh/GOOD attributively in combination with a following modified noun.
3.6 Descriptors : BIG, SMALL
3.6.1 Lexicon
The concepts of BIG and SMALL are expected to be easily identified cross-linguistically (Wierzbicka, 1996a: 54). The proposed primitive meanings can be grasped by the following canonical sentences:
I saw a big/small tree. I saw a big/small dog (bird, etc.).
The Korean exponents for these primes are khu- and cak-, respectively. These words belong to the adjective class similar to the counterparts of the evaluators. The proposed Korean exponents khu-/BIG and cak-/SMALL can take different suffixes according to their syntactic roles in sentences. Wierzbicka (1996a: 54) points out that BIG and SMALL can be considered as "relative" terms, although they are used with or without relatum. This is valid in Korean as will be shown in examples (1) or (2) in which the Korean exponents khu-lBIG and cak-/SMALL are used either attributively or predicatively, respectively.
120

Chapter 3

(1) cip an-ey

mwuesinka khu-/cak-(u)n kes-i iss-ta.

house inside-LOC something big/small-PM thing-NM there is-DC

There is something big inside the house.

(2) i salam-un khu-/cak-ta.
this person-TC big/small-DC
This person is big.

As in English the word khu- (big) is unmarked in Korean.

(3) ku namwu-ka elmana khu-ni?

that tree-NM how

big-INT

How big is that tree?

It is proposed that the primes BIG and SMALL can be combined with SOMETIDNG, SOMEONE, PEOPLE, KIND, PART, and PLACE. All combinations are tested and found to be consistent with the proposed universal grammar as follows:

(1) i-kes-un khu-/cak-(u)n salam~eykey coh-un kes-i-ta.

(3) na-nun khu-/cak-(u)n kos-eyse sal-ko·siph-ta.

(4) na-nun khu-/cak-(u)n kes-ul kac-ko·siph-ta.

The Korean counterparts are not used in combination with the temporal concept
ttay/TIME, as this is incomprehensible.

*khun ttay (big time) *cakun ttay (small time)
It is claimed that both BIG and SMALL can be combined with the intensifier VERY. This is valid in Korean as seen below:
121

Chapter 3

It is commonly reported that BIG and SMALL are used in an abstract sense. Goddard calls for cross-linguistic investigation (2002b: 136) on whether this abstract use of the word BIG and SMALL is universal. The Korean counterparts, khu- and cakare used in this sense commonly and naturally in everyday contexts, as can be seen in examples (6) and (7) below:

(6) mwues-inka khu-n il-i

like this small- PM event-about worry-not-INF-IMP

In both sentences, khu-/BIG and cak-/SMALL seem to mean the 'importance' of the matter, not physical size.

3.6.3 Summary
Two pdmes BIG and SMALL have been tested in both lexicalisation and syntax. As a result of testing against the canonical sentences, it has been found that these concepts are lexicalised in khu- and cak- respectively, and all the proposed combinations of these primes are consistent with the proposed universal grammar. Consequently, hypotheses in relation to these primes are fully supported including the metaphorical use of these primes, which is natural and idiomatic in Korean.

3.7 Mental predicates: THINK, KNOW, WANT, FEEL

3.7.1.2 a/-/KNOW
The prime KNOW is proposed as one of the mental predicates. The meaning of this concept can be identified by the canonical sentences as follows:
We know (that) this is bad.
I know (that) he went this way. I don't know where he is now.
This person knows something about me. I know (don't know) many things about him.
I know (don't know) that person. I know this person (place) well.
For the translational equivalent of KNOW, I propose al-62 as the Korean counterpart. This word can be used either to express 'know a fact' or 'know a person'. Unlike some languages, such as Italian or German, there is no lexical distinction between 'knowing how to' and 'knowing a person' in Korean. However, the syntactic construction for each case differs somewhat. In order to express the former the nominaliser -ci or -cwul precedes the exponent al-lKNOW introducing a subordinate clause, while 'a person' can be a direct complement taking the accusative particle -(l)ul.

I know how to cook.
