3.7.1.3 V+ ko·siph-, wenha-lWANT
The concept WANT is proposed as one of the universal semantic primes. The meaning of this concept can be identified by canonical sentences as follows :
I want something. I want (don't want) this. I want to go to the village (market/beach etc.). I want to be with my grandfather. I want (don't want) you to .do it. I want (don't want) this to happen.
For the conceptual equivalent for WANT, I propose two expressions: wenha- and -ko·siph-. These two expressions are. commonly used both in colloquial and in written contexts. The latter expression is bimorphemic consisting of -ko and -siph. According to Son (1995: 147) the meaning of -ko·siph- as 'hoping' can be expressed only when the
two morphemes are used together. Distribution of the two expressions 1s
complementary: wenha- is used as a main predicate, while -ko·siph- is used as an
auxiliary verb suffixed to a main verb stem. The usage of the two different expressions is exemplified as follows:

125

(12) mwues-ul wenha-ni?
what-AC want-INT
What do you want?

Chapter 3

(13) i kes-ul wenha-n-ta. ·
this thing-AC want-IN-DC
I want this.

(14)a na-nun ne-hako i il-ul ha-ko·siph-ta.
I-NM you-with this thing-AC do-want-DC
I want to do this with you.

(15)a na-nun i kes-ul po-ko·siph-ta.
I-TC this thing-AC see-want-DC
I want to see this.

One of the proposed exponents wenha- is used with nominal complements in (12) and (13), while -ko·siph- is used in combination with another predicate in (14)a and (l.S)a . When there is more than one predicate in a sentence -ko·siph- is favoured although wenha- can also be used in the same syntactic frame by taking the nominalised
second predicate as its complement. Among others WANT can be used in combination with such predicates as THINK, KNOW, SEE, HEAR, DO, MOVE, FEEL, and SAY (e.g.
want to think, want to know, want to see, etc.) in NSM explications. The combination of
WANT and other predicates can be expressed in two different ways in Korean due to the
fact that there are two exponents of the same concept of WANT: (i) verb stem+ ko·siph(ii) verb stem + nominaliser + optional accu'sative marker (l)ul + wenha-. The former is
exemplified in (14)a and (15)a and the latter is exemplified in (14)b and (15)b below:

(14)b na-nun ne-hako-kathi i il-ul ha-ki(-lul) wenha-n-ta.
I-NM you-with-together -this thing-AC do-NOM(-AC) want-IN-DC
I want to do this with you.
(15)b na-nun i kes-ul po-ki(-lul) wenha-n-ta.
I-TC this thing-AC see-NOM(-AC) want-IN-DC
I want to see this.
Examples (14)b and (15)b contain nominalised predicates (by adding the nominaliser
suffix -ki after the verb stem) that are complements of the predicate wenha- as shown
above. Compared to (14)b and (15)b, (14)a and (15)a are more widely used as natural expressions. The choice between the two proposed counterparts is determined by the syntactic structure of the sentence: when the concept WANT combines with another
predicate in a sentence, -ko·siph- is the preferred choice, while wenha- is used
otherwise, although this is not obligatory. The two exponents can be considered as
126

Chapter 3
combinatorial allolexes for there is no meaning difference between the two forms when they are used in the given canonical sentences.
One may argue that if -ko·siph- is an allolex of wenha-, then, -(u)myen·siph- also
has to be considered as an allolex of wenha-, since -ko·siph- and -(u)myen-siph- are
regarded as synonyms. For example, Sung (1987: 249) claimed that there is no meaning difference between the two expressions. According to him, the only difference between -ko·siph- and -(u)myen·siph-, is that -ko·siph- can be used only with action verbs and the tense has to be zero form, while -(u)myen·siph- has no restriction with the attached verb type and the tense marker has to be past or past perfect form. It is true that formal constructions of the sentences containing these morphemes are different, and their syntactic distribution is complementary: (i) -ko·siph has the syntactic structure of 'verb
stern+ ko·siph-' (ii} -(u)myen·siph- has 'verb stem + (a/e)-ss (past tense marker) +
(u)myen·siph-'. Examples (14)c and (15)c are presented in comparison to (14)a and (1S)a to show the difference in syntactic construction.

(14)c na-nun ne-wakathi i il-ul

hay-ss-umyen·siph-ta.

I-NM you-with this thing-AC do-PST-want-DC

I want to do this with you.

(1 S)c na-nun i . kes-ul po-ass-umyen·siph-ta.
I-TC this thing-AC see-want-DC
I want to see this

However, in order to examine whether the claim that the two expressions are the same in terms of meaning but differ only in their syntactic frames is valid, they have to be tested against more canonical sentences as follows:

(16)a hanunim-un motun salam-ul wihay

God-TC

all person-AC for

coh-un il-ul

ha-ko·siph·e·ha-si-n-ta.

good-PM work-AC do-want-SH-IN-DC

God wants to do good things for all people.

(16)b hanunim-un motun salam-ul wihay

God-TC

all person-AC for

cohun il-ul

ha-yss-umyen-siph ·e·ha-si-n-ta.

good work-AC do-PST-want-SH-IN-DC

God wants to do good things for all people.

127

Chapter 3
The meanings of the two sentences (16)a and (16)b do not seem to be the same. Example (16)b does not sound right because it seems to express a 'weaker desire' of God of wanting to do something good for people than (l 6)a. The examples below present a situation where a person who was diagnosed as having a terminal illness begs a doctor for his/her life.
(17)a na-nun kkok sal-ko·siph-e-yo.
I-TC really live-want-INF-POL
I really want to live.
(17)b ?na-nun kkok sal-ass-umyen·siph-e-yo.
I-TC really live-PST-want-INF-POL
I really want to live.
In a desperate situation where one cries out for his/her own life, one would not say (17)b. This suggests the existence of a difference in meaning between the two expressions.
Other·examples indicate a difference in meaning also:
(18)a na-nun ney-ka cikum i il-ul ha-ki-lul wenha-n-ta.
I-TC you-NM now this thing-AC do-NOM-AC want-IN-DC
I want you to do this now.
(18)b na-nun ney-ka cikum i il-ul hay-ss-umyen·siph-ta.
I-TC you-NM now this thing-AC do-PST-want-DC
I want you to do this now.
The expression -(u)myen·siph- is used when someone does not want to impose on someone else too much when expressing wanting this person to do something in a sentence like (18)b above. Therefore an expression such as (18)b is much more acceptable pragmatically than that of (18)a. Imposing on the interlocutor by expressing the speaker's desire strongly can be impolite and is culturally not favoured. Although (18)b is commonly accepted as a pragmatic expression for wanting someone to do something~ -(u)myen siph- should not be treated as an allolex of wenha- and -ko·siph-, due to the meaning difference.
One more point has to be accounted for in relation to the expression -ko·siph-. This morpheme can be used only for the first person. There is a suffix distinction between' I want' and 'someone else wants' in Korean, presumably because the 'desire' or 'wish' is personal and it is hard to know other people's 'wanting' without being told.
128

Chapter 3
In the case of having a second or third person as an agent of WANT, instead of -ko·siph-, ko·siph-e· ha-63, occurs. Compare the following:

(l 9)a na-nun i kes-ul
I-TC this thing-AC
I want to know this

al-ko·siph-ta.
know-want-DC

(19)b i salamtul-un i kes-ul al-ko·siph·e·ha-n-ta.
this people-TC this thing-AC know-want-IN-DC
These people want to know this.

-Although the two different suffixes are indexical and give extra information on the subject, there is no conceptual difference between the two forms in that they express the equivalent concept of WANT. This view is supported by example (20) in which the proposed exponent -ko·siph- is used with the second person pronoun.

(20) ne-nun mwe ha-ko·siph-ni?
You-NM what do-want-INT
What do you want to do?
Therefore the different forms should be considered as combinatorial allolexes, which occur depending on the subject due to the requirement of Korean grammar. The Korean exponents of WANT can be presented as follows:
WANT • wenha-lN or NP
-ko·siph-Nerb stem_ /1st person -ko·siph·e·haNerb stem_ /2nd and 3rd person

3.7.1.4 nukki-lFEEL
The prime FEEL plays an essential role in describing human emotions and there have been a number of cross-linguistic investigations related to the meaning of emotion words or expressions. .The meaning of this concept can be identified by the following canonical sentences:

When that happened (or: when I heard that), !felt something good/bad. I feel like this.
At that time (then), I didn't feel anything. I feel something (bad, strange, etc.) in this part ofmy body (chest, stomach, etc.)

63 Which has a verbal suffix -e-ha- (complementiser + do) attached to -ko·siph-.

129

Chapter 3
!feel good today I don't know why.
Identifying the counterpart of FEEL in Korean is not straightforward. Although at first glance the equivalent concept seems to be the verb nukki-, there are some canonical sentences where the use of this word sounds awkward. This will be dealt with in detail later in this section. Another point that has to be accounted for in relation to Korean emotion terms is that the Korean language has many emotion words in which the concept of 'feel' is already intrinsic. In other words, most expressions of emotion and sensation are lexicalised with a built-in concept of 'feeling'. Therefore, the word nukkiis not often used, presumably because it would be repeating the same concept. Examples below show that there are two ways of expressing ' feeling sad' in Korean: using a separate word for 'feel sad' (in the case of (21) sulph-) and using the word nukki- and a nominalised emotion word (in the case of (22), sulphum-sadness).

(2l)a na- nun sulphu-ta.
I-TC sad-DC
I am sad. (I feel sad)

(21)b na-nun sulphum-ul
I-TC sadness-AC
I feel sadness.

nukki-n-ta.
feel-IN-DC

(22)a na-nun pay-ka
I-TC stomach-NM
I am hungry.

kophu-ta.
hungry-DC

(22)b na-nun paykophum-ul nukki-n-ta.

I-TC hunger-AC

feel-IN-DC

I feel hungry.

Semantically, there seems to be no meaning difference between (a) and (b) in (21) and (22). This means that many words which carry either psychological or physical 'feeling' (that may be expressed with the prime FEEL in English sentences for instance) can be expressed without the corresponding word for 'feel' in Korean. These words can, of course, be decomposed into a few different concepts presumably including the semantic primitive FEEL (See Chapter 5 for emotion concepts that include this semantic element.).
Having pointed out the relatively limited range of use for the Korean exponent of FEEL in natural discourse, the proposed counterpart nukki- is tested as follows : (23) na-nun mwenka-lul nukki-n-ta.
I-TC something-AC feel-IN-DC
I feel something.

(24) na-nun ilehkey nukki-ess-ta.

I felt like this.

I-TC big-PM joy-AC

feel-PST-DC

I felt great joy.

(26) na-nun amwu-kes-to nukki-l·swu·eps-ta.
I-TC anything-too fee I-can-DC
I can't feel anything.

The word nukki- obligatorily takes complements as shown in example (23), expressing
emotion (e.g. sadness, anger, joy), physical stimulation (e.g. hunger, coldness), and unclear psychological events in the mind (e.g. feeling something). However, when the complement is something good/bad it is less straightforward. Note the contrast in the examples below:

(27)a *na-nun mwenka coh-um-ul

nukki-n-ta.

I feel good. (lit. I feel goodness.)

bad-PM

feeling-AC feel-IN-DC

The unacceptability of (27)a and (28)a seems to be the result of the verb nukki- not being able to take chum (goodness) or napped (badness), nominalised Korean
counterparts of the primes GOOD/BAD, as its complements. In order to express the
equivalent of feel something good/bad, nukki- has to take the combination of the attributive use of cohun/nappun (good/bad) and the nominal nukkim (feeling) as its
complement as exemplified in (27)b and (28)b above. From the Korean perspective the semantic structure of the English arrangement I feel good/bad or I feel something good/bad seems to be something like I feel a good/bad feeling or I feel some good/bad feeling.

131

Chapter 3
Apart from the expression (cohun/nappun) (good/bad) nukkimul nukki- (lit. feel (good/bad) feeling), there are a few ways of expressing one's feelings. One of the most common and natural fixed expressions for talking about feeling is nukkimi tunta and
nukkimul kacinta as presented in examples (29) and (30) below:

(29) coh-un nukkim-i tu-n-ta.
good-PM feeling-NM enter-IN-DC
A good feeling came into me.
(30) coh-un nukkim-ul kac-key-toy-n-ta.
good-PM feeling-AC have-ADV-become-IN-DC
I begin to have a good feeling.
These are more idiomatic and widely used expressions than the combination of the Korean exponent nukki- and its complement nukkim (feeling) in the same sentence. Although the latter arrangement is not ungrammatical, its use in natural conversation is very marginal and restricted. Therefore, although the expression cohun/nappun nukkimul .nukki (feel good/bad) is proposed for the purpose of the Korean-based NSM explication, the natural expressions presented in (29) and (30) are worth exploring in order to be used as pragmatically preferred alternatives. However, the substitution has to be based on the examination of whether there is a difference in meaning between them and the proposed expression.
By the same token, in order to express the equivalent of I feel good/bad naturally
in Korean there are more idiomatic and pragmatically preferred expressions than (27)b and (28)b, for example:

(31) na-nun kipwun-i coh-/nappu-ta.
I-TC feeling-NM good-DC
I feel good.
This expression is used especially in the context that one feels good in the morning without any reason for it. The word kipwun is a polysemous noun and may often be interpreted as 'feeling' or 'mood'. Examples below show how this word is used:

(32) onul way-n-ci molukey

kipwun-i coh-/nappu-ta.

today why-PM-NOM not know-ADV feeling-NM good/bad-DC

I feel good/bad today without any reason.

Example (32) reads idiomatically. Compare this with other examples presented below:

132

Chapter 3

(32)a ? onul way-n-ci

molu-key coh-un nukkim-ul nukki-n-ta.

today why-PM-NOM know-ADV good-PM feeling-AC feel-IN-DC

I feel good feeling today without any reason.

(32)b onul way-n-ci molu-key coh-un nukkim-i nukki-e-ci-n-ta.
today why-PM-NOM know-ADV good-PM feeling-NM feel-INF-CAU-IN-DC
I came to feel good feeling today without me knowing why.

(32)c onul way-n-ci molu-key

coh-un nukkim-i tu-n-ta.

today why-PM-NOM not know-ADV good-PM feeling-NM feel-IN-DC

A good feeling came into me today without me knowing why.

All the expressions are idiomatic except for (32)a which is slightly unnatural. The difference between this and other natural expressions seems to be the implication of the 'passive status' of the person who experiences the 'good feeling'. In (32)b and (32)c this notion is expressed by the causative suffix (-ci) and the predicate 'enter in'. The conventionalised way of expressing one's emotion via other means as shown above, seems to suggest Koreans may think that one is not in control of one's feelings and that an emotional state is a result of whatever is happening inside one. The notion of passivity seems to be reflected by the grammatical construction used in (32)b and (32)c. However, it is not clear where this is a part of the meaning or only an indexical meaning
due to the implicature of the form. Nonetheless, in order to express I feel good/bad in a Korean-based NSM, there
seem to be two possibilities: (i) use predicate nukki- (feel) with nukkim (feeling) modified by cohun/nappun (good/bad) as its complement, (ii) use kipwun (feeling) with
predicate use of coh/nappu (good/bad). One may argue about using the word nukkim (nominalised exponent nukki-lFEEL)
instead of the word kipwun. However, there seems to be a meaning difference between
them. The use of nukkim (feeling) in place of kipwun brings a different meaning from I
feel good/bad. Compare the two examples (32)d below and (32) above:

(32)d onul way-n-ci molu-key

nukkim-i coh-/nappu-ta.

today why-PM-NOM not know-ADV feeling-NM good/bad-DC

I somehow feel that something good/bad may happen today.

In an expression like (32)d, the speaker wants to express his/her anticipation of having a good happening or bad luck. Due to this implication in (32)d the nominalised form of the exponent nukki-lFEEL should not be used with the predicate use of evaluators in
Korean.

3.7.1.5 po-/SEE and tut-/HEAR

Chapter 3

The primes SEE and HEAR are proposed as universal human concepts. Their meanings can be identified by the following canonical sentences:

I saw something (a dog, bird, etc.) over there.
I hear something (over there in the bushes.
She was singing - I heard it.
When I heard those words, I felt something bad.

It is relatively straightforward to identify the equivalent concepts of these primes in Korean: I propose po- and tut- 64 as the Korean counterparts of SE~ and HEAR, respectively. However, the Korean exponents have a wider use than the English counterparts. The word po~ covers all kinds of visual perception (i.e. English 'see' and 'watch' etc) and tut- means all kinds of auditory perception (i.e. English word 'hear' and 'listen'). Also the Korean exponents po-lSEE and tut-/HEAR can take progressive aspect -ko·iss-. These are illustrated as follows:

(33) na-nun namwu-wi-ey iss-nun say-lul po-ass-ta.
I-TC tree-above-LOC exist-PM bird-AC see-PST-DC
I saw a bird on the tree.

(34) na-nun ku pitio-lul po-ass-ta.
I-TC that video-AC see-PST-DC
I watched that video.

(35) · pakk-uy soli-lul tul-ess-ta.
out-ATR sound-AC hear-PST-DC
I head the noise from outside.

(36) na-nun kanguy theiph-lul tut-ko·iss-ta.

I-TC lecture tape-AC

hear-PRG-DC

I am listening to the lecturer's tape

3.7.2 Syntax

3.7.2.1 sayngkakha-lTHINK

It is hypothesised that the prime THINK .takes personal substantives, such as I, YOU, SOMEONE, or PEOPLE, as 'subjects'. This claim is valid in Korean as shown in the following examples:

64 The stem tut- has an allomorphic variant tul- depending on the following phoneme.

134

Chapter 3

(1) na/ne/salamtul-un sayngkakha-l·swu·iss-ta.

I/you/people-TC

think-PRO-can-DC

I/You /People can think.

(2) nwukwunka ilehkey sayngkakha-l·swu·iss-ta:

someone

like this think-can-DC

Someone can think like this:

As in other natural languages it seems to be impossible for the predicate sayngkakhalTHINK to take the non-personal semantic agent mwues!SOMETHING in Korean.
It is hypothesised that THINK allows either a substantive topic or a clausal
complement (think something and think that) as well as an illocutionary topic (think about). This claim is tested and found to be supported as follows:

(3)a na-nun mwenka-lul sayngkakha-n-ta.
I-TC something-AC think-IN-DC
I think something.

.(3)b

na-nun mwusun sayngkak-ul ha-n-ta.

I-TC some

thought-AC do-IN-DC

I think something.

(4) na-nun 'i kes-i khu-ta '-ko sayngkakha-n-ta.
I-TC this thing-NM big-DC-Q think-IN-DC
I think that 'this is big'.

As mentioned previously there is no meaning difference between the two formulae65,
--
and accordingly the meanings of sentences (3)a and (3)b are identical. A clausal complement is marked by the postpositional quotative particle -(la)ko as in (4). The illocutionary topic is marked by the postposed particle -ey tayhay (about) or allomorphic
variant -ey tayhaye as in (5) and (6) below:

(5) na-nun ne-ey tayhay sayngkakha-n-ta.

I-TC you-about

think-IN-DC

I think about you.

However, when the exponent sayngkakha- takes a complement like 'something good/bad' or 'good/bad things' , it preferably takes an adverbial complement rather than the combination of attributive coh-!GOOD or nappu-lBAD and the substantive. Note the contrast between (6)a and (6)b where (6)b is more idiomatic than (6)a.

65 Although sayngkakha- is one concept meaning the equivalent of THINK, this word can be split into two parts sayngkak (thought) and ha- (do) linked by the accusative marker -ul as in (3)b.

Chapter 3

(6)a na-nun i salam- eytayhay coh-/nappu-(u)n sayngkak-ul ha-n-ta.

I-TC this person-about

good/bad-PM

thought-PL-AC do-IN-DC

(6)b na-nun i salam- ey tayhay coh-/nappu-key sayngkakha-n-ta.

I-TC this person-about

good/bad-ADV think-IN-DC

I think good things about this person.

It is not clear whether the two sentences above differ semantically. However, (6)a can be interpreted as saying that the thinker is thinking of some pleasant memories about the person bing thought about. While (6)b does not carry this kind of secopd meaning. Therefore, not only (6)b is idiomatic when one expresses one's attitude toward someone or something, but also it is semantically unambiguous in reflecting the proposed meaning of the canonical sentence in Korean. The preference for taking an adverbial complement is parallel to the predicate SAY.
It is proposed that THINK can be used to form a sentence structure of sometimes a person thinks like this: "_" or this person thought: "_". Korean has verb ending word order, accordingly these expressions are limited and found mostly in written forms. There is more than one way to express the quotational clause that parallels the case of the prime SAY as follows:

(7)a na-nun 'i kes-un nappu-ta' ko sayngkakha-n-ta.
I-TC this thing-TC bad-DC Q think-IN-DC
I think: 'This is bad'

(7)b na-nun sayngkakha-n-ta. 'i kes-un nappu-ta' lako

I-TC think-IN-DC

this thing-TC bad-DC Q

I think: 'This is bad'

It is unnatural to have a verb prior to an object (either a word or an entire clause) in Korean. Although it is not ungrammatical or unacceptable to have the equivalent of the English sentence as in (7)b, (7)a sounds more natural. In both cases the quotational particle -(la)ko has to occur. For the purpose of NSM explications, which are not colloquial but occur in written texts, the use of the colon can also be allowed as in (7)c below:

(7)c na-nun ilehkey sayngkakha-n-ta: ' i kes-un nappu-ta '

I-TC like this think-IN-DC

this thing-TC bad-DC

I think: 'This is bad'

136

Chapter 3
This expression is not, of course, naturally used in everyday situations. However, the meaning of this sentence seems to be along the same lines as the English canonical sentence. In natural discourse, sometimes people talk about what they are thinking
starting with "na-nun ilehkey sayngkakhay" (I think like this), especially when there are
lots of things they have to say before the verb that occurs at the end of the sentence (e.g. when giving advice or various suggestions). This structure can also be used when a speaker would like to draw his/her interlocutors' attention fully to what s/he is talking about. For the purpose of the Korean-based NSM, the same frame as (7)c can be used without raising a significant problem.

The prime WANT is claimed to take SOMETHING as complement (want something), and to be used in combination with DO (want to do something). This is test~d and found to be valid in Korean as follows:

It is claimed that WANT can be used in both 'equi' (want to do something {to someone/something}) and 'non-equi' (want someone to do something or want something to happen {to someone/ something}) contexts. This can be tested as follows:

The claimed constructions are made possible in Korean by employing the postposed

while both can be used in the equi context.

3.7.2.4 nukki-lFEEL
The prime nukki-lFEEL is claimed to take the preverbal nominal (SOMETHING) with a
preposed modifier (GOOD/BAD). This claim is tested and found to be valid in Korean as follows:

Chapter 3
(18) na-nun mwenka-lul nukki-n-ta.

I feel something.

It is claimed that FEEL can be combined with a pre-positioned adverbial adjunct like this, and is able to take a clausal complement (feel that). These claims are tested as follows, and found to be supported in Korean:

The clausal complement is marked by the quotative case marker -(la)ko as seen in (21)
above.

3.7.2.5 po-/SEE and tut-/HEAR
Both exponents are claimed to be able to take either nominal or clausal complements. In the case of SEE, both SOMETHING and SOMEONE can be complements, whi1e in the case of HEAR, only SOMETHING is proposed as a complement. These claims are tested and found to be supported in Korean as follows:

(22) na-nun mwenka-lul po-ass-ta.
I-TC something-AC see-PST-DC
I saw something.
(23) na-nun nwukwunka-lul po-ass-ta.
I-TC something-AC see-PST-DC
I saw someone.
(24)a na-nun mwenka-lul tul-ess-ta.
I-TC something-AC see-PST-DC
I heard something.
(24)b na-nun mwusun soli-lul tul-ess-ta.
I-TC something sound-AC see-PST-DC
I heard something.

139

Chapter 3
Although sentence (24) is acceptable, the more idiomatic way to express the same meaning is (24)b, which has a specified noun soli (sound) as the complement of the verb
tut-lHEAR.
Both primes are used only for the direct perception of speakers. It would be pragmatically awkward to have other psychological substantives, such as ne/YOU, i salamtul! THESE PEOPLE, and nwukwunka!SOMEONE in the place of nail in all the examples above unless they are used on the basis of pre-given information.
3.7.3 Summary
Prin:ies of the mental predicates, such as THINK, KNOW, WANT, FEEL, and experiential predicates SEE and HEAR are examined in this section in terms of lexicon and syntax. As a result of investigation the following was found: firstly, all primes are found to have equivalent concepts in Korean. The proposed Korean exponents are sayngkakhalTHINK, al-lKNOW, wenha-lWANT, nukki-/FEEL, po-lSEE, and tut-lHEAR. Allolexes are proposed after testing their meanings within the same canonical context: in the case of WANT, wenha- and -ko·siph-, are proposed. Among these Korean exponents, one of the counterparts of WANT, -ko·siph-, is found to be differentiated in terms of suffix, depending on the person. When the subject is non-first person, for instance, is third person, -e-ha- has to be suffixed. The semantic rational behind this constraint with nonfirst person subject can be pursued in further study. The predicate FEEL, however, shows a somewhat different pattern as it has variants. When the proposed exponent nukki- takes evaluators as its complement (I feel good/bad), the sentence structure seems to be that of a nominal kipwun (feeling or mood) and the predicate use of evaluations (i.e. kipwun-i coh-/nappu-ta).
Secondly, the proposed combinations and valency options of all the predicates are tested and found to be supported in Korean by employing different grammatical function words. It is observed that when the predicate THINK takes something good/bad as a complement in an illocutionary topic valency, it prefers to take an adverbial complement (coh-/nappu-key) in Korean.
Finally, it is found that although the proposed combinations are possible in Korean, there are more idiomatic and natural ways of expressing certain combinations of the Korean exponents, for instance, 'I feel good' can_be expressed more naturally by 'coh-un nukkim-i tu-n-ta' (good feeling is entering) rather than by 'coh-un nukkim-ul nukki-n-ta' (feel good feeling). The structure of the former is relatively passive in
140

Chapter 3
comparison to that of the latter. In the former sentence structure, the subject is not doing
anything active and yet the feeling is coming into the person (the elliptical subject is marked either by topic contrast or dative ·case, and nominative case is awkward), while in the latter the autonomy of the subject is expressed (marked by nominative case). For the purpose of a workable Korean-based NSM, the possibility of using more natural expressions can be considered on the basis of testing the difference in their meanings.

3.8 Speech: SAY, WORD, TRUE
3.8.1 Lexicon
3.8.1.1 ma//WORD
The meaning of WORD can be identified by the following canonical sentences:
Don't say that word! She said the same thing, but in other (i.e. different) words. In Language X, the wordfor 'good' is palya. These people's (e.g. another tribe's) wordfor Xis 'palya '. Ifyou say one more word, .. . It is goodfor people ifthey hear these words.
I propose ma! for the Korean exponent for WORD. The noun ma! is polysemous and it
means either word or language according to context. This is a purely Korean word and
1· is used widely both in spoken and written contexts. The two meanings of ma!, language and word, can be distinguished primarily by the context since when ma! means language
it is usually preceded by a country or province name or previously given information. The two different meanings are expressed as follows:

(1) i phyenci-uy etten mal-i

ceyil coh-ni?

this letter-GEN which word-NM the most like-INT

Which word of this letter do you like most?

(2) i nala mal-un paywu-ki elyep-ta.
this country word-TC leam-N0M hard-DC
It is hard to learn the language of this country.

In sentence (1 ), ma! means word, while in (2) it means language, and there 1s no
problem of isolating the primitive meaning of this word in Korean.

141

Chapter 3
As for many other words in Korean, ma! (word) has its corresponding honorific word malssum, which is used for pragmatic reasons. The issue of considering a pragmatically preferred form in NSM text is mentioned in 1.5 .2 where I suggested considering the substitution ?f a pragmatically unmarked form for the semantically unmarked one when its use sounds awkward. Choosing a pragmatically preferred form from different possibilities is so important that if this is not done it implies the speaker's negative attitude toward the addressee or reference (pragmatically). The choice between them often seems to depend on the speaker's judgment of the context and the relationship with the addressee or reference. Consider the examples below:

(3)a i chayk-ey-nun hanunim-uy malssum-i

this book-at-TC God-GEN

word(H)-NM

In this book, the words of God are written.

ssu-i-e· iss-ta.
write-CAU-be-DC

(3)b i chayk-ey-nun hanunim-uy mal-i

this book-at-T

God-GEN

word-NM

In this book, the words of God are written.

ssu-i-e· iss-ta.
write-CAU-be-DC

(4) apenim, kulehkey coh-ci·anh-un malssum-un ha-ci·ma-sey-yo.
father, like that good-not-PM word(H)-TC do-not-SH-POL
Father, please do not say such words, which are not good.

The meanings o_f the two sentences (3)a and (3)b are the same except that the use of the honorific form carries extra pragmatic information on the speaker's attitude towards the addressee or referent. The pragmatically preferred choice for 'word' in a context like (3) is malssum, and not ma!. It is quite possible to hear the sentence (3)b from someone whose religion is not Christian and who wants to refer to the 'words of God' without necessarily paying any respect. Especially, in a sentence like "Although Christians claim that the words of God are written in this book, I don't believe any of these words", the plain form ma! is expected to be found. If, however, the speaker wants to avoid any negative reaction from Christians s/he would use the honorific form regardless of his/her real belief. Therefore, the choice between them is determined by the speaker's judgement on whether s/he needs to pay respect or not, or whether s/he wants to use a pragmatically unmarked expression in order to avoid any negative social feedback. Therefore, having proposed ma! for the Korean exponent of WORD, I also propose to consider the use of malssum, the pragmatically preferred pair of ma! (perhaps as a "pragmatic allolex", only if it can be so called) as an alternative in contexts where the replacement can reduce unwanted pragmatic interference. This kind of alternative can be paraphrased for it presumably has extra semantic components apart from the
142

Chapter 3
primitive meaning of mal/WOiill. The same issue has been raised by Enfied (2002) on Lao and Goddard (2002b) on Malay. This aspect of Korean deserves further research.
The pragmatic reason for using the honorific form in Korean culture can be related to various factors. Yun (1993) argues that the politeness rules do not exist as a separate set of rules in pragmatics. Various means of honorific speech are used for differentiating formality, respect, and intimacy. He proposes a new submaxim 'respect others by modesty' accounting for the cultural specific value of 'self-humbling' in the Korean politeness system. According to this analysis, two essential concerns of Korean honorification seem to be paraphrased along similar lines to I think good things about you and I am not someone like you. The first message is to avoid wrong messages (i.e. I think bad things about you) and the second message is to acknowledge the lower status of the speaker. The full meaning analysis of honorific forms or lexical items is not pursued here, leaving open the possibility of using these (malssum instead of ma! depending on context) on the basis of paraphrase, for the Korean-based NSM.
3.8.1.2 ma/ha-/SAY
The meaning of SAY can be identified by the following canonical sentences:
This is good', I said. 'Ouch/damn', he/she said. He said the same. He said something to me, but I didn't hear it. He said it to Mary, not to me, but I know that he wanted me to hear it. Did he say anything about these people? Don't say that word! She said the same thing, but in other (i.e. different) words. In Language X, the wordfor 'good' is palya. These people's (e.g. another tribe's) wordfor Xis 'palya '. Ifyou say one more word, ....
I propose malha- as the Korean counterpart, which is a bimorphemic verb consisting of the noun ma! (word) and ha- (do-verbaliser). This word, like all other compounding verbs verbalised by ha-, can be split into two parts having the accusative marker -ul in between. Compare the two cases exemplified below:
(5)a na-nun ne-eykey i kes-ul malha-n-ta.
I-TC you-DAT this thing-AC say-IN-DC
I say this to you.

(6)b i motun mal-ul ha-ko ku-nun ka-ss-ta.

The examples above show that the word malha- can be decomposed into two other primitives ma! and ha- 66 , although the meaning does not differ from the_~ proposed primitive SAY. The two possibilities for expressing the concept SAY are: (i) use the verb malha-!SAY, (ii) use jointly the noun mal/WORJJ and the postposed accusative marker -ul combined with the verb ha-67IDO. Although there is no meaning difference between the two different expressions when they are used in canonical sentences, they are different in syntactic association with other words (e.g. mal-ul ha- is the only possible option between the two when negating this concept).

3.8.1.3 sasi//TRUE
The prime TRUE has been added to the NSM inventory recently. Its meaning can be identified by the following canonical sentences:

That (i.e. something someone said) is not true. Mary said such-and-such (e.g. that Max did it), but John knew that this was not

It can be noted that the proposed primitive meaning of TRUE is not the same as the philosophical absolute concept of 'truth', nor the related formal semantic concept of truth conditions. The proposed concept seems to be closely related to 'words' or 'what someone said', and perhaps also to what someone thinks (Wierzbicka, 2002a: 102;
Hahn 2002). There may be a few candidates for the Korean counterpart of TRUE: cham, cinsil, and sasil. The words cham (PK} and cinsil (SK) are polysemous, meaning either 'true' or 'genuine', while sasil (SK) means either 'true' or 'what happened'.
The words cham and cinsil can express the proposed primitive meaning in a
sentence like what he said is true. They are also widely used to express 'genuineness' or
'sincerity' in such expressions as 'cham salang' (true love, real love), 'cham salam' (genuine man), 'kutay salang cinsil ilamyen' (If your love is true or sincere), 'cinsilo tongcenghata'(feel real or sincere sympathy). Note the following examples:

He worked sincerely at his work place.

(12) cham-toy-key

sal-ko·siph-ta.

sincere-become-ADV live-want-DC

I want to live sincerely.

In these sentences, where cham and cinsil are interpreted as ' sincerely' or ' genuine',

meaning of TRUE. However, when cham and cinsil are used in relation to 'word' , it

Despite the versatility of these words, they are highly colloquial. Therefore, in

whether the words cincca and cengmal can be regarded as allolexes of sasil in limited
contexts. The prime TRUE is relatively new and requires more exploration in terms of its virtual value within the metalanguage, so that the boundary of the primitive meaning, which is different from that of the natural English meaning, can be clarified.

3.8.2 Syntax

The syntactic characteristics of the three primes described above are examined together in this section.
It is claimed that the prime SAY opens the illocutionary topic slot (saying about something), and this claim is tested and found to be supported in Korean as follows:

(l)a nwuka mwusun mal-ul hay-ss-ta.
someone some word-AC do-PST-DC
Someone said something.
(1 )b nwuka muenka-lul malhay-ss-ta.
Someone something-AC say-PST-DC
Someone said something.
(2) na-nun i kes-eytayhay (mwenka-lul) malha-ko·siph-ta.
I-TC this thing-about (something-AC) say-want-DC
I want to say (something) about this.

Both (1 )a and (1 )b are used to express the same meaning. The illocutionary topic is
marked by the postposed particle -ey tayhay (about) as shown in (2). The complement
of SAY in (2) is optional. It is proposed that universally the prime SAY allows the addressee slot (say
something to someone), and this is tested and found to be supported in Korean as follows:

(3) nwukwu-eykey i mal-ul hay-ss-ni?

who-DAT

this word-AC do-PST-INT

To whom did you say this?

Chapter 3

(4) na-nun i kes-ul ne/nwukwunka-eykey malha-ko·siph-ta.

I-TC this thing-AC you/someone-DAT

say-want-DC

I want to say this to you/someone.

The addressee slot in Korean is marked by the postposed dative case marker -eykey (to)
as shown (3) and (4) above.
It is claimed that the prime SAY is able to take a combined complement, such as something good/bad. This is tested as follows:

(S)a na-nun ne-ey tayhay (mwenka) coh-/nappu-(u)n mal-ul hay-ss-ta.

possibilities are due to the two different Korean counterparts of SAY proposed in 3.8.1.2. There is no conceptual difference, except that (5)a sounds more idiomatic than (S)b. Sentence (5)c has a somewhat different syntactic construction from (5)a and (S)b
in that coh- and nappu- are used adverbially as the complement of malha-lSAY. This
expression is the most idiomatic among the three that express the equivalent of say something good/bad about, and this expression is used most frequently. 68 Note the following example which is idiomatic and widely ~sed for someone's positive reputation is critical in Korean culture:

3.8.3 Summary
The proposed primes of speech: WORD, SAY and TRUE are tested in this section. As a
result of testing, they are found to have equivalent concepts in Korean: mal/WORD, malha (or malul ha)ISAY, and sasil (cengmal and cincca)ITRUE are proposed as the
Korean exponents. All the proposed combinatorics in relation to the three exponents including the valency options, are supported in Korean. A need for including alternative pragmatically preferred forms is observed in order to reduce pragmatic interference occurring in some contexts. A further exploration in relation to the prime TRUE seems to be required.

150

Chapter 3
3.9 Action, Events, and Movement: DO, HAPPEN, MOVE
3.9.1 Lexicon
3.9.1.1 ha-1D0
It is relatively straightforward to identify the equivalent concept of DO in Korean. The primitive meaning of DO can be identified by the following canonical sentences:
What did you do? I want to do the same. You did something good/bad. I don't know what he did (to you). She did something bad to me/you. Those people did something goodfor me. What do people do with things like this (spoon, needle, etc.)? He did this with a knife (axe, stick, etc.).
I did it in the same place. I did it with my brother.
The Korean counterpart, ha-71 can be proposed, although this word has a wider range of use than the proposed prime DO in Korean natural discourse. The meanings and functions of ha- are tested as follows:

(1) na-nun i-kes-ul

hay-ss-ta.

I-TC this-thing-AC do-PST-DC

I did this.

(2) na-nun i-kes-ul

ha-ko·siph-ta.

I -TC this-thing-AC do-want-DC

I want to do this.

In the above context -ha expresses the equivalent of DO. This word can also be used as a verbaliser attached to nouns or adjectives as seen
in (3):

(3) na-nun kongpwuha-ko· iss-ta.
I-TC study-PRG-DC
I am studying.

(4) na-nun i-kes-ul

coh-a-ha-n-ta.

I-TC this-thing-AC good-INF-do-IN-DC

I like this.

71 With its allomorphic variant -hay- when 'ha' is followed by such phonemes as 'ss', 'e', 'yo' .

151

Chapter 3
The word kongpwu (study) in (3) is a noun and -ha- functions as a verbaliser transforming the noun to a verb. Many verbs of this kind are found in Korean, in fact most Sino-Korean words are verbalised in this way. The adjective coh- (good) in (4) is changed into a verb when -a-ha (complementiser +do) is attached. Nonetheless, of its two main roles only the main verb ha- is proposed as the Korean counterpart of the prime DO. There are several Korean exponents that contain the verbaliser -ha (e.g. sayngkakha-lTHINK, malha-lSAY, and wenha-lWANT) 72 , which share the same syntactic characteristics in common with the exponent ha!DO.73

3.9.1.2 ilena-, sayngki-/HAPPEN
The proposed primitive meaning of HAPPEN can be grasped by the following canonical sentences:
What happened? Something good/bad happened to this person (to me). What happened to this (thing)? Something bad happened in that place. If this happens, I don't know what I will do. What will happen to you ifyou have no food? The same thing happened today.
There seem to be two candidates for Korean counterparts of HAPPEN: ilena- and sayngki-. Two words are equally widely used to express the concept of HAPPEN in natural discourse. Both words are polysemous: ilena- also means 'stand up' or--'get up', and sayngki- also means 'come to have' . However, there is no problem in isolating the primitive meaning from their polysemous meanings. When the word ilena- is used with inanimate subjects, the only possible interpretation is 'happen' and not 'stand up'. For in NSM texts, the prime HAPPEN is not used with psychological substantives, so there is no concern of causing ambiguity of meaning when ilena- is used as the Korean counterpart of HAPPEN. The meaning of ilena- cannot be interpreted as HAPPEN in the context of (5), while it can only be interpreted as HAPPEN in the context of (6).

(5) na-nun ·6si-ey

ilena-ss-ta.

I-TC 6 o'clock-at happen-PST-DC

I got up at six o'clock.

The case of sayngki- is somewhat different. Consider the example below in which meaning ambiguity arises:

(7) i salam-eykey mwues-i

sayngki-ess-ta.

this person-DAT something-NM happen-PST-DC

Something happened to this person. Or

This person came to have something.

The two possible readings of (7) may be caused by the polysemy of the word sayngki-. This word can either mean 'happen' or 'come to have' according to the quality of the subject. Only when the subject is 'an event' does it mean 'happen'. It means 'come to have (happen to be to)' when the subject is a tangible object. Since the non-specific exponent mwues/SOMETHING used in (7) can mean both a tangible thing or an event in Korean (cf. see the 3.1.1 for the detail), the predicate sayngki- can cause ambiguity of meaning. This can be avoided by specifying the subject. This means that when mwusun ii (some event/4 is used instead of mwues (something or some event), the meaning of sayngki- can only be HAPPEN. Thus the use of two variants, mwues and mwusun ii, has to be differentiated in relation to the use of sayngki-lHAPPEN. The two words are tested as follows:

(8) nay-key mwusun coh-un il-i

ilena-ss-ta.

I-DAT some good-PM event-NM happen-PST-DC

Something good happened to me.

(9) nay-key mwusun coh-un il-i

sayngki-ess-ta.

I-DAT something good-PM event-NM happen-PST-DC

Something good happened to me.

On the basis of testing the meanings of (8) and (9), I propose both ilena- and sayngki- as Korean exponents for HAPPEN, for their meanings in all canonical sentences are the same. They have to be considered as allolexes although there is no morphosyntactic relation between them as is in most cases of allolexes.

74 The word mwusun-il is proposed as a combinatorial allolex of the Korean counterpart of the prime SOMETHING. This is because when the predicate is HAPPEN, instead of mwues, mwusun il sounds more
natural. See 3.2.1.3 for detail.

It is relatively straightforward to identify the Korean exponent of MOVE. The meaning of this concept can be identified by the following canonical sentences:

(Look!) Something is moving over there. Don't move! (e.g. while putting on a bandaid or removing head/ice) I was very scared. I couldn 't move.

I propose wumciki- as the Korean exponent, and this is tested as follows:

(10) cikwu-nun wumciki-n-ta.
planet-NM move-IN-DC
This planet is moving. ·

(11) nwuka chayksang-ul wumciki-ess-ta.

someone desk-AC

move-PST-DC

Someone moved the desk.

Between the two meanings of MOVE exemplified above, only the meaning of (10) is proposed as a universal. The Korean counterpart wumciki- is neutral like the English word MOVE in that it can mean both internal motion and translational motion. The examples (10) and (11) also show that wumciki-lMOVE can be used either as intransitive or transitive verbs in Korean.

3.9.1.4 tah-/TOUCH {Hypothetical prime)
There are a few putative primes that do not have their status proved cross-linguistically. One of them is TOUCH, which is _useful for the explication of physical sensitivity (feeling with touching). The intended sense of 'touch' is physical contact between one thing and another. The meaning of TOUCH can be identified by the following canonical sentences:

This thing (e.g. cup) is touching this other thing (e.g. vase). Something was touching my hand. (I couldfeel it.)

The best Korean candidate for this concept is tah-. There are examples as follows:

(12) i-kes-i

ce- kes-kwaley tah-a·iss-ta.

this-thing-NM that-thing-with/at touch-STA-DC

This thing is touching this other thing.

154

Chapter 3
There are other words kentuli- and manic-, which can be used with animate semantic
agents. These are shown in (13) and (14):
(13) i-salam-i i kes-ul kentuli-n-ta.
this thing- NM this thing-AC touch-IN-DC
This person is touching this other thing.
(14) na-nun i-kes-ul manci-n-ta.
I-TC this thing-AC touch-IN-DC
I am touching this thing.
In order to refer to a person or animal doing something for physical contact, the other
words, kentuli- or manci-, are readily used to describe the ' action of touching'.
However, this is not the proposed semantic primitive meaning, since it has been noted that some languages draw a lexical distinction between physical contact and someone's
action of touching. As seen in (1 ), the word tah- has to be expressed in combination
with the static mood verbal suffix -a·iss- to describe the status of being touched. The literal interpretation will be this thing is in contact with this other thing. This syntactic construction seems to reflect the passivity of something that is being touched with something else in Korean.

3.9.3 Summary
In this section three primes, DO, HAPPEN, and MOVE are attested in terms of lexicon and syntax. It has been found that there are equivalent concepts of these three primes in Korean. The proposed Korean exponents are ha-1DO, ilena-(sayngki)IHAPPEN, and wumciki-lMOVE. In the case of the prime HAPPEN, two different Korean exponents are proposed since no meaning difference is found between them in the context of canonical sentences. It is noted that the subject of the Korean exponents ilena- and sayngkilHAPPEN has to be specified by using the word mwusun il (some event) in order to avoid ambiguity of meaning.
In terms of syntactic characteristics, all the proposed combinations and valency options related to these primes are fully supported. It is noted that the three primes: DO, HAPPEN, and MOVE seem to be cohere to form a sub-class that can be labelled 'events'.
159

Chapter 3
The fact that only ha/DO and ilena-lHAPPEN among all the Korean predicates take
mwusun ii in preference to mwues as subject seems to be supportive of this view.

3.10 Existence and Possession: THERE IS/ARE, HAVE
3.10.1 Lexicon
3.10.1.1 iss-/THERE IS/ARE
The proposed primitive meanings of THERE IS/ARE can be identified by the following canonical sentences:
(Look!) There are baby birds in that nest. (Look!) There is someone in the garden. There is/are no [e.g. water/spiders] here. There are no ghosts (unicorns, etc.). There are many kinds ofnuts (yams, bats. etc.). There are no baby birds in this nest, but there are some in that other nest.

At first glance, two candidates are noted as the Korean exponents of this prime:
iss- and concayha-. The two words can be tested against canonical sentences as follows:

(1 )a kwisin-un iss-ta.
ghost-TC there is-DC
There are ghosts.

(1 )b kwisin-un concayha-n-ta.
ghost-TC exist-IN-DC
Ghosts exist.

(2)a nay cwumeni-an-ey mwe-ka

iss-ta.

my pocket-inside-Loe something-NM there is-DC

There is something in my pocket.

(2)b ??nay cwumeni-an-ey mwe-ka
my pocket-inside-Loe something-NM
There is something in my pocket.

concayha-n-ta.
exist-IN-DC

_Both words can be used in (1) expressing more or less the same meaning while
concayha- sounds awkward in a context like (2). The word iss- is familiar even to young children and is used naturally in all canonical sentences, while concayha- is acquired at
160

Chapter 3
a later stage of language development and brings awkwardness in some contexts presumably due to a difference in meaning from iss-. Therefore, I propose iss- as the Korean counterpart of THERE IS while pointing out that number distinction is not marked in Korean.
The verb iss-/THERE IS is used either as a main verb or as an auxiliary verb in Korean sentences. Among the different views on the function and meaning of the word iss-, Kim C (1982) claims that the meaning of the word iss- in different sentences, either as a main verb or an auxiliary verb, is in fact 'existence'. Lee H's (1997) analysis is consistent with this view. For the purpose of a Korean-based NSM, however, the main verb iss- is enough to capture all usages of the existential prime THERE IS. The function .of iss- as an auxiliary verb is related mainly to the tense or aspect of the main verb. It is easily distinguished whether iss- is used as a main verb or an auxiliary verb in a sentence since when it is used as an auxiliary verb it is preceded by the complementisera or -ko as shown in example (3) below and (4) in the next section:

(3) i-il-un

ku salam-i sal-a·iss-ul-ttay ilena-ss-ta.

this-thing-TC 3SG person-NM live-STA-PRO-when happen-PST-DC

This thing happened when he was alive.

3.10.1.2 kac-/HAVE
Although the English word have is polysemous and has various grammatical functions, only the meaning of 'possession' is proposed as the semantic prime in the NSM theory. The meaning can be identified from the canonical sentences presented below:
John has a knife (dog, etc.). · This thing (knife, dog, etc.) belongs to John. This person has many things.
I don't have anything.
For this concept, I propose kac- as the Korean exponent although there is another word soyuha- (possess), which is used interchangeably in many cases with kac- . The word soyuha- is not used in practice in such contexts as, 'He has two ducklings' . Or 'This child has many toys'. On the other hand, the word kac- is widely used and is familiar to all kinds of people regardless of educational background and age. This word is tested as follows:

161

Chapter 3
(4) i salam-un manh-un kes-ul kac-ko·iss-ta.
this person-TC many-PM thing-AC have-PRG-DC
This person has many things.

(5) na-nun i-kes-tul-ul

kac-ko· siph-ta.

I-TC this-thing-PL-AC have-want-DC

I want to have these things.

3.10.1.3 Existential and possessive primes in Korean
Having identified the equivalent concepts of 'existence' and 'possession' in Korean, one of the Korean language specific features requires an explanation in relation to these
primes. While the Korean word iss- expresses the conceptual equivalent of THERE IS,
there are cases where the same word is used to express 'possession'. Shin S (1998) and
Lee. C (1997) analysed the meaning of iss- as 'existence' and 'possession (either
alienable or inalienable)'. It is a common and widely spread tendency in Korean to use
iss- in order to express the meaning of ' possession', which would be expressed via the
word 'have' in English sentences. This is exemplified as follows:

(6) ku-nun cip-i

twu-chay iss-ta.

3sg-TC house-NM two-CL there is-DC

He has two houses;

(7) na-nun 200 paykman-won-i iss-ta.

I-TC 2million-won-NM

there is-DC

I have two million wons.

(8) na-nun amwukes-to eps-ta.
I-TC nothing-too there is not-DC
I don't have anything.

It has been shown that Korean draws a lexical distinction between existence and
possession evidenced from having two separate lexical items, iss-lTHERE IS and kaclHAVE. However, the word iss-lTHERE IS, proposed as the exponent of the existential
primitive, can express both existence and possession. There are instances in which the
use of iss-lTHERE IS is preferred over kac-lHAVE in expressing possession in natural discourse. The possessional exponent kac-lHAVE can also be substituted for the existential exponent iss-lTHERE IS in most contexts such as (6) to (8).
The kinship relation is not expressed via kac-/HAVE in Korean. This is not a part
of the proposed primitive meaning of HAVE either.

162

Chapter 3
It appears that the proposed Korean exponent of the existential prime, that is iss-,
has a wider range of use than the English equivalent. It has been predicted that there may be considerable differences in the range of use of various exponents of primitives across languages, and consequently it is not surprising that iss- can also express another primitive meaning 'possession'. 75 The central concern for the present research is
whether the two meanings of iss-, 'existence' and 'possession' can be distinguished in
some way.
Compare the pairs of example sentences where iss- is used to express two different meanings:

(9)a ku-ka ton-i

iss-ta-myen, ....

3SG-NM money-NM there is-DC-if

If he has money ...

(9)b ku-ka i kos-ey iss-ta-myen, ....
3SG-NM this place-LOC there is-DC-if
If he is in this place ....

The contrast between (9)a and (9)b shows that the two meanings are distinguished mainly by two different cues: (i) similar to Goddard's (2002b: 117) observation on
Cantonese and Malay, the meaning of 'possession' with iss- in Korean always occurs in
a two argument f~ame, while the meaning of 'existence' occurs in a single argument frame. (ii) The existence of the locus expression as shown in (9)b (although this expression can sometimes be omitted when context allows). However, an ambiguity of meaning may arise if the arguments are omitted, which is very common in Korean discourse. Note the contrast between (1 O)a and (1 O)b:

(1 O)a (na-nun) chayk-i
(I-TC) book-NM
I have many books.

manh-i iss-ta.
many-ADV there is-DC

(1 O)b (i tosekwan-ey) chayk-i manh-i iss-ta.
(this library-Loe) book-NM many-ADV there is-DC
There are many books in this library.

The ambiguity can be clarified either by context or by overt use of an argument. Therefore, in order to avoid a possible ambiguity in a Korean-based NSM, arguments
should not be omitted when the exponent iss- of THERE IS is used. The elliptical

There are more examples where 'possession' is expressed by the existential

as shown in (13)b. The contrast above shows that one cannot substitute the other in that context, and therefore two exponents have to be included and used correctly according
to the context. Similarly the word kac- is the only possible option to express

(14)a manh-un kes-ul kac-nun kes-un cwungyoha-ci anh-ta.

It is not important that there are many things.

Example (14)b is a marginal expression in which the word iss- can still be interpreted as
the meaning of 'possession'.

In emphatic expressions such as (6) and (7), another word, concayha- (exist) tends to be used more frequently although using iss- does not raise any problem semantically or pragmatically. However, if children ask about the meaning of concayha-, then iss- will
be used for them to understand the meaning.
The question whether the sentence can contain a verb within the scope of the existential word is still under cross-linguistic testing. Note the examples below:

(8) i cip-an-ey-nun

There is someone who wants to do this.

The modifying clause is marked by the pre-modifying suffix -nun in the same way as
other attributive words in Korean sentences. Examples (8) and (9) do not appear to have a relative clause but contain a modifying clause. The clause that is treated as a relative clause in English is treated in the same way as a modifying noun or noun phriise in a Korean sentence.

3.10.3 Summary
The existential and possessive primes are tested in this section. As a result they are found to have equivalent concepts in Korean, and all the proposed combinatorial characteristics are found to be along the same lines as the proposed universal grammar of the NSM theory. The findings can be summarised as follows:
The counterpart of the prime THERE IS/ARE is the main verb iss-, and that of HAVE is kac-. Despite these distinctive Korean exponents, it is observed that the
exponent of iss-/THERE IS has two meanings: 'existence' and 'possession'. However, they can be distinguished in different syntactic frames: (i) the number of arguments (ii) the existence of the locus expression in a sentence. This is consistent with what Goddard points out: the meaning of 'existence' is monovalent and can take a locus
166

Chapter 3
argument, whereas the meaning of 'possession' is bivalent, takes a personal substantive as ·subject and generally does not allow a locational adjunct. .

3.11 Life and Death: LIVE, DIE
3.11.1 Lexicon

The meanings of the two relatively new primitives LIVE and DIE are illustrated by the canonical sentences below:
(In stories ofolden days) These people livedfor a long time. These two people lived at the same time. At that time she was living with her sister. Turtles live for a long time. Fish live in the sea. (After e.g. spearing a kangaroo) Is it is still alive. No, it 's dead. His dog died. Jesus died a long time ago.
The exponents of the prim~s LIVE and DIE are identified relatively straightforwardly. I
propose sal- 76 /LIVE and cwuk-/DIE as the Korean exponents. Lee K (1999) has
attempted to describe their meanings by the semantic network method. Her m_~in point is that their meanings are contrastive and conceptualised as antonyms in the minds of Korean people. From the NSM perspective these meanings are not decomposable and can be understood intuitively. They appear together in contrast in many fixed phrases or expressions such as in (1) and (2) below:

(1) cwuk-ki animyen sal-ki-ta.

die-NM

or

living-NM-DC

It is matter of dying or living.

(2) cwuk-ko sa-nun kes-un hanul-ey talli-ess-ta.
die-and living-PM thing-TC heaven-LOC depend-PST-DC
Dying and living depend on heaven.

The Korean exponent sal- means not only the primitive meaning of LIVE but also
'dwelling' or 'inhabit' as shown below:

3.11.2 Syntax
The combinatorial characteristics of the two primitives, LIVE and DIE, are not symmetrical. One of the differences between the two primes LIVE and DIE_}s that the
prime LIVE readily appears with comitative valency, while the prime cwuk-lDIE does
not. This is tested in Korean as follows:
(1) na-nun i salam-kwa sal-ko·siph-ta.
I-TC this person-with live-want-DC
I want to live with this person.
(2) ??na-nun i salam-kwa cwuk-ko·siph-ta.
I-TC this peroson-with live.:want-DC
I want to die with this person.
Sentence (2) is not ungrammatical, since one can say it, though only under very unusual circumstances, for instance, when one wants to choose someone with whom one wants to commit suicide. On the other hand, the comitative valency option with the prime LIVE is natural and widely used in daily conversation. This valency option is possible
by employing the postposed commitative particle -(k)wa as shown in (1) above.

168

Chapter 3

The second difference is in the possibility of having time duration with the prime LIVE, but not with DIE.

(3) salam-un olay tongan sal-ass-ta.

This person lived for a long time.

(4) *i salam-un olay tongan cwuk-ess-ta.
this person-TC long time for die-PST-DC
This person died for a long time.

As suggested in the universal grammar of semantic primitives the meaning of (4) is not comprehensible although the sentence is not ungrammatical.
In Korean both primes are freely combined with temporal or spatial adjuncts as shown below:

3.11 .3 Summary
The two primes LIVE and DIE are found to have conceptual equivalents in Korean: sa/and cwuk-. All the proposed combinations are found to be supported in Korean.
Identifying the Korean counterparts of the proposed primes and testing their syntactic characteristics in relation to these two concepts appeared to be relatively straightforward.

3.12 Time: WHEN!TIME, BEFORE, AFTER, NOW, A LONG TIME, A SHORT TIME, FOR SOME TIME, MOMENT
3.12.1 Lexicon
3.12.1.1 ttay(encey)/WHEN/TIME
The concept of TIME/WHEN can be identified by the following sentences:
When did you do it? I did it when everyone was asleep. I don't know when it happened. At that time, I didn 't know anything about it. At the same time, something happened to me. When we went to the zoo, we... When I did this, I felt something bad. I know something about that time (e.g. some time, period, season, etc.). At times like these, it is good not to do (say) anything. Sometimes dogs bark at night, sometimes they don't (bark). This happened two times (twice). I did this many times (often). It happened many times. This dog always barks at night.
There seem to be a few candidates for the Korean exponent of TIME/WHEN: sikan, sikak, and ttay. All of them are used as the translational equivalents of 'time' depending on context. However, they do not seem to be semantically identical. The word sikak is used when one refers to time as one point, while sikan is used when one refers to a period of time. Both words are borrowings from Sino-Korean. They are used in natural discourse as follows77:
(1) na-nun pap mek-ul sikan-i eps-ta.
I-TC meal eat-AC time-NM there is not-DC
I don't have time to eat.
(2) sikan-ul com naycwu-Z.swu·iss-ni?
time-AC little give-can-INT
Could you spare some time for me?
In the sentences above, sikak cannot be used in the place of sikan, while vice versa is possible in the examples below:

The impossibility of using sikan in (8) and (9) seems to suggest that ttay means more general time than sikan. In fact sikan is often used to refer to a specific time, while ttay can be used for both specific time as well as non-specific and general time as shown in (8) and (9). The specific durational temporal concept as in (10) and (11) is expressed by sikan not ttay, although ttay can also mean temporal duration as seen in (9). When applying the two words against the canonical sentences provided, ttay can be used in all cases while the use of sikan is restricted. On this basis, I propose ttay as the equivalent concept of TIME/WHEN for the purposes of NSM, leaving two questions open: (i) what exactly is the difference in meaning between sikan and ttay? , and (ii) can sikan be paraphrased via ttay?
When ttay is used in questions or embedded sentences another lexical item replaces it. This word is encey, which is not semantically different except for the sentence type as seen in the following examples:

In interrogative sentences like (12)b, the proposed Korean counterpart ttay cannot be used by itself but must occur with the interrogative pronoun enu (what). Alternatively, and more naturally encey (when) can be used for the combination of the question word (enu) and ttay/TIME. There is no meaning difference between (12)a and (12)b, or between the embedded sentences in (13)a and (13)b. Therefore encey has to be regarded as an allolex of ttay, which is used in interrogative or embedded sentences. The distribution of ttay and encey is complementary.

3.12.1.3 cikum/NOW
The meaning of the prime NOW can be understood by the following sentences:
I want you to do it now. I want you to do it now. This is happening now. I want to do it now.
There are two candidates for the Korean counterpart of this prime: cikum (PK) and hyencay (SK). Both of them are tested as follows :

(20) na-nun cikum/hyencay ka-l-swu·eps-ta.
I-TC now/present go-cannot-DC
I can't go now.

(21) na-nun ney-ka ku kes-ul cikuml*hyencay ha-ki-lul wenha-n-ta.

happen-PRG-DC

(23) na-nun cikum/??hyencay ku kes-ul ha-ko·siph-ta.

I-TC now

that thing-AC do-want-DC

I want to do it now.

Judging from these sentences, cikum -appears to be a better exponent for the concept of NOW. Usually cikum refers to a period longer than the moment of speech. Lee (1976) claims that cikum means a period of time which is very close to present. He believes that the meaning of cikum includes a very short time in past and future. These claims are
consistent with the primitive meaning of NOW.

SOME TIME

The proposed meanings of the primes A LONG TIME, A SHORT TIME, and FOR SOME TIME can be identified by the following canonical sentences:

We walkedfor a long time, but we still didn't get there.

175

Chapter 3
He lived here for a long time. She sleptfor a long time/for a short time. He lived in that place for some time. She thought about it for some time. He (only) stayed there for a short time, but she stayed there for a long time. A short time after (that), I heard a noise. I did these things some time ago (i.e. some time before now).
Despite the morphological complexity (in English), the meanings of the expressions A LONG TIME and A SHORT TIME are claimed to be indefinable. The translational equivalents of A LONG TIME and A SHORT TIME are lexicalised by a single morpheme
in Korean: olay and camkkan, respectively. For the concept of indefinite duration FOR
SOME TIME, I propose elma tongan (vague quantity (some) + temporal duration). The
use of the postpositional temporal duration word tongan is optional with olay/A LONG TIME and camkkan/A SHORT TIME, while it is obligatory with elma tongan!FOR

3.12.1.5 swunkan/MOMENT
The concept MOMENT was ·recently proposed as an additional primitive for crosslinguistic investigation (Goddard and Wierzbicka (eds.), 2002). This concept is found to
be lexicalised in all six languages studied in Meaning and Universal Grammar.
However, the status of this new prime is relatively weaker than others, and requires more exploration through a wider range of cross-linguistic study.
This concept is expected to capture the meaning of immediacy in such complex
concepts as 'realise' or 'notice'. I propose the Sino-Korean word swunkan as the Korean
exponent. Since there are not many canonical sentences available, I present a few examples that are used in natural discourse:

3.12.2 Syntax
3.12.2.1 ttay(encey)/WHEN/TIME The concept of TIME is claimed to combine with quantifiers, ONE, TWO, MANY, ALL, and SOME. Each combination is examined in tum as follows:

177

Chapter 3

(l)a i-il-un

han *ttay/twu *Uay ilena-ss-ta.

this-event-TC one time/ two time happen-PST-DC

This happened one time/two times.

(l)b i-il-un

(2) i-il-un

cen-ey myeth pen/*Uay ilena-ss-ta.

this-event-TC before-at some times

happen-PST-DC

The contrast between (1 )a and (1 )b shows that the proposed ~orean exponent ttay cannot be used in combination with the quantifiers han/ONE, twu/TWO, and myech!SOME. Instead, another lexical item, pen, has to be used. The impossibility of using the same lexical item in action with a quantifier has been reported from other
languages such as French, Malay, and Cantonese (cf. Peeter, 1997; Tong et al, 1997). It
is still under discussion whether this impossibility suggests that when-time and occurrence-time are two distinct concepts or that they are two realisations of one concept. In the Korean case, however, the impossibility of using the same lexical item can be interpreted in a somewhat different way. The numerical quantifiers in Korean cannot take a noun directly, but have to be combined with classifiers as mentioned previously in 3.4.2.79 Therefore, the noun ttay cannot be used and has to be replaced by the classifier pen. The word pen is described as a classifier for 'occurrence of happening or action' in Korean grammar by Koh (1996) and Lee Y (1997). On this basis, I propose the classifier pen as an allolex of ttay when it combines with quantifiers, han!ONE, twu!TWO, and mych!SOME.
The combination of some and time in a context like sometimes a person can
think like this: seems to be different from the combination of some and time in li
happened some times before. They are expressed by etten ttay and myech pen in Korean, respectively. While the former refers to when-time, the latter expresses occurrence.
The quantifier manh-lMANY and motun!ALL do not behave in the same way as numerical quantifiers and the vague quantifier myech!SOME. The two words can directly combine with normal nouns but not with classifiers. Therefore, not the classifier pen, but the noun ttay/TIME has to be used in combination with these two words, manhand motun, as shown below:
79 This grammatical requirement affects other situations, for instance, the combination of quantifiers and
the Korean exponent of SOMETHING. The two classifiers, kay, or kaci (classifiers for unspecific things)
are suggested for the general noun kes.

this-thing-TC the same- PM time(-at) happen-PST-DC

This happened at the same time.

There is a more common and idiomatic expression for at the same time in Korean:
tongsiey, which can be considered an optional portmanteau for the combination of TIME
and THE SAME.

3.12.2.2 cen/BEFORE, hwu/AFTER, cikum/NOW
The combination of the prime NOW and BEFORE or AFTER was once proposed as universal to capture the past and future tense in NSM. This claim, however,-~has been revised due to the counterexamples from different languages including Cantonese,
French, Mangaaba-Mbula, and Yankunytjatjara. In Korean cen!BEFORE and hwulAFTER can be combined with any time reference except for cikum!NOW.

(8)a * cikum cen-ey hay-ka tt(u)-ess-ta.
now before sun-NM rise-PST-DC
The sun has risen before now.
(8)b (i) cen-ey hay-ka tt(u)-ess-ta.
(this) before sun-NM rise-PST-DC
The sun has risen before (this).
(9)a *cikum hwu-ey hay-ka ttu-l·kes·i-ta.
now after-at sun -NM rise-PRO-be-DC The sun will rise after now.

180

Chapter 3
(9)b (i) hwu-ey hay-ka ttu-l·kes·i-ta.
(this) after-at sun-NM rise-PRO-be-DC
The sun will rise after (this).

(10) 6 si

cen-ey hay-ka tt(u)-ess-ta.

6 o'clock before sun-NM rise-PST-DC

The sun has risen before 6 o'clock.

(11) (na-nun) 3 nyen hwu-ey ku il-ul ha-l·kes·i-ta.
(I-TC) 3 year after that work do-PRO-be-DC
I will do that after three years.

(12) i-il-un

cen-ey ilena-ss-ta.

this-thing-TC before-at happen-PST-DC

It happened before.

From the Korean language perspective, it seems to be that when no time reference is given the present time is assumed as the reference point although it is not explicit. The
notion of future and past tense can well be expressed by cen13EFORE and hwulAFTER in Korean without being combined with cikum/NOW. When no time reference point is given, the two temporal concepts, cen/BEFORE and hwulAFTER, serve adverbially, as
recently described in the universal grammar of the NSM theory (Goddard and Wierzbicka, 2002b: 67-70).

3.12.2.3 o/ay (tongan)IA LONG TIME, camkkan (tongan)IA SHORT TIME, e/ma tongan/FOR SOME TIME, swunkan/MOMENT
It is proposed that the primes, A LONG TIME, and A SHORT TIME are used in combination with the intensifier VERY. This claim is valid in Korean, as shown below:

(13) ku-nun acwu olay-tongan sal-ass-ta.
3SG-TC very long time live-PST-DC
He lived for a very long time.

(14) ku-nun ku il-ul acwu camkkan-tongan hay-ss-ta.

3SG-TC that work-AC very short time-for

do-PST-DC

He did it for a very short time.

It is proposed that the primes A (VERY) LONG TIME and A (VERY) SHORT TIME are used in combination with BEFORE NOW and AFTER NOW. This claim is tested and
found to be valid by employing the linking word pwuthe (from), as shown in the
following examples:

181

Chapter 3

(15) ku-nun (cikum pwuthe) olay

cen-ey sal-ass-ta.

3SG-TC (now from)

long time before live-PST-DC

He lived a long time before now.

(16) ku-nun (cikum pwuthe) acwu olay

cen-ey sal-ass-ta.

3SG-TC (now from) very long time before live-PST-DC

He lived a very long time before now.

(17) ku-nun ku kes-ul (cikum pwuthe) camkkan hwu-ey ha-l·kes·i-ta.
3SG-TC that thing-AC (now from) short time after-at do-PRO-be-DC
He will do it a short time after now.

(18) ku-nun ku kes-ul (cikum pwuthe) acwu camkkan hwu-ey ha-l·kes·i-ta.
3SG-TC that thing-AC (now from) very short time after-at do-PRO-be-DC
He will do it a very short time after now.

The examples above show that the proposed combinations are possible either with or
without the intensifier acwuNERY. As discussed in 3.12.2.2, while the direct combination of cikum!NOW and
cen/BEFORE and hwulAFTER is impossible, cikum!NOW can be used in combination with cen!BEFORE and hwu!AFTER when other temporal concepts are also used to specify them. For instance, when temporal duration concepts such as olay (tongan)IA (VERY) LONG TIME and camkkan (tongan)IA (VERY) SHORT TIME are used adverbially modifying cen!BEFORE and hwu!AFTER, the time reference cikum!NOW is
optional, as indicated by the parenthesis in the above examples. The syntactic properties for the new prime MOMENT have not been established
well enough to test them. At this stage I will present possible combinations of
swunkan!MOMENT with other primitives in the lexicon. The Korean exponent swunkan
is nominal and can be used in combination with several specifiers such as determiners
like i/THIS, and quantifier han!ONE, and motun/ALL.

(19) sanpwul-ttaymwuney motun-kes-ul han-swunkan-ey ilh-ess-ta

bush fire-because

all thing-AC one-moment-at

loose-PST-DC

We have lost everything in a ·moment because of the bush fire.

(20) i swunkan-ul yengwenhi kiekha-ko·siph-ta

this moment-AC forever

remember-want-DC

I want to remember this moment forever.

(21) ku-wa hamkkey cinayn motu-n swunkan-ul kiekha-ko·siph-ta.
3SG-with togeher stay all-PM moment-AC remember-want-DC
I want to remember all the moments that I have spent with him.

182

Chapter 3
These combinations may not be universal. A detailed study of the syntactic profile of the tentative primitive MOMENT is not pursued further in this research because it
requires more research is required to discover its possible combinatorial properties.
3.12.3 Summary
The six different temporal concepts are examined in terms of both lexicon and syntax. As a result, it has been found that all the proposed semantic primes have equivalent concepts in Korean and that their combinatorial characteristics are along the same lines as the proposed universal grammar.
The word ttay is proposed as the Korean exponent and a few lexical variants, encey (interrogative and embedded sentence), and pen (when combining with numerical quantifiers and myech (some)) are also identified. There are severnl portmanteau forms; hansang or nu! for the combination of TIME and ALL, tongsi(ey) for the combination of THE SAME and TIME, and cacwu for the combination of MANY and TIME. The allolexes and portmanteaus are proposed on the basis of testing the meaning within the given contexts of the canonical sentences.
The meaning of the new tentative primitive MOMENT seems to be expressed by the Korean word swunkan.
Although the direct combination between NOW and BEFORE and AFTER is not possible in Korean, cen/BEFORE and hwu/AFTER can be used adverbially to express past and future tense without being combined with cikum!NOW.

3.13 Space: WHERE/PLACE, ABOVE, BELOW, HERE, NEAR, FAR SIDE, INSIDE

3.13.1 Lexicon
3.13.1.1 kos (et,j/PLACE/WHERE
The proposed primitive meaning of PLACE/WHERE can be identified by the following canonical sentences:

Where did you do it? I did it here. Where is he now?

The best candidate for the counterpart of PLACE seems to be kos. This word is tested as

There is another word, cangso , which is used interchangeably with kos in many

Chapter 3
People would not choose kos in a sentence like (5) although its use is not incomprehensible. The word kos can be substituted for cangso except that kos sounds less natural on its own, while cangso cannot be substituted for kos in examples (6) and (7) above. The word cangso seems to be preferred when people want to specify a place for some event rather than a general place. In most cases kos is used in combination with other attributive words such as in i kos (this place), kathun kos (the same place). I propose kos as the Korean exponent of WHERE/PLACE because this word expresses the equivalent primitive concept in all canonical sentences unlike cangso which seems to
have another semantic component beyond the proposed primitive meaning (e.g. I am
thinking about something happening in this place)
The word kos has an interrogative allolex, eti (where), which is used in questions and embedded sentences. This is parallel to the temporal concept ttay and its allolex

often used symmetrically in natural conversation in Korean.

3.13.1.2 wi/ABOVE, a/ay/BELOW/UNDER
The meanings of the two relative spatial concepts ABOVE and BELOW can be identified by the following canonical sentences:

There were black clouds above us.
The sky is above everything. This thing is above/below this other thing.

185

Chapter 3
For the Korean exponents for ABOVE and BELOW, I propose wi and alay, respectively.
These words are tested as follows:

(10) wuli wi-ey kemun kwulum-i iss-ess-ta.
lPL above-L0C black cloud-NM exist-PST-DC
There were black clouds above us.

(11) ne- wa na-nun hanul alay-ey sa-n-ta.
you-and you-TC sky below live-IN-DC
You and I live under the sky.

(12) i-kes-un

talu-n kes alay-ey iss-ta.

this-thing-TC other-PM thing below-Loe there is-DC

This thing is below the other thing.

There is another candidate for BELOW apart from alay. The word used synonymously with alay is mith. While the two words are used interchangeably in most cases including
examples (10) to (12), there are cases where one is chosen over the other as follows:

(13) nay sinpal ?alaylmith-ey mweka iss-ta.
niy shoes under-L0C something there is-DC
Something is under my shoes.

(14)

pata ?alay/mith-ey mweka iss-nunci

na-nun molu-n-ta.

sea under-Loe something there is-whether I-TC

not know-IN-DC
