(15) chayksang wi alay/?mith-(l)ul ta po-ass-ni?

desk above below-AC

all see-PST-INT

Have you searched both above and below the desk?

It is not clear at this stage whether there is a difference in meaning between the two words. There appears to be a convention of combination when these two words are used in association with other words. The dictionary definitions of the two words are
also circular. However, I propose alay as the counterpart since this word can substitute for the prime BELOW in all canonical sentences and is used symmetrically with wi in all fixed expressions, for instance, wi alay lul molunta (not knowing aboveness and belowness) or wi alay-lul maklonhako (no matter whether above or below).
The Korean counterparts wi and alay are nominal, meaning also 'the top', or 'the
upper part of something', and 'the base' or 'the foot', respectively. When they are used as nominals they can be topicalised and behave in exactly the same way as other nouns. It has been pointed out by Goddard (1998a) that there are some languages where the concepts of ABOVE and BELOW are expressed as noun-like forms having polysemous
186

Chapter 3
meanings. However, it is possible to distinguish whether these words, wi and alay are
used in order to express the spatial relative meanings (the primitive meaning) or other meanings such as 'top part' or 'bottom' by the difference in syntactic construction. The
examples below illustrate the contrast of meanings of wi and alay. In (16) wi means 'the top', while it means 'above' in (17). In (18) alay means 'bottom' while it means
'below' in (19). The number of arguments can be a cue for distinguishing the two meanings as shown in (16) to (19) below:

(16) san

wi-nun chwup-ta.

mountain top-TC cold-DC

It is cold at the top of the mountain.

(17) san

wi-ey-nun

kwulum-i

mountain above-L0C-TC cloud-NM

The cloud is above the mountain.

iss-ta.
there is-DC

(18) alay-lul cal tephe-la.
bottom-AC well cover-IMP
Cover well the bottom part.

(19) i-kos alay-ey maul-i iss-ta.
this-place below-L0C town-NM there is-DC
There is a town below this place.

The metaphorical application of the concept ABOVE is claimed to be universal, or at least nearly universal. This claim is valid in the context of Korean where the ~ords wi
and alay are used metaphorically with reference to relative social positions of people. In fact, wi and alay are frequently used in everyday conversation, for instance, sentences like 'nay-ka ne pota wi ta' meaning 'I am older than you are' or 'wis salam ekey cal hayla' meaning 'Be good for the elders'. It is so widely used that I suspect that in Korean 'wi' has the polysemous meaning of 'superior' or 'older' in itself. 'wi-s salam' (above person) and 'alay salam' (below person)' means 'a person who is superior or
older' and 'a person who is inferior or younger' respectively. This word is very common and is widely used in Korean society where the social structure is hierarchical. There are more examples showing this as follows:

(20) salam wi-ey salam eps-ko

salam alay salam eps-ta.

person above-L0C person there is not-and person below person there is not-DC

There is no person above a person and there is no person below a person.

187

Chapter 3
It is not clear, however, whether the meaning of wi in (21) is used spatially or metaphorically. It appears to be both.

(21) hanunim-i wi-eyse naylyetapo-ko·kyesi-n-ta.
God-NM above-LOC look down-,PRG there is (SH)-IN-DC
Being above us, God is looking down on us.
The metaphorical use of wi and alay may be helpful to capture the notion of higher or lower status of human relations in a community like Korea. Although a phrase such as this person is not like me can express the difference in social status or age, it does not specify the relation in terms of high and low, which is a very common way of categorising people in Korea.

3.13.1.3 yeki/HERE
The meaning of HERE can be identified by the following canonical sentences:
Is it far from here to that place [e.g. GundarooJ?
I live near here. She is not here anymore. Something is happening here.
I propose yeki as the Korean exponent, and this word is tested as follows:

(22) i-kes-un

yeki-eyse ilena-ss-ta.

this-thing-TC here-LOC happen-PST-DC

This happened here.

(23) na-nun yeki-ey sa-n-ta.
I-TC here-LOC live-IN-DC
I live here.

(24) i salam-un yeki-ey iss-ci·anh-ta.
this person-TC here-LOC be-not-DC
This person is not here.

The meaning of yeki in canonical sentences is found to be equivalent to the prime HERE. Lee (1974) claims that yeki originates from i (this) combined with eki (place). The word yeki (here) is frequently used symmetrically to the temporal concept of cikum (now) in natural discourse.

Chapter 3

Is itfar from here to that place [e.g Gundaroo}? (I am sad because) I am far from my mother. That knife (basket, etc.) is (too) far from me -1 can't reach it. Was Fred standing near John? I live near here. Atfirst, she was standingfar from him; then she sat down near him.
I propose kakkap- and mel- as the Korean counterparts of NEAR and FAR, respectively.
These morphemes have allomorphic variants that are phonologically conditioned:
kakka- and me-. Their usages are tested as follows:

The proposed Korean exponents can be used adverbially, attributively or predicatively by taking different suffixes according to their syntactic roles in the sentence. There is no meaning difference between (25)a-c. The two Korean counterparts do not require a relatum (i.e. from this place) obligatorily as indicated in the examples above. The reference point is optional, and can be anything among 'people', 'place', or 'thing'.

3.13.1.5 ccok/SIDE The proposed meaning of SIDE can be identified by the following canonical sentences:

189

Chapter 3
John is now [standing/sitting} on this side (of me). Mary was [standing\sitting} on my right hand side, and Sally was on my left
hand side. There were people standing on both sides ofthe road. The fire was burning on all sides (of us).
There are two candidates for the concept of SIDE: pyen (SK) and ccok (PK). Both of
them can be used in all the canonical sentences. The reference point of SIDE is claimed (Wierzbicka, 1996a: 136) to be 'people', 'thing' , or ' place'. The proposed Korean exponents are tested as follow:

(28) na-nun ne-uy i phyen/ccok-ey iss-ta.

there is-DC

stand-STA-DC

There were people standing on both sides of the road.

The reference point is marked by the postposed genitive case marker -uy as seen in the . above examples. The meaning of ccok is polysemous (means, ' side', 'piece', or 'cut'), and can be ambiguous out of context. In all the canonical sentences, however, the meaning of ccok is used to express the concept of SIDE.

There is an insect inside this [e.g. walnut}. Some possums made their nest inside a hollow tree. This cave is very big - lots ofpeople could live inside it. It happened inside the house (cave, .etc.)
For the Korean counterpart, there are two candidates: an and sok. The dictionary definition for an is 'inside', while for sok it is ' deeper inside'.

The possibility of using (ON) SIDE in combination with other directional (right or left) expressions in Korean is illustrated in the examples below:

3.13.3 Summary
As a result of testing them against canonical sentences, it has been found that all the proposed spatial primes have equivalent lexical exponents in Korean, and their syntax is
consistent with the proposed universal grammar. While I proposed an as the counterpart
of INSIDE for its· meaning in the context of canonical sentences is equivalent to the
proposed primitive, the re1ationship between an and sok is not pursued here in depth. A
196

Chapter 3
further investigation on the semantic relationship between the two words is required and a choice of rule depending on referents has to be explored.
Temporal and spatial concepts are claimed (Wierzbicka, 1996a) to be closely linked to each other. This claim is valid in the context of Korean where the spatial concepts kakkap-lNEAR and mel-lFAR are also used in a temporal sense. The metaphorical use of wilABOVE and alay!BELOW has been found to be possible in Korean. In fact, the metaphorical use of them appears to be useful to capture the notion of higher and lower status in terms of social position and age in Korean society.

3.14 Logical Concepts: NOT, MAYBE, CAN, BECAUSE, IF
3.14.1 Lexicon
3.14.1.1 an (ci·anh-)INOT
The prime NOT is proposed as one of the universal semantic primitives. Its meaning can be identified by the following canonical sentences:
There was a lot ofnoise; because ofthis, I couldn't sleep. The dog died because the man hit it on the head (not because ofsomething
else). If it rains tomorrow, I won't come. I didn't do it (see it, etc.). I don't want you to do it.
Korean is known to have two different types of negation as shown below:

(l)a na-nun i kes-ul an po-ass-ta.
I-TC this thing-AC not see-PST-DC
I did not see this.
(l)b na-nun i kes-ul po-ci·anh-ass-ta.
I-TC this thing-AC see-not-do-PST-DC
I did not see this.

The structure of (1 )a is called short form negation or pre-verbal negation, and that of (l)b is long form negation or post-verbal negation.. The latter is made up of the negative nominaliser ci and anh- 82 . The question of whether there is a meaning difference

82 This is the abbreviation of the negative adverb an (not) and ha- (do).

197

Chapter 3
between the two different structures has been an issue for many researchers. Among the claims made, Yoon (1997) believes that the two different negation structures originally had different meanings and functions since their scopes are different: the long-form negation structure was originally generated by the need to confine the scope of negation to the verb phrase, while the short form negation structure was generated by the need of negating the verb. He believes, however, that there is no meaning difference in the generation of both negation structures in modern Korean.
On the contrary, Kim (1996) believes that the occurrence of two forms is not arbitrary and that they serve different purposes in discourse. She indicates that the short form is used more often as a negative answer to a confirmation question than to a normal question in order to strongly deny a presupposed assertion. According to her, the short form is preferred in a contrastive environment, even though the long form is tolerable. Despite the fact that I acknowledge the subtle difference in pragmatic choice
between the two forms, I am inclined to allow both forms for the purpose of a Korean-
based NSM since the meanings in the given contexts of canonical sentences do not
appear to be different. More importantly the difference pointed out by Kim (1996) does
not seem to be paraphrasable, at least with intuitively intelligible terms. However, further exploration is required in order to determine in which context they differ
semantically from each ·other. For the time being I propose to use both negating
structures for the Korean-based NSM on the basis testing their meaning in a given context of canonical sentences.
While both forms, an + verb and verb+ ci·anh, can be used to express the
equivalent meaning of NOT in all canonical sentences in Korean, one explanation in relation to the syntactic characteristics of the two different forms is required. The long form has a wider scope of negation than the short form. According to Sohn (1994: 132133), while verbs are relatively free in occurring in both forms, most adjectives are more natural in long forms than in short forms. Also when a negated verb is a compound, an/NOT cannot usually come before the compound. If, · however, a
compound is separable, for example, as in malha- (say), (which consists of ma! (word) and ha (do)), an can be placed before the constituent verb stem (i.e. before -ha) as seen
in (2)b.

(2)a *na-nun i kes-ey tayhay ku-eykey
I-TC this thing-about 3SG-DAT
I didn't say about this to him.

an malhay-ss-ta.
not say-PST-DC

198

Chapter 3

(2)b na-nun i kes-ey tayhay ku-eykey mal(-ul) an hay-ss-ta.

I-TC this thing-about

3SG-DAT word (-AC) not do-PST-DC

I didn't say about this to him.

However, when a compound is inseparable (e.g. keleka-, which consists of ket- (walk) and ka (go)), the same rule applied in (2) is not allowed. The negation word an has to be placed before the compound as shown in (3).

(3)a *ku-nun i kif-lo

kele an ka-ss-ta.

3SG -TC this road-toward walk not go-PST-DC

I didn't say to him about this.

(3)b ku-nun i •kif-lo

an keleka-ss-ta.

3SG -TC this road-toward not walk-PST-DC

I didn't say to him about this.

Among the identified Korean exponents there are several compound verbs, such as malha-lSAY, sayngkakha-lTHINK, and wenha-lWANT, among which the two previous compound verbs are separable while the last one is inseparable. The syntactic constraints on the use of the short form negation an should be accounted for in the composition of the Korean-based NSM.

3.14.1.2 V + (u)l·swu·(ka)·iss-lCAN
The proposed primitive meaning of CAN may be identified by the following canonical sentences:
I can't do it now, but maybe my brother can. I can 't do it now, but 1 can do it later. I can 't do it now but I could do it before. I can 't do this. Why did you do that? - Something bad could have happened to you. Bad things can happen to everyone/good people. This thing can move.
There is no such distinction as a possibility-can and an ability-can in NSM. In the canonical sentences both meanings are expressed without any formal distinction and

Chapter 3
interpreted by the context. I propose -l-swu(ka)·iss- as the Korean counterpart of CAN
and this expression is tested as follows:

(4) na-nun i il-ul ha-l·swu·(ka)·iss-ta.
I-TC this thing-AC do-can-DC
I can do it.

(5) ne-nun i mal-ul ha-Z.Swu·(ka) ·eps-ta.

(6) ne-eykey nappu-n il-tul-i

you-DAT bad-PM event-PL-NM happen-can-DC

Bad things can happen to you.

The Korean counterpart of CAN consists of four morphemes among which one (-ka) is optional: -(u)l·swu·(ka)- iss-. This combination is made up of a relative clause ending in the prospective modifier suffix -(u)l, followed by the defective head noun swu (literally 'way' 'method', and 'ability'), an optional nominative particle -ka, and the existential adjective iss- (exist). Despite morphological complexity, the meaning corresponds to the
proposed prime CAN in the context of canonical sentences as shown above. There are examples where this combination of formatives (or morphemes) is found in natural discourse, which shows how basic this usage is.

(7) ku-ka kkwum-ul ilwu-l·swu·(ka) ·iss-ki pala-n-ta.
3sg-NM dread-AC accomplish-can-NOM hope-IN-DC
I hope he can accomplish his dream.

fog-NM set out-if air plain-NM delay-do-become-can-DC
If there's fog about, the airplain could be delayed.
(9) piano-lul chi-l·swu·(ka)·iss-ni?
Piano-AC play-can-INT
Can you play piano?

This expression is obligatorily used in combination with predicates. As seen in the examples above, the Korean exponent is used for both 'potentiality' and ' possibility' without formal distinction.
In the context of various grammatical categories and in relation to the obligatory
verbal suffixes and optional honorific suffixes slots concerned, the proposed exponent
-(u)l·swu·(ka)·iss- does not involve any formal change as shown in the examples below:

The inflections that depend on tense, aspect, or mood are achieved by suffixing after the proposed counterpart -(u)l-swu·(ka)-iss-.

3.14.1.3 ama V+ (u)/·kes·i-/MAYBE
The meaning of MAYBE can be identified by the following canonical sentences:
Maybe it will rain -tomorrow. Maybe he will come tomorrow, maybe he won't (come tomorrow). I don't know. (Who broke the pot?) Maybe John did it. (Where's Mary?) I don't know. Maybe John knows.
The equivalent concept of MAYBE seems to be encoded in two separate morphemes, which are used jointly in a sentence: ama (preverbal) and (u)l·kes·i- (post verbal)83 . The latter consists of three morphemes: the prospective modifier suffix -(u)l, followed by the bound noun kes (literally 'thing'), and the copular i (be). Despite the morphological complexity the meaning of -(u)l-kes·i- is known as 'conjecture' or 'assumption' (Jang, 2000). The proposed expression is used as follows:

(13)a ama ku-ka i il-ul

ha-l·kes·i-ta.

Maybe he will do it.

(14)a ama ku-nun i mal-ul

Maybe he will not say this.

do-not-maybe-DC

3.14.1.5 (manyak) -(u)myen/lF
The meaning of IF can be identified by the following canonical sentences:
If it rains tomorrow, I won 't come. Ifyou do this, people will say something bad about you. Ifhe hits you, don 't hit him back. Ifhe comes here, don 't open the door.
The counterpart of the prime IF seems to be (manyak) -(u)myen. It is known that in Korean there are two conditional markers, -(u)myen and -tamyen (so called 'cleftconditional ') on which there are different views among linguists. Lee (1994) argues that Korean employs two different forms of conditional construction: -tamyen is preferred over -(u)myen to mark the unexpected and remote possibility or the unrealistic situation with a counterfactual flavour, whereas -(u)myen prevails over -tamyen when the content of the protatis is viewed as the expected or immediate possibility.
Regardless of dispute, it is agreed that -(u)myen functions to mark conditionality in Korean although it can also function as a linker of a temporal clause that have nothing to do with conditionality. The two uses can be distinguished by the fact that (u) myen can be used as a temporal linker only when it is used ·on its own. When -(u)myen is used with manyak, it should be interpreted as conditional. Therefore, the two parts, manyak and -(u)myen should be proposed as the Korean exponent eventhough the first part can be omitted in normal discourse. The morpheme -(u)myen can serve to express the equivalent concept of IF within the given contexts of canonical sentences. Therefore, for the purpose of this research I propose the combination of the
204

Chapter 3
optional morpheme manyak and the morpheme -(u)myen as the Korean counterpart of
IF. The following examples support this.

(22)a

manyak ney-ka i-il-ul

ha-myen nappu-n

If you-NM this thing-AC do-if bad-PM

il-i ilena-l·kes·i-ta.
thing-NM happen-PRO-thing-DC

If you do this, bad things will happen (to you).

In natural discourse another word manyak is frequently used in collaboration with the proposed verbal suffix-like morpheme -(u)myen. The morpheme manyak is optional
since the equivalent concept of conditional can be expressed only by the second part of
the proposed expression -(u)myen, and not vice versa. This is exemplified as follows:

(22)b ney-ka i-il-ul

ha-myen nappu-n il-i

ilena-l·kes·i-ta.

you-NM this-thing-AC do-if

bad-PM thing-NM happen-PRO-DC

If you do this, bad things will happen (to you).

(22)c *manyak

this-thing-AC

ha nappun
do bad-PM

thing-NM happen-PRO-DC

From the contrasts in (22)b and (22)c, it can be noted that the necessary part of the
expression is -(u)myen rather than manyak. However, the optional part manyak is not insignificant in the sense that the meaning of the temporal linker of -(u)myen is ruled
out when manyak is present. While I propose the expression (manyak) -(u)myen as the Korean exponent of IF
on the basis of testing its meanings as presented previously, I leave a closer examination on the s emantic relationship between this morpheme and so called cleft conditional
, morpheme, -tamyen, for further s.tudy.

Chapter 3
The unacceptability of (l)b, (2)b, and (12)b is due to the syntactic constraint on the use of the short form negation for compound verbs. All the predicates except for al-lKNOW and iss-lTHERE IS are found to combine with NOT in Korean as exemplified.
In relation to the two exceptions an explanation is required. In Korean there are a
few words that have corresponding negative lexical items. These kinds of negation are described as constituent negation or lexical negation (Sohn, 1994: 134). Sohn points to the existential adjective eps- (there is not) and molu- (not know) as the most typical predicates in this category. Since the negativity is inherent, no kind of negative adverb can combine with these words. The meaning of the combination of NOT and KNOW is lexicalised in the expression molu- as seen in examples (13) and (14) below:

(13) na-nun i salam-ul molu-n-ta.
I-TC this person-AC not know-IN-DC
I don't know this person.

(14) na-nun ne-eykey i il-i

ilena-ss-nunci

mol(u)-ass-ta.

I-TC you-DAT this event-NM happen-PST-whether not know-PST-DC

I did not know that this happened to you.

The word molu- (not know) should be considered as an obligatory portmanteau. In the same fashion there is a separated lexical item eps- for the combination of iss-lTHERE IS and NOT.

(1 S)a ku salam-un i kos-ey
that person-TC this place-L0C
That person is not in this place.

iss-ci·anh-ta.
there is-not-DC

(1 S)b *ku salam-un i kos-ey an iss-ta.
that person-TC this place-L0C not there is-DC
That person is not in this place.

(l 5)c ku salam-un i kos-ey
that person-TC this place-L0C
That person is not in this place.

eps-ta.
there is not-DC

Therefore, I propose the word eps- as the p01imanteau combining the primes THERE IS
and NOT. Although (1 S)a is not awkward (1 S)c is a more idiomatic expression. The prime NOT is proposed to·combine with CAN. This is tested as follows:

two structures resemble the two different negating rules presented in 3.14.1.1. 85 The
word mos (cannot) can be treated as a portmanteau, which is the combination of CAN
and NOT. When this is used post-verbally -ci·mos·ha- is the corresponding expression of
mos86• There are some more examples as follows:

3.14.2.3 ttaymwun(ey)/BECAUSE (manyak)-(u) myen/lF
It is proposed that the fundamental combinatorial frame for BECAUSE is the ' causal
adjunct' expression because of this, where THIS refers to a situation spelt out in the
212

Chapter 3
preceding clause. This causal adjunct expressed by BECAUSE OF THIS has an important role in semantic analysis in various situations. This claim is tested as follows:

(44) X nun Y-ey tayhay mwenka nappu-n mal-ul hay-ss-ta.
X-TC Y-at about something bad-PM word-AC do-PST-DC
X said something bad about Y.

i ttaymwuney salamtul-un Y- ey tayhay nappu-key sayngkak-ul hay-ss-ta.

this because

people-TC Y-at about bad-PM thought-AC do-PST-DC

Because of this, people thought something bad about Y.

From testing, it has been found that the claim about the 'causal adjunct' is supported in
Korean where the exponent ttaymwuney is used to link two clauses in order to express the 'cause'. There are more examples where the word ttaymwuney is used as a linker:

(45) .Y ka ilena-ss-ki

ttaymwuney X-ka ilena-ss-ta.

Y-NM happen-PST-NOM because

X-NM happen-PST-DC

X happened because Y happened.

(46) ney-ka Y-lako malhay-ss-ki ttaymwuney (na-nun) X-lul hay-ss-ta.

you-NM Y-Q say-PST-NOM because

(I-TC) X-AC do-PST-DC

I did X because you said Y.

The concept of conditional IF allows the only bi-clausal sentences in NSM analysis. The possibility of having the same construction in Korean is tested as follows:

(47) (manyak) ney-ka i il-ul ha-myen
(if) you-NM this thing-AC do-if

salamtul-i ne-ey tayhay nappu-n mal-tul-ul ha-l·kes·i-ta.

If you do this, people will say bad things about you.

ilena-myen, (ku kes-un) na-eykey coh-ul· kes· i-ta.

this-event-NM happen-if (that thing-TC) I-DAT good-PRO-DC

If this happens, it will be good for me.

(49) ney-ka i il-ul

ha-myen

you-NM this thing-AC do-if

i motun salamtul-i ttokkath-un il-ul ha-l·kes· i-ta.

this all

people-NM the same-PM thing-AC do-PRO-DC

If you do this, all these people will do the same.

The Korean exponent -(u)myen is suffixed to the stem of predicates or attached to the declarative sentence marker -ta. While this morpheme expresses the conditional in the
213

Chapter 3
provided canonical sentences, the following example seems somewhat different from other previously presented examples presumably due to the negation in the sentence.

(50) ku-ka o-myen mwun-ul yel-ci

ma-la.

3SG-NM come-if door-AC open-NOM not-IMP

If he comes here, don't open the door.

The meaning of the Korean sentence can be interpreted something along the lines as 'if I (or anybody) come, open the door, but if he comes don't open', of which the first clause is elliptical. When the condition co-occurs with negation in the same sentence, the natural way to express this combination is -telato (even if) as shown in (51) below:

(51) ku-ka o-telato

mwun-ul yel-ci

ma-la.

3SG-NM come-even if door-AC open-NOM not-IMP

Even if he comes here, don't open the door.

The meaning of the canonical sentence, which is out of context, is not clear enough to decide which is the right interpretation between (50) and (51 ). The expression -telato appears to be a negative allolex of -(u)myen from the contrast of the two sentences above although this shallow examination is not sufficient to decide their relationship yet. The meanings and syntactic properties of -telato should be examined exhaustively in further study.

3.14.3 Summary
The five primitives, CAN, MAYBE, NOT, BECAUSE and IF are attested against canonical sentences in terms of both lexicon and syntax. As a result of the investigation it has been found, firstly, that all these primes have equivalent concepts in Korean: For the equivalent concept of NOT, two different expressions are proposed after testing the difference in meaning between them: an and V+ ci·anh-. For the primes CAN and MAYBE, verbal suffixes which consist of a few discrete morphemes are proposed as the Korean exponents: -(u)l·swu·(ka)·iss- and ama -(u)-kes·i-. The first part of the latter, ama, is found alone to carry the equivalent meaning of MAYBE only when the verbal phrase is elliptical with a contextual pem1ission. For the primitive of cause and conditional, two Korean exponents, ttaymwuney/BECAUSE and (manyak) -(u)myen /IF, are proposed, respectively.

214

Chapter 3
Secondly, all the proposed combinatorial characteristics are supported in Korean. There are cases where the combination of two primes is lexicalised in a single word and I propose these words as obligatory portmanteaus: molu- as the combination of KNOW and NOT, mos as that of CAN and NOT, and eps- as that of THERE IS and NOT.
While the combinatorial characteristics of (manyak) -(u)myen !IF are found to be
consistent with the proposed universal grammar, the question whether or not te lato (even if) is the negative allolex of IF is left open. More canonical sentences with contexts would be useful for determining the meaning of the combination of IF and NOT occurring in the same sentence.

3.15 Intensifier and Augmenter: VERY, MORE

3.15.1 Lexicon
3.15.1.1 acwu/VERY
The prime VERY is proposed as one of the semantic primitives since the validity of the earlier definition of this concept via 'more' was questioned and all languages appear to have a corresponding word to this concept (Wierzbicka, 1996a: 65-66). The meaning of VERY can be identified from the following canonical sentences:

It is very good/bad. It is very big/small. This place [e.g. Gundaroo] is very far from here. It happened a very long time ago. He lived there for a very long time. I very much want to do this.

There are a number of potential exponents of the concept of VERY in Korean: acwu, maywu, mopsi, and mwuchek. All of them are widely and interchangeably used as shown in the following examples:

(1) i kos-un acwu/maywu/mopsilmwuchek coh-un kos-i-ta.

this place-TC very

good-PM place-be-DC

This is a very good place.

(2) i kes-un acwu/maywu/mopsilmwuchek khu-ta.

this thing-TC very

big-DC

This is very big.

215

Chapter 3
They are all pure Korean words and belong to the same word class. Although there might be differences in the range of use of these words (presumably there may be certain conventional preferences depending on contexts), they do not seem to differ
semantically at least in the given contexts of the canonical sentences such as in (1) and
(2). Their combinatorial characteristics with other primes in the NSM lexicon are equally natural. Son (2000) claims that among other intensifiers in Korean mwuchek, mopsi, maywu, acwu, and kacang can equally express the high degree of intensity.87 However, I propose acwu among others as the Korean exponent since it seems to be used most frequently regardless of the context even among young children. In fact, judging from ordinary observation this word seems to be acquired earlier than other words. There are some more examples with the proposed Korean exponent acwul VERY as follows:
(3) i salam-un acwu khu-ta.
this person-TC very big-DC
This person is very big.
(4) i kos-un sal-ki-ey acwu nappu-ta.
this place-TC live-N0M-for very bad-DC
This place is very bad for living.
3.15.1.2 te/MORE
The meaning of the proposed universal semantic primitive MORE is 'augmentative' not 'comparative', The proposed meaning can be identified by the following canonical sentences:
More! (e.g. a child crying for food)
(I don't know much about it) I want to know more.
Don't say anything more (about it)!
I want to see more. I want more.
I ate a lot quickly, and then I didn't want any more.
I propose teas the Korean counterpart on the basis of the following examples:
(5) na-nun te po~ko·siph-ta.
I -TC more see-want-DC
I want to see more
87 I think that the word kacang (the most) is not semantic equivalent of VERY. However, this matter is not pursed further here for it is beyond the goal of this thesis.
216

Chapter 3
(6) na-nun te tut-ko ·siph-ta.
I-TC more hear-want-DC
I want to hear more.
(7) na-nun te al-ko·siph-ta.
I-TC more know-want-DC
I want to know more.
(8) na-nun te sal-ko·siph-ta.
I-TC more live-want-DC
I want to live more.
(9) na-nun te kac-ko·siph-ta.
I-TC more have-want-DC
I want to have more.
The comparative meaning of 'more' (in fact, more than) is not proposed as universal.
However, the word te in Korean sentences can sometimes be interpreted as comparative
meaning depending on context. When the intended meaning is comparative the relatum
pota (than) co-occurs optionally preceding the exponent te. Consider the following
examples:

(1 O)A na-nun ku-il-ey tayhay nemwu al-ko·siph-e.
I-TC that-thing-about too much know-want-IN
I want to know about it very much.
B na-nun (ne-pota) te al-ko ·siph-e.
I-TC (you-than) more see-want-DC
I want to know it more than you do.

In (10), what B intends to express is 'I want to know more than you do' , which is
comparative meaning. The ambiguity between comparative and augmentative of te may arise when the relatum pota is elliptical with contextual permission. Then there is no syntactic cue for distinguishing the two meanings of te. In natural discourse the context mostly gives a clue for the correct interpretation of the meaning of te. In (11) below, te
clearly expresses the 'augmentative' meaning:

(l l)A i-kes mek-ko te mek-e-la.
this-thing eat-and more eat-INF-IMP
Eat more after you finish eating this.
B te cwu-sey-yo.
more give-SH-POL
Give me more please.

217

Chapter 3
There is a negative allolex of te that is used when negation concurs in the same sentence. The expression te isang (anymore) replaces te!MORE when it is used in combination with NOT. Choi (1999) shows that the distribution of the expression te
isang is syntactically restricted. She treats this as a negative polarity word that cannot
co-occur with non-negative predicates. Examples below appear to be consistent with this analysis:

(12)a ku salam-un te isang i kos-ey sal-ci anh-nun-ta.
that person-TC anymore this place-LOC live-NOM not-IN-DC
That person does not live in this place anymore.
(12)b *ku salam-un te i kos-ey sal-ci·anh-nun-ta.
that person-TC more this place-LOC live-not-IN-DC
That person does not live in this place anymore.
This is parallel to the English case where 'anymore' is the natural expression when MORE is used in combination with NOT.

3.15.2 Syntax
3.15.2.1 acwu/VERY
It is proposed that the prime VERY is combined with evaluators (GOOD/BAD), descriptors (BIG/SMALL), and the quantifier MANY/MUCH. This claim is tested and found to be valid as shown in the following examples:

(1) i kes-un acwu coh-/nappu-(u)n kes-i-ta.

this thing-TC very good/bad-PM

thing-be-DC

This is a very good/ bad thing.

(2) i kes-un acwu khu-/cak-ta.
this thing-TC very big/small-DC
This is very big/ small.

(3) salamtul-i acwu manh-ta.
people-NM very many-DC
There are many people.

It is proposed that VERY can be used in combination with the durational temporal concepts, FOR A LONG TIME and FOR A SHORT TIME, and the spatial concepts such as NEAR and FAR. They are tested and found to be supported as shown below_:

218

Chapter 3
(4) ku il-un acwu olay tongan ilena-ss-ta.
that event-TC very long time for happen-PST-DC
It happened for a very long time.

(5) na-nun i il-ul acwu camkkan tongan hay-ss-ta.

I-TC this thing-AC very short time for

do-PST-DC

I did this thing for a very short time.

(6) ne-nun acwu mel-/kakka-(l)i sa-n-ta.
you-TC very far/near-ADV live-IN-DC
You live very far/near.

The combination of VERY and the quantifiers ONE, TWO and SOME is claimed to
be incomprehensible. This is found to be the case in Korean as shown below:
(7) *i kos-ey salam-i acwu hana iss-ta.
this place-L0C person-NM very one there is-DC
*There is very one person in this place.
(8)b *i kos-ey salam-i acwu twul iss-ta.
this place-Loe person-NM very two there is-DC
*There are very two people in this place.
(9) *i kos-ey salam-i acwu myech iss-ta.
this place-L0C person-NM very some there is-DC
*There are very some people in this place.

It is proposed that the prime MORE is used in combination with WANT and some other

These combinations are tested below and found to be possible. However, there are two possibilities of expressing the same meaning resulting from the fact that there are two Korean exponents of WANT (see 3.7.1 for detail). All the proposed combinations are possible as seen in the examples below:

Wierzbicka (1996a: 78) claims a special psychological link between WANT and MORE. This seems to be convincing from the observation of ordinary life in which
people frequently use such sentences as I want more. The translation of this sentence in
Korean, however, does not seem t"o be as straightforward as in English.

(15) na-nun te wenha-n-ta.
I-TC more want-IN-DC
I want more (than elliptical relatum).
Out of context, the Korean sentence (15) can be interpreted either as 'I want more than' (comparative) or 'I want more' (augmentative). When a sentence like (15) is interpreted as comparative meaning, this is based on the assumption that there is the elliptical
clause 'ne-pota' (than you), which is common in natural discourse. As mentioned
previously, Korean is highly contextual such that clauses and constituents are often omitted naturally with contextual permission. Consider the example (16):
220

Chapter 3
(There is only one ticket for the concert. Someone is offering the ticket to two of his/her friends.)

(16)A nwuka te wenha-ni?
who more want-INT
Who wants more?
B nay-ka [i chinkwu-pota} te wenhay-yo.
I-TC [this friend-than] more want-POL
I want more (than this friend does).
The meaning of the word te!MORE in (16) is comparative, and not augmentative. 88
However, when the part in the square bracket is omitted the meaning ambiguity between comparative and augmentative arises. The correct interpretation can often be made
based on contextual clues in natural discourse. Another context is exemplified as follows:
(At the dinner table, someone is offering food by asking the people around the table)
(17)A nwuka te wenha-ni?
who more want-INT
Who wants more?
B na-nun toy-ss-ta.
I-TC fine-PST-DC
I am fine.
C ce-nun te wenhay-yo.
I-TC more want-DC
I want more.
D te cwu-sey-yo.
more give-SH-be-DC
Please give me more.
E na-nun te mek-ko·siph-e-yo.
I-TC more eat-want-INF-POL
I want to eat more.

Among different answers to 'who wants more' presented above, the sentence (l 7)C is the literal translation of the English sentence I want more. Even though the direct combination of teMORE and wenha-lWANT is not awkward the use of sentence (17)C in natural conversation is marginal. Instead, (17)D or (17)E is the pragmatically preferred choice. This seems to be because the word wenha-lWANT cannot take

88Although the meaning of ( l 6)A can be understood, the idiomatic expression will be 'nwuka te kackosiphni?' (who wants to have it more).

221

Chapter 3
te/MORE as a complement. The predicate wenha-/WANT takes a nominal complement and te is an adverb that modifies the predicate. Therefore, the meaning of (17)C can
normally be interpreted as 'I want more than' out of the context. However, with the contextual clue (response to the question of ' who wants more ' ) it can be interpreted as 'I want more'. This interpretation is made on the assumption of the elliptical
complement 'mek-ki-lul' (eat-nominaliser-accusative marker: meaning 'to eat'), which is
in fact 'I want to eat more'. Therefore, without a given context, (l 7)C may cause ambiguity of meaning
between I want more and 'I want more than'. In order to express augmentative meaning
with the word te!MORE in Korean, the complement has to be explicit: i.e. 'te mekilul wenhanta' (want to eat more) or 'te kackilul wenhanta' (want to have more). Therefore, the meaning ambiguity of 'te wenha-' (want more) between I want more and 'I want
more than' can be solved by providing a context with some preceding sentences, or expressing 'what is wanted more' explicitly.
It is claimed that the prime MORE is able to combine with various 'stative' predicates to convey a kind of temporal meaning related to endurance or persistence
over time. This claim is found to be valid in Korean by using negative allolex te isang,
as shown in the following:

(18) X-nun te isang sal-a·iss-ci·anh-ta.

X-TC more

live-STA-not-DC

X is not living anymore.

(19) X-nun te isang khu-ci·anh-ta.

X -TC more

big-not-DC

X is not big anymore.

3.15.3 Summary
As a result of testing the proposed primes VERY and MORE, it has been found that they
have lexical equivalents in Korean: acwu and te , respectively, and their combinatorial
characteristics are consistent with the proposed universal grammar.
It is observed that ambiguity of meaning can arise when the Korean exponent te is used in direct combination with predicates, in particular, wenha-lWANT. In other words,
the meaning of te!MORE can be interpreted either as augmentative or comparative (i.e. I
want more or 'I want more than'). However, it has been shown that this ambiguity can

222

Chapter 3
be clarified by expressing overtly the complement of the predicate WANT, which is 'what is wanted more' (e.g. 'want to eat' or 'want to have').

3.16 Taxonomy and Partonomy: KIND OF, PART OF
3.16.1 Lexicon
3.16.1.1 cong/yu(uy)/KIND OF
The concept of KIND (OF) is proposed as one of the semantic primitives on the basis of the important role of taxonomic classification in all languages and cultures. Although there have been claims that ethno-biological classification is predominantly nontaxonomic in non-Western societies, recent investigations by anthropologists and linguists have shown that the concept of KIND (OF) is universal (Wierzbicka, 1996a: 6263). The primitive meaning of KIND (OF) can be identified by the following canonical sentences:
There are two kinds ofyam (bat, etc.). There are many kinds offish (bird, etc.). (of two kinds offish, fruit, etc.) This kind offish is good to eat. This (other) kind is not good to eat. Two kinds ofpeople live here.
In order to express the equivalent concept in Korean, the word conglyu 1s normally used. This Sino-Korean word consists of cong and lyu. These can be used also to express the same concept as conglyu depending on context. The following is exemplified to show that the word conglyu is the conceptual equivalent of KIND (OF):

(1) i-kes-un

ttokkath-un sayngsen-i anila

this-thing-TC the same-PM fish-NM not

ttokkath-un conglyu-uy sayngsen-i-ta.
the same-PM kind-ATR fish-be-DC
This is not the same fish, but it is the same kind of fish.

(2) hankwuk-ey-nun manh-un conglyu-uy namwu-ka iss-ta.
Korea-LOC-TC many-PM kind-ATR tree-NM there is-DC
There are many kinds of trees in Korea.

223

Chapter 3
(3) seysang-ey-nun twu conglyu-uy salamtul-i iss-ta.
word-LOC-TC two kind-ATR people-NM be-DC
There are two kinds of people in the world.
The postposed particle -uy has to be employed to link the modified noun as seen in the
examples above. According to Wierzbicka (1985), the concept of KIND (OF) plays an essential role in human conceptualisation of the world as humans do tend to classify things.
3.16.1.2 pwupwun(uy)IPART (OF)
It is acknowledged that different cultures do not show the same degree of interest in the concept PART (OF). While speakers of technologically complex modem societies tend to emphasise on viewing various aspects of reality in terms of complexes analysable into 'parts', other groups for example, Australian Aborigines are less inclined to talk about this concept. (Wierzbicka, 1996a: 61). However, despite the difference in degree of interest, the meaning of PART (OF) is claimed to be universal which can be identified by the following canonical sentences:
A blade is part ofa knife (and/or: a knife has a blade). An axe has a handle. (and/or: a handle is a part ofan axe). This thing has two parts (and/or: there are two parts to this thing). This thing has many parts. (and/or: there are many parts to this thing). All ofthese parts are small.
As the Korean counterpart of PART(OF), I propose pwupwun(uy). This word is tested as
follows:

(4)a pal-un mom-uy han pwupwun-i-ta.
feet-TC body-GEN one part-be-DC
The foot is a part of the body.
(4)b pal-un mom-uy ilpwu(-i)-ta.
feet-TC body-GEN one part(-be)-DC
The foot is a part of the body.
(S)a kho-nun elkwul-uy han pwupwun-i-ta.
nose-TC face-of one part-be-DC
The nose is a part of the face.
(S)b kho-nun elkwul-uy ilpwu-ta.
nose-TC face-of one part-DC
The nose is a part of the face.

224

Chapter 3

In (4) and (5) pwupuwn is used in combination with han (one) in order to express a part. The more idiomatic expression of this combination in Korean is ilpwu (one part) in
which the two concepts, ONE and PART, are encoded as seen in (4)b and (5)b. There are
more examples that contain the concept PART.

(6) khal-un twu pwupwun-ulo mantul-e-ci-e iss-ta.

knife-TC two parts-with

make-INF-CAU-STA-DC

A knife is made of two parts.

(7) kkochiph-un kkoch-uy han pwupwun-i-ta

petal-TC

flower-GEN one part-be-DC

A petal is a part of a flower.

3.16.2 Syntax

3.16.2.1 cong/yu(uy)/KIND (OF)

It is proposed that the concept KIND(OF) can be combined with determiners (THIS, THE SAME, OTHER), and quantifiers (ONE, TWO, MANY/MUCH, ALL, SOME). This claim is tested as follows:

(1) i conglyu-uy say-tul-un i kos-ey sa-n-ta.

this kind-of

bird-PL-TC this place-at live-IN-DC

This kind of bird lives in this place.

(2) i-kes-un ttokkath-un conglyu-uy kkoch-i an-i-ta.

this-thing-TC the same-PM kind-of

flower-NM not-be-DC

This is not the same kind of flower.

(3) na-nun talu-n conglyu~uy kes-ul kac-ko·siph-ta.

I-TC other-PM kind-of

thing-AC have-want-DC

I want to have another kind of thing.

(4) yeki-ey-nun hanltwulmyech conglyu-uy namwu-ka iss-ta.
here-at-TC one/two/some kind-of tree-NM there is-DC
There is/are one/two/some kind(s) of tree in here.

(5) yeki-ey-nun manh/motu-(u)n conglyu-uy namwu-tul-i iss-ta.

here-at-TC many/all-PM

kind-of

tree-PL-NM there is-DC

There are many/all kinds of trees in here.

The Korean exponent conglyu/KIND is nominal and it can be used to specify another noun by taking the postposed attributive particle -uy as shown above. All the proposed
combinations are found to be possible in Korean.
225

Chapter 3
The meaning of the prime KIND!conglyu(uy) in the following sentences is claimed
to be universal:

(6)a X- nun tongmwu/;..uy han conglyu-ta.
X-TC animal-GEN one kind-DC
Xis a kind of animal.
(6)b X- nun tongmwul-uy ii cong-i-ta.
X-TC animal-GEN one kind-be-DC
X is a kind of animal.
(6)c X-nun ii cong-uy tongmwul-i-ta.
X-TC one kind-ATR animal-be-DC
X is a kind of animal.
The meanings of the sentences (6)a, (6)b, and (6)c are the same. The fact that there is more than one choice of expression for the same meaning of a kind is partly due to the double numeral system (from PK and SK) as well as the flexibility of constituent order
in Korean. The expression 'ilcong '89 (lit. a kind of) in (1 )c is widely used as an idiomatic and fixed expression which does not semantically differ from 'han (one) conglyu (kind of)' in meaning.90
3.16.2.2 pwupwun(uy)IPART OF
The prime PART (OF) is claimed to be combined with determiners and quantifiers and also with GOOD, BAD, BIG, and SMALL with which KIND (OF) cannot possibly combine. This claim is tested and found to be valid as follows:

3.16.3 Summary

Two concepts, KIND (OF) and PART (OF), are tested in this section in terms of both lexicon and syntax. As a result, it has been found that they have equivalent concepts in
Korean, which are conglyu(uy) and pwupwun(uy), respectively. The proposed
combinations in relation to these concepts are found to be consistent with the universal grammar proposed in the NSM theory.

3.17 Similarity: LIKE
3.17.1 Lexicon
The · concept of similarity encoded in LIKE was added to the list of the universal semantic primitives in 1996. This concept plays a crucial role in explicating the
227

Chapter 3
prototypes of human language and cognition. The proposed meaning of the prime can
be identified by the canonical sentences as follows:
You are like me. I am not like other people. This is like lilac (or: any plant), but it is not lilac. At a time like this; In a place like this; He did it like this: ... It happened like this. These two things were moving like this. She said it like this: ... It is good ifyou do good things for other people, like you want other people to
do good things for you.
As the translational equivalent of the meaning of LIKE in such sentences above, I propose kath-.91 This word is used in Korean as follows:

(1) na-nun ku il-ul

i (-wa) kath-i hay-ss-ta.

I-TC that event-AC this (-with) like-ADV do-PST-DC

I did it like this.

(2) na-nun i salamtul (kwa) kath-ci anh-ta.

I-TC this person-PL

like-NOM not-DC

I am not like these people.

(3) na-nun X kath-un salam-ul al-ko·siph-ta.
I-TC X like-PM person-AC know-want-DC
I want to know someone like X.

(4) i kkoch-un cangmi kath-ci-man cangmi-ka an-i-ta.
this flower..:Tc rose like-NOM-but rose-NM not-be-DC
This flower is like a rose, but it is not a rose.

As shown in examples above, the proposed Korean counterpart kath- can be used either attributively as in (3), predicatively as in (2), or adverbially as in (1 ), by taking different suffixes or sentence markers according to syntactic roles. The word kath- can be used either with or without the relational particle '-(k) wa' as shown in the examples above. However, this particle should not be used when kath- is used attributively since this may cause meaning ambiguity between 'sameness' and 'similarity' as discussed in 3.3.1.2. The absence of the particle '-(k)wa' can keep the meaning of 'similarity' distinct from 'sameness'. It has been pointed out that the syntactic difference can be the cue for

Chapter 3
distinguishing two different meanings of kath-. Examples below show the difference in syntactic construction of kath- when it expresses either similarity or sameness.

(6) na-eykey-to kath-un il-i

Example (6) and (9) are interpreted as 'sameness', while (7) and (10) express

(In a restaurant, two people are ordering their meals)

maple tree

When the intended meaning needs to be expressed precisely (1)c is likely to be chosen
perhaps with extra information, such as saykkkal-i (in colour), or moyang-i (in shape) (i.e. ikesun cekeskwa moyangi pisushata: This thing is similar to that thing in shape). The word pisusha- (similar) can be regarded as a lexical variant when LIKE is used

It is claimed that the primitive LIKE can form an attributive expression that can appear as part of a substantive phrase. The possibility of forming something like this as a part of a substantive phrase is fully supported as seen in (3) and (4) below:

It is proposed that the combination of LIKE and THIS can occur as an adverbial 'manner adjunct' with a range of predicates. This is tested as follows:

(6) ilehkey hay/sayngkakhay/nukki-(e)la.

The claim is supported as seen in (5) above by combining directly i/THIS and kathi-
(LIKE + adverbialiser). However, a more common and idiomatic expression for this
combination is ilehkey (like this) as shown in (6) above. This expression can be
considered as an optional portmanteau of the combination of LIKE and THIS. There is
no meaning difference between the two expressions: i kathi and ilehkey as shown in (5),

Finally, the Korean exponent kath- is tested as to whether it can introduce a kind
of modifying clause, an 'analogy' clause as below:

(13) i salam-i na-eykey mwenka nappu-n il-ul ha-n kes

this person-NM I-DAT

something bad-PM thing-AC do-PM thing

like-ADV I-too this person-DAT something bad-PM thing-AC do-want-DC
I want to do something bad to this person like this person did something bad to
me.

The claim is valid in Korean with a different clause order. The analogy clause is
introduced preceding the word kathi (like-adverbialiser). The particle -to appears to be
obligatory in this analogy construction in order to express the equivalent meaning of the canonical sentence.

3.17.3 Summary
The prime LIKE is tested in this section in terms of lexicon and syntax. As a result, it
has been found that the equivalent concept is encoded in kath- (and pisusha- when it is used predicatively), and that the syntactic characteristics of kath- are consistent with the
proposed universal grammar.

232

3.18 Conclusion

Chapter 3

In this chapter, the elements of the current inventory of the NSM primitives have been attested with their combinatorics. As a result of testing, the hypotheses of the NSM theory have been generally upheld. All the putative semantic primitives are found to have conceptual equivalents in Korean and their combinatorial characteristics are also found to be consistent with the proposed universal grammar. There are three cases in which the hypothesis of the NSM theory were not fully supported in the context of Korean: the lack of a generic term for indefinite plurality expressed by the English primitive SOME, and the incompatibilities of the quasi-substantive use of i/THIS and the predicate use of coh-!GOOD with personal substantives.
Regarding the primitive concept of SOME, it has been found that the two different
expressions, myech and etten -tu!, can express in collaboration the proposed primitive · meaning of SOME depending on context: myech for indefinite quantifier and etten -tu!
for existential partitive. With respect to the quasi-substantive use of i/THIS, although a bare i can be used
as a substantive this use is limited to the anaphorical use. When i/THIS is used to function as a substantive, the overt use of nominal is required. The restriction of the predicate use of coh-lGOOD with personal substantives in Korean is not due to the syntactic property of coh-!GOOD but to the possible ambiguity of meaning resulting from the polysemous quality of this word. While the word coh-!GOOD can be used predicatively with any substantives to express positive evaluation, it would be better to be used attributively taking a following modified noun to avoid any possible ambiguity of meaning between the proposed primitive meaning and 'psychological attraction'. Therefore, it is not about the impossibility of using a positive evaluative primitive as a predicate, but about finding a clear way of expressing the proposed primitive meaning.
There was only one word found that expresses two primitive meanings, similarity
and sameness: kath-. The two meanings, however, have been found to be distinguished
by the different syntactic frames. Different kinds of allolexes of different exponents are proposed on the basis of
testing meanings between different variants, and the conditions for the variants are also discussed. The various portmanteaus of a number of exponents, either optional or obligatory, are identified.
It is observed that there are more natural ways of expressing the proposed combinations. For instance, the predicates THINK and SAY prefer an adverbial

233

Chapter 3
complement rather than the combination of attributive and nominal in a phrase like
people will think/say good/bad things about you. The expression of cohkey/nappukey sayngkakha- (think well/badly o:f) or malha- (say) is natural and idiomatic. Also the alternative way of expressing feel something good/bad is pointed out (i.e. nukkimul kackeytoyta : 'become to have a feeling', or nukkimi tulta: a feeling came into) as a
natural means of expression. The trading between the pragmatically preferred expressions and those most accurate from a semantic point of view should be considered in order to improve the readability of the Korean-based NSM.
It is also noted that canonical sentences play a critical role in identifying the proposed primitive meanings. However, there are some primitives, for instance, i/rHIS
or ttokkath-lTHE SAME, or myen/IF (also IF and NOT) for which the canonical sentences
(of the sentence level) are not sufficient to identify the equivalent counterparts in Korean. These primitives require a context that consists of more than one sentence so that the intended meaning is clearly expressed. This points to a need for having canonical sentences with more extensive context.
Generally the proposed primitives are identified with no significant problems in Korean with the given guidelines and developed tactics, which enables this research to be cross-linguistically comparable. The proposed Korean exponents based on testing against canonical sentences are presented in Table 2 on the next page in which only chief exponents are presented due to space limits. The identified allolexes and portmanteaus are presented in the summary of each section of this chapter. __

Chapter 4
Chapter 4 Textual Structure of the Korean
Metalanguage
4.1 Introduction
In this chapter, the textual structure of NSM explications based on the Korean metalanguage is illustrated through the practice of transposing two semantic texts composed originally in an English based metalanguage. This attempt aims to test the hypothesis of isomorphism of the metalanguage by proving inter-translatability between NSM texts based on different languages.
Chapter 3 focused mainly on identifying Korean exponents of the proposed semantic primitives and on testing the full sets of hypotheses of the NSM theory in relation to their inherent grammar. Although the research findings generally support the hypotheses of the NSM theory in terms of lexicalisation and combinability, the test has been conducted at the single sentence level (mostly through canonical sentences) and not at the integrated textual structure level. Since explication in the NSM method involves the paraphrase of the meanings of lexical items, grammatical categories, and units of discourse (texts), the textual structure of the semantic texts is as important as the combinability of the primitives in order to represent the conceptual structure adequately. Text types that have been explicated include conversational routines and strategies, proverbs, parables and hortatory texts (cf. e.g., Ameka, 1987, 1994; Wierzbicka, 1998b, 2001 b; Goddard and Wierzbicka, 1997). Explications consist of varying numbers of semantic components and the overall coherence and ordering of them are of critical importance (Goddard and Wierzbicka, 2002b: 79-81 ).
Therefore, it is necessary to test whether the Korean-based metalanguage has the same expressive power as other language-based NSMs92 at the textual level. Without a proof of this kind the NSM texts cannot be cross-linguistically comparable. For this purpose two semantic texts originally composed in an English-based NSM (slightly adjusted here in the light of the latest research, as presented in Goddard and Wierzbicka
92 Theoretically NSM explications must be able to be translated into any natural language in the world.
236

Chapter 4
(eds.), 2002) are transposed into a Korean-based NSM: the Good Samaritan script (from the Bible) and the middle way of Chinese Philosophy (from traditional Chinese teachm. g).93
