4.4 Discussion of the Korean textual structure -
The two texts are transposed into the Korean-based NSM, as shown above, without any loss or alteration of the meaning of the two texts. There are, however, a few issues that deserve comments.
4.4.1 Pronouns
Korean generally allows the omission of the first and second person pronouns where there is no risk of misinterpretation, and in fact the overt use of them is marked, as has already been mentioned in section 1.4.1. They can either be omitted as the first appearance of the second person pronoun in (c) of Text A or put in brackets as in the second appearance of the same pronoun in the same sentence. The later case is also found in line (b) or (e) of Text A. The two cases are slightly different in terms of the number of occurrences in the same sentence. In case (c) the second pronoun is omitted since its overt use within a single sentence would cause pragmatic markedness, while in case (b) or (e) the second pronoun is indicated in brackets because there is not such a
241

Chapter 4
strong pragmatic interference, at least not so much that it should be omitted. The Korean assumption that a sentence like (c) is automatically addressed to an interlocutor can perhaps be likened to the English imperative, where "Come in!" without the second
perso11 pronoun is unmarked, and "You come in!" is distinctly marked. Without the
second person pronoun in (b), the sentence reads more naturally, although there is an implication that the sentence message is of general value, perhaps for everyone. Due to the nature of the text as a religious teaching addressed generally, this implication does not seem to clash with the intended meaning of the particular text.
On the other hand, the explicit use of the pronoun brings a more direct tone addressed to the reader. In this case a slight pragmatic interference results from the use of the non-polite form (addressing reader). However, if the speaker and the addressee are known to be in an appropriately close relationship or the speaker's social status or age is known to be higher than that of the addressee (e.g. ' Jesus speaking to individuals' or 'a teacher speaking to a student'), the use of 'ne' (non-polite form) would not cause
pragmatic awkwardness. Wherever pronouns can be omitted without conveying meaning difference, I am
inclined to put them in brackets, so that the texts can be read in two ways: naturally and semantically clearly. In cases like (c) where the same pronoun occurs more than once in the same sentence, it seems to be better to omit it as otherwise it is awkward and
marked. One more issue related to the requirements of the English-based NSM which
leaves gaps in the Korean version seems to be the so called dummy subject, for instance
the use of 'it' in (c) of Text A, It is good if you do it. This use of 'it' occurs again in line
(h) of Text B (it is good if I think like this). There are two ways to deal with this phenomenon in Korean as below: ·

(1) ku-il-ul

ha-myen (ku-kes-un) coh-ta.

this-thing-AC do-if

(this-thing-TC) good-DC

If you do it, (it is) good.

(2) kulehkey ha-nun kes-un coh-ta.
like this do-PM thing-TC good-DC
Doing like this is (a) good (thing). Or It is good to do this.

The transposed Korean sentence (1) does not seem to differ semantically from that of English, and reads more naturally without the part indicated in brackets. Therefore, the dummy subject can be dealt with in the same way as for the redundant pronouns.
242

Chapter 4
However, (2) is stylistically preferable and its naturalness seems to be related to the omission ofIF/(-(u)myen in such a context. This is a sentence in Korean without the
conditional primitive -(u)myen. The meaning of IF in this context appears different from
that of the other canonical sentences: i.e. if you do this, people will think bad things
about you. Without IF the meaning of the latter is unclear, while the omission of it in such sentences as (2) brings no difference in meaning in Korean. The difference appears to be due to the fact that there is no causal relationship between the protasis (if you do it) and .the apotheosis (it is good) in the case of (2), whereas in the other canonical sentences there is a causal as well as a conditional relationship (e.g. between 'you doing this' and 'people thinking bad things about you') (Harkins, personal communication). In fact, in current NSM theory, it is claimed that the 'GOOD/BAD IF' construction is treated somewhat differently from the meaning of IF in other clauses. In other words, this kind of construction is not a true IF-clause but rather a kind of clausal complement of GOOD/BAD. Therefore, an interpretation like (2) is close to current thinking of the NSMtheory.
Furthermore, the manner of the action, kulehkey (the combination of LIKE and THIS) rather than kukesul (this thing) is a conventionalised expression in Korean.94 This convention has been noticed in several other languages (e.g. the Arrernte alakenhe
Harkins and Wilkins, 1994). It has been reported that in Arrernte, although in a context like (2) the combination of LIKE and THIS is the natural-sounding expression, it remains entirely possible to distinguish between DO THIS (THING) and DO LIKE THIS. This is parallel to Korean. However, this matter does not significantly interfere with the comprehension of line (c) of Text A or line (h) of Text B (i.e. it is good if you do this or · it is good if I think like this). The latter is exemplified below showing the two different ways of expressing the same thing-in Korean.

(3)a nay-ka llehkey sayngkakha-myen (ku-kes-un) coh-ta.

I-NM like this think-if

(that-thing-TC) good-DC

It is good if I think like this:

(3)b nay-ka llehkey sayngkakha~nun kes-un coh-ta.

I-NM like this think-PM

thing-TC good-DC

Thinking like this is (a) good (thing).

Since the semantic difference between the two possibilities: with or without 'if in line (c) of Text A, has not been tested thoroughly here, I will leave the strictly transposed
94 Instead of i, ku is used to adjust to the Korean textual requirement. However, the meaning of ku is defined via i in 3.3 .1.
243

Chapter 4
line from the original English version for the time being, leaving the matter of dealing with the use of IF in such contexts as (2) in Korean to further study.

4.4.2 Predicate use of coh-/GOOD

The problematic situation of using coh-!GOOD as a predicate has been discussed in 3.5.1. Between the two possibilities for line (f) of Text B, as presented below, I have chosen (4)b due to the risk of causing ambiguity between this is good and 'I like this', even though (4)a can also be understood as equivalent to (4)b. The possible Korean interpretation of (4)a is to assume it has an elliptical first person subject, giving the interpretation 'I like this'. Nonetheless, (4)a literally means only This (thing) is good. But because of the possibility of mis-interpretation in everyday usage, (4)b may be seen as a preferable formulation for the Korean NSM. The additional THING in (4)b introduces no new semantic elements; all that is added is an element that agrees with THIS THING, so there should be no obstacle to this formulation from the NSM perspective.

(4)a i-kes-un

colt-ta

this-thing-TC good-DC

This is good.

(4)b i-kes-un

coh-un il-i-ta

this-thing-TC good-PM thing-be-DC

This is good.

4.4.3 The prime THIS in Korean
The details related to the three different Korean demonstratives are discussed in 3.3 .1. The word i is proposed as the Korean exponent of THIS, and two other words, ku and ce are analysed via the prime i. In Text A, ku is chosen as the translational counterpart of
THIS rather than the identified Korean exponent i. The purpose of the alteration is to
enhance the readability of the Korean text. Through empirical testing of the meanings of
Korean demonstratives I proposed to use ku in place of i when there is textual
requirement. However, this should be allowed only in conjunction with a careful

244

Chapter 4
meaning analysis of ku, as detailed in 3.3.1 where this matter and the advantage of
focusing on naturalness are discussed with regard to this prime. One more issue related to the prime THIS in Korean seems to be the necessity of
pv~rt u;,e of Piiµb~t.ruitive,s tog'$th01· with this prime . Sine~ t,h e Vet¢' o f th¢ ql.!Mi,,.~4.b~t~1Jl.v~
THIS in Korean is not supported, as shown in 3.3 .2, the substantive has to be used explicitly with this prime in order for the resulting text to be grammatical. For instance, in Text A, line (k) contains the prime THIS used to refer to the previous sentences: God
wants this (to happen). This prime has to be transposed into 'ikes' (this thing) rather
than a bare i/rHIS, which is a bound morpheme. In the same fashion, another prime THE SAME also requires a following substantive. In line (i) of Text A, the sentence It is
good if you want to do the same is transposed into Korean as ' ... ttokathun ilul hakilul... ' (... do the same thing ... ) to make the sentence grammatical. Alternatively the Korean exponent 'ttokkath-' (THE SAME) can be used adverbially as in the Korean
version of the metalanguage. This sounds much more idiomatic than the first option:
'ttokkathi hakilul... ' (do the same).
Finally, the overt use of 'it' (THIS THING) in one sentence can sometimes cause confusion. When 'it' is used to refer to something previously spelt out as in (c) of Text A: If you can do it, it is good if you do it, it can be difficult to know what exactly 'it' refers to. The second 'it' does not cause a problem since it can be dealt with as mentioned previously (which is by putting it in brackets or even omitting it). If the first 'it' and the last 'it' refer to the same thing, which is something good to this person, the explicit repetition of the phrase may be helpful to clarify the meaning of thaf sentence, along the lines of If you can do good things to this person, it is good if you do it.

(5) ku-salam-eykey coh-un - il-ul ha-l·swu·iss-ta-myen
that-person-DAT good-PM thing-AC do-can-de-if

kulehkey ha-myen coh-un il-i-ta

like that do-if

good-PM thing-be-DC

If you can do good things to this person, it is good if you do it.

A more idiomatic Korean sentence would be something like 'ha! swu isstamyen kulehkey hanun kesi cohta' (if you can, it is good to do like this), which reads naturally.
One of the recent hypotheses concerning the inventory of primitives of NSM has to do with the gap where there is no deictic counterpart to SOMETHING (corresponding to INOU for SOMEONE, HERE for SOMEWHERE, and NOW for SOMETIME). Goddard
(2002d) has observed that the equation 'it = this + thing' could result in referential

245

Chapter 4
confusion in some contexts. In other words, there are some contexts in which this thing could not be substituted for 'it', while the replacement is possible in most cases. For instance, the meaning of the second 'it' in example (6) is not the same as this thing, while the first is.
(6) Jonh-un Mary-ka i il-ul hay-ss-ta-ko sayngkakha-n-ta.
John-NM Mary-NM this thing-AC do-PST-DC-Q think-IN-DC
John thinks that Mary did it.
haciman na-nun mit-ci·anh-nun-ta
but I-TC believe not-IN-DC
but I don't believe it.
The need for including the deictic use of 'it' seems to arise. However, the
counterpart of this use of it in Korean is not explicit. The situational it seems to be taken
for granted without being explicit in Korean conversation as shown in (6). It is unclear whether this means that the newly proposed contextual deictic prime IT can be transposed in the Korean-based NSM as something understood by context without being expressed. The question requires further exploration.
4.4.4. Prime nukki-/FEEL
In relation to the Korean exponent nukki-/FEEL, two possibilities are noted when transposing line (e) and (k) of Text B. The equivalent meaning of the sentence_! will not feel something very good/bad can be expressed either way: (i) nukki- (feel) is used taking the combination of coh-un nukkim (good feeling) as a complement, while (ii) kipwun (feeling), (the nominal variant of nukki-lFEEL) is used with the predicate use of
GOOD or BAD. Compare the two examples below:
(7)a na-nun acwu coh-un nukkim-ul nukki-ci-nun anh-ul-kes· i-ta.
I-TC very good-PM feeling-AC feel-NOM-TC not-PRO-be-DC
I will not feel a very good (feeling).
(7)b na-nun kipwun-i acwu coh-ci-nun anh-ul·kes·i-ta.
I-TC feeling-NM very good-PM-TC not-PRO-be-DC
I will not feel very good.
Although the meaning of (7)a is semantically clear, pragmatic preference falls on (7)b. I have chosen (7)a in Text B (putting (7)b adjacently in brackets) assuming there is no meaning difference. However, one may argue that these two are not the same
246

. Chapter 4
semantically, that they have a difference in nuances. Nonetheless, it is not clear whether this difference is paraphrasable. Although the choice between the two has to be validated in further study, the two options are treated as semantic equivalents for the time being.
Assuming that (7)b can be used when transposing into Korean text, one more
issue is worth mentioning. The topic contrast marker -nun after coh-ci (good) (shown in
square brackets in (7)c to distinguish it fom1 the topic marker after 'I') plays a crucial role since without it an ambiguity of meaning arises:
(7)c na-nun kipwun-i acwu coh-ci[nun} anh-ul·kes·i-ta.
I-TC feeling-NM very good-[TC] not-PRO-be-DC
I will not feel very good. Or I will feel very not good (very bad).
The two possible meanings are incompatible and the ambiguity results from the fact that
acwu (very) as an adverb can modify either coh-ci ·anh (not good) as a unitary unit or only the word coh- (good). Accordingly the meanings become very different. Using the topic marker -nun to separate coh- (good) and anh- (not) solves this problem, since acwu can modify only coh- and not anh-(not). This specific feature has to be accounted
for whenever this kind of combination occurs. In addition there are more idiomatic expressions than (7)a as follows:
(8) na-nun acwu coh-un nukkim-i tul-ci-nun anh-l·kes·i-ta.
I-TC very good-PM feeling-AC enter-NOM-TC not -PRO-be-DC
I will not feel something very good.
The use of nukki- in combination_with tu!- (enter into) seems to suggest that the feel
something in Korean implies passivity so much that it is expressed as ' a feeling coming into a person'. This may result from a different perspective of Koreans on ' feeling ' as implying something happening inside a person' s heart rather than an action of someone. The possibilities of subtle meaning differences between (8) and (7)a-b should be examined closely in further study.
247

Chapter 4
4.4.5 lndigenising a Korean NSM (case markers, punctuation, honorification)
Some issues related to the naturalness of the Korean semantic metalanguage are dealt
with in this section: the choice of a case marker, the use of honorification, and the meaning of NSM punctuation. First, there are cases where the wrong choice of a case marker brings awkwardness in Korean sentences. Note the following examples:
(9)a ne-to ttokkath-un il-ul ha-ko·siph·e·ha-myen
you-too the same-TC thing-AC do-want-if
ku-kes-un coh-ta
that-thing-TC good-DC
It is good if you too want to do the same
(9)b ney-ka ttokkath-un il-ul ha-ko·siphe·ha-myen
you-NM the same-TC thing-AC do-want-if
kukes-un coh-ta
that-thing-TC good-DC
It is good if you want to do the same ·
Using either -to (too) or -ka (nominative) out of context causes no problem. However, in a given context like line (i) of Text A, -to has to be chosen instead of -ka not only because it makes the text flow smoothly, but also because using -ka seems to suggest a
subtle meaning difference. Although the semantic quality of different case markers is not dealt with in the present research, it appears that they are related to the sentence meaning when they are transposed into a Korean based-metalanguage at textual level. Systematic study of the meaning of different cases markers in Korean seems to be required for a fully functional Korean metalanguage.
The use of honorification in line (k) of Text A where God is referred to, is noted.
The insertion of the honorific verbal suffix -si- in wenha- (WANT) is made to prevent
pragmatic interference. This point has already been discussed in Chapter 1 as well as in 3.8.1, for the lack of honorification in such a context is pragmatically marked. The insertion of the verbal suffix carries the notion of respect towards God in addition to the sentence meaning.
The most unnatural expression in Korean seems to be line (k) of Text A: God wants this to happen. Although this is transposed into Korean straightforwardly, the
alternative is indicated in brackets 'hanunimun ilul wenhasinta' (God wants this), on the
assumption that there is no meaning difference between the two sentences. The
248

Chapter 4
awkwardness seems simply to result from the unfamiliarity of that kind of expression in Korean. The use of the Korean exponent of HAPPEN, ilena- or sayngki-, in such a context is unconventional. The question whether there is a meaning difference, however
subtle, between God wants this and God wants this to happen should be answered in
order to substitute the former for the latter. Finally, no problem is found in using NSM punctuation marks, such as the colon
(:) and the quotation mark ("_") in the transposed Korean texts. Indenting is used in both texts to indicate that the speaker is 'saying or thinking about something'. This is consistent with usage in the context of English. The semantic components are arranged in order in both texts in a way that reflects the speakers' cognitive structure, and no problem with the order of components is found in the two Korean-NSM texts despite the difference in word order between the two languages.
4.4 Conclusion
As a result of the exercise of transposing two selected texts into a Korean-based NSM, no significant problem has arisen. Consequently, the expressive power of the Koreanbased NSM is found to be consistent with that of the English version. However, several Korean-language specific features that are worth noting are pointed out.
The main concern in this exercise of transposing NSM texts into the Korean-based metalanguage seems to be the naturalness of the Korean-based metalanguage-within the formal framework of the NSM theory. Although naturalness of the metalanguage should not raise significant problems as long as the text is semantically clear, the conventionalised way of expressi9n in Korean may certainly be helpful for ordinary readers of the Korean language.
249

Chapter 5
Chapter 5 Explicating Korean Concepts within the NSM Framework
5.1 Introduction
In this chapter the possibility of using the NSM method for semantic analysis of Korean
concepts will be explored on the basis of the results of testing the NSM hypotheses. Although the Korean-based metalanguage has been established in Chapter 3, and its inter-translatability and textual structure have been displayed in Chapter 4, it has not yet been applied to semantic analysis. Thus, this chapter has two aims: (i) to explore the utility of the NSM approach in the semantic analysis of Korean concepts, and (ii) to test the expressive power of the Korean-based NSM explications. For this purpose five concepts are selected: two concepts are parts of a person and three are anger-like emotion tem1s. The explications are presented both in the English and the Korean metalanguages.
Knowing other cultures and understanding their cultural values and__norms is critical in this era. The close link between the life of a society and the lexicon of the language spoken by that society has been validated (cf. e.g., Sapir, 1951; Wierzbicka 1997a). However, without a rigorous linguistic framework for semantic analysis, the exact meaning of culture specific concepts cannot be represented to outsiders of the culture in question because different cultures develop different psychology and different conceptualisational patterns. Consequently these concepts cannot be translated crosslinguistically without a change in their meaning. However, the NSM approach has been found to be a practical semantic analysis tool for culturally salient concepts since it is independent of language and culture specificity (cf. e.g., Wierzbicka, 1999: Goddard, 1996a,· 1996b, 1997a, 2001a; Harkins and Wierzbicka (eds.), 2001; Hasada, 2000, Ye; 2000, see also 2.5 for detail).
Among the different perspectives on the study of meaning, the NSM theory assumes that there are semantic invariants that are understood and communicated among the speakers of the language in question, and that meaning analysis has to be
250

Chapter 5
able to articulate these invariants clearly enough to insiders as well as to outsiders of the language. The NSM approach is interested in capturing the necessary semantic
components of the defined concept and paraphrasing them into a simple language that is self-explanatory.
In this chapter, an attempt is made to paraphrase highly distinctive Korean concepts with the NSM method. The aim of this attempt is to enable outsiders to see the cognitive structure of the analysed concepts through the same window as native speakers of the language in question. Without an appropriate and systematic analysis tool, the meaning of cultural concepts will not be adequately comprehended by outsiders. Among others, Poitras (1982: 15) acknowledged the difficulty or impossibility of translating Korean concepts after his attempt to translate modern Korean poems into English:
The Korean language is highly onomatopoetic, hierarchical in structure, and exceedingly subtle with its distinctions in description. The task of · translation is so formidable that many Korean commentators have, perhaps half in jest, concluded that it should not even be attempted.
In order to explicate selected lexical items, examples of natural linguistic usage have been collected from different sources: journals, popular songs, novels, n~wspaper articles and the Korean cor· pora.95 The examples used all reflect contemporary usage of the defined words among speakers of the Korean language in South Korea. Speculation on the basis of linguistic evidence is essential for semantic analysis using --the NSM approach. The collected examples are examined first to establish preliminary explications based on the linguistic use of native speakers. As a second step, the examples are tested by being _substituted for the defined expressions. All the explications in this chapter are proposed through this process. Nonetheless, these explications are left open to further discussion or modification as it is possible that a counterexample may stand out. The aim is for explications to be open to improvement and be compatible with all usages.

95 The details can be found in 'Index of the used sources'.

251

Chapter 5
5.2 The Meaning of maum and kasum
In Korean there are two words that are frequently used in daily conversation as well as
in literature: maum and kasum. The frequency of use of these two words is particularly
high in the everyday life of Korean people. The concepts are semantically distinct, although their translations into English sometimes overlap. For example, maum and kasum are both translated as 'heart' in English as seen in the following sentences:

nay maum-un hoswuyo (my heart i~ a lake) 96 nay kasum-ey cokoman selwumuy dengi (a lump of grief stuck in my heart)97

Koreans express their feelings and thoughts by referring to one of these two expressions. However, the difference in meaning between the two words is not shown in dictionaries as the following examples show:

maum: I .mind; spirit; mentality; an idea; a thought 2. heart; feeling mood; 3. sincerity wholeheartedness 4. consideration; sympathy 5. attention 6. will; intention; inclination

kasum: 1. the breast; the chest; the thorax, the bust; the bosom 2. the heart 3. maum; one's heart: one' s mind; feelings

(Dong-A, 1996)

maum (o} g): 1. an entity for all kinds of mental activities including thinking, feeling etc
2. a personality 3. a mental activity for judging right and wrong 4. an inner thought that is not expressed 5. some feeling that can be changed according to the situation 6. a thoughtful attitude towards other people 7. an interest in something

kasum (7}~ ): 1. the body part between neck and belly

2. euphemistic expression for breast

3. heart or lung

4. maum or thought

(MT: K-> E, Kumsengphan, 1991)

As seen above, the meanings of the two words are not clear. Moreover, kasum is defined in terms of maum in both bilingual (Korean-English) as well as in Korean

96 A title of a poem written by Kim Tong-Myong and translated by O'Rourke (1982: 99) 97 A part of a poem written by Kim Sowol (translated by Kim, 1975: 40-41)

equivalents are sometimes heart, mind, or spirit, which are all very distinctive and specific Anglo cultural concepts.

The examples above suggest strongly that the two Korean words do not have equivalents in English and perhaps in other languages as well (although the closest ones are selected depending on contexts and translators). Since these two words are the names of the two essential entities responsible for Korean .psychological activities or/and emotional experiences it is critical to know what exactly they mean from a Korean perspective.
5.2.1 maum
The high frequency of use of maum indicates that this concept is at the centre of Korean life. The importance of this concept is reflected in various combinations of this word

• maumchelem kansahankey epsta: A person's maum can be changed very easily according to self-interest.

The word maum is used symmetrically to mom (body). The customary contrast between maum and mom in colloquial everyday use, as well as in the literature of various fields including psychology, suggests that they are considered as two parts of a person. For instance, there is a huge body of literature written on the subject 'mom and maum' (body and maum). The following are some selected book titles that have been published in Korea in the last decade (1990-2002)99•
• thunthunhan mom kenkanghan maum: The healthy body and the healthy maum • momkwa maumuy sayngmwulhak: The biology of body and maum • momuy poyak maumuy poyak: Healthy medicine for body and maum • maumkwa momuy pyeng: The diseases of body and maum • maumulo momul kochinta: Cure the body with maum • maumkwa mom: The maum and the body • momkwa mawnuy kwankye: The relationship between the maum and the body
Owing to the symmetrical use of maum and mom (body), one may think that maum is something similar to the English concept of 'mind' because of the predominant Anglo symmetry of 'body' and 'mind'. Also the conventional translation of maum into either the 'mind' or the '-heart' has had an influence on many people so that they are not aware of the function of maum as the same as either of them. In order to ascertain what the concept of maum is for Koreans and what maum does in a person, one needs to closely examine exhaustive examples of its use.
The concept maum is used to describe various psychological activities that cannot be experienced in the body. The symmetry of mom (body) and maum suggests that Koreans think that maum is one of the two parts of a human. The part maum exists exclusively in human beings not in other living creatures. Koreans believe that while mom (body) belongs to the physical, material world, maum is invisible and intangible and exists as a psychological entity. However, maum is conceptualised as being located somewhere inside one's chest area. These aspects can be captured in the following components:

In ordinary times, (you) move the body, speak with the mouth, and think with the maum.. ·. (MT: K->E, KAIST)
However, the maum is not the primary part for thinking. There is another body part to which all the cognitive or intellectual functions are ascribed; the meli (brain; lit. head). Therefore, one can think not because of maum but can think in maum if one wants to think sincerely, and as it were morally. Since the meli can think but can also miss out things such as moral or ethical aspects of a matter, thinking in the maum or with the maum (presumably in collaboration with meli) is highly valued in Korean culture, as shown in examples (15) and (19). Thinking only in the meli (brain) is not as good as thinking in collaboration with the maum, since the meli can calculate things according to self-interest. Whereas one can think without being contaminated by egoistic calculation if one tries to think with the maum. It is unclear whether this suggests that the maum is a primary good part of a person that enables a person to be altruistic. More examples are required in order to determine whether this aspect is a part of the meaning of maum. Nonetheless, maum includes being able to think and this can be accounted for with the general positive evaluation of the community: when a person thinks about something, it is good if this person thinks in this part
Furthermore, Koreans believe that they can act with maum when they intend to carry out various things decently, including occupational duties, craft, or even daily routines. People evaluate the result positively when someone does things with maum or by putting their maum in their deeds. People often think that good results come from one's maum not from experience or skill. Therefore, having maum through the whole process of the action is highly valued and regarded as genuine and noble. One can do many things with maum, including listening, making things (including crafts), or forgiving, as seen in the examples below:

According to the above examples, doing things with maum seems to mean concentrating all one's psychological energy and activating one's ability of feeling and thinking. However, this is not appropriate in cases involving immorality or inhuman deeds. Not all kinds of action can be done with maum. One would not use the expression 'maumul tahay totwukcilul hata' (stealing with one's maum) or maumul tahay ssawuta' (fighting with one's maum). Therefore, good will comes from one's maum, and presumably because of this intrinsic aspect of maum people positively value actions done with the maum. Anything that comes from the maum is evaluated as valuable and genuine. The expression 'maum kiphun kosulopwutheuy ... ' (from the deep inside of maum) may occur with appreciation, apologies, regrets, praises, respect, and forgiveness. When someone does any action or performance with maum, people generally recognise it positively. This aspect can be captured in the component: when a person does something, it is good if this person thinks at the same time in this part like this : "I want to do this thing well".
Someone's maum is often described with value terms such as good or bad, beautiful or ugly, decent .or indecent, and kind or unkind, as seen in the various examples presented previously and below:

People's maum can be sometimes good and sometimes bad. If one maintains good maum all the time one is considered as a virtuous and righteous person. There are two points worth mentioning in relation to components (h) and (i). One
may argue that these are not a part of the meaning of maum but that of other expressions involving maum and various actions or thinking. There are expressions such as 'maumulo semkita or senchalhata' (serve someone with maum or examine one's conscience with maum) that are already metaphoric on their own. One may take the view that these expressions have to be explicated separately from maum, and a component like (h) or (i) can be included in their explications. This possibility is worth exploring in more depth in further study. Explications in relation to the concept of maum can be elaborated on the basis of comprehensive studies with an extensive range of examples. However, at this s~age, I am inclined to include the two components (h) and (i) as a part of the meaning of maum on the basis of the examples that are presented previously as well as the consistent combinatorial pattern between maum and words for
thinking or doing in Korean. Another aspect that should be improved is the phrasing of component (h). In terms
of grammar of the metalanguage, the valency option 'think in' is not proposed as universal. One can consider the expression 'think in this part' as something like 'when a person thinks in part, this person can think that s/he is doing something with a part of the body', which reflect the Korean point of view in relation to maum. Koreans use great deal of maum in relation to 'think' it is important to capture that aspect in the explicaiton. However, this expression should be improved with better pharsing since the

Chapter 5
valency option of THJNK does not include think in in the current NSM, which means that it can be be translated into all languages.
5.2.2 kasum
When Koreans refer to their feelings the word kasum seems to be one of the most frequently used words. As mentioned previously, maum and kasum are used interchangeably in many contexts. However, there are instances where kasum is not likely to be replaced by maum. The following book titles (published recently in Korea) show contexts where kasum prevails over maum.
• nelohaye wunun kasumi issta: There is a kasum which is _crying because of you • hangsang kasum ttellinun cheumipnita: It is always like the start when one's
kasum trembles • silin kasumeyto kkochun phiko: A flower blossoms in a chilled kasum • nelul sayngkakhamyen kkok kumankhum kasumi silita: When I think about you,
my kasum aches deeply • ku nwukwunka-lul kasum celye kitalintanunken: What waiting for someone with
an aching.kasum means • han mincokuy kasumey thanun pwul: The burning fire in the kasum of the han
race (Korean) • kasumi thatolok kµliwun salama: You whom I long for with a burning kasum • onulpamto kasum alhnun salama: You who are sick at kasum • cham kasum ccinghan iyaki: A story that moves the kasum
The word kasum is used symmetrically to meli as shown below:
• salangun kasumulo hako kyelhonun melilo hanunkeya: One should love with kasum and marry with meli
• melilo ponun kulim kasumulo nukkinun kulim: A picture seen with meli, a picture felt with kasum.
Some of the compound words that contain kasum are as follows:

In the examples above k asum refers to a body part (except for the first and second usages in (42)). When referring to part of a woman's body, this word is often translated as 'breast', although in relation to both men and women it is more frequently translated as the 'chest', the 'heart, the 'lungs', or the 'bosom'. Since this word is polysemous between the physical body part term and the emotional entity, there are other devices that clarify the intended meaning in natural discourse. The collocations and contexts can also give a clue for the correct interpretation, for instance, kasum twulley (girth), and kasum sen (bust line), or kasum ppye (chest bone), or kasumkkey (chest area). All the
aspects discussed so far can be accounted for in the following components:

103 This means roughly that she will regret. The fixed expression kasumul chita literally means 'hitting one's kasum '. The actual motion is also often observed.

Chapter 5
a part of a person's body this part is in the middle of the upper part of a person's body this part of a woman's body is not like this part of a man's body

The primary function of this part seems to be that of feeling various kinds of physical change in this part of the body caused by various reasons, but mostly by negative or positive emotions. Although it is true that purely physical symptoms, such as illness of the heart or lungs, can also be felt in this part, this is not part of the meaning of kasum (I) since all other body parts can feel this kind of sensation. However among the different body parts only kasum (1) reacts to emotions, accordingly this aspect is
significant enough to be included as a part of the meaning. Koreans use such words as aphu- (sick), celi- (roughly, be numbed), ali- (roughly,
tingling), or ttakap- (roughly, sting) in order to describe negative sensations and tnry-
(roughly, pound) to denote surprise in combination with kasum. It appears that people notice any change in this part immediately. The first noticeable reaction of the kasum (1) is beating, or throbbing hard at either positive or negative emotions. There are many expressions describing this movement of kasum (1) (presumably the 'heart' although kasum (1) includes other organs such as lungs or throat). Self-awareness of bodily changes is expressed in psychomimetic words, such as khwungkhwang 104 , wulleng wulleng, twukun twukun, pelleng pelleng (biting hard or pulsating hard), which express strong movement occurring in the kasum (I). Although another word, simcang, which is strictly a physical term for the heart can also be used, kasum (I) is more fr~quently used aniong ordinary people. In the following examples, people express the physical changes
in kasum (I):

Component (a) indicates that kasum (1) is a part of the human body. The location of kasum (1) around the chest area is captured in component (b). The concept of kasum (1)
appears to include both the inside and outside of that particular part of the body. While people can see this area of the body when they refer to the breast or chest, the inside of this area which contains various physical organs, such as the heart, the lungs, and the
throat, is not visible. The word kasum (1) does not seem to concern visibility. However,
the difference in this part, between male and female has to be included in the meaning of the word, since it is used to refer to the 'breast' (perhaps euphemistically, since there
are other words that refer only to this body part: yupang). The obvious visible difference
between male and female is captured in component (c). One may argue that the word
kasum (1) is .polysemous and that it can mean also women's breast. The question whether or not this aspect of kasum (1) needs to be elaborated positing polysemy can be
explored in depth in further study based on more examples. Component (d) indicates that people can feel some kinds of physical sensation in
this part when their body reacts either to nice surprises or to negative emotions, see (43), and (44) to (47). The components (e), and (f) account for the fact that people
conceptualise what they experience in kasum (1) as 'something happening' by using
various mimetic words. The change seems to be caused mainly by the movement of the heart, although example (48) suggests other organs, such as the throat or the lungs (inferred by the mention of breathing problems) can also be referred to. When people
realise that there are changes in their kasum (1 ), they can be aware of some sound as the
consequence of the movement. This is shown in (46) and (47) where the sound is
represented as a clicking sound, and in (49) where the sound is symbolised by bwuk105 .
This aspect is accounted for in component (g) . Although one may argue that this component is not necessary, I am inclined to include it because this aspect appears to be
highly distinctive of that particular part of the body.

105 A Korean drum that makes a loud noise and can be heard from a distance.

Having posited the explication for kasum (1), the second meaning of kasum can be examined in different examples. There is no symmetrical use of kasum (2) and mom (body) but kasum (2) is used symmetrically with meli (head or brain) as mentioned previously. This seems to suggest that the main function of kasum (2) is feeling while that of meli is thinking. The contrast between meli and kasum (2) is illustrated earlier in this section and in the example below:

The term of kasum (2) refers to a part of a person in a different way from kasum (1). This part is exclusively human and does not refer to any other creatures in the world. The location of this part is inside the chest area, precisely inside kasum (1). When people refer to kasum (2), using expressions such as ' kasumuy sangche iculkil epsney' ( I cannot forget the scar left in my kasum) 106, they think that the kasum is like a part of a person which is invisible and intangible. Although this expression is metaphoric, the meaning of kasum (2) can still be articulated. These elements can be represented in the following components:
a part of a person people think about this part like this:
"this part of a person is inside kasum (1) people cannot see this part of a person because of this part, a person is not like other living things"
The primary function of kasum (2) appears to be that of experiencing various feelings as shown in (51 ). Moreover, this part is able to feel emotions immediately and instantly. It is not influenced by other thoughts from the meli or maum at the time of the event. This is shown in (52) and (53). All the examples illustrate that one can feel extreme feelings in one's kasum (2), either positive as in (61) or negative as in the other examples from (55) to (60). One can choose to use maum for the place of kasum (2) in the same examples above. However, a subtle meaning difference may result, implying less intense feelings of the experiencer.
The intense negative feelings are often described with bodily symptoms, such as alita (roughly, tingling), ssulita (roughly, sting), silita (roughly, be numbed), and ttakapta (roughly pungent), and these collocations are typical and characteristic for the concept of kasum (2), and distinctive from the use of maum. While maum can make an attempt to moderate a painful feeling, perhaps by 'thinking' to allay one's anxiety, kasum cannot do anything but 'feel' as it is, no matter how painful the situation is. When one feels such strong emotions in one's kasum (2), one seems to be out of control
of this painful state.

The function of kasum (2) in feeling extreme emotions is captured in the following:
when something very good happens to a person. this person can feel something very good in this part, and when something very bad happens to a person, this person can feel something very bad in this part.
Although maum and kasum (2) are used interchangeably in many contexts, kasum (2) is not likely to be replaced by maum when a physical process is used to describe what the person feels like, see (62) and (63). Predicates such as mwuneci(collapse), pwuseci- (break down), naylyeanc- (sink), makhi- (be choked, be clogged), cciceci- (tear apart) 107 are also typically used in combination with kasum (2), although the combination of these words with maum is not unacceptable. All these collocational characteristics of kasum (2) suggest that Koreans express intensive feelings via kasum (2) rather than maum although maum functions to feel various emotions as well. Similar to the use of kasum (l), kasum (2) is commonly used in combination with psychomimetic or psycho-onomatopoetic expressions as shown in (64) and (65) with which maum does
not tend to co-occur.

Apart from these expressions, there are more commonly used psycho-onomatopoetic
expressions used in combination with kasum (2) such as, ccilkkum, ttukkum, telkhetek, telkhek, ppeng, and khwak.
When the word kasum (2) is used in a context such as a mother's loss of her only
child, it would be difficult to draw a clear distinction as to where the acute pain occurs,
in kasum (l) or kasum (2). It can be both, since a grieving mother must feel a powerful
negative feeling in her body as well as in her inner psychological entity. Koreans
strongly believe in the close link between the body and what is happening in kasum (2) (as much as in maum). This close relationship between the psychological entity and the
human body appears to be a significant aspect that has to be captured as a part of the meaning. The evidence is shown in various examples where physical sensation terms
107 The English translations are the closest possible but they are not equivalent since these predicates do not have equivalents in English.

and psychomimic words that resemble physical changes, are used in combination with kasum (2). It is believed that the powerful experience of feeling extreme emotions may cause a physical reaction, especially when it is repetitive. No matter what happens in the human body in reality, people feel like something happened to their body, which is the chest area, when they go through a strong emotional process. For instance, the expression 'selewun nay kasumey mengi tulessney' 108 (my kasum is bruised) is used
often to describe one's broken heart resulting from unrequited love. This aspect is · reflected in the component when these things happen to a person, it is like something

In the same respect, there is an expression that is common when one loses one's child. 'casikun kasumey mwutnunta' (One buries one's own child in one's kasum). This expression means perhaps that one can never forget one's own child no matter how much time passes after the death. The examples presented above suggest that people generally believe that once something gets into one's kasum (2), regardless of one's will the memory of the feeling at the moment the kasum (2) reacted to it can be preserved as it is and does not disappear for a long time. This is possible presumably because kasum (2) does not have the function of thinking, and consequently the thing kept in kasum (2) can remain without being influenced by any other ideas or thoughts of kasum (2). It is like a deep root of a big and old tree or an engraving. There are expressions such as 'kasumey maychin han' (han that is knotted in kasum) which suggests something can accumulate in the kasum ( 2) regardless of a person's will. This is captured in the component when this person feels some things in this part this person can feel these
things for a long time. Unlike maum, the word kasum (2) tends not to be used in combination with value
terms, although there are expressions such as 'kasumi ttattushan salam' (a person with a warm kasum), which denotes a good personality that is compassionate and sympathetic toward others. There are no expressions like 'good kasum' or 'bad kasum--.! while there are the expressions 'kasumi chakapta' (kasum is cold: cold blooded) and 'kasumi ttattushata' (kasum is warm). The expression 'kasumi epsnun seysang' (Kim, 1993), (a society without kasum) is used as a book title to refer to the modem Korean society where one experiences all kinds of inhumane treatment. The meaning is not 'a society without any feeling' but 'a society without compassion, affection and sympathy'. This element of meaning is reflected in the component because of this part, a person can feel
good things towards other people. On ·the basis of the points discussed above, I posit the meaning of kasum (2) as
follows:

something very good in this part (h) when something very bad happens to a person, this person can feel

5.2.3 Discussion of the two concepts
The two concepts maum and kasum (2) are essential for understanding Korean folk psychology. The maum is seen as the faculty of all kinds of psychological activity and is used as a metaphor for a person, whereas kasum (2) is seen as the emotional seat of Korean people. Although the two words are used interchangeably when they are used to express the function of feeling emotions, they are not the same concepts. The overlapping capability of feeling various emotions is captured in their explications.
The main difference between maum and kasum, apart from the fact that kasum is a polysemous word while maum is not, is perhaps that maum can think while kasum does not have this function. Furthermore, kasum (2), rather than maum, carries out the function of experiencing extreme emotions, although maurri can also feel various kinds
277

Chapter 5
of emotion. This is suggested by the prevailing use of kasum (2) rather than maum in association with physical sensation terms such as sili- or ali- (kasum/*maum silin iyaki: a story which makes the kasum ach~. These expressions are often used to refer to an
extreme emotional state. 109
Finally, evaluative concepts are not used in combination with kasum while they are with maum. For instance, there are no expressions like 'cohun/nappun kasum' (good/bad kasum) or 'chakhan/miwun kasum' (virtuous/ugly kasum). This can be
related to the Korean perspective on emotional states as something that cannot be
controlled by the human will. Consequently, what happens in one's kasum is a reaction
to an antecedent, which should not be judged in terms of positive or negative value.
It is interesting to compare the explications of the maum and the kasum with those
of 'mind' and 'heart', which are frequently used translational correspondents of the two
concepts m. Eng11· sh.110
heart (a) a part of a person (b) one cannot see it (c) one can imagine that it is a part of a person's body [in the middle of the upper half of the body (d) one can hear its movements] (e) because of this part, a person can feel good things and bad things (f) because of this part, a person can feel good things towards other people (Wierzbicka 1992: 48, line order added)
mind (a) one of two parts of a person (b) one cannot see it (c) .because of this part, a person can think and know (Wierzbicka, 1992: 45, line order added)
There are also other closely related words; the Japanese word kokoro, the Malay word hati, and the Russian word dusa, which have all been explicated within the NSM
framework.
109 One often refers to bodily symptoms when experiencing intense emotions. 110 The explications of 'heart' and 'mind' in Wierzbicka 1992 were phrased in an older version ofNSM.
In the last decade, this version has been substantially modified.
278

Chapter 5
kokoro (a) a part of a person (b) people think it is like something inside the upper part of a body (c) people think it is something people cannot see (d) because of a person has this part, this person can feel many kinds of things (e) because of a person has this part, a person wants to do something (f) because of a person has this part, a person can think of other people (another person) (g) because of this, a person can do good things for other people (another person) (h) when a person does not have this part, a person cannot be a good person (i) because of this part, a person can be good/bad
G) other people cannot know what this person feels/thinks inside this part, if this
person does not want them to know (Hasada, 2000: 115-116)

hati orang (a person's hati)

sometimes something good, sometimes something bad (f) when something happens in this part of a person,
this person can think: this is like something inside me is saying something to me (g) other people can't know what happens in this part of a person, if this person doesn' t want them to know
(Goddard, 2001a: 171-178)

dusa (a) one of two parts of a person (b) one cannot see it (c) because of this part, things can happen in a person this cannot happen in anything other than a person (d) these things can be good or bad (e) because of this part, a person can feel things

279

.Chapter 5
that nothing other than a person can feel (f) other people can't know what these things are (g) because of this part a person can be a good person
(Wierzbicka, 1992: 51 , line .order added)
Similarities and differences between the two Korean concepts, maum and kasum, and others, 'mind', 'heart', kokoro, hati, dusa, can be observed from the explications
presented above. In terms of similarity, maum is linked to kokoro , dusa, and 'mind' in
that they all have the components one of the two parts of a person and one cannot see it mcommon.
The location of maum is similar to that of the kokoro and the 'heart' , which is the
chest area (in the middle of the upper part of a persons body), while it is different from
that of the 'mind' (head area) and hati (middle of the body). The exclusive human quality of maum is expressed in component (e): because of
this part, a person is not like other living things. The concept of dusa shares a similar
aspect with this, which is found in component (c) and (e): because of this part, things can happen in a person this cannot happen in anything other than a person and because of this part, a person can feel things that nothing other than a person can feel.
Among others, ma!l-m shares various aspects with kokoro. For instance, both parts are the faculty of emotions (component (g) of maum: a person can feel some things in this part when this person thinks about something, and component (d) of kokoro:
because of a person has this part, this person can feel many kinds of things), and have
the function of desiring (component (:f) of maum: because of this part, a person can want to do many things, and component (e) of kokoro: because of this part, a person wants to do something). The concept of hati is also connected to maum in this respect as seen in
component (e): because of this, this person wants to do something sometimes something good, sometimes something bad.
However, the extensive collocations of maum in relation to eagerness or intention to do something (exemplified in 5.2.1) suggest that maum is more prominent than kokoro in the function of motivating a person. The part maum appears to be a dynamic
centre of a person from which spiritual strength comes.
Another difference between maum and kokoro is that while one can be said not to have kokoro in Japanese, as shown in component (h): when a person does not have this
part, a person cannot be a good person, one is not described in the same way in Korean.
All human beings are thought to have maum whether good or bad in Korean. Even
280

Chapter 5
though there is a metaphoric expression 'kunun kuiley maumi epsess ta' (He does not have maum in that work) to mean that 'He does not want to do that work', this has to be used in association with a locus expression (linked with the locational particle -ey,
although it can be elliptical according to the context). Thus, it is possible for one not to ·
have one's maum in something or someone (when one is not interested), while it is not possible for one to not have one's maum within oneself.
Regarding confidentiality, kokoro can not be known to other people as shown in
G): other people cannot know what this person feels/thinks inside this part, if this person
does not want them to know, whereas maum does not have this aspect as a necessary component of the meaning. Despite the fact that people think about maum as a private or secret place, one's maum can be known to others involuntarily. The concept hati shares the hidden nature with kokoro as shown in (g): other people can't know what
happens in this part of a person, if this person doesn't want them to know.
The concept kasum (2) shares a similarity with kokoro, dusa, and 'heart' in the
sense that these parts are the source of good will in a person. All of them are linked to the altruistic attitude although they do not exactly match in their meanings. This aspect
is shown in component (1) of kasum (2): 'because of this part, a person can feel good things towards other people, component (g) of dusa: because of this part, a person can be a good person, component (g) ofkokoro: because of this, a person can do good things
for other people (another person), and component (e) of the heart: because of this part, a person can feel good things towards other people.
The Korean concepts, maum and kasum (2) have been compared to other concepts, particularly to the Japanese concept kokoro, in order to show the possibility of cross-
cultural and cross-linguistic comparison of concepts. This is made possible only because all the concepts are described by the same analytical tool (i.e. the NSM framework). The similarities and differences of the presented concepts have been pinpointed by their cognitive content which all reflect different folk views on similar psychological entities. The overlap and the difference between these explications can explain precisely why they can serve as translational equivalents in some contexts but not in others.
281

Chapter 5
5.3 The meaning of the three anger-like emotion terms: hwa,
wulhwa, ppichi-
According to recent research (Kim H, 200: 220), hwa is one of the basic Korean
emotions. This claim is based on Hofstede (1995), and Choi (1997) who believe that Korean people have a high rate of anxiety about uncertainty. Osgood (1951 :331) shares this perspective:
All in all, we reconstruct a picture of a people with some smouldering violence in their nature, struggling against an environment in which for each brief victory there was a period of severe constraint. ... First rest, then exuberance, followed by the compression of long nights, the load of pent-up emotions, fears, speculations, and anxiety. The imagined prototype of the Korean personality is an introverted individual with considerable emotional instability, capable of the sustained quiescence of the hibernating bear and of the fury of the goaded tiger.
What does 'considerable emotional instability, capable of sustained quiescence of the hibernating bear and of the fury of the goaded tiger' mean? Osgood may have observed two extreme sides of the Korean personality, quiet and static on the surface but with the potentiality to explode in the next moment like a furious animal. There are a number of words related to the anger-like emotion that can be grouped together under the heading
of 'I don't want things like this to happen' : hwa, wulhwa, pwunno, pwun, uypwun, seng, pwua, wulpwun, noyewum, kyekno and kol. Also there are a large number of expressions
and set phrases that are conventionalised to refer to this kind of emotion:
nopaltaypalhata, chika ttelita (teeth are trembling) sacika ttelita (arms and legs are trembling) soki twiciphita (inside is turned out) pwuakanata (lung extends or comes up) and yelichisosta (the fire comes out).
Lim (2000) illustrates how 'hwa' is conceptualised in Korean through various
metaphors involved with body parts, bodily sensations, and bodily changes. His research shows that Koreans often describe their negative emotion through expressions involving physical process. These words or expressions used as a reference to anger-like emotions, are often treated synonymously although the difference of degree of intensity between them is acknowledged. However, the qualitative differences among them have
not been examined thoroughly in the literature. Among these words, hwa, wulhwa, and ppichi-ta are selected for examination in this section as they are used relatively
frequently in everyday conversations in contemporary Korean society.
282

Chapter 5
Korea has a history that is very dramatic and includes many wars and invasions from surrounding countries. Under foreign military occupation, Korea has been abused and damaged. At the same time people have gone through various stages of the country's history with difficulty and suffering over long periods of time. These historical, cultural and political influences may have had an impact on the people' s way of thinking. The way of dealing with the negative emotion of Koreans may be reflected in anger-like emotion words. Kim 111 believes that Koreans have a collective subconscious psyche that is expressed by the folk term han whose cause is suppressed
hwa. To quote him:
Each war, each political and social upheaval, brought about suffering, personal losses and unbearable woes to Koreans. Few have been spared the loss, pain and suffering. Most have fought and struggled for survival, and somehow managed to exist. Some even have thrived. But they harbor deep feelings ,of haan: suppressed anger, resentment and underlying depression. They have suffered feelings of indignation at having been unfairly victimized. For their own survival, however, as well as to live in harmony with the teachings of Confucius, they have had to swallow and suppress their feelings.
The contrast of a great deal of suffering, misery, and victimisation, with the absolute powerlessness ·of people over these situations, can be observed in the daily lives of Koreans throughout its history. Suppression in order to regulate their negative feelings against hardships and traumatic events is particularly characteristic in Korean people. Suppressed hwa is known to become wulhwa and finally causes a psychological illness named hwapyeng. This syndrome is reported as a culture-specific .or culturebound syndrome, which is found only in Korean people (Lin, 1983). Although psychiatrists acknowledge suppressed hwa and wulhwa (defined roughly as a depressed status because of suppressed hwa) as the main cause of the syndrome of hwapyeng (hwa syndrome), there does not seem to be a clear meaning definition of these words on which they can base a discussion for dealing with hwapyeng. The words hwa and wulhwa are used on the assumption that the two words mean more or less the same (hwa becomes wulhwa when it is not expressed outwardly) and are also similar to the English concepts of 'anger' and 'suppressed anger', respectively. However, the two concepts seem to differ, beyond the degree of intensity and the suppressed quality.

m Kim, Luke. "The negative Side of Jeong". Korean Culture. (Printed by permission of Korean Quarterly), http://www.parentsplace.com/fertility/adoptioncentraVarticles, 27 Sep. 2002.

283

Chapter 5

The dictionary definitions are circular, and accordingly, the exact meanings from the perspective of ordinary Korean speakers are not clearly shown despite the fact that these words are used a great deal in the everyday life of Korean people.

5.3.1. Hwa
In the literature, the Korean word hwa is often chosen as the translational equivalent of the English concept of 'anger' and vice-versa. However, it is not clear to what extent they share semantic components. Anger is known to be "one of the most frequently discussed emotion concepts and the range of the interpretations varies widely" (Wierzbicka, 1999: 87). However the Korean concept hwa and the English concept of 'anger' cannot be compared to each other unless they are analysed by the same measure. 112 Thus the given task of this section is to capture the semantic invariants of hwa on the basis of the linguistic evidence and paraphrase them into the natural semantic metalanguage.
When one feels hwa one can either express it or suppress it. It is possible that one does not express it despite feeling it quite strongly. The use of the predicate tn each case differs: nata (lit. comes out) for feeling hwa, and nayta (lit. let it out) for expressing hwa. One can say 'hwaka nassciman hwalul naycianhassta' (lit. hwa came up but I didn't let it out: I felt hwa but I didn't express hwa). The concept of hwa can be used either with or without the causer. One can say, for instance, that ' X-eykey hwaka nassta' 113 (I felt hwa towards X) in the case of wanting to point out the causer. This is a typical scenario of hwa where the causer does some damage to the experiencer and the feeling of hwa is the consequence of that action, see examples (4) to (7) presented below. When hwa is used in this frame the cognitive scenario has to have a causer that can be expressed something along similar lines to someone did something bad.
However, the use of hwa is broader than this. For instance it can be used in situations where the experiencer cannot identify clearly who caused hwa. In (3), the
112 The explication of 'anger' is in Wierzbicka (1999: 88). The comparison of the two concepts are not discussed here. 113 The predicate nass is the part tense form of na- (come up).

of hwa will be examined in a syntactic frame 'Xnun hwaka nassta' (hwa comes up to
X), which can be both something bad happened because someone did something bad as
well as something bad happened.

The component something bad happened is less specific but can cover all the
cases where the experiencers feel hwa in the examples above.
Another element to be tested is to whom the undesirable events happened. In the
case of hwa what happened is not necessarily directed to the experiencer as shown in (7), where the experiencer feels hwa towards a priest who was rude to a mother of a crying baby during the mass. The experiencer felt hwa while he was observing the
situation where the mother was embarrassed in front of other people. Although nothing
happened to him, he felt hwa because he felt it was not right for the priest to express that kind of attitude towards someone like that mother. In fact hwa can be used not only
for individual damage but also when facing social injustice or environmental damage
caused by unspecified people. For instance, one can feel hwa after listening to news reports on illegal hunting. Things that are against people's volition appear to cause hwa.
These aspects can be captured in the component I don't want things like this to happen.
Regardless of the expressed action caused by feeling hwa, all who experience
being involved in undesirable events may want to do something. Many people may want
to pay back those who caused hwa, as in (5), or to reverse what has happened. Since
what happened is against one's volition, it appears to be natural for the experiencer to want to do something about it. This is reflected in the component I -want to do something because of this. Whether this desire is activated or not seems to hinge on individual circumstances. In most cases, however, the experiencer ends up doing things like shouting, trying to use physical violence, or drinking alcohol as the above examples
show. Despite the desire to do something, often people do not know what they can do,
perhaps because they know that there is nothing for them to do as in the case of (7), or because they cannot think logically when in such an emotional state, as shown in examples (3) to (6). It is interesting to note a fixed expression that is very commonly
used, hwaskim(-ey) (lit. because of hwa, at the fit of anger, be blinded by anger), which typically goes with pwulcilu- (set fire) or sepangcilha- 114 (having a love affair with another man). This expression seems to suggest that experiencing hwa can lead one to
do something extremely absurd. This aspect is shown in the examples below:

Although the words die or death are often used between couples who are arguing, or between children who are fighting, no one really thinks that someone really may kill or may die. It is also rare that someone dies in a strike although people use extreme slogans like give us death if you will not give us this or that. >own houses in a fit of anger (hwa). Breaking things in a house is understandable

These examples show that the feeling of hwa often leads people to very i~ational and illogical, as well as destructive, actions directed not only at the causer but also at themselves. It is not clear why people do harm themselves even when they could harm the causer. For example, the experiencer in (8) could do something to his son rather than setting fire to his own house. It may perhaps be because the father blames himself for not being able to prevent his son from being rebellious. From being in an emotional state of hwa, the inability to think logically is inferred. The consequence of going through this emotional state is often shown in absurdity, as mentioned previously. These aspects can be captured in the following components:
I don't know what I can do now I cannot think about anything else now
In both components, the experiencer' s instant reaction is reflected in the prime now, which implies the experiencer' s temporary thought about the incapability of reasoning at least for sometime until it settles down. The concept of hwa seems to be distinct from
288

Chapter 5
that of 'fury' 115 and 'rage', which have components like I want to do something bad to this person now, and I want to do something bad, maybe something bad will happen because of this, respectively.
When a person feels hwa, s/he does not necessarily do something harmful, (although in many cases that is the case). In the example mentioned earlier about illegal hunting, the experiencer may want to donate money to promote the idea of protecting wild animals that are facing extinction. The environmentalist can say 'hwaskimey issnun ton-ul ta naypelyessta' (In a fit of hwa I put all I had) about his/her donation for the prevention of illegal hunting. In this case the consequence of experiencing hwa is not harmful to anyone (except for himselflherself who may regret the action later). More commonly the experiencer's feeling of hwa is suppressed, as seen in the examples below. This is more likely to be so when the experiencer is female, young, or belongs to
a low social class.

She might have felt that she was being teased since she never experienced that kind of approach before. (MT: K-> E, Choi, 2001: 93)
Having no solution for a situation may lead people to tum a negative feeling inward and this is one of the encouraged social values for community members in Korean society. An intention to get revenge may occur in some situations but not necessarily to all experiencers. As seen in (7), (11) and (12) most experiencers do not

5.3.2. Wulhwa
The word wulhwa116 can be used either as an emotion term by ordinary speakers or as a diagnostic term that is sometimes used by medical practitioners, especially those who
use traditional methods including acupuncture and herbal medicines. It is not clear how
exactly the two meanings of wulhwa are different. Other words, cung (syndrome) or pyeng (disease), are normally attached (wulhwacung or wulhwapyeng (disease)), in order that wulhwa is expressed as a cause of physical mal-function in a clinical context. Suppressed wulhaw is known to be common among Koreans as noted below:

people regardless of their original meanings in the Chinese characters.

One continues to experience a feeling of frustration in the kasum, and a feeling of something hot ·spouting from kasum to the face. One also feels that there is a lump in the throat and the kasum. In addition, one feels dramatic ups and downs, becomes sensitive without reason, and easily expresses surges of anger (hwa),
and no interest in anything at all. One becomes depressive, anxious, as well as easily surprised and shrank.
(···) going through psychological conflict and changes like this so much so that
one can hardly carry on one's normal life is called wulhwa or hwapyeng. Hwapyeng is a disease w_hich explodes like fire after an accumulation of mortified feelings. In fact hwapyeng is typically Korean, because this is not
found in medical references of countries in the same cultural area, such as in China or in Japan. (MT: K- > E, Korean weekly, http://wvvw.hankooki.com/whan/200101/w200l01161910326151141.htm, 5 Sep. 2002)
A few possible meaning elements suggested by the comments made by experts are
'feeling mortified', and 'not being able to do anything over the situation'. The sense of
being mistreated or victimised unfairly may cause an intensively negative emotion
which can be paraphrased along the following lines:

something bad happened to me not because I did something I want to do something because of this

291

Chapter 5
at the same time, I know that I cannot do anything I feel something very bad because of this

In this section, however, the meaning of wulhwa is examined only on the basis of
ordinary people's usage in daily conversations and excludes technological or clinical
usage.
The first clue for distinguishing the meaning of wulhwa from that of hwa is found
in the difference of the choice of predicate. From the corpora and other linguistic
sources, it is noted that the word wulhwa is used in combination with chimil- (lit: well up, seize or raise up), or theci- (burst or explode), while hwa is normally used in combination with na- (come up) although the interchange of predicates is not ungrammatical. There is one more expression that is closely related to wulhwa, wulhwathong (the bucket of wulhwa), which is used in the same fashion as wulhwa in terms of the choice of predicates. When chimil- (well up, spring out) is used, it denotes that wulhwa is felt but not necessarily shown in an outward explosion while when theci(explode or burst) is used wulhwa is likely to be expressed outwardly.
In this section the concept of wulhwa will be examined in the syntactic frame 'wulhwaka chimilessta'(wulhwa welled up), despite the fact that wulhwa can also be used in a syntactic frame such as 'X ttaymwuney wulhwaka chimilessta' (wulhwa wells up because of X) when pointing out the causer. This is because wulhwa can commonly
be used without mentioning a causer since in Korean sentences the subject and object
are often omitted. The word wulhwa seems to be more subjective than hwa -and is likely
to be used with .the first person, although one can report or describe a third person's
emotional state using wulhwa (as in 'kunun wulhwaka chiminun kes katha poyessta: he
looked like he felt wulhwa) according to context. In order to examine the meaning of wulhwa, a number of different examples are
presented. In (13) below it is shown that an undesirable thing causes wulhwa

To my mother all things troubled her, when her daughter-in-law came back home early, it was the same as when she came home late. When she came home early, my mother burst with wulhwa, thinking about those days that my wife
292

Chapter 5
came late, when she came home late my mother thought my wife was coming late on purpose. Even the issue of money was enough for my mother to feel hwa. At that time, my wife's monthly salary was 4700,000 won. From that amount 100,0000won went to my mother. But that was a lot less than she was getting from working as the assistant of the Church minister. It was understandable that she burst with her wulhwa. She also had the work of taking care of her grandchildren. She thought that she was not treated as well as she should be. Even though her daughter-in-law was not a pleasing person to her, she had to receive money from her daughter-in-law. From time to time she shouted loudly because she couldn't control her wulhwa. (MT: K-> E, Choi, 2001: 63)
In the above example (and also in other examples), the experiencer feels a more intense negative emotion with wulhwa than in the case of hwa. However, the intensity does not seem to result from the nature of events such as a huge disaster that caused great damage. What happens can be similar to what happens in the case of hwa, in terms of importance or gravity. What leads to the intensive emotional state seems to be the feeling of 'I have had enough' or 'I cannot put up with this kind of happening anymore'.
In example (13), a mother-in-law who is dealing with a working daughter-in law
bursts out with wulhwa. This mother-in-law did not like her daughter-in-law in the first place and was against her only son's marriage for a long time. Despite his mother's opposition, her son married and his mother must have felt betrayed by him. Also she had to look after her grandchildren because her daughter-in-law was the breadwinner as her son was still studying. Therefore, for the experiencer, many bad things have happened previously and recent happenings have triggered an explosion of ~~r wulhwa.

period of time is seen in (14) below:

It was him, a young lawyer with glasses. He seemed to believe that the audience
was respectfully in favour of him. At the moment of seeing him, wulhwa boiled at

Chapter 5
The experiencer is overwhelmed by the heaviness of matters about which s/he cannot do anything. Situations include facing the unfairness of authority, the injustice of the law, corruption of the government, abusive attitudes of superiors and inconsistency of the
world. These situations are typical for wulhwa, although other matt~rs of a personal
dimension, and individual everyday happenings such as conflicts between close friends
or family members can provoke one's wulhwa. Acknowledgement of the experiencer's
powerlessness over the given situation seems to be the very essence of the meaning of
wulhwa. The contrast between a strong volition to do something and the obvious selfconsciousness of incapability makes one's wulhwa burst, in collaboration with a feeling of intolerableness. In the cognitive scenario of wulhwa one admits that one cannot
control or moderate the overwhelmingly powerful feeling at the time whether one expresses it outwardly or not. This element is compatible with the imminence of
wulhwa indicated in the collocation of the predicate chimil- (well up) or theci- (explode) in combination with wulhwa. Other psychoonomatopoetic words such as, pelkhek, walak, telkhek, or pelek used in combination also offer support for this aspect. These
factors can be reflected in the following components:
I want to do something if I can I know that I cannot do anything · I feel something very bad because of this now I cannot not feel this
--
The predicates used in combination with wulhwa and hwa when expressing resolving or suppressing these emotions are sakhi- (mitigate), and nwulu- (oppress), respectively. It is more natural to say 'wulhwaka ssahyese' (because wulhwa is piled up, or lie on) than '?? hwaka ssahyese' (because hwa is piled up), and 'hwalul chamta (suppress hwa)' rather than 'wulhwalul chamta' (suppress wulhwa). This seems to suggest that one can suppress hwa rather than wulhwa and hwa is not something piled up while wulhwa117 is. This aspect can be a clue for the duration of feeling involved in
wulhwa, which covers a longer period of emotion. It is awkward to say ' kunun
wulhwaka chimilessciman kumpang phwulecyessta' (He felt wulhwa but it soon passed) while it is natural to say 'kunun hwaka nassciman kumpang phwuleciessta' (He felt hwa but it soon passed). Although the duration of feeling with wulhwa seems to vary
according to individual differences it seems to be conceptualised as something that lasts.

117 People believe that wulhwa is accumulated hwa from the tranlstaion of the Chinese character.

All the points that have been discussed so far can be said to describe a prototypical situation in which one would naturally use the word wulhwa. However, the use of wulhwa is not restricted only to individual disasters of the experiencer. This word can also be used when something bad happens to some other people to whom the experiencer relates himself/herself. In the following examples, the experiencer feels wulhwa not because something directly happened to him/her but because s/he identifies
