Therefore, people can use the word wulhwa either when unwanted events happen to them directly (as in most cases) or to someone else whom they identify with. There may be various motivations for the identification. For instance in (21) the victim's socioeconomic position is similar to that of the experiencer and in (22) the victims are common people the same as the experiencer, whose safety is often neglected by authority. Identifying with the experiencer can also be due to being of the same gender, or being in the same situation (e.g. students under the pressure of exams, or workers under the same unfair treatment at work). Therefore wulhwa can be used when something bad happens to someone else only if the experiencer recognises the victim as someone like himself/herself. This aspect is captured in the component: X can feel like this when something bad happens to someone else if X thinks this person is like X.
On the basis of the semantic components discussed so far, I propose the following
explication of wulhwa: ·

wulhwa (Xnun wulhwa-ka chimil-ess-ta: for X, wulhwa welled up )
(a) X felt something because X thought something about something (b) sometimes a person thinks: (c) "something bad happened to me now (d) things like this have happened to me before (e) I don't want things like this to happen (f) I want to do something because of this if I can (g) I know that I cannot do anything" (h) when this person thinks like this, this person feels something very bad for a
long time (i) this person can feel like this when something bad happens to someone else if this
person thinks like this: "this person is someone like me"
G) X felt something like this
(k) because X thought like this

5.3.3. ppichi-

The expression ppichi-ta is a predicate made up of the stem ppichi- and the declarative

sentence marker -ta to describe someone's emotional state. This word is widely and

colloquially used among young people or when people address or refer to young people

or women. It sounds awkward when it is used to describe the emotional state of ·

someone who is mature or whose social status is higher than the speaker. This Korean

word is much more widely used in everyday life than the corresponding English word

'sulky' (although the two words are clearly different concepts). It is worth mentioning

that there are new words, which are derived from the predicate ppichi-ta, that have

appeared

recently:

the

nouns

ppichim

(including

the

phonological

variant

ppicim

118 ),

ppitoli (a male who does ppichi- too often), and ppiswuni (a female version). The

following is an example where one of these is used. In the homepage of a family where

each of the family members is introduced by their nicknames, a young boy is introduced

as ppitoli because he easily becomes sulky (ppichi-ta) .

(23) 7}~ ~ ~ qftj- 0 1%
0}1rn}-~u} (~iti ~7} L}~ -9-,;_J 71 u:R~oll)
~P}-?
%"'E (~i11)-llHl ~ol (1{:- llHl ~)
Nicknames of family members and the reasons. Dad -Devil (very scary when he feels h wa) Mom-? Brother (Cwunwon)-ppitoli (easily ppichim)) (MT: K-> E, http://2ll.46.2l8.58/sch2/y-11/family.htm, 15 Aug. 2002)
This lexical expansion suggests that the word ppichi- is now used in contemporary Korean society not only as an emotion term but also as a description of personality, although the usage seems to be restricted to casual or informal register. This personality type is thought to be childish and immature, and is used more to describe

A typical situation involves someone's bad or inconsiderate actions that cause the
experiencer to feel ppichi-. However, the same word is also used to describe someone' s emotional state without involving a causer. The meaning of ppichi- is slightly different according to the syntactic frame: 'X-nun Y-eykey ppichiessta ' (Xis sulky towards Y)
and 'X-nun ppichiessta' (Xis sulky). The two meanings are dealt with in tum. The first meaning ofppichi- in the syntactic frame 'Xnun Yeykey ppichiessta ' (X
is sulky towards Y) appears to require a component Y did something bad to me. In all the examples above, the experiencers think that someone did something bad to him/her, although the deed itself does not necessarily seem to be 'bad' from other people' s perspective. These deeds can be 'bad to the experiencer' only from his/her point of view. In most cases what caused ppichi- are small things of daily life. One would not use the word ppichi- over something serious and grave such as the experience of
300

Chapter 5
discrimination in job offers. Also this word would not be used in the case of 'something bad happening to someone else', unless the experiencer identifies this other person. Therefore, what makes someone feel ppichi- is something fairly insignificant involving self-interest. The reason why a person feels bad is mainly that s/he has an expectation that the causer would not do this kind of things to herself/himself. There has to be a relationship between the experiencer and causer, which is not necessarily intimate. If there is no expectation one would not use the word ppichi-. For instance, one would not feel ppichi- when someone whom one is just getting to know does something bad to him/her. A slight feeling of betrayal or disappointment is involved. All these aspects can
be shown in the examples below:

In order to capture the aspects of meaning discussed so far, the following semantic
components are proposed:
something bad happened to me because Y did something I did not think that Y could do things like this to me
When one is in the state of feeling ppichi-, it is typical that one withdraws from
doing something with, or talking to the causer. The first reaction is to be silent by shutting oneself off from the causer, although sometimes one does the same thing to
301

Chapter 5
other people who happen to be on the spot accidentally, perhaps to let the causer know about one's emotional state more obviously. Nonetheless, the experiencer still wants the causer to figure out hows/he feels. At the same time the experiencer does not want to express his/her emotional state verbally, assuming that the causer can read his/her maum
with 'nwunchi' (being tactful; roughly means, 'capable of knowing something without
being told') if this causer wants to. On this assumption the experiencer does not feel any need for explicitness. The shutting down can be noticed by others, including the causer, from the experiencer's reactions such as avoiding eye contact, turning the face away, and/or not responding to or answering the causer. Another typical reaction is getting away from the causer to demonstrate the emotional state, as shown in examples (29) to (30).

The points discussed can be captur:ed as elements of meaning along the following lines:

I don't want to do anything with Y now I don't want to be near Y now I don't want to say anything to Y now I want Y to know how I feel now I think that Y can know how I feel I don't have to say anything because of this

302

Chapter 5
The period of ppichi- may vary depending on individuals and causes. The insignificance of the matter involved does not necessarily imply that this emotional state will last only for a short time. It is natural to say that 'nanun olaystongan kueykey ppichiese kulul pocianhassta' (I did not see him for a long time because I was sulky
(ppichiese) toward him). It takes tirhe for the experiencer to send the message and for
the causer to figure it out and to fix the problem. This can be captured in X feels like this for some time, which is vague in duration.
As a final element, people's general evaluation of the matter as insignificant, is included. When the word ppichi- is chosen, there is an implication that the matter is not critical or serious. That is why people think about someone who feels ppichi- easily as immature or child-like. Sometimes one wants to claim that one is not feeling ppichi- but feeling hwa instead, as in (32), because of this intrinsic notion of immatureness in the
concept ofppichi-.

ppichi-(1 ).

ppichi- (1) (X-nun Y-eykey ppichi-ess-ta)

(a) X felt something because X thought something

(b) sometimes a person thinks: (c) "something bad happened to me because Y did something

(d) I didn't think that Y could do things like this to me

(e)

I don't want Y to do things like this to me

(g) I don't want to be near Y now

(h) I don't want to say anything to Y now

(i) I want Y to know how I feel now

G) I think that Y can know how I feel

(k) I don't have to say anything because of this " (1) when this person thinks like this, this person feels something bad for some time

(m) X felt something like this

I will now turn to the second meaning of ppichi-(2) used in a syntactic frame like 'X-nun ppichi-ess-ta' (Xis sulky) to describe someone's general emotion. In this case the causer is not identified because either there is no specific causer or the causer cannot be known. In example (33) below nobody did anything bad to the experiencer to cause the feeling of ppichi- (2), but she is sulky because she could not see the roe deer that

In (34), a child who used to behave normally in the childcare centre suddenly becomes sulky (ppichi- (2)) too often and there is no specific reason for it, except for her yearning
for her father. In (35) the experiencer's emotional state is described as sulky (ppichi-),
presumably because she could not go where she wanted to go. Therefore the commonly shared component will be something bad happened to me.
However, unlike ppichi- (1), the expectation of the experiencer is not necessary as is inferred from examples (33) and (34). The cause of the particular emotion of feeling sulky (ppichi-) does not apparently involve a sense of surprise to the experiencers as
they did not have any anticipation. When one experiences this feeling of ppichi- (2), one normally wants to be left
alone for some time. It is not clear what is the exact hidden reason for wanting to be left alone other than signalling to others something about one's emotional state (so that others can do something for us) and also desiring not to be seen by others. However, the reaction of feeling ppichi- is expressed by not doing what one is supposed to do, being silent, or even leaving the scene. These reactions are very similar to those ofppichi- (1). In both situations in (33) and (34), two young children express that they are in the state ofppichi-, presumably by their facial expressions, and their silence and lack of response to others. These reactions are easily noticeable, paralleling those of ppichi- (1), which include the component I don't want to do anything now. This aspect contrasts with the component I want to do something in the explications of hwa and wulhwa. On the basis of the discussion related to ppichi- (2), I propose the following explication:

ppichi- (2) (X-nun ppichi-ess-ta)
(a) X felt something because X thought something about something (b) sometimes a person thinks: (c) "something bad happened to me (d) because of this, I don't want to do anything now (e) I don't want to be near anyone now

305

Chapter 5
(f) I don't want to say anything to anyone now (g) I want someone to know how I feel now (h) I think that someone can know how I feel (i) I don't have to say anything because of this"
G) when this person thinks like this, this person feels something bad for some time
(k) X felt something like this
(1) because X thought like this
(m)other people can think: "something happened to X (n) it is not very bad"

Regarding component (n) of the above explication (same as (p) in ppichi- (1 )), it is
possible that instead of the phrase it is not very bad, another phrase 'it is not a big thing' ,would better capture the notion of insignificance of the matter. From the-~ examples where the defined word is used, the undesirable events can be described by lightness in term of importance in human life rather than weaker misfortune, although they seem to be linked to each other (what is _not too bad can be regarded as something not that significant). However, the universality of the metaphorical use of BIG has not been approved in the NSM theory (although it appears to be reasonably wide-spread). Therefore, instead of BIG, the concept of BAD has been used to reflect the fact that what happened is not considered as something disastrous.
5.4 Conclusion
In this chapter, the meanings of the five selected Korean concepts are examined and explicated in the NSM framework. The analyses are presented both in the English and the Korean-based metalanguages on the basis of the identified Korean exponents and
306

Chapter 5
the Korean-based metalanguages on the basis of the identified Korean exponents and their combinatorial characteristics tested in Chapter 3. This is the first attempt at exploring the utility ofNSM in the semantic analysis of Korean cultural concepts.
As a result of semantic analysis based on various examples taken from natural contemporary usage in Korean society, the differences and similarities between the two
concepts, maum and kasum, are illustrated. Moreover, the translational corresponding
words in English, Japanese, Malay, and Russian are presented in order to show that culture-specific concepts can be compared to each other if they are analysed by ·the same measure. The cognitive scenarios of the three anger-like emotion terms are also presented so that the qualitative difference between those frequently used anger-like terms can be understood.
There are a few issues related to the Korean version of the explications that are worth mentioning. Firstly, it is noticed that one can choose a particle according to context to fit natural Korean expressions. Since English does not have this grammatical category, it is left to those who transpose the English version into Korean. For instance
one can choose either the particle -un (topic marker) or -i (nominative marker) for the Korean sentence of (p) ofppichi- (1) (same as in line (n) ofppichi- (2)). Between the two
the former seems to be appropriate in this context.

(o)a acwu nappu-n il-un an-i-ta

It is not very bad.

(o)b acwu nappu-n il-i

It is not very bad.

If one composes an explication in the Korean-based metalanguage one would choose

into the same sentence in the English version. Therefore it is not clear whether the different nuance resulting from the different choice of case particles is paraphrasable. According to the suggestion made on the meaning of topicalisation (cf. Wierzbicka 2002b: 275) (o)a can be interpreted as the following
I want to say something about this:
it is not very bad (acwu nappun ilun anita).

Chapter 5
The meaning difference between the case particles that can replace each other in the same sentence requires close examination in further study.
It has been observed that Korean predicates THINK and SAY preferably take adverbial complement in illocutionary topic valency (think something good about
someone). This seems to be parallel to the predicate feel used in component (1) of kasum
(2): because of this part, a person can feel good things towards other people. The next comment concerns the structure of the prototypical cognitive scenario
for emotion terms. There is agreement that emotion is something that results from cognition (cf. Solomon, 1995). This aspect is accounted for in NSM as someone felt something because this person thought something. Another aspect to be captured is the prototypical situation to which the individual experiencer in a certain emotional state ascribes how s/he feels. · The explications of emotion terms posited in this chapter, therefore, contain components such as the following:
(a) X felt something, because X thought something sometimes a person thinks:
when this person thinks like this this person feels something
(e) X felt something like this
(f) because X thought like this
This frame enables us to capture the fact that to feel a certain emotion means to feel like a person who has certain specifiable thoughts. Individuals may feel differ~~ntly due to the different thoughts they have even when they encounter the same situational antecedents. Therefore, it will be impossible to describe each person's exact emotional state. However, it is possible to attribute someone's emotional state to a prototypical state that results from specified thoughts as shown above in component (c) above (the number of these specified thoughts varies in practice). However, a question may arise whether ordinary people are able to apprehend this particular theoretic ground for this framework. Although someone may grasp the point by thorough reading, an explanation with this aspect of the NSM explication will be helpful.
Regarding the Korean version of line (f) of the explication of maum (applies to (d) of kasum (1), and (d) of kasum (2)) a brief comment will be in order. The component is
because of this part, a person can feel some things when this person thinks about something. The translation of the component will be as follows:

The phrase (f)a is the explanation that an emotion is caused from cognition. Despite the conceptual simplicity it is not a conventionalised expression, and consequently it sounds unfamiliar and odd. However, (f)b is more comprehensible to Koreans.
An issue may arise in relation to the unnaturalness of the explication since the Korean metalanguage does not read as naturally as ordinary Korean texts. According to Enfield (2002: 244), while the meanings of these semantically basic elements are clear and simple, there are cases where some complex NSM formulas sound distinctly unidiomatic in Lao. However, .he notes, as far as the metalanguage for semantic representation is concerned the real issue is semantic clarity, and not naturalness. One should bear in mind that the metalanguage used is a natural language-like semi-artificial language that is semantically clear but less natural than the natural language.
To conclude, the exploration of the NSM approach as a means of semantic analysis for Korean concepts has been found to be satisfactory: the culturally salient and complex concepts have been accurately and intelligibly paraphrased. Furthermore, the expressive power of the Korean metalanguage is found to be the same as that of the English metalanguage. This is demonstrated by representations in the two languagebased NSMs. Consequently, the meaning of the proposed explications is expected to be grasped with the same understanding by insiders and outsiders of the Korean culture. Regarding the accuracy of the posited explications, any component is open to
309

Chapter 5
verification put under dispute based on counterexamples. If it is possible to dispute these explications this is significant as this means that they are intelligible and have potential to be improved by further research. All the proposed analyses are submitted as a first attempt and a reference point for further investigation.
310

Chapter 6
Cha ter 6 Conclusion
This research aims to test to what extent the hypotheses of the NSM theory are valid with reference to Korean, and to explore the possibility of applying the NSM framework as a tool for the semantic analysis of Korean concepts.
In this chapter, firstly the main findings from testing the NSM hypotheses in terms of lexicalisation and syntax, and inter-translatability of metalanguage are summarised. Secondly, some general conclusions that emerge from explicating five cultural concepts are presented. Finally, in the light of the present research a number of areas for further research are discussed. These are outlined below.
6.1. Summary of Findings from Testing the NSM Hypotheses
The present research shows that the NSM hypotheses can be tested on a cross-linguistic basis by using various methodological and theoretical strategies. The role of canonical sentences has been found to be crucial for identifying Korean exponents after postulated primes, especially when more than one candidate are in synonymous relationships that can be used interchangeably in n_atural discourse. As a result of testing against canonical sentences nearly all of the NSM hypotheses are supported in the context of Korean, and consequently a Korean-based metalanguage has been constructed and experimented with by both transposing and explicating specific Korean concepts. The findings from this testing are summarised here in terms of lexicalisation and syntactic characteristics.
6.1.1. Lexicalisation
I. All the proposed semantic · primitive concepts have been found to be lexicalised in Korean. The only primitive that has no general (unitary) term in Korean is the quantifier SOME. Two different expressions are proposed to subsume its meaning
for all canonical sentences: myech and etten -tu!. The former is used as an indefinite
311

Chapter 6
quantifier (i.e. there are some people in this place) while the latter is used for contrastive meaning and existential partitive (i.e. some people can do this, some people cannot do this). However their usage in the selective valency construction does not seem to be semantically different (i.e. some of these are good and some of these are bad).
II. Different kinds of allolexes resulting from the morphosyntactic characteristics of Korean have been identified on the basis of testing. It has also been found that the Korean exponents of SOMETHING and SOMEONE have lexical variants
depending on the predicates used and the existence of specifiers. For instance ii instead
of kes is preferred when the concept of SOMETHING occurs as a complement of the predicate DO or HAPPEN. Also instead of the non-specific term mwues!SOMETHING, the bound morpheme -kes (thing) has to be used when the concept of SOMETHING combines with determiners or quantifiers. The proposed variants are tested and found to have no difference in meaning when used in the given canonical sentences.
III. The syntactic behaviour of quantifiers is found to vary. While han/ONE, twu/TWO, and myech!SOME combine with classifiers instead of nouns, others such as manh-lMANY, and motun!ALL combine with nouns. Consequently, there are instances where classifiers replace nouns that are associated with quantifiers. For instance, when occurrence of time is specified by han!ONE, twu/TWO, or myech!SOME, instead of the proposed exponent ttay, the classifier pen has to be used. This is parallel to the classifier kwuntey for kos/PLACE, kay or kaci for kes/SOMETHING, and myeng (plain form) or pwun (honorific form) for salam!SOMEONE. The use of classifiers ts one of the grammatical requirements in Korean, which does not affect the semantic level.
· IV. The Korean counterparts of WANT and NOT are found to have two exponents that differ in syntactic scope. For instance, the short form of negation is constrained for bimorphemic predicates (e.g. malha-lSAY, wenha-lWANT, sayngkakhalTHINK). Regarding the Korean exponents of WANT, wenha- and -ko·siph-, the verbal suffix-like exponent -ko·siph- IWANT is restricted to mono-predicate sentences as well
as non-equi contexts. V. There are several primitives that have more than one conceptual
equivalent simply due to lexical elaboration. For instance, either one of the two exponents of HAPPEN, sayngki- and ilena-, can be used in order to express the proposed meaning in the given context of canonical sentences (also mwes and etten kes, nwukwu and etten i, acwu and maywu, etc).
VI. The only case of polysemy is found in the exponent kath- which can convey either 'similarity' or 'sameness'. While I proposed ttokkath-, which is
312

Chapter 6
bimorphemic consisting of ttok Gust) and kath-, as the Korean counterpart of THE SAME, the syntactic cues for distinguishing the two meanings of kath- have been pointed out through various examples.
No concern is raised in relation to the adverbial use of kath- to express similarity within the given context of canonical sentences. When kath- is used adverbially in combination with i/THIS to express manner, as in it happened like this (ku ilun i kathi ilenassta) or when it is used forming an attributive phrase, as in people like this (i kathun salamtul), there is no ambiguity. When kath- is used attributively there are two syntactic cues that can keep the meaning of 'sameness' distinct from 'similarity': the existence of a relatum and a particle. In order to express 'similarity' the use of the relatum is obligatory without the particle -(k)wa (or -hako). The ambiguity in meaning between sameness and simil~rity arises only when kath- is used predicatively.
VII. The existential prime in Korean is found to be used to express both 'existence' and 'possession' although there is also a separate lexical item for the concept of HAVE. The two meanings of iss-lTHERE IS can be distinguished by the syntactic frame: monovalent for existence and bivalent for possession. Also when the exponent iss-lTHERE IS expresses possession it takes a personal substantive as a semantic agent and generally does not allow a locational adjunct. As the number of arguments can be a cu·e for the correct interpretation of the meaning of iss-lTHERE IS, the subject should not be omitted in the Korean-based NSM when it may cause ambiguity of meaning.
VIII. Although the word an is proposed as the Korean counterpart of INSIDE, another word, sok, which is known to be synonymous with an has not been included as an allolex. This is because their relationship has not been clearly determined. There are instances in which only one can occur, as well as others where both are allowed to be used. The qualitative difference of the environments where these exponents can occur has not been discovered. For the purpose of the Korean metalanguage, however, the word an can be used without any difficulty.
IX. Several portmanteaus, either optional or obligatory, are identified: ilehkey or ichelem for the combination of i/THIS and kath-!LIKE, hangsang (or nu!) for the combination of motun/ALL and ttay!TIME, eps for an/NOT and iss-lTHERE IS, mos for an/NOT and (u)l swu (ka)iss-!CAN, and molu- for NOT and KNOW.
313

Chapter 6
6.1 .2. Syntactic characteristics
I. There are numerous hypothesised combinatorial patterns of primitives that are posited as universal (cf. Goddard and Wierzbicka (eds.), 1994, 2002). All the proposed combinations are tested against Korean and they are generally upheld. Incompatibility is found only in the quasi-substantive use of i/THIS and the predicate use of coh-!GOOD.
First, the exponent i/THIS has to be used in combination with substantives in order to function as a substantive. Second, the predicate use of coh-!GOOD may cause ambiguity of meaning between positive evaluation and psychological attraction when used with a psychological substantive. Therefore, it is not a case of syntactic restriction of the Korean exponent coh-!GOOD as a predicate, but rather of a need for clarifying the
proposed primitive meaning by avoiding any ambiguity in meaning. In order to avoid
ambiguity of meaning, coh- has to be used attributively in combination with a modified noun.
II. It is noted that the Korean exponents of determiners, such as THJS, THE SAME, and OTHER should have overt substantives, for they are adnominal when they
are used as determiners. The need of substituting ku for i/rHIS according to pragmatic textual requirement is pointed out. However, i is replaced in conjunction with the meaning analysis of ku, which is a non-primitive concept.
One more point deserving comment in relation to determiners is th~ fact that the
usual canonical sentences were not sufficient to identify the conceptual equivalents in Korean. For instance, the Korean exponent i/THIS has been identified through more examples providing a wider context. Out of context the meaning difference between three different Korean demonstratives could not have been discovered. Also the proposed primitive meaning of THE SAME (kath-) appears to be easily identifiable in a context constituted by more than one sentence. For instance, the meaning of THE SAME is clearer when there is a previously given sentence, as in the following sentences:
Something bad happened to me. The same thing can happen to you. Something like that can happen to you.

Canonical sentences with context would be useful to clarify the proposed primitive meanings, particularly that of determiners, which often rely upon contextual clues in natural discourse as well.
III. Some predicates are found to take adverbial complements rather than the combination of attributive and substantive. For instance, the predicate think (in a phrase like think something good/bad about someone) preferably takes cohkey/nappukey (well of/badly) rather than ihe noun phrase cohun/nappun kes (something good/bad) as its complement in Korean in order to express the conceptual equivalent of the English canonical sentence. This is parallel to the predicates SAY and FEEL.
IV. It has been observed that when the concept of SOMETHING is used as a complement of various Korean exponents of predicates (such as tut-lHEAR, sayngkakha-lTHINK, malha-lS.AY, ilena- (sayngki-)IHAPPEN, and ha-lDO), it tends to be specified as much as possible at least by the nature of the used predicates. For instance, when the concept of SOMETHING is a complement of tut-lHEAR (I can hear something), rather than the proposed exponent mwues (mwuesinka), which is a nonreferential term, another word soli (sound) is more readily used. In the same pattern, for the predicate SAY, the preferred choice for the complement is mal!WORD (e.g. I said something good/bad about you), for the predicate DO and HAPPEN, it is il (event or work), for the predicate FEEL, it is nukkim (feeling), and so forth. The substitution of the stylistically preferred forms makes the sentence read more naturally and idiomatically.
V. Regarding the Korean exponent nukki-lFEEL, I proposed two possibilities to express the phrase feel (something) good/bad: (i) use the combination of
315

Chapter 6
attributive coh-lnappu- (good/bad), and nukkim (feeling) as the complement of the proposed Korean exponent nukki-lFEEL (i.e. cohun/nappun nukkimul nukkita: feel good/bad feeling), or (ii) use nominal kipwun (mood or feeling) combined with coh- and nappu- used predicatively (i.e. kipwuni cohta/napputa: feeling is good/bad). However, other means of expressing the equivalent of feeling (something) good/bad are pointed out as natural expressions: e.g. nukkimi tulta (feeling comes into) or nukkimul kackeytoyta (become to have feeling). The way of expressing one's feeling in Korean, whether positive or negative, seems to be somewhat different from that of English expressed by the exponent FEEL. From the Korean perspective, one seems to view the feeling as 'something that happens to one' who experiences the consequence of happening rather than 'something that one does actively (feeling)'. This view seems to be reflected in the grammatical construction (passive or causative) as mentioned previously. It is not clear whether the different grammatical construction in relation to FEEL can be considered as semantic resonance. This deserves further research.
6.1 .3. Transposing two NSM texts in Korean metalanguage
The cross-linguistic comparability of the NSM texts is tested through the exercise of transposing two scripts, 'Good Samaritan' and 'Philosophy of the Middle Way' in Chapter 4. The expressive power of the established Korean metalanguage at textual level was found to be satisfactory. However, a few points relevant to naturalness of the Korean-based NSM have been discussed. Following is the summary of the observations made.
First, it has been point~d out that the overt use of the first and second person pronouns in Korean-based NSM is pragmatically marked. Secondly, the sentence with the dummy subject 'it' of the English-based NSM text has been transposed into Korean with a different sentence structure, which has no counterpart of the dummy subject. Thirdly, the restricted use of predicate coh-!GOOD, resulting from its second meaning has been mentioned in relation to transposing a sentence like this is good that appears in Text B. Fourth, the use of different determiners (not only i/THIS but also ku) according to Korean textual requirements has been mentioned, based on the findings in Chapter 3 (section 3.3.1). Finally, the natural way of expressing feeling something good in Korean has been discussed leaving the question whether these expressions are conceptually equivalent to the canonical sentences, open to further study. Although these points have been dealt with in Chapter 3, where the lexicalisation and syntactic property of each
316

Chapter 6
primitive are discussed, they re-emerged in the exercise of transposing the English texts, which suggests the importance of dealing with them in practice.
Overall, the two NSM texts were transposed into the Korean-based metalanguage without any significant loss or alteration of their meanings. The exercise has indicated that the expressive power of the two NSMs (English and Korean based) is not different. The texts in the Korean metalanguage are not as natural as ordinary texts, just as the texts in the English-based metalanguage sound less natural and less elegant than ordinary texts. However, stylistic awkwardness in semantic paraphrase of the unfamiliar means of expression should not be confused with inappropriateness of the metalanguage as long as the metalanguge expounds concepts clearly and accurately.
6.2. Remarks on explicating Korean concepts by the NSM method
The possibility of utilising the NSM method of semantic analysis as a tool for explicating Korean concepts is explored in Chapter 5. Five selected concepts are . explicated by the NSM method and their cognitive content is shown explicitly. As a result, the practicality of the NSM framework for a semantic analysis of specific Korean concepts has been found to be satisfactory. The summary of the conclusions 1s as follows:
Firstly, it has been found that the five complex concepts can be decomposed and paraphrased by the proposed semantic primitives, and that the posited explications are unambiguous and not circular. The two terms closely related to Korean folk psychology, maum and kasum, were found to be distinctive and to have no conceptual equivalents in English although they are often translated as the 'mind' or the 'heart' in literature. The different cognitive scenarios of three anger-like terms, hwa, wulhwa, and ppichi-ta, are also presented. The qualitative differences as well as similarities between the explicated concepts are demonstrated.
The explications are composed of various semantic components and are considerably different from the pattern of ordinary dictionary definitions or from other types of formal analysis. One may feel awkward about the unfamiliar way of representing meaning by the NSM framework, especially if one is familiar with highly technical conventions of other formal approaches. As NSM is also a metalanguage,
317

Chapter 6
although derived from natural language, it seems to require reasonable time to learn and get used to for people to handle the different way of expressing meaning.
The posited explications seem to be meaningful in two respects: (i) they attempt to articulate the meanings of culture-specific concepts, which are normally defined in circular or abstract modes, in simple and self-explanatory terms. Accordingly, the posited explications are testable and verifiable by ordinary speakers of the Korean language, for they are at least intelligible. And (ii), all the proposed formulas are submitted as a reference point from which further improvements can proceed. They are open to further study so that they can be revised until an optimal version is reached.
Explications of the selected Korean concepts are presented in two language based NSMs, offering further support for the inter-translatability between NSMs based on different languages. From a formalist's perspective this view may not be convincing due to the specificity of the surface grammar of the two languages. However, this thesis concerns the underlying grammar that is conceptual and semantic and it provides a further support for the claim of the NSM theory that the grammar of the metalanguage is based on meaning.
It has been shown that the NSM method enables different concepts to be compared with the same measure. For instance, the Korean concept of maum can be compared to components of meaning that are part of the explications of the Japanese concept of kokoro, the Malay concept of hati, as well as the English concept of 'heart'. Consequently, people from different cultural and language backgrounds _may come to understand the folk views of various cultures through concepts that are closely linked to each other.
6.3. The implications for further study
Apart from these matters that have already been mentioned elsewhere in this thesis with respect to further study, a few areas are suggested by the present research.
The most relevant issue observed in the course of transposing NSM texts into the Korean metalanguage and of explicating Korean concepts by using the NSM framework, seems to be the naturalness of the Korean-based NSM. In order to utilise the metalanguage as a tool for semantic analysis, various kinds of language-specific features, such as using a pragmatically unmarked form (i.e. malssum for ma!), choosing conventionalised expressions in accommodating Korean textual requirements (e.g.
318

Chapter 6
replacing ku for i on the basis of the adjunct meaning analysis), or rephrasing in a natural way (e.g. feeling (something) good/bad) should be closely examined in further study. The issue of trading between 'naturalness' and 'authenticism' of the metalanguage and of the readers' interests in better comprehension seems to arise here. One can persist using only primitives regardless of the pragmatic oddness, or at the same time one can indigenise the explications for ordinary speakers familiar only with conventionalised expressions. If the more idiomatic expressions do not alter the meaning of the canonical sentences or explications in any explicable (discrete and specifiable) way, they may be regarded as semantically equivalent. However, if a semantic difference is found between the natural expressions and the strictly transposed expressions, the difference has to be paraphrased via primitives. Then the natural expressions can replace the authentic NSM ones on the basis of the simultaneous meaning analysis. Since the metalanguage is not a natural language, one should not expect the texts in the metalanguage to read as naturally as ordinary texts. However, using more natural expressions (on the basis of a clear meaning definition) can enhance the readability of NSM texts. Not only the formal aspects of metalanguage, but also issues related to the naturalness of different language-based metalanguages should be accounted for in the development of the NSM theory.
There have been several changes in the NSM inventory of primitives and hypothesised combinations of primitives since this research began. There are new tentative primitives added recently because of their usefulness in capturing various notions crucial for semantic analysis. Several amendments have been made mainly due to counterexamples from empirical testing in various languages. 119 Details concerning the new developments in relation to syntax of the primitives are given in Meaning and Universal Grammar (Goddard, 2002c) in which numerous innovations that need crosslinguistic empirical testing are illustrated. These innovations are worth exploring in further study, in addition to those that were discussed and suggested for further examination in Chapter 3. The NSM theory has been developed on the basis of crosslinguistic investigations and is still developing by testing its hypotheses against a wider range of languages and by experimenting with its practicality in semantic analysis. Therefore, any further research in Korean based on the new hypotheses will contribute to the description of the universal grammar.
119 For example, the direct combination of NOW and BEFORE or AFTER is no longer valid and PERSON as an allolex of PEOPLE is also revised.
319

Chapter 6
The constructed Korean-based NSM can be utilised as an aid for language teachers and learners. The lexicon and .syntax of this subset of Korean is relatively simple and easy to learn. Since languages differ enormously in terms of surface grammar, language teachers often have difficulties in explaining various languagespecific features and grammatical categories. However, apart from the lexical meanings, grammatical relations, interjections, discourse routines and metaphors can also be explained by the NSM method to those who have only a limited knowledge of Korean. If the NSM method can be developed and applied in articulating the meaning of language-specific grammatical categories, it may make a remarkable contribution to the
area of second language acquisition. It is interesting to note the results of a survey 1989
on frequency of use of Korean words, conducted by the Yensei University dictionary publishing laboratory (Lee P, 1999). This survey shows that there is a correlation between the frequently used Korean words and the proposed Korean exponents of the semantic primitives:
iss (there is) (30.625), toy (become) (12,241), eps (there is not) (11.222), anh- (not) (10248), ha- (do) (7,959), kath- (like)(5481), khu- (big) (4,111), manh- (many) (3,762), mos ha- (cannot do) (3,692), malha-(say) (2,771), coh- (good)(2,673), pat- (receive) (2,465),,·poi- (be seen)(2,465), ani-(not be)(2,414), sayngkakha- (think) (2,279) (Lee P, 1999: 4). 120
This seems to suggest that the proposed Korean metalanguage can be practical in the area of second language acquisition. Therefore a way of utilising the Xorean-based NSM with respect to the area of teaching Korean as a second language can be explored in further study.
Although the utility of the NSM method as a tool for semantic analysis of Korean concepts has been explored in this research, it has been limited to a few concepts. This analysis can be expanded to more expressions including emotion terms, proverbs (Korean has a wealth of proverbs), metaphors, interjections, and communicative strategies (important in particular with respect to politeness). The potential of the NSM method can be experimented with in various semantic domains including different parts of speech Korean (e.g. particles and sentence ending markers), non-verbal expressions (facial expressions and ritualistic gestures), and human relationships (e.g. within the same age group and with different people in different social status or kinship relationships). Then on the basis of an exhaustive semantic analysis within the NSM
120 Result of the survey based on corpus (Seo, 1998) also shows a similar tendency that the proposed Korean exponents are ranked high in the frequency or use.
320

Chapter 6
framework Korean cultural norms, tactics and values can be represented. Consequently folk-philosophy and folk-psychology of Korean people can be elucidated for outsiders. Systematic research on various aspects of Korean language and culture may bring about profitable results that can significantly contribute to inter-cultural communication and cross-cultural understanding.

Appendix Canonical sentences for NSM primes

Cliff Goddard and Anna Wierzbicka, January 2001.
This list can be used as an aid to identifying exponents of semantic primes, for testing hypotheses about the universality of certain combinatorial patterns involving semantic primes, and for eliciting a semantically-based sample of the core grammar of any language.
Please note that despite its length, the list is not exhaustive. There is a good deal of overlap between the sections.

SUBSTANTIVES: SOMEONE, SOMETHING/THING, PEOPLE (PERSON), BODY

Someone took it. I don't know who. I saw someone there.
[Often this context will favour an expression like 'one person' or 'one human being' rather than the pure indefinite 'someone'.]
Who did it? This person is a good/bad person. These people are (not) like other people. All people do this. Many people don't know this. People think that snakes are clever (or: some commonly held view). If I do this, people will say bad things about me. What happened? What is this? I see something over there. Something bad (good) happened. I did something bad. Sometimes bad things happen to (good) people I see two things. I said something. I want to say something to you. Two things. This thing is big/small. This thing moved. Something happened to a part of John's body. The spleen (stomach, etc.) is part of the body. There is something inside this person's body. The body of a man is not like the body of a woman.

MENTAL PREDICATES: THINK, KNOW, WANT, FEEL, SEE, HEAR
People think (that) this is bad. I think (that) she is asleep, but I don't know. I thought (that) it was a possum (snake, bat, etc.), but I wasn't sure. I thought about this for a long time. This person thinks something bad about me.

322

Appendix
We know (that) this is bad. I know (that) he went this way. I don't know where he is now. This person knows something about me. I know (don't know) many things about him. I know (don't know) that person. I know this person (place) well.
[The word for 'knowing (someone)' will often be different to the word for 'knowing 'something). The relationship between these two is not yet settled.]
I want something. I want (don't want) this.
I want to go to the village (market/beach etc.).
I want to be with my grandfather. I want (don't want) you to do it. I want (don't want) this to happen. When that happened (or: when I heard that), I felt something good/bad. I feel like this. At that time (then), I didn't feel anything. I feel something (bad, strange, etc.) in this part of my body (chest, stomach,
etc.) I saw something (a dog, bird, etc.) over there. I hear something (over there in the bushes. She was singing - I heard it. When I heard those words, I felt something bad.
SPEECH: SAY, WORDS
[There may be several common words in this domain. Try to find the most versatile one. Complex but common "saying-words" may have meanings like 'say to someone', 'say about something', 'say some things to someone'.]
'This is good', I said. 'Ouch/damn', he/she said. He said the same. He said something to me, but I didn't hear it. He said it to Mary, not to me, but I know that he wanted me to hear it. Did he say anything about these people? Don't say that word! She said the same thing, but in other (i.e. different) words.
In Language X, the word for 'good' is palya. These people's (e.g. another tribe's) word for Xis 'palya'.
If you say one more word, .... That (i.e. something someone said) is not true. Mary said such-and-such (e.g. that Max did it), but John knew that this was not
true (or: something true). These words (e.g. a proverb or other "wisdom saying") say something true. If is good for people if they hear these words.
323

Appendix
ACTIONS, EVENTS, MOVEMENT, CONTACT: DO, HAPPEN, MOVE, TOUCH
What did you do? I want to do the same. You did something good/bad. I don't know what he did (to you). She did something bad to me/you. Those people did something good for me. What do people do with things like this (spoon, needle, etc.)? He did this with a knife (axe, stick, etc.). I did it in the same place. I did it with my brother. What happened? Something good/bad happened to this person (to me). What happened to this (thing)? Something bad happened in that place. If this happens, I don't know what I will do. What will happen to you if you have no food? The same thing happened today. (Look!) Something is moving over there. Don't move! (e.g. while putting on a bandaid or removing headlice) I was very scared. I couldn't move. This thing (e.g. cup) is touching this other thing (e.g. vase). Something was touching my hand. (I could feel it.)
[The intended sense of 'touch' is simply physical contact between one thing and another. Some languages have a separate item for one person "touching' another person with the implication of some potential social or emotional significance.]
EXISTENCE AND POSSESSION: THERE IS/EXIST, HAVE/BELONG
[Some languages have several "stance" or "posture" verbs which may serve existential functions, at least, where something concrete is being located in space. But we expect there will be one with a broader range of application, which can be used about nonphysical things.]
(Look!) There are baby birds in that nest. (Look!) There is someone in the garden. There is/are no [e.g. water/spiders] here. There are no ghosts (unicorns, etc.). There are many kinds of nuts (yams, bats. etc.). There are no baby birds in this nest, but there are some in that other nest. John has a knife (dog, etc.). This thing (knife, dog, etc.) belongs to John. This person has many things. I don't have anything.
[The "possessive" sense we are interested in is roughly that of ownership of physical things.]
324

Appendix
LIFE AND DEATH: LIVE, DIE
(In story of olden days) These people lived for a long time. These two people lived at the same time. At that time she was living with her sister. Turtles live for a long time. Fish live in the sea.
[In many languages, a meaning approximating 'dwell' cannot expressed as in English
by 'live' + place adjunct; however, it may be that 'live' allows a place adjunct in
generic contexts as above.]
(After e.g. spearing a kangaroo) Is it is still alive. No, it's dead. His dog died. Jesus died a long time ago.
[In many cultures, the topic of death and dying has to be spoken of with care, so it may be necessary to cast around to find less sensitive situations.]

DETERMINERS: THIS, THE SAME, OTHER
[In some languages, the form for 'this' cannot occur in a substantive-like function by itself, but only in combination with a substantive word such as 'thing' (or a variant).]
(They know about it.) This (thing) is good/bad. This is a big/small thing (dog, basket, etc.). I did this (thing). This (thing) happened to me. This (thing) is like this other thing. (I saw) this other person; these two people; all these people. He did it (sang, danced, etc.) like this. (I saw) the same person; the same people. It happened at the same place/time, not at another place/time. She did/said the same (the same thing). She thought/wanted/felt the same. I did the same as you. (It was John, but) she thought (that) it was someone else. (After that), I saw another thing (something else). I didn't do it here- I did it in another place (somewhere else). (about something with many parts) This is one part. This is another part. This is not a sparrow (robin, magpie, etc.) It is another kind of bird.

QUANTIFIERS: ONE, TWO, MANY, SOME, ALL
I saw you and two other people. She has one child (two children). They all died. All his sons/daughters are now married. Because of all this, I was late (missed the train, etc.) This person knows all about it. (No-one lives forever.) Everybody (all people) dies. Everyone (all people) knows it is bad to do this. The fire burnt everything (e.g. in the house, on the island)

325

Appendix
(From the top of the mountain) I could see everything (below me). God made everything. How many children does she have? She has many daughters. I didn't say much. How much water is there (in that well/bowl etc.)? There is much/a lot of water here. Not many people live here. There are many places like this. (There were some people there.) After that, one/two of these people (one/two of them) left. Some of these people moved to another camp, and some of them stayed. Many of them went to the east, but some of them went to the west. Some people can do this- some people can't. Some of these yams (lemons, etc.) are good, but some are rotten.
In some places there are lots of fish (but it is not good to fish here).
EVALUATORS:GOODBAD
[Many other examples are scattered throughout the list.] Good people do not do things like this. This was good/bad for me. It is good/bad if someone does this. It is not good (bad) if someone says this about other people. It is good (not good) if people can do something like this.
[Needless to say, it is not expected that other languages will have anything resembling the English "dummy subject" 'it'. Also, it is not necessarily expected that a dependent clause in the scope of 'good' or 'bad' (e.g. it is good/bad if this happens) will necessarily be introduced by the equivalent of 'if.]
If this happens, it will be bad for you. It will be good for you if you do this. It will be bad for you if you do this. I know this person (place) well. Some people can do this well, some people can't.

DESCRIPTORS: BIG, SMALL
I saw a big/small tree. I saw a big/small dog (bird, etc.).

TIME: TIME/WHEN, NOW, BEFORE, AFTER, A LONG TIME, A SHORT TIME,
FORSOMETIME,MOMENT

When did you do it? I did it when everyone was asleep. I don't know when it happened. At that time, I didn't know anything about it. At the same time, something happened to me. When we went to the zoo, we... When I did this, I felt something bad.

326

Appendix
I know something about that time (e.g. some time, period, season, etc.). At times like these, it is good not to do (say) anything. Sometimes dogs bark at night, sometimes they don't (bark). This happened two times (twice). I did this many times (often). It happened many times. This dog always barks at night. They live in X now. They lived in Y before (this). It happened beforehand/ afterwards. After this, she got very sick.
[It is not expected that 'before' and 'after' will be adpositional in syntax. Adverbial syntax is probably more universal. The status of 'before' and 'after' as "clause linkers, as in the example below, is unsettled.]
This happened before/after you were born (before/after you left, etc.). This happened a long time ago. We walked for a long time, but we still didn't get there. He lived here for a long time. She slept for a long time/for a short time. He lived in that place for some time. She thought about it for some time He (only) stayed there for a short time, but she stayed there for a long time. A short time after (that), I heard a noise. I did these things some time ago (i~e. some time before now). At that moment something happened. A moment after, something else happened. I saw him/her at that moment. I didn't see him/her the moment before
SPACE: PLACE/WHERE, HERE, ABOVE, BELOW, INSIDE, ON (THIS) SIDE,
INSIDE, NEAR, FAR
Where did you do it? I did it here. Where is he now? I don't know where he is. He is here. (At that time) I was somewhere else (in another place). Is it far from here to that place [e.g. Gundaroo]? (I am sad because) I am far from my mother. That knife (basket, etc.) is (too) far from me- I can't reach it. Was Fred standing near John? I live near here. At first, she was standing far from him; then she sat down near him. There were black clouds above us. The sky is above everything. This thing is above/below this other thing.
[There may be several words expressing positional relationships, cf. English 'above' vs. 'over'; 'below' vs. 'under' vs. 'beneath' vs. 'underneath'. Try to find one which is purely about relative position, without implying anything about visibility or "covering", e.g. re. two flags on a flagpole.]
327

Appendix
John is now [standing/sitting] on this side (of me). Mary was [standing/sitting] on my right hand side, and Sally was on my left
hand side. There were people standing on both sides of the road. The fire was burning on all sides (of us). There is an insect inside this [e.g. walnut]. Some possums made their nest inside a hollow tree. This cave is very big-lots of people could live inside it. It happened inside the house (cave, etc.)
LOGICAL CONCEPTS: BECAUSE, IF, NOT, MAYBE, CAN
There was a lot of noise; because of this, I couldn't sleep. The dog died because the man hit it on the head (not because of something else). If it rains tomorrow, I won't come. If you do this, people will say something bad about you. If he hits you, don't hit him back. If he comes here, don't open the door. I didn't do it (see it, etc.). I don't want you to do it. Maybe it will rain tomorrow. Maybe he will come tomorrow, maybe he won't (come tomorrow). I don't know. (Who broke the pot?) Maybe John did it. (Where's Mary?) I don't know. Maybe John knows. I can't do it now, but maybe my brother can. I can't do it now, but I can do it later. I can't do it now but I could do it before. If I could do it, I would do it, but I can't. I can't not do this. Why did you do that? - Something bad could have happened to you. Bad things can happen to everyone/good people. This thing can move.
INTENSIFIER, AUGMENTOR: VERY, MORE
It is very good/bad. It is very big/small. This place [e.g. Gundaroo] is very far from here. It happened a very long time ago. He lived there for a very long time. I very much want to do this. More! (e.g. a child crying for food) (I don't know much about it) I want to know more. Don't say anything more (about it)! I want to see more. I want more. I ate a lot quickly, and then I didn't want any more.
TAXONOMY AND PARTONOMY: KIND OF PART OF
There are two kinds of yam (bat, etc.). There are many kinds of fish (bird, etc.). (of two kinds of fish, fruit, etc.) This kind of fish is good to eat. This (other)
kind is not good to eat.
328

Appendix
Two kinds of people live here. A blade is part of a knife (and/or: a knife has a blade). An axe has a handle. (and/or: a handle is a part of an axe). This thing has two parts (and/or: there are two parts to this thing). This thing has many parts. (and/or: there are many parts to this thing). All of these parts are small.
SIMILARITY: LIKE
You are like me. I am not like other people. This is like lilac (or: any plant), but it is not lilac. He thought (that) someone like me could do it. At a time like this; in a place like this. He did it like this: ... It happened like this. These two things were moving like this. She said it like this: .. It is good if you good things for other people, like you want other people to do
good things for you.
329

The examples used, mainly in Chapter 5, are taken from various sources: popular song verses, novels, newspaper articles, novels, two corpora, and online manuscripts. These sources are indicated either by titles or by the author's name when available. When these are not specified, (especially when they are from web sites) the web directories are indicated with the last day of access.
